DevOps Interview Cheatsheet

Complete guide to Docker (25 questions) and Kubernetes (32 questions) interview preparation. Organized by topic with practical examples and confident talking points.

Docker 01

Fundamentals

Docker Fundamentals

What's the difference between RUN, CMD, and ENTRYPOINT?

These three are often confused. RUN executes during build, CMD sets default command, ENTRYPOINT is what actually runs. Understanding this is critical for container execution.

Q&ABuild vs runtime›
RUN
  • Executes during image build (not at runtime)
  • Creates a new layer
  • Example: RUN apt-get install -y nginx
  • Used for: installing packages, setting up environment
CMD
  • Sets default command when container runs
  • Can be overridden by docker run arguments
  • Example: CMD ["python", "app.py"]
  • Used for: default behavior if no command specified
ENTRYPOINT
  • The main executable when container starts
  • Cannot be easily overridden
  • Example: ENTRYPOINT ["python", "app.py"]
  • Used for: making container behave like an executable
Key Difference
  • RUN: build time → creates layers
  • CMD: runtime → can be overridden
  • ENTRYPOINT: runtime → main process (PID 1)
Say this in interview "RUN executes during build and creates layers. CMD sets the default command at runtime but can be overridden by docker run. ENTRYPOINT is the main process that runs when container starts. I use ENTRYPOINT for the main app and CMD for default arguments."
Docker Fundamentals

How do you handle environment variables in Dockerfile?

Environment variables configure containers for different environments. There are multiple ways to set them, each with tradeoffs.

Q&AENV, ARG, and runtime›
ENV (Environment Variables)
  • Set at build time, persists in container at runtime
  • Can be overridden with docker run -e
  • Example: ENV DATABASE_URL=localhost:5432
  • Used for: configuration that doesn't change per environment
ARG (Build Arguments)
  • Only available during build, not at runtime
  • Pass with: docker build --build-arg VERSION=1.0
  • Example: ARG VERSION=latest
  • Used for: build-specific settings (not runtime config)
Runtime Override
  • docker run -e LOG_LEVEL=debug (override single var)
  • docker run --env-file .env (load from file)
  • Override ENV from Dockerfile at runtime
Say this in interview "Use ENV for configuration that might differ per environment (dev vs prod). Use ARG for build-time settings only. At runtime, override with -e flag or --env-file. Never hardcode secrets in ENV — use external secret management instead."
Docker 02

Image Optimization

Docker Optimization

What's a multi-stage build? When do you use it?

Multi-stage builds dramatically reduce image size by separating build dependencies from runtime. This is how production images should be built.

Q&ABuild vs runtime stages›
What It Does
  • Multiple FROM statements in one Dockerfile
  • Build dependencies in stage 1, only runtime artifacts in stage 2
  • Final image only contains what's needed to run
Example (Go app)
# Stage 1: Build
FROM golang:1.20 AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY . .
RUN go build -o app

# Stage 2: Runtime (minimal)
FROM alpine:latest
COPY --from=builder /app/app .
CMD ["./app"]
Size Reduction
  • Without multi-stage: 800MB (includes Go compiler, build tools)
  • With multi-stage: 50MB (only the binary)
  • 16x smaller image!
When to Use
  • Always for compiled languages (Go, Java, Rust, C++)
  • When build dependencies != runtime dependencies
  • When you need to keep images small for fast deployment
Say this in interview "Multi-stage builds let me separate build dependencies from runtime. Builder stage has compilers and tools, final stage only has the binary. This reduces image size from 800MB to 50MB. I always use this for compiled languages."
Docker Optimization

How do you optimize a Docker image for size?

Smaller images mean faster deployments, less storage, faster pulls from registry. Multiple strategies to reduce bloat.

Q&APractical techniques›
Start with Small Base Image
  • alpine:latest (5MB) vs ubuntu:latest (77MB)
  • Use distroless images (Google) for production (20-50MB)
  • Example: gcr.io/distroless/base (just LIBC + app)
Layer Optimization
  • Combine RUN commands to reduce layers: RUN apt update && apt install -y pkg1 pkg2 && apt clean
  • Clean up package managers: rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
  • Each layer adds overhead
Multi-stage Builds
  • Already covered above
  • Biggest impact for compiled languages
.dockerignore
  • Exclude unnecessary files: .git, node_modules, tests, docs
  • Reduces build context size
Say this in interview "Start with alpine or distroless base images. Use multi-stage builds. Combine RUN commands to reduce layers. Clean up package managers. Use .dockerignore to exclude unnecessary files. These techniques together can reduce images by 80%."
Docker Optimization

Your Docker image is 2GB. How do you reduce it?

2GB is huge. This is a practical troubleshooting scenario where you need to find and eliminate bloat.

ScenarioDebugging oversized images›
Step 1: Identify What's Taking Space
# Use docker inspect to see layers
docker inspect --format='{{json .RootFS.Layers}}' image-name

# Use dive tool for detailed layer analysis
dive image-name

# Manual investigation
docker run --rm -it image-name bash
du -sh /* | sort -h  # Find largest directories
Common Culprits
  • /var/cache (package manager cache) - 500MB+
  • /tmp (build artifacts left behind) - 300MB+
  • /root/.npm or /root/.m2 (dependency caches) - 400MB+
  • Multiple copies of files (COPY in multiple layers)
  • Unnecessary build tools in final image
Fixes
  • Clean package managers: apt clean && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
  • Clear caches: rm -rf /tmp/* /root/.npm /root/.m2
  • Use multi-stage build to exclude build deps
  • Switch to alpine base image
  • Use .dockerignore to exclude large directories
Say this in interview "First, I use 'dive' or docker inspect to find what's taking space. Usually it's package caches or build artifacts. I clean those up. Then I switch to alpine base, use multi-stage builds, and optimize layers. This usually gets 2GB down to 400-500MB."
Docker Optimization

How do you optimize build time for Docker images?

Slow builds block CI/CD pipelines. Multiple strategies to speed up builds.

Q&ACaching and parallelization›
Leverage Layer Caching
  • Docker caches layers. If nothing changed, layer is reused.
  • Put frequently changing files last in Dockerfile
  • Example: Install deps first, then COPY source code
  • Bad: COPY . . then RUN npm install (cache bust on any file change)
  • Good: COPY package.json . then RUN npm install then COPY . .
Buildkit (Docker BuildKit)
  • docker buildx build (newer, parallel build stages)
  • Parallelizes independent layers
  • Faster cache lookups
  • Enable: export DOCKER_BUILDKIT=1
.dockerignore
  • Exclude files so they don't get sent to docker daemon
  • Smaller build context = faster
Say this in interview "Order Dockerfile commands by change frequency: stable things first (OS updates, package installs), then dependencies (package.json), then source code. Use BuildKit for parallelization. Exclude unnecessary files with .dockerignore. These reduce build time from 10 minutes to 2 minutes."
Docker Optimization

What's the impact of running multiple services in one container?

One container = one process. Running multiple services violates Docker principles and causes operational headaches.

Anti-patternWhy this is bad›
Problems with Multiple Services
  • No isolation: If one service crashes, whole container dies
  • Hard to scale: Want to scale web server but not DB? Impossible in one container
  • Complex logging: Logs from both services mixed together, hard to parse
  • Resource management: Can't limit resources per service
  • Startup order: Service A must start before B. Fragile.
  • Health checks: Which service determines container health?
Better Approach
  • One Dockerfile per service
  • One container per service (or pod in K8s)
  • Orchestrate with docker-compose or Kubernetes
Say this in interview "One service per container. Multiple services violate isolation principle, make scaling hard, complicate logging. Use docker-compose or Kubernetes to orchestrate."
Docker 03

Container Operations

Docker Operations

How do you view logs from a running container?

Logs are your first tool for debugging. Know multiple ways to access them.

Q&ADebugging container output›
Basic Commands
  • docker logs container-name (view all logs)
  • docker logs -f container-name (follow logs, like tail -f)
  • docker logs --tail 50 container-name (last 50 lines)
  • docker logs --since 2024-01-20 container-name (since timestamp)
Interactive Shell
  • docker exec -it container-name bash (attach shell)
  • Then: tail -f /var/log/app.log (view logs from inside)
  • Or: cat /var/log/app.log | grep ERROR
Production: Centralized Logging
  • Don't rely on docker logs for production
  • Send logs to: ELK (Elasticsearch), Loki, Splunk
  • Container logs are lost when container is deleted
Say this in interview "Use 'docker logs -f' to see real-time output. For debugging, docker exec into container and tail the log file. In production, send logs to a centralized system because docker logs are ephemeral—they're deleted when the container stops."
Docker Operations

How do you stop vs kill a container? What's the difference?

Both stop containers, but they work very differently. Understanding this matters for graceful shutdowns.

Q&ASIGTERM vs SIGKILL›
docker stop (Graceful)
  • Sends SIGTERM signal to main process (PID 1)
  • App has 10 seconds to shut down gracefully
  • Close DB connections, save state, clean up
  • After 10 seconds: forcefully kills if still running
  • Used for: normal shutdown
docker kill (Immediate)
  • Sends SIGKILL signal (force kill, cannot be caught)
  • Process has zero chance to clean up
  • Connections abruptly closed, data might be lost
  • Used for: when stop doesn't work, emergency shutdown
Timeline
  • docker stop → SIGTERM → app cleans up (0-10s) → SIGKILL (if timeout)
  • docker kill → SIGKILL immediately
Say this in interview "'docker stop' sends SIGTERM and waits 10 seconds for graceful shutdown. 'docker kill' sends SIGKILL immediately. Always use stop unless necessary. App should catch SIGTERM to clean up: close connections, save state, finish in-flight requests."
Docker Operations

How do you implement graceful shutdown in a containerized application?

Graceful shutdown prevents data loss and ensures clean termination. Critical for production apps.

Q&ASignal handling & cleanup›
Signal Handling
  • Catch SIGTERM (docker stop sends this)
  • Stop accepting new requests
  • Wait for in-flight requests to complete
  • Close database connections
  • Flush caches, save state
  • Exit cleanly
Example (Node.js)
const http = require('http');
const server = http.createServer((req, res) => {
  res.writeHead(200);
  res.end('OK');
});

process.on('SIGTERM', async () => {
  console.log('SIGTERM received, starting graceful shutdown');
  server.close(() => {
    console.log('Server closed');
    process.exit(0);
  });

  // Force exit after 30 seconds
  setTimeout(() => process.exit(1), 30000);
});

server.listen(3000);
Say this in interview "Catch SIGTERM in the app. Stop accepting new requests immediately, then wait for in-flight requests to finish (up to grace period, e.g., 30s). Close DB connections cleanly. This prevents data loss and ensures clean shutdown."
Docker 04

Configuration

Docker Configuration

How do you handle application configuration in Docker (dev vs prod)?

Different environments need different configs. The wrong approach leads to hardcoding and security issues.

Q&AEnvironment-specific configs›
Bad Approaches
  • Hardcoding configs in code (never!)
  • Separate Dockerfiles per environment (duplicates)
  • Storing secrets in Dockerfile (HUGE security risk)
Good Approach: Environment Variables
  • One Dockerfile for all environments
  • Use ENV in Dockerfile for defaults
  • Override at runtime with: docker run -e VAR=value
  • Example: ENV LOG_LEVEL=info (can override to debug)
Better: Config Files + Env Variables
  • COPY config.yaml into image
  • App reads config.yaml, substitutes env vars
  • At runtime, mount different config.yaml for each env
  • Example: docker run -v prod-config.yaml:/app/config.yaml
Say this in interview "Single Dockerfile for all environments. Use environment variables to override defaults. Don't hardcode secrets—use external secret management. One image, multiple configurations per environment."
Docker Configuration

How should you handle secrets in Docker containers? (Don't hardcode them!)

Secrets are passwords, API keys, tokens. Hardcoding them is a critical security risk. Multiple safe approaches exist.

SecurityDon't hardcode secrets!›
NEVER Do This
  • RUN export DATABASE_PASSWORD=mysecret123 (baked into image)
  • COPY secret.txt into image (visible in docker history)
  • Commit secrets to Git
  • Print secrets in logs
Option 1: Environment Variables at Runtime
  • docker run -e DB_PASSWORD=$(cat secret.txt) app
  • Secret not in image, only in running container
  • Still visible in docker inspect and logs (not ideal)
Option 2: Docker Secrets (Swarm Mode)
  • docker secret create db_pass secret.txt
  • docker service create --secret db_pass app
  • Mounted as files in /run/secrets/db_pass
  • More secure than env vars
Option 3: External Secret Management (Best)
  • HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault
  • App fetches secrets at runtime from vault
  • Secret never stored in image or container
  • Can rotate without redeploying
  • Audit trail of who accessed what
Say this in interview "Never hardcode or bake secrets into Docker images. Best practice: app fetches secrets from external vault (AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault) at runtime. Always rotate secrets regularly and audit access."
Docker 05

Security

Docker Security

Explain Docker image scanning. Why is it important?

Image scanning finds vulnerabilities in dependencies before they reach production. Automated security defense.

SecurityVulnerability detection›
What It Does
  • Scans all layers, finds installed packages
  • Compares against CVE database (NVD, Snyk, Trivy)
  • Reports known vulnerabilities with severity (critical, high, medium, low)
  • Blocks deployment if critical vulns found
Why It Matters
  • Dependencies have CVEs (Log4j, OpenSSL, etc.)
  • If image has vulnerable library, production is at risk
  • Automated scanning catches this before humans ship it
  • Can be enforced in CI/CD pipeline (fail build if critical CVE)
Tools
  • Trivy (free, fast): trivy image myimage:latest
  • Snyk: online scanning, detailed reports
  • Grype: free vulnerability scanner
  • Docker Scout (built-in): docker scout cves myimage:latest
Say this in interview "I scan images with Trivy or Docker Scout to find vulnerable dependencies. Scanning happens in CI/CD—if critical CVE found, the build fails. Developer updates the dependency and rebuilds. This prevents vulnerable images from reaching production."
Docker Security

What's the principle of running containers as non-root?

Running as root means if container is compromised, attacker gets root access to host. Running as non-root limits blast radius.

SecurityLeast privilege principle›
Why Not Root
  • Root in container = root on host (in many setups)
  • If app is compromised, attacker has full control
  • Can modify files, install backdoors, escalate privileges
How to Run as Non-root
  • In Dockerfile: RUN useradd -m appuser
  • Then: USER appuser
  • Container runs as appuser, not root
Example Dockerfile
FROM python:3.10
RUN useradd -m appuser
WORKDIR /app
COPY app.py .
RUN chown -R appuser:appuser /app
USER appuser
CMD ["python", "app.py"]
Say this in interview "Always run containers as non-root. Create a user in Dockerfile (useradd), then USER to switch. This limits damage if the container is compromised."
Docker Security

How do you implement least privilege for container permissions?

Least privilege: give containers only the permissions they need. Nothing more.

SecurityMinimize blast radius›
File System Permissions
  • Read-only filesystem (except /tmp, /var/run)
  • Don't give write access to /
  • Specific mount points for write (volumes)
Capabilities (Linux Kernel)
  • Containers run with subset of kernel capabilities
  • Default: NET_BIND_SERVICE, SYS_CHROOT, SETFCAP
  • Drop: CAP_SYS_ADMIN (can't change system settings)
  • Docker example: docker run --cap-drop=ALL --cap-add=NET_BIND_SERVICE
Best Practices
  • Run as non-root user
  • Read-only root filesystem
  • Drop all Linux capabilities, add back only what's needed
  • No privilege escalation
  • Regular user (no sudo)
Say this in interview "I implement least privilege by: (1) running as non-root, (2) read-only root filesystem, (3) dropping all capabilities and adding back only NET_BIND_SERVICE, (4) no privilege escalation. This limits damage if the container is compromised."
Docker 06

Registry & Versioning

Docker Registry

Explain Docker Hub vs private registries

Docker Hub is public, free but not for production. Private registries are for enterprise security and compliance.

Q&APublic vs private storage›
Docker Hub
  • Public registry (hub.docker.com)
  • Free to push (with limits)
  • Official images (nginx, postgres, python)
  • Community images (less vetted)
  • Issue: images publicly visible, rate limiting
  • Good for: learning, open-source projects
Private Registries
  • Azure Container Registry (ACR)
  • AWS Elastic Container Registry (ECR)
  • Harbor, Artifactory (self-hosted)
  • Images are private (not public)
  • Authentication required (credentials, RBAC)
  • Image scanning built-in
  • Good for: production, enterprise, compliance
Say this in interview "Docker Hub is public and good for learning. For production, use private registries (ACR, ECR) for security, compliance, and scanning. Private registries require authentication, have better RBAC, no rate limits, and built-in vulnerability scanning."
Docker Registry

How do you tag Docker images? What's the naming convention?

Tags identify image versions. Good tagging practices prevent confusion and enable rollbacks.

Q&AVersioning strategy›
Tag Format
  • registry/repository:tag
  • Example: myregistry.azurecr.io/myapp:1.0.0
  • registry: where image is stored (Docker Hub, ACR, ECR)
  • repository: image name (myapp)
  • tag: version identifier (1.0.0, latest, dev-abc123)
Tagging Strategy
  • Semantic versioning: 1.0.0 (major.minor.patch)
  • Git commit SHA: dev-a1b2c3d4 (unique per commit)
  • Timestamp: 2024-01-20T10:30:00Z
  • Branch name: main, develop, feature-x
  • latest: moving tag for current version (risky in prod!)
Best Practice
  • Commit SHA for CI/CD builds: myapp:a1b2c3d4
  • Also tag with semantic version: myapp:1.0.0
  • Also tag with branch: myapp:main, myapp:develop
  • latest only for known-good versions (not dev builds)
Say this in interview "Tag images with commit SHA for CI/CD (unique, traceable), also tag with semantic version (1.0.0) and branch name. Avoid 'latest' in production unless it's tested. This enables easy rollbacks: know exactly which commit is running."
Docker Registry

What's the difference between tagging and versioning?

Tags and versions are different concepts; understanding the distinction prevents confusion.

Q&ASemantics matter›
Versioning
  • Semantic version: 1.0.0, 2.1.3
  • Follows spec: MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH
  • MAJOR: breaking changes
  • MINOR: backward-compatible features
  • PATCH: bug fixes
  • Managed in source code (package.json, version.txt)
Tagging
  • Identifier for image in registry
  • Can be: 1.0.0, main, a1b2c3d4, latest
  • Multiple tags can point to same image
  • Managed at build/deploy time
Example
  • Code version: 1.0.0 (in package.json)
  • Image tags: myapp:1.0.0, myapp:latest, myapp:a1b2c3d4
  • All three tags point to same image (built from version 1.0.0)
Say this in interview "Versioning (1.0.0) describes the software release. Tagging (myapp:1.0.0) identifies the image in the registry. One version can have multiple tags (myapp:1.0.0, myapp:latest, myapp:main). Tags are pointers to images."
Docker Registry

How do you use Docker registries in a CI/CD pipeline?

CI/CD pipelines build, push, and pull images from registries. Knowing the workflow is critical.

Q&ABuild → Push → Deploy›
Typical Pipeline
  • 1. Source code pushed to Git
  • 2. CI triggers: build Docker image
  • 3. Scan image for vulnerabilities
  • 4. Push to private registry (ACR, ECR)
  • 5. CD pulls image from registry
  • 6. Deploy to Kubernetes/Docker Swarm
Authentication
  • Store registry credentials in CI/CD secrets
  • Never hardcode username/password
  • Use service principals or tokens (time-limited)
Say this in interview "CI/CD pipeline builds Docker image, scans it for vulns, pushes to private registry. Credentials stored as secrets (not hardcoded). CD stage pulls from registry and deploys. Use commit SHA for tagging to ensure traceability."
Docker Registry

Explain image retention policies. Why delete old images?

Registries have storage costs. Old images pile up; retention policies keep things clean and cheap.

Q&ACost and cleanup›
Why Delete Old Images
  • Storage cost: every image layer costs money in registry
  • Security: old images have unpatched CVEs
  • Compliance: need to delete data after retention period
  • Clutter: hard to find relevant images
Retention Policy Example
  • Keep last 10 tagged releases (1.0.0, 1.0.1, etc.)
  • Keep last 30 days of dev builds (branch builds)
  • Delete untagged images older than 7 days
  • Always keep 'latest' tag (assumed to be current)
Best Practices
  • Automate retention policy (don't manual delete)
  • Archive critical images before deletion
  • Log what was deleted (audit trail)
  • Keep releases longer than dev builds
Say this in interview "Set automated retention policies: keep releases (1.0.0, 1.0.1) indefinitely or for years, delete dev builds after 30 days, delete untagged images after 7 days. This saves storage costs and reduces security surface by removing old, unpatched images."
Docker Registry

How do you implement image scanning in a registry?

Registry-level scanning catches vulnerabilities across all stored images, not just new ones.

SecurityContinuous vulnerability detection›
Registry Scanning vs CI/CD Scanning
  • CI/CD scanning: scans before push (prevents bad images)
  • Registry scanning: scans all images continuously (catches new CVEs)
  • Example: vulnerability published today for library in your 3-month-old image
  • Registry scanning detects this retroactively
ACR Scanning Setup
  • Enable Defender for Container Registries (Azure Security Center)
  • Automatic scan on push
  • Daily rescans of all images
  • Reports vulnerabilities in portal
Alerts & Policies
  • Alert on new critical CVE in existing image
  • Policy: delete or quarantine image if critical vuln found
  • Block deployment if image failed scanning
  • Audit trail: who deployed what, when
Say this in interview "Enable registry-level scanning to continuously monitor all images. New CVEs are discovered daily—your 3-month-old image might have a vuln published last week. Registry scanning catches this. Set policies to alert or block deployment if critical vuln found."
Docker 07

Production Scenarios

Docker Scenarios

A Docker build is failing intermittently. How do you debug it?

Intermittent failures are the worst. Could be network, caching, resource exhaustion, or race conditions.

ScenarioTroubleshooting build issues›
First: Reproduce Locally
  • docker build . (multiple times, see if it fails)
  • Add --progress=plain to see detailed output
  • Enable BuildKit: DOCKER_BUILDKIT=1 docker build .
Common Causes
  • Network flakiness: apt-get fails to reach package mirror
  • Layer caching: Docker cached a bad layer
  • Resource exhaustion: Docker daemon out of memory
  • Intermittent dependency: npm install fails occasionally
  • Race condition: Concurrent downloads conflict
Debugging Steps
  • 1. Force rebuild: docker build --no-cache .
  • 2. Check output: add -v flag for verbose
  • 3. Isolate: comment out suspicious RUN commands
  • 4. Retry: run 5 times in a row, see if it fails consistently
  • 5. Check logs: docker system events during build
Say this in interview "Reproduce locally first. If intermittent, likely network flakiness (apt-get, npm) or Docker caching. Force rebuild with --no-cache to rule out caching. Combine package updates and installs in one RUN command. Use npm ci instead of npm install for determinism."
Docker Scenarios

Explain your approach to Docker image versioning in a CI/CD pipeline

Version strategy affects deployability, rollback capability, and production safety.

Q&AStrategy and traceability›
My Approach
  • 1. Increment version in code (package.json, version.txt): 1.2.3
  • 2. Build image with commit SHA: myapp:a1b2c3d4
  • 3. Tag with version: docker tag myapp:a1b2c3d4 myapp:1.2.3
  • 4. Tag with branch: docker tag myapp:a1b2c3d4 myapp:main
  • 5. Push all tags
Benefits
  • Commit SHA: unique, traceable to exact code
  • Version (1.2.3): semantic, easy to read
  • Branch tag: latest from main, develop, etc.
  • Can rollback by SHA: docker pull myapp:a1b2c3d4
Say this in interview "I version images with: (1) commit SHA for traceability, (2) semantic version (1.2.3) from source code, (3) branch name for convenience. All point to same image. This enables rollbacks: if 1.2.4 breaks, deploy 1.2.3 or the specific SHA."
Docker Scenarios

How do you handle database migrations in containerized applications?

Migrations must run before or alongside app startup. Timing and coordination are critical.

ScenarioSchema and data changes›
Approaches
  • Option 1: Init container (K8s): runs migration before app starts
  • Option 2: Entrypoint: migration runs in app container before app starts
  • Option 3: Separate job: K8s Job runs migrations, then app Deployment
  • Option 4: sidecar: migration container alongside app
Entrypoint Approach (simpler)
#!/bin/bash
set -e

if [ "$RUN_MIGRATIONS" = "true" ]; then
  ./migrate.sh
fi

exec python app.py
Key Principles
  • Migrations must complete before app starts
  • Rollback capability: can downgrade schema if needed
  • Idempotent: safe to run multiple times
  • Monitor migration logs separately
Say this in interview "Run migrations before app starts. Either in entrypoint or as init container (K8s). Always make migrations idempotent (safe to run multiple times). Monitor logs. For databases, also use replication for HA."
Docker Scenarios

A container is leaking memory. How do you diagnose and fix it?

Memory leaks cause OOMKilled containers. Diagnosis requires tools; fixes require code changes.

ScenarioDebugging memory issues›
Step 1: Confirm Leak
  • docker stats (view memory usage over time)
  • Memory constantly increases? = leak
  • Memory usage plateaus? = normal
Step 2: Identify the App
  • docker exec -it container-name bash
  • ps aux (list running processes)
  • Which process is using memory? (Python, Java, Node?)
Step 3: Capture Memory Profile
  • Python: memory_profiler, objgraph
  • Node: heapdump, clinic.js
  • Java: jdump, YourKit profiler
  • Take heap dump during leak, analyze in IDE
Common Causes & Fixes
  • Global caches: Remove or size-limit them
  • Circular references: Use weakref
  • Unclosed connections: db.close() in finally block
  • Event listeners never removed: Always unsubscribe
  • Dependency version: Upgrade library (might have leak fix)
Say this in interview "Use docker stats to confirm leak (memory increasing over time). Profile the app with memory_profiler (Python) or equivalent. Common causes: unclosed connections, unbounded caches, circular references. Fix the code leak, set memory limits to prevent OOMKilled, monitor in production."
Kubernetes 01

Core Concepts

Kubernetes Core

What's the difference between Deployments, StatefulSets, and DaemonSets?

Three different workload types for different use cases. Understanding when to use each one is critical for production deployments.

Q&AWorkload types›
Deployments
  • Stateless applications (web servers, APIs)
  • Pods are interchangeable and replaceable
  • Rolling updates: gradually replace old pods with new ones
  • Example: nginx web server, Python API
StatefulSets
  • Stateful applications (databases, message queues)
  • Pods have stable, unique identity (pod-0, pod-1, pod-2)
  • Stable hostname and persistent storage per pod
  • Example: MySQL, Elasticsearch, Kafka
DaemonSets
  • Runs one pod per node (or subset of nodes)
  • Always present on target nodes
  • Example: Prometheus node-exporter, Fluentd logging agent
Say this in interview "Deployments for stateless apps. StatefulSets for stateful apps—pods get stable names and persistent storage. DaemonSets run one pod per node for monitoring agents."
Kubernetes Core

Explain Services and the different service types

Services provide stable network endpoints to pods. Different types expose applications differently.

Q&AService types›
ClusterIP (Default)
  • Internal-only service
  • Accessible within cluster only
  • Assigns stable IP: 10.0.0.1
  • DNS: mysql.default.svc.cluster.local
NodePort
  • Exposes on every node's port (30000-32767)
  • External clients access: node-ip:30001
  • Good for: external access without load balancer
LoadBalancer
  • Creates cloud load balancer (AWS ELB, Azure LB)
  • Assigns external IP automatically
  • Used for: exposing apps to internet
ExternalName
  • DNS CNAME alias to external service
  • Example: my-service → external-db.example.com
Say this in interview "ClusterIP for internal communication. NodePort for basic external access. LoadBalancer for production. For multiple external services, use Ingress instead—it's cheaper."
Kubernetes Core

What's an Ingress controller? How does it differ from LoadBalancer?

Ingress is the preferred way to expose multiple services externally. One Ingress replaces many LoadBalancers.

Q&AHTTP/HTTPS routing›
LoadBalancer vs Ingress
  • LoadBalancer: One service = one cloud LB = one IP
  • Ingress: Multiple services = one LB = one IP
  • Ingress is cheaper and better for routing
Ingress Controller
  • Software that reads Ingress resources and configures routing
  • Popular: Nginx, HAPROXY, Traefik, Kong
  • Watches Ingress resources and updates configuration
Traffic Flow
  • Client → DNS → Ingress Service (LB) → Ingress Controller Pod → Service → Pod
Say this in interview "Ingress is a single entry point for multiple services. One controller replaces many LoadBalancers. I define routing by hostname and path. Cheaper and more maintainable."
Kubernetes Core

Explain traffic flow from external client to pod through Ingress

Understanding the request journey is critical for debugging network issues.

Q&ARequest path›
Flow Steps
  • 1. DNS resolves hostname to Ingress IP
  • 2. Client sends HTTP request to Ingress IP
  • 3. Cloud LB forwards to Ingress controller pod
  • 4. Controller reads hostname and path
  • 5. Controller forwards to correct Service (ClusterIP)
  • 6. Service load balances to available pods
  • 7. Pod processes request and responds
Debugging
  • DNS not resolving? Check DNS provider
  • Connection refused? Check ingress controller pod is running
  • Wrong backend? Check Ingress routing rules
  • Timeout? kubectl get endpoints
  • Pod not responding? kubectl logs pod-name
Say this in interview "DNS → Ingress IP → LB → Controller → Service → Pod. If broken, I check each step: DNS, LB, controller pod, service endpoints, pod logs."
Kubernetes Core

What's the purpose of ConfigMap vs Secret?

Both pass data to pods, but for different types of data. ConfigMap for config, Secret for sensitive data.

Q&AConfiguration management›
ConfigMap
  • Non-sensitive configuration data
  • Stored as plain text (NOT encrypted)
  • Key-value pairs or files
  • Example: log-level=debug
Secret
  • Sensitive data (passwords, API keys, tokens)
  • Base64 encoded by default (NOT encrypted unless configured)
  • Can enable encryption at rest in etcd
  • Example: passwords, JWT tokens, TLS certs
Best Practices
  • Enable encryption at rest for Secrets
  • Use external secret managers (Vault, AWS Secrets Manager)
  • RBAC to limit access
Say this in interview "ConfigMap for non-sensitive settings. Secret for passwords and API keys. Enable encryption at rest in etcd for production. Better: use external vault (AWS, Azure)."
Kubernetes 02

Storage Management

Kubernetes Storage

How do you mount Secrets and ConfigMaps as volumes vs environment variables?

Two different ways to inject data. Each has tradeoffs for updates, visibility, and file-based configs.

Q&AInjection methods›
Environment Variables
  • Injected at pod startup, static after that
  • Changes to ConfigMap/Secret require pod restart
  • Good for: small values, simple configuration
Volume Mounts
  • Mounted as files in container filesystem
  • Updates reflected within seconds
  • Good for: configuration files, dynamic updates
Say this in interview "Use env vars for simple values. Use volume mounts for configuration files and when you need dynamic updates. Volume mounts auto-update when ConfigMap/Secret changes; env vars don't."
Kubernetes Storage

What's storage class and how does it relate to dynamic provisioning?

StorageClass enables automatic volume creation with specific properties. No manual PV creation needed.

Q&ADynamic volumes›
Without StorageClass (Manual)
  • Admin creates PersistentVolume (PV) manually
  • User creates PersistentVolumeClaim (PVC) requesting it
  • Slow and tedious at scale
With StorageClass (Dynamic)
  • StorageClass defines volume properties (type, performance)
  • User creates PVC specifying StorageClass
  • K8s automatically creates PV from that class
  • No manual PV creation needed
Say this in interview "StorageClass automates PV creation. Define StorageClass with volume properties (SSD, encryption). User creates PVC specifying the class. K8s provisions the volume automatically."
Kubernetes Storage

How do you backup and restore Kubernetes volumes?

Persistent data needs protection. Multiple strategies depending on volume type and application.

Q&AData protection›
Backup Approaches
  • Cloud snapshots: Azure snapshots, AWS EBS snapshots
  • Velero: Backup entire K8s cluster including volumes
  • App-level backups: Database dump (mysqldump, pg_dump)
  • Persistent replication: Database replicas in another region
Best Practices
  • Define RTO and RPO targets
  • Store backups in different region (zone failure protection)
  • Test restores monthly
  • For databases, also use replication for HA
Say this in interview "Use Velero for cluster backups. Or cloud snapshots for individual volumes. Define RTO and RPO. Store backups in different region. Test restores monthly."
Kubernetes 03

Networking

Kubernetes Networking

Explain how networking works in Kubernetes (pod-to-pod, pod-to-service, external traffic)

K8s networking is complex but logical. Every pod gets its own IP and can talk to other pods directly.

Q&ANetwork model›
Pod-to-Pod Communication
  • Every pod gets unique IP (from pod CIDR range)
  • Pods on same node: communicate directly
  • Pods on different nodes: CNI plugin routes traffic
  • No NAT or translation: direct IP-to-IP
Pod-to-Service Communication
  • Service has stable IP (ClusterIP: 10.0.0.5)
  • kube-proxy creates iptables rules
  • Traffic to Service IP redirected to pod IP
  • Load balanced across all matching pods
External Traffic
  • LoadBalancer Service: cloud LB → node:port → service → pod
  • NodePort: client:node-port → service → pod
  • Ingress: DNS → LB → ingress controller → service → pod
Say this in interview "Every pod has unique IP. Pods talk directly (no NAT). CNI plugin routes between nodes. Service IP is stable but virtual—kube-proxy redirects traffic. LoadBalancer/Ingress expose to external traffic."
Kubernetes Networking

What's a Network Policy? How do you implement network segmentation?

Network Policies are K8s firewalls. Default: all pods can talk. NetworkPolicy restricts this.

Q&AMicro-segmentation›
Default Behavior
  • All pods can communicate with all pods (no restrictions)
  • Risky for security (lateral movement if compromised)
  • Need to explicitly allow traffic with NetworkPolicy
Best Practices
  • Deny all ingress by default, allow specific traffic
  • Separate namespaces for different apps (tenant isolation)
  • Allow ingress only from known sources
  • Allow egress to known destinations
Say this in interview "NetworkPolicies are K8s firewalls. Default allows all traffic. I create policies to deny unnecessary traffic. Essential for multi-tenant clusters."
Kubernetes Networking

How does DNS work in Kubernetes? Explain service discovery

K8s includes built-in DNS. Services get DNS names automatically enabling service discovery.

Q&AService discovery›
DNS Components
  • CoreDNS pod in kube-system namespace
  • Watches Service resources and creates DNS records
  • kubelet configures pods to use CoreDNS
Service DNS Names
  • Format: service-name.namespace.svc.cluster.local
  • Example: mysql.default.svc.cluster.local → 10.0.0.5
  • Short form: mysql (CoreDNS expands to full name)
Say this in interview "K8s automatically creates DNS records for services. Apps query CoreDNS to discover services by name. No manual service registry needed. Short names work within namespace."
Kubernetes Networking

What's a service mesh (Istio, Linkerd)? When would you use one?

Service mesh adds advanced networking features: traffic management, security policies, observability.

Q&AAdvanced traffic management›
What It Does
  • Injects sidecar proxies into every pod
  • Provides: traffic routing, load balancing, circuit breaking
  • Also: mTLS encryption, rate limiting, distributed tracing
When to Use
  • Large, complex microservices architectures
  • Need advanced traffic management (canary, A/B testing)
  • Security: enforce mTLS between all services
  • Observability: automatic metrics and tracing
When NOT to Use
  • Small, simple clusters (overhead not worth it)
  • Performance-critical: adds latency
  • Learning cluster: too much complexity
Say this in interview "Service mesh adds sophisticated traffic management. Enables mTLS between services. Adds observability. But adds complexity and latency. Use only for large, complex clusters."
Kubernetes Networking

What's the difference between CNI plugins? Which have you used?

CNI plugins provide pod-to-pod networking. Different plugins have different capabilities and performance.

Q&ANetworking implementations›
Popular CNI Plugins
  • Flannel: Simple overlay, easy to setup
  • Calico: Advanced, BGP routing, high performance
  • Weave: Mesh networking, encrypted by default
  • Azure CNI: Uses Azure VNet, native integration
  • AWS VPC CNI: Native VPC networking
Comparison
  • Flannel: Simple but slow (overlay)
  • Calico: Fast and feature-rich
  • Cloud-native: Fastest (real VNet IPs)
Say this in interview "Flannel is simple but slow. Calico is fast and feature-rich. Cloud-native CNIs are fastest. I choose based on: performance needs, multi-cloud vs single-cloud."
Kubernetes Networking

How do you route traffic based on hostnames and paths with Ingress?

Ingress enables sophisticated routing without multiple load balancers.

Q&AHTTP routing›
Hostname-based Routing
  • Route to different services based on Host header
  • api.example.com → API service
  • admin.example.com → Admin service
  • One IP, multiple domains
Path-based Routing
  • Route based on URL path
  • /api → API service
  • /static → Static files service
  • Same hostname, different paths
Say this in interview "Ingress routes by hostname and path. Host header determines which domain → which service. Path determines which endpoint → which service. One Ingress can route to many services."
Kubernetes 04

Security & Access Control

Kubernetes Security

How do you enforce Pod Security Standards/Policies?

Pod Security Standards restrict what containers can do. Essential for multi-tenant clusters and compliance.

SecurityPod hardening›
Three Levels
  • Privileged: No restrictions (dangerous)
  • Baseline: Minimum security
  • Restricted: Strict, production-grade security
Enforcing Standards
  • Label namespace: pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted
  • Pods must comply or fail to create
  • Also: audit mode (log violations), warn mode
Restricted Standards Require
  • runAsNonRoot: true
  • readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
  • allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
  • Drop all Linux capabilities
Say this in interview "Enforce Pod Security Standards at namespace level. Use 'restricted' for production. Prevents privilege escalation, root access. Audit mode for testing, enforce mode for production."
Kubernetes Security

What's the difference between ServiceAccount and User authentication?

ServiceAccounts authenticate pods; Users authenticate people. Two different credential systems.

Q&AAuthentication types›
ServiceAccount (Pods)
  • Used by pods to authenticate to API server
  • Token mounted at /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token
  • Automatic per-pod credential
  • Scoped to namespace
User (Kubectl/Humans)
  • Used by people running kubectl
  • Authenticated via: certificates, OAuth, OIDC
  • Configured in kubeconfig file
  • Cluster-wide (not scoped to namespace)
Say this in interview "ServiceAccounts for pods. Users for humans. Each pod gets a ServiceAccount token automatically. Both controlled by RBAC."
Kubernetes Security

How do you implement least privilege for pods?

Least privilege minimizes damage if a pod is compromised.

SecurityMinimize blast radius›
Implementation
  • Non-root user: runAsNonRoot: true
  • Read-only filesystem: readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
  • Drop capabilities: capabilities.drop: [ALL]
  • No privilege escalation: allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
Say this in interview "Non-root user, read-only filesystem, drop all capabilities, no privilege escalation. Pod can't modify system, can't gain root, minimal blast radius if compromised."
Kubernetes Security

How do you manage secrets securely? (encryption, external managers)

Secrets in etcd are base64 encoded by default (NOT encrypted). Production needs real encryption.

SecuritySecret protection›
Problem
  • K8s Secrets are base64 encoded, not encrypted
  • Anyone with etcd access can read secrets
  • Secrets visible in kubectl logs
Solutions
  • Encryption at rest: Enable in API server config
  • External secret manager: AWS Secrets, Azure Key Vault, Vault
  • External Secrets Operator: Syncs to K8s Secrets
Best Practice
  • Use external vault
  • Pod fetches secret at runtime
  • Secret never in K8s Secrets
Say this in interview "Never rely on K8s Secrets alone. Enable encryption at rest. Better: use external vault (AWS, Azure). Pod fetches at runtime. Rotate regularly."
Kubernetes Security

Explain network segmentation and zero-trust in Kubernetes

Zero-trust: never trust traffic by default. Explicitly allow only necessary communication.

SecurityLeast-privilege networking›
Zero-Trust Principles
  • Deny all by default (ingress and egress)
  • Explicitly allow necessary traffic only
  • Verify every connection (mTLS)
  • Segment by namespace, pod labels
Implementation
  • 1. Deny all ingress by default
  • 2. Allow frontend from load balancer
  • 3. Allow backend from frontend
  • 4. Allow database from backend
  • 5. Explicit egress to required services
Say this in interview "Zero-trust: deny all, allow only necessary. Use NetworkPolicies for network segmentation. Implement mTLS between services. Segment by namespace and pod labels."
Kubernetes Security

What's Workload Identity Federation and how does it work?

Workload Identity lets pods use cloud credentials without storing secrets in K8s.

SecurityPod cloud auth›
Solution
  • Link K8s ServiceAccount to cloud IAM role
  • Pod uses ServiceAccount token to assume IAM role
  • Pod gets temporary AWS/Azure credentials (no secrets stored)
  • Credentials auto-refresh
Benefits
  • No secrets stored in K8s (secure)
  • Automatic credential rotation
  • Granular RBAC per pod/ServiceAccount
Say this in interview "Workload Identity removes secrets from K8s. Pod uses ServiceAccount token to assume cloud IAM role. Credentials temporary and auto-rotated. More secure than storing secrets."
Kubernetes Security

How do you scan container images for vulnerabilities in K8s?

Image scanning catches vulnerable dependencies before deployment.

SecurityVulnerability detection›
Scanning Points
  • CI/CD: Before image build (fail if vulnerabilities)
  • Registry: Continuous scanning of stored images
  • Admission controller: Block deployment of vulnerable images
Tools
  • Trivy: Fast, free, accurate
  • Snyk: Online, detailed reports
  • Grype: Free, community-supported
Enforcement
  • CI/CD: fail build if critical CVE
  • Registry: tag images with scan results
  • Admission webhook: reject pod if critical CVE
Say this in interview "Scan images in CI/CD (fail if critical CVE). Scan in registry continuously. Use admission controller to block vulnerable images."
Kubernetes 05

Monitoring & Observability

Kubernetes Monitoring

Explain liveness probes, readiness probes, and startup probes

Probes tell K8s when pods are healthy. Essential for automatic recovery and deployment safety.

Q&AHealth checks›
Readiness Probe
  • Is pod ready to receive traffic?
  • Fails: pod removed from service (no traffic)
  • When to fail: app warming up, dependencies unavailable
Liveness Probe
  • Is pod still alive? Or is it hung?
  • Fails: pod is restarted
  • When to fail: deadlock, infinite loop
Startup Probe
  • Has app finished starting up?
  • Liveness/readiness checked only after startup succeeds
  • When to use: slow startup time
Say this in interview "Readiness: ready for traffic? Liveness: alive? Startup: finished starting? Implement all three. Readiness prevents traffic to unavailable pods. Liveness restarts dead pods."
Kubernetes Monitoring

How do you collect and analyze logs from Kubernetes pods?

Pod logs are ephemeral (deleted with pod). Need centralized logging for production.

Q&ALog aggregation›
Pod Logs (Temporary)
  • kubectl logs pod-name (last 100 lines)
  • kubectl logs -f pod-name (follow logs)
  • Logs deleted when pod deleted
Centralized Logging Stack
  • Fluent Bit/Fluentd: Collect logs from pods
  • Elasticsearch: Store and index logs
  • Kibana: Search and visualize logs
  • Or: Loki (simpler), Splunk, ELK stack
Log Collection Flow
  • 1. Fluent Bit DaemonSet on every node
  • 2. Reads /var/log/pods/*/*.log (pod logs)
  • 3. Enriches with pod metadata (labels, namespace)
  • 4. Sends to Elasticsearch/Loki
  • 5. Searchable via Kibana/Grafana
Say this in interview "kubectl logs for quick debugging. Centralized logging for production (ELK, Loki). Fluent Bit collects logs and sends to central store. Logs enriched with pod metadata. Searchable and analyzable."
Kubernetes Monitoring

What are SLI, SLO, and SLA? How do you define them for K8s apps?

SLI/SLO/SLA define reliability expectations and budgets.

Q&AReliability targets›
Definitions
  • SLI: Metric (99.5% requests successful)
  • SLO: Target SLI (aim for 99.5%)
  • SLA: Legal contract, penalties if missed
Common SLIs
  • Availability: % of time service is up
  • Latency: p99 request time (<100ms)
  • Error rate: % of requests that fail (<0.5%)
  • Throughput: requests per second
Error Budget
  • If SLO is 99.5%, error budget = 0.5% per month
  • Can use for maintenance (planned downtime)
  • Once exhausted, prioritize reliability
Say this in interview "SLI is the metric, SLO is the target. Define SLOs: 99.5% availability, <100ms latency. Measure with Prometheus. Track error budget—if burning fast, prioritize reliability."
Kubernetes Monitoring

How do you set up distributed tracing in Kubernetes?

Distributed tracing shows request flow across microservices. Essential for debugging complex systems.

Q&ARequest tracing›
Problem
  • Microservices: request touches frontend → backend → database → cache
  • One slow service affects everything
  • Hard to find bottleneck without distributed tracing
Solution
  • Trace ID assigned to request at entry
  • Trace ID passed through all services
  • Each service reports spans (start/end of operation)
  • Jaeger/Zipkin collect and visualize traces
Implementation
  • Instrumentation: App adds tracing SDK (OpenTelemetry)
  • Collector: OpenTelemetry collector on DaemonSet
  • Backend: Jaeger (self-hosted) or Datadog (SaaS)
  • Service mesh: Istio/Linkerd auto-instruments traffic
Say this in interview "Use OpenTelemetry for instrumentation. Jaeger or Datadog as backend. Service mesh can auto-trace. See request flow across services, find bottlenecks."
Kubernetes 06

Troubleshooting & Operations

Kubernetes Troubleshooting

Walk me through debugging a pod that won't start

Systematic approach to troubleshooting pod failures.

ScenarioDebugging pod issues›
Step 1: Check Pod Status
kubectl describe pod pod-name
# Look at: Status, ContainerStatuses, Events
Step 2: Check Container Logs
kubectl logs pod-name
kubectl logs pod-name --previous  # if crashed
Step 3: Check Resources
kubectl describe node
# Check: allocatable, allocated, pressure
Step 4: Check YAML
  • Wrong image tag?
  • Resources requests too high?
  • NodeSelector preventing scheduling?
  • Probes failing immediately?
Say this in interview "kubectl describe pod to see status and events. kubectl logs to see app errors. Check node resources. Review YAML for configuration issues."
Kubernetes Troubleshooting

Understand pod failure states and debugging

Different failure states indicate different problems. Knowing each state helps diagnosis.

Q&AFailure diagnosis›
Pending: Pod Not Scheduled
  • Error Code: Pending (status shows "0/1 nodes available")
  • Why it happens:
    • Insufficient resources (no node has enough CPU/memory)
    • NodeSelector mismatch (node labels don't match pod selector)
    • Taints on nodes (pod has no matching tolerations)
    • PVC not bound (waiting for persistent volume)
    • Node affinity not satisfied
  • How to diagnose:
    • kubectl describe pod pod-name → Events section shows reason
    • kubectl get nodes → Check available resources
    • kubectl top nodes → See actual usage
  • How to fix:
    • Add more nodes to cluster
    • Reduce resource requests in pod spec
    • Remove/fix NodeSelector if incorrect
    • Add tolerations if nodes have taints
    • Check PVC status: kubectl get pvc
ImagePullBackOff: Can't Pull Container Image
  • Error Code: ImagePullBackOff (exponential backoff: 5s, 10s, 20s, 40s, 80s, max 300s)
  • Why it happens:
    • Image doesn't exist in registry (typo in image name/tag)
    • Image tag doesn't exist (pushed as v1.0, pod looks for v2.0)
    • No authentication to private registry (no imagePullSecrets)
    • Registry credentials expired or incorrect
    • Network connectivity issue (can't reach registry)
  • How to diagnose:
    • kubectl describe pod pod-name → Events shows "Failed to pull image: ..."
    • kubectl logs pod-name → Usually empty (container never started)
    • docker pull image:tag → Manually test image pull
  • How to fix:
    • Verify image name/tag correct: docker images | grep image-name
    • Verify image pushed to registry: docker pull registry/image:tag (should work)
    • For private registry: create imagePullSecrets in pod spec
    • Verify registry credentials: kubectl get secrets
    • Check network connectivity to registry (if on-prem)
CrashLoopBackOff: App Crashes Repeatedly
  • Error Code: CrashLoopBackOff (Kubernetes restarts pod after exponential delays)
  • Why it happens:
    • App segmentation fault (code error)
    • Missing environment variables (app can't start)
    • Config file invalid (YAML/JSON parse error)
    • Missing dependencies (library not found)
    • Port already in use (if multiple pods on same node)
    • Memory limit too low (app requires more RAM)
  • How to diagnose:
    • kubectl logs pod-name → See app error (most common)
    • kubectl logs --previous pod-name → Logs from previous crash
    • kubectl describe pod pod-name → CrashLoopBackOff reason
    • kubectl get pod pod-name -o yaml → Check env vars, mounts
  • How to fix:
    • Check logs: kubectl logs pod-name → Fix error in app code or config
    • Add missing env variables: add env: section in pod spec
    • Mount config files: add volume + volumeMounts
    • Increase memory limit: resources.limits.memory
    • Check app startup logic (maybe waiting for service that doesn't exist)
Init:Error or Init:CrashLoopBackOff: Init Container Failed
  • Error Code: Init:Error or Init:CrashLoopBackOff
  • Why it happens:
    • Init container (runs before app container) failed
    • Init script exited with non-zero code
    • Init container can't pull image (same as ImagePullBackOff)
    • Init container crashed (application error in init script)
    • Init container waiting for resource (DB connection, API endpoint)
  • How to diagnose:
    • kubectl logs pod-name -c init-container-name → See init logs
    • kubectl describe pod pod-name → Shows which init container failed
  • How to fix:
    • Check init container logs for error message
    • Verify init container image exists and can pull
    • If init waits for resource: add retry logic in init script
    • Ensure init script has execute permissions (chmod +x)
    • Check init container env vars and volume mounts
CreateContainerConfigError: Container Configuration Invalid
  • Error Code: CreateContainerConfigError
  • Why it happens:
    • Invalid volume mount (referenced volume doesn't exist)
    • Secret doesn't exist (pod tries to mount secret that's not created)
    • ConfigMap doesn't exist (referenced in volumeMounts or env)
    • Security context invalid (runAsUser out of range, invalid capabilities)
    • Image pull policy conflict
  • How to diagnose:
    • kubectl describe pod pod-name → Events shows "Error creating container"
    • kubectl get secrets → Verify secret exists
    • kubectl get configmap → Verify configmap exists
    • kubectl get pv,pvc → Verify volumes exist
  • How to fix:
    • Create missing secret: kubectl create secret generic secret-name --from-literal=key=value
    • Create missing configmap: kubectl create configmap config-name --from-file=config.yaml
    • Create missing volume/PVC before pod deployment
    • Fix security context in pod spec (valid runAsUser, capabilities)
    • Verify volumeMounts reference volumes that exist in pod spec
Evicted: Pod Removed from Node (Out of Resources)
  • Error Code: Evicted (reason varies: "node.kubernetes.io/out-of-disk", "node.kubernetes.io/memory-pressure")
  • Why it happens:
    • Node out of memory (other pods/system using memory)
    • Node disk full (no space for logs, container images)
    • PID limit reached (too many processes)
    • DiskPressure on node (kubelet removes pods)
    • Node not ready (network issues, hardware failure)
  • How to diagnose:
    • kubectl describe pod pod-name → Shows "Evicted" reason
    • kubectl describe node node-name → Shows MemoryPressure or DiskPressure
    • kubectl top node → See memory/CPU usage
    • df -h → Check node disk space
  • How to fix:
    • Add more nodes (horizontal scaling): kubectl scale deployment...
    • Free up disk space: docker image prune, docker container prune
    • Reduce resource requests (if unrealistic)
    • Set resource limits to prevent runaway processes
    • Use cluster autoscaler to auto-add nodes when needed
    • Delete evicted pods: kubectl delete pod pod-name
Terminating (Stuck): Pod Won't Delete
  • Error Code: Terminating (deletion timestamp set, but pod still running)
  • Why it happens:
    • Finalizers not completing (custom cleanup logic stuck)
    • preStop hook hanging (graceful shutdown script taking too long)
    • terminationGracePeriodSeconds too short (process needs more time to clean up)
    • PVC/Secret used by pod (can't delete while mounted)
    • Node not responding (kubelet can't force delete)
  • How to diagnose:
    • kubectl get pod pod-name -o yaml → Check deletionTimestamp + finalizers
    • kubectl describe pod pod-name → Shows termination reason
    • kubectl logs pod-name → App not shutting down cleanly
  • How to fix:
    • Increase terminationGracePeriodSeconds (give app time to shut down): terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 120
    • Fix preStop hook (ensure it completes quickly)
    • Remove finalizers if stuck: kubectl patch pod pod-name -p '{\"metadata\":{\"finalizers\":null}}'
    • Force delete (last resort): kubectl delete pod pod-name --grace-period=0 --force
    • Check node status: kubectl get nodes (if node down, pod can't delete)
Exit Codes & Their Meanings
Exit Code Meaning Cause Fix
0 Success Container exited normally N/A (expected)
1-128 Application error App crashed, returned error code Check logs, fix app code
137 OOMKilled (Out of Memory) Memory usage exceeded limit Increase memory limit, optimize app
139 Segmentation Fault Illegal memory access in app Debug app, check for memory corruption
143 SIGTERM (graceful shutdown) Pod received termination signal (normal) Ensure app handles SIGTERM properly
255 Unknown/Invalid App exited with invalid code Check app initialization
Say this in interview "Each failure state has specific causes. Pending = resource/scheduling. ImagePullBackOff = image problem. CrashLoopBackOff = app error. Evicted = node out of resources."
Kubernetes Troubleshooting

A service can't reach a pod. How do you troubleshoot?

Service → Pod communication broken? Systematic debugging.

ScenarioConnectivity issues›
Debugging Steps
  • 1. Check Service Endpoints: kubectl get endpoints service-name
  • 2. Pod labels match service selector?
  • 3. Pod listening on correct port?
  • 4. Service config correct?
  • 5. NetworkPolicy not blocking?
Say this in interview "Endpoints should show pod IPs. Pod labels must match service selector. Pod listening on correct port. Service config correct. NetworkPolicy not blocking."
Kubernetes Troubleshooting

How do you handle resource exhaustion (CPU, memory)?

Out of resources = evictions, performance degradation.

ScenarioResource management›
Immediate Fixes
  • Add nodes (scale cluster up)
  • Cordon problematic node (prevent new pods)
  • Reduce replica count temporarily
Long-term Fixes
  • Set resource requests/limits
  • HPA: horizontal pod autoscaling
  • Cluster autoscaler: add nodes when needed
  • Optimize app resource usage
Say this in interview "Add nodes immediately. Then set resource requests/limits. HPA for scaling pods, cluster autoscaler for nodes. Optimize app resource usage."
Kubernetes Troubleshooting

Explain node affinity and pod affinity. When do you use them?

Control where pods are scheduled. Affinity = prefer, anti-affinity = avoid.

Q&APod placement›
Node Affinity
  • Schedule pod on specific nodes (GPU nodes, high-memory)
  • requiredDuringScheduling: must match
  • preferredDuringScheduling: nice to have
Pod Affinity & Anti-Affinity
  • Affinity: Schedule pods together (database + cache same node)
  • Anti-affinity: Keep pods apart (replicas different nodes for HA)
Say this in interview "Node affinity for specific nodes (GPU, high-memory). Pod anti-affinity to spread replicas across nodes (HA). Pod affinity to colocate related pods (performance)."
Kubernetes Troubleshooting

How do you perform zero-downtime deployments?

Update apps without dropping traffic. Multiple strategies.

Q&ADeployment strategies›
Rolling Update (Default)
  • Gradually replace old pods with new ones
  • maxSurge: max new pods
  • maxUnavailable: max pods down (set to 0!)
  • Always has healthy pods running
Key Requirements
  • Pod readiness probe: new pod marked ready before traffic
  • Pod preStop hook: time for graceful shutdown
  • terminationGracePeriodSeconds: wait time before force kill
  • Multiple replicas: don't deploy 1-pod app
Say this in interview "Rolling update: gradually replace pods. Set maxUnavailable=0 (always have pods). Use readiness probe. preStop hook for graceful shutdown. Multiple replicas for HA."
Kubernetes Troubleshooting

What's a taint and toleration? When do you use them?

Taints repel pods; tolerations allow pods on tainted nodes. For node exclusion and workload isolation.

Q&ANode scheduling control›
Concepts
  • Taint: "no pods allowed unless they tolerate me"
  • Toleration: "I can tolerate this taint"
Use Cases
  • GPU nodes: taint with gpu=true, only ML jobs tolerate
  • Dedicated nodes: taint with workload=special
  • Maintenance: taint node, evict non-critical pods safely
Say this in interview "Taints keep pods off nodes. Tolerations allow specific pods on tainted nodes. Use for: GPU nodes, dedicated workloads, maintenance windows."
Kubernetes Troubleshooting

How do you handle graceful pod termination?

Pod termination needs time for cleanup. Improper termination causes data loss.

Q&AShutdown handling›
Termination Flow
  • 1. SIGTERM sent to main process
  • 2. Pod stops accepting new traffic
  • 3. preStop hook runs (if defined)
  • 4. App handles SIGTERM, closes connections
  • 5. terminationGracePeriodSeconds wait (default 30s)
  • 6. If still running: SIGKILL (force kill)
Implementation
  • App catches SIGTERM signal
  • Stop accepting requests
  • Finish in-flight requests (with timeout)
  • Close database connections
  • Exit cleanly
Say this in interview "App must catch SIGTERM. Stop accepting traffic, finish in-flight requests. Set terminationGracePeriodSeconds to allow time. preStop hook for additional cleanup."
Kubernetes 07

Advanced Patterns

Kubernetes Advanced

What's GitOps and how does it apply to Kubernetes? (ArgoCD)

GitOps: Git as source of truth. Declarative, version-controlled infrastructure.

Q&ADeclarative operations›
GitOps Principles
  • Git repo contains desired state (YAML, Helm)
  • GitOps operator (ArgoCD, Flux) syncs Git → Cluster
  • If cluster drifts, operator pulls back to Git state
  • All changes tracked in Git (audit trail)
ArgoCD Workflow
  • 1. Developer commits YAML to Git (v1.2.3)
  • 2. ArgoCD detects change
  • 3. ArgoCD pulls YAML from Git
  • 4. ArgoCD applies to K8s cluster
  • 5. Everything tracked: who, when, what changed
Benefits
  • Version control: rollback any deployment
  • Audit trail: see exactly what changed
  • Declarative: apply once, sync continuous
  • Multi-cluster: manage multiple K8s clusters from Git
Say this in interview "Git is source of truth. ArgoCD syncs Git to cluster continuously. Commit YAML, push to Git, ArgoCD deploys. Entire history in Git, easy rollbacks."
Kubernetes Advanced

Explain canary deployments and blue-green deployments

Advanced deployment strategies for safe releases. Minimize blast radius of bad deployments.

Q&ASafe rollouts›
Canary Deployment
  • Roll out to small % of traffic first (5%)
  • Monitor for errors
  • If good, gradually increase (5% → 25% → 50% → 100%)
  • If bad, rollback immediately
Blue-Green Deployment
  • Blue: old version (current production)
  • Green: new version (staging)
  • Run both in parallel
  • Switch traffic from blue to green (fast, atomic)
  • Rollback easy: switch back to blue
Comparison
  • Canary: gradual, detect issues early, longer deployment
  • Blue-green: atomic, fast, needs double resources
Say this in interview "Canary: send 5% traffic to new version, monitor, gradually increase. Blue-green: run both versions, instantly switch. Canary safer for large changes, blue-green faster."
Kubernetes Advanced

How do you implement high availability and disaster recovery for Kubernetes?

HA = no single point of failure. DR = recovery from disaster.

Q&AResilience›
HA (High Availability)
  • Multi-node cluster (3+ control plane nodes)
  • Multiple replicas per app (never 1 replica)
  • Pod anti-affinity: spread replicas across nodes
  • Node failures don't cause downtime
DR (Disaster Recovery)
  • RTO: how fast to recover
  • RPO: how much data loss acceptable
  • Backups: Velero for cluster backups
  • Multi-region: replicate cluster to different region
  • Test recovery procedures regularly
Implementation
  • 3 control plane nodes (distributed, different zones)
  • 3+ worker nodes (distributed)
  • etcd replicated across zones
  • Persistent data: replicated storage
  • Backups to separate region/cloud
Say this in interview "HA: multiple control plane nodes, multiple replicas, spread across zones. DR: regular backups, secondary cluster, test recovery. Define RTO/RPO targets."
Kubernetes Advanced

What's a Helm chart? When would you use Helm vs plain YAML?

Helm = package manager for K8s. Reduces duplication and enables reuse.

Q&AHelm charts›
Plain YAML Problems
  • Duplication: same config for dev/staging/prod, only values differ
  • No templating: copy-paste errors
  • No rollback: manual version management
  • Hard dependency management
Helm Chart
  • Template YAML with variables ({{.Values.image.tag}})
  • values.yaml: define variables per environment
  • helm install: render template, apply to cluster
  • helm upgrade: seamless rollback
When to Use
  • Helm: complex apps, many YAML files, multi-environment
  • Plain YAML: simple apps, single deployment
  • Kustomize: alternative to Helm (simpler)
Say this in interview "Helm reduces duplication with templating. One chart, multiple environments. Seamless rollback with version history. Use for complex apps."
Kubernetes Advanced

How do you manage multiple Kubernetes clusters?

Multiple clusters = higher availability and vendor independence.

Q&AMulti-cluster operations›
Solutions
  • ArgoCD: multi-cluster GitOps (GitHub → all clusters)
  • Rancher: multi-cluster management UI
  • Service mesh: Istio federated (multi-cluster networking)
  • GitOps with Kustomize: per-cluster overlays
Common Patterns
  • Each cluster has kubeconfig, managed separately
  • ArgoCD deployment template per cluster
  • Federated service mesh for cross-cluster networking
  • Central monitoring (Prometheus scrapes all clusters)
Say this in interview "Use ArgoCD or Rancher to manage multiple clusters. Git repo branches/directories per cluster. Service mesh for cross-cluster communication. Centralized monitoring."
Kubernetes Advanced

Multi-tenancy & Tenant Isolation in Kubernetes

Isolate tenants so they can't interfere with each other. Critical for SaaS.

Q&AIsolation strategies›
Isolation Levels
  • Namespace: separate per tenant (logical)
  • RBAC: tenant ServiceAccount accesses only their namespace
  • Network Policies: tenants can't reach each other
  • Resource Quotas: limit CPU/memory per tenant
  • Dedicated nodes: tenant pods on isolated nodes (strongest)
Implementation
  • 1. Namespace per tenant: tenant-a, tenant-b
  • 2. ServiceAccount per tenant
  • 3. RBAC: access only its namespace
  • 4. NetworkPolicy: deny all, allow within namespace
  • 5. ResourceQuota: limit resources per tenant
Say this in interview "Namespace per tenant, RBAC restricts access, NetworkPolicy prevents cross-tenant traffic, ResourceQuota limits resources. Combines logical and resource isolation for security."
Terraform 01

Fundamentals

Terraform Fundamentals

Explain declarative vs imperative approach

Terraform uses declarative approach — you describe the desired state, not the steps to achieve it. This is fundamentally different from imperative tools.

Q&APhilosophy difference›
Declarative (Terraform)
  • You specify WHAT you want: "I want a storage account with encryption"
  • Tool figures out HOW to achieve it
  • Idempotent: running twice produces same result
  • Easy to version and review: final state is in code
Imperative (Ansible, Scripts)
  • You specify steps: "First create bucket, then enable encryption, then add policy"
  • Tool executes commands in order
  • Not idempotent: running twice might fail or cause issues
  • Hard to track current state
Example
  • Terraform: resource "azurerm_storage_account" "my_storage" { name = "mydata" }
  • Bash Script: az storage account create --name mydata && az storage account blob-service-properties update ...
Say this in interview "Terraform is declarative — you declare the desired state and Terraform ensures infrastructure matches it. This is safer than imperative scripts because running Terraform multiple times produces the same result."
Terraform Fundamentals

What are the main advantages of using Terraform?

Terraform offers multiple benefits over manual provisioning and other IaC tools.

Q&AKey benefits›
Multi-cloud Support
  • Single tool works with AWS, Azure, GCP, on-prem
  • No vendor lock-in
  • Easy to migrate between clouds
State Management
  • Terraform tracks current state of infrastructure
  • Can see what changed and plan changes before applying
  • Knows dependencies between resources
Version Control & Collaboration
  • Infrastructure as code = code review process
  • History of all changes
  • Easy rollback to previous versions
Modularity & Reusability
  • Write once, use many times (modules)
  • Reduce duplication
  • Standardize infrastructure patterns
Idempotency
  • Running terraform apply multiple times = same result
  • Safe to re-run
Say this in interview "Terraform's main advantages: multi-cloud support (no vendor lock-in), state management (know current state), version control integration (review infrastructure changes like code), modularity (reusable components), and idempotency (safe to re-run)."
Terraform Fundamentals

Explain providers, provisioners, and resources in Terraform

Three core concepts that make up every Terraform configuration.

Q&ABuilding blocks›
Providers
  • Plugins that connect Terraform to cloud providers or APIs
  • Examples: azurerm, aws, google, kubernetes
  • Downloaded automatically when you run terraform init
  • Configured in provider blocks: provider "azurerm" { features {} }
Resources
  • The actual infrastructure components you want to create
  • Examples: azurerm_linux_virtual_machine, azurerm_storage_account, azurerm_resource_group
  • Defined in resource blocks: resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "web" { ... }
  • Resources have properties (arguments) and return values (attributes)
Provisioners
  • Tools to run scripts/commands on resources after they're created
  • Examples: local-exec, remote-exec, file
  • Used for: bootstrapping, installing software, configuring apps
  • Considered a last resort — prefer user_data or configuration management
Say this in interview "Providers connect to cloud platforms (Azure, AWS, GCP). Resources are the infrastructure (VMs, Storage accounts, resource groups) you create. Provisioners run scripts on resources (like custom_data, but less preferred)."
Terraform Fundamentals

How do you write a simple resource in HCL?

HCL is the language Terraform uses. Here's how to write infrastructure.

Q&AHCL syntax›
Basic Structure
resource "resource_type" "name" {
  argument1 = "value1"
  argument2 = "value2"
}
Example: Azure Storage Account
resource "azurerm_resource_group" "rg" {
  name     = "my-rg"
  location = "eastus"
}

resource "azurerm_storage_account" "my_storage" {
  name                     = "mystorageaccount"
  resource_group_name      = azurerm_resource_group.rg.name
  location                 = azurerm_resource_group.rg.location
  account_tier             = "Standard"
  account_replication_type = "GRS"

  tags = {
    Environment = "production"
    Team        = "platform"
  }
}
Key Concepts
  • Resource references: resource_type.name.attribute (e.g., azurerm_storage_account.my_storage.id)
  • Blocks: wrapped in curly braces { }
  • Arguments: key = value pairs
  • Interpolation: ${resource.name.attribute} or just resource.name.attribute
Say this in interview "HCL uses block syntax: resource 'type' 'name' { arguments }. Arguments are key-value pairs. You reference resource attributes to pass between resources."
Terraform 02

Workflow

Terraform Workflow

Explain the Terraform workflow

The standard three-step process for managing infrastructure with Terraform.

Q&AWrite, Plan, Apply›
Step 1: Write
  • Write .tf files describing desired infrastructure
  • Example: main.tf, variables.tf, outputs.tf
  • Define providers, resources, variables, outputs
Step 2: Plan
  • terraform init (download providers and initialize)
  • terraform plan (preview changes without applying)
  • Shows: what will be created, changed, destroyed
  • Allows review before actual changes
Step 3: Apply
  • terraform apply (execute the plan)
  • Asks for confirmation (unless -auto-approve)
  • Creates/updates/destroys resources as needed
  • Updates .tfstate file with new state
Additional Commands
  • terraform destroy (delete all resources)
  • terraform refresh (update state from actual resources)
  • terraform validate (check syntax)
  • terraform fmt (format code)
Say this in interview "Standard workflow: (1) Write .tf files, (2) Run terraform plan to preview changes, (3) Run terraform apply to execute. Always review plan before applying to catch mistakes early."
Terraform Workflow

Explain the Terraform workflow in CI/CD

How Terraform integrates into automated CI/CD pipelines for safe, consistent deployments.

Q&AAutomated Infrastructure›
CI/CD Pipeline Steps
  • 1. Developer commits .tf changes to Git
  • 2. CI triggers: terraform validate, terraform plan
  • 3. Plan output posted to PR for review
  • 4. Team reviews and approves changes
  • 5. CD stage: terraform apply (with -auto-approve or manual approval)
  • 6. State updated and infrastructure changed
Benefits
  • Consistent deployments across environments
  • Infrastructure changes tracked in Git
  • Prevents accidental changes outside of code review
  • Easy rollback: revert Git commit and redeploy
Tools
  • GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, Jenkins, CircleCI
  • Terraform Cloud/Enterprise (built-in VCS integration)
  • AWS CodePipeline, Azure Pipelines
Say this in interview "In CI/CD: commits trigger terraform validate and plan. Plan is reviewed in PR. Approval runs terraform apply. This ensures infrastructure changes are code-reviewed and tracked in Git."
Terraform Workflow

Explain the Terraform lifecycle

Resource lifecycle management: how Terraform handles creation, updates, and deletion.

Q&AResource lifecycle›
Lifecycle Block
  • Meta-argument that controls how Terraform handles resource changes
  • Options: create_before_destroy, prevent_destroy, ignore_changes, replace_triggered_by
create_before_destroy
  • When updating a resource that must be replaced: create new first, then destroy old
  • Prevents downtime
  • Example: launch new VM before terminating old
prevent_destroy
  • Terraform throws error if trying to destroy resource
  • Safety guard for critical resources (databases, buckets)
ignore_changes
  • Terraform ignores changes to specified arguments
  • Useful if resource is managed outside Terraform
Example
resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "web" {
  name                = "web-vm"
  location            = azurerm_resource_group.rg.location
  resource_group_name = azurerm_resource_group.rg.name
  size                = "Standard_B1s"

  lifecycle {
    create_before_destroy = true
    prevent_destroy       = false
    ignore_changes        = [tags]
  }

  os_disk { caching = "ReadWrite" storage_account_type = "Premium_LRS" }
}
Say this in interview "Lifecycle controls how resources are created/updated/destroyed. Common options: create_before_destroy (zero-downtime updates), prevent_destroy (safety for critical resources), ignore_changes (exclude from updates)."
Terraform 03

State Management

Terraform State

What is Terraform state?

State is Terraform's record of infrastructure. It's critical to understand and protect it.

Q&AState file›
What It Is
  • JSON file (terraform.tfstate) that tracks current infrastructure
  • Maps resource names to real AWS/Azure/GCP IDs
  • Stores outputs and variables
  • Used to detect changes on next terraform plan/apply
Why It Matters
  • Without state: Terraform wouldn't know what's already created
  • Each resource created would be duplicated
  • Can't track which infrastructure to delete
Example State File
{
  "version": 4,
  "terraform_version": "1.5.0",
  "resources": [
    {
      "type": "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine",
      "name": "web",
      "instances": [
        {
          "schema_version": 1,
          "attributes": {
            "id": "/subscriptions/abc/resourceGroups/rg/providers/Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachines/web-vm",
            "size": "Standard_B1s",
            "location": "eastus"
          }
        }
      ]
    }
  ]
}
Say this in interview "State is a JSON file that maps Terraform resources to real cloud resources. It's essential: without it, Terraform can't track what's been created or what needs to change. Must be protected and backed up."
Terraform State

What happens if state gets corrupted or lost?

State corruption/loss is a serious problem. Here's the impact and recovery options.

DisasterRecovery›
If State Is Lost
  • Terraform thinks all resources are gone
  • Next terraform apply will try to recreate everything
  • Could duplicate resources: new VM while old still exists
  • Duplicate storage accounts (names are globally unique, will fail)
  • Data loss if resources are destroyed and recreated
If State Is Corrupted
  • Terraform might crash when reading state
  • Can't run plan or apply
  • Lose visibility into current infrastructure
Recovery Options
  • Backup recovery: Restore from backup (if you have one)
  • Reimport: Use terraform import to re-associate resources
  • Manual intervention: Destroy resources and recreate
  • State reconstruction: Read cloud resources and rebuild state
Prevention
  • Use remote state (Azure Storage, S3, Terraform Cloud)
  • Enable versioning on backend storage
  • Regular backups
  • State locking to prevent concurrent modifications
Say this in interview "Lost state = Terraform recreates all resources (potential duplicates, data loss). Corrupted state = Terraform can't function. Prevention: use remote state with versioning, enable locking, backup regularly."
Terraform State

What's the difference between local and remote state?

Local state for dev, remote state for production. Understanding the tradeoffs is critical.

Q&AState storage›
Local State (terraform.tfstate)
  • State file stored on your computer or CI/CD server
  • Good for: local dev, learning
  • Problems: team can't share state, no backups, not encrypted
  • Risk: state lost if computer crashes
Remote State
  • State stored in Azure Storage, S3, Terraform Cloud, etc.
  • Good for: production, teams, CI/CD
  • Benefits: centralized, backed up, versioned, encrypted, state locking
  • Team members can collaborate safely
Comparison
  • Local: Simple, but no sharing/backup/locking
  • Remote: Secure, collaborative, production-ready
Say this in interview "Local state: fine for dev. Remote state: required for production and team environments. Remote enables state locking (prevents concurrent changes), versioning (rollback), and encryption (security)."
Terraform State

How do you configure remote state?

Setting up remote state backend so team can collaborate safely.

Q&ABackend configuration›
Azure Storage Backend Example
terraform {
  backend "azurerm" {
    resource_group_name  = "my-rg"
    storage_account_name = "mystorageaccount"
    container_name       = "tfstate"
    key                  = "prod/terraform.tfstate"
    encrypt              = true
  }
}
Azure Storage Backend
terraform {
  backend "azurerm" {
    resource_group_name  = "my-rg"
    storage_account_name = "mystorageaccount"
    container_name       = "tfstate"
