    key                  = "prod.tfstate"
  }
}
Terraform Cloud Backend
terraform {
  cloud {
    organization = "my-org"
    workspaces {
      name = "production"
    }
  }
}
Steps
  • 1. Create backend storage (Azure Storage account with blob container)
  • 2. Add backend block to Terraform code
  • 3. Run terraform init (migrates state to remote)
  • 4. Verify state is in remote (check Azure Storage)
Say this in interview "Configure backend block with provider details (bucket, region, encryption). Run terraform init to migrate state. For production, always enable encryption and state locking."
Terraform State

What's the difference between terraform refresh, plan, and apply?

Three commands that work with state in different ways.

Q&AState operations›
terraform refresh
  • Updates state file to match actual cloud resources
  • Queries cloud: "What actually exists?"
  • Fixes state drift (if someone manually changed resource properties)
  • Doesn't modify infrastructure, only updates state file
terraform plan
  • Compares state with code
  • Shows what WILL change if you apply
  • Doesn't modify state or infrastructure
  • Safe way to preview changes
terraform apply
  • Executes the plan
  • Modifies infrastructure to match code
  • Updates state file with new IDs/attributes
  • Asks for confirmation before making changes
Workflow
  • 1. terraform refresh (optional, but recommended)
  • 2. terraform plan (review changes)
  • 3. terraform apply (execute changes)
Say this in interview "Refresh updates state from cloud. Plan shows what will change. Apply makes the changes. Always review plan before applying to catch mistakes."
Terraform State

How do you handle state secrets? (passwords, API keys)

State files contain sensitive data. They must be protected.

SecuritySecret protection›
The Problem
  • State files store passwords, API keys, database credentials
  • If state is exposed: security breach
  • Local state files especially risky (sitting on disk)
Solutions
  • Remote state + encryption: Azure Storage with encryption, S3 with encryption
  • State locking: Prevents unauthorized access
  • .gitignore: Never commit state to Git
  • Access control: IAM/RBAC to limit who can access backend
Encryption Tools
  • Azure Storage encryption: Storage Service Encryption (automatic, managed keys)
  • Customer-managed keys: Azure Key Vault with CMK for storage account
  • Terraform Cloud: Automatically encrypts state at rest
  • Vault: HashiCorp Vault for advanced secret management
Best Practices
  • Never store secrets in Git
  • Use remote state with encryption
  • Enable MFA for backend access
  • Audit who accesses state
  • Rotate secrets regularly
Say this in interview "State contains secrets, so: (1) Use remote state (not local), (2) Enable encryption at rest, (3) Enable state locking, (4) Use IAM to restrict access, (5) Never commit state to Git, (6) Rotate secrets regularly."
Terraform 04

Modules

Terraform Modules

What's the difference between root module and child modules?

Modules are reusable building blocks. Root and child modules have different roles.

Q&AModule hierarchy›
Root Module
  • The main Terraform code in your working directory
  • Contains main.tf, variables.tf, outputs.tf
  • What you run terraform apply on
  • Can call child modules
Child Modules
  • Reusable components defined in subdirectories
  • Encapsulate infrastructure patterns
  • Called from root module
  • Have their own variables and outputs
Example Structure
project/
├── main.tf (root module)
├── variables.tf
├── outputs.tf
└── modules/
    ├── vpc/
    │   ├── main.tf (child module)
    │   ├── variables.tf
    │   └── outputs.tf
    └── ec2/
        ├── main.tf (child module)
        ├── variables.tf
        └── outputs.tf
How to Call Child Module
module "vnet" {
  source = "./modules/vnet"
  rg_name  = azurerm_resource_group.rg.name
  location = azurerm_resource_group.rg.location
}

module "web_vm" {
  source = "./modules/vm"
  rg_name      = azurerm_resource_group.rg.name
  location     = azurerm_resource_group.rg.location
  subnet_id    = module.vnet.subnet_id
}
Say this in interview "Root module is where you run terraform apply. Child modules are reusable components in subdirectories. Root calls child modules and passes variables, receives outputs."
Terraform Modules

How do you structure a module?

Best practices for organizing a reusable module.

Q&AModule best practices›
Recommended Structure
vpc-module/
├── main.tf (resources)
├── variables.tf (inputs)
├── outputs.tf (return values)
├── terraform.tf (terraform version, required providers)
├── versions.tf (provider versions)
├── locals.tf (local values)
├── README.md (documentation)
└── examples/
    └── main.tf (example usage)
main.tf
  • Actual resources: VPC, subnets, security groups, etc.
  • References to variables: var.cidr_block, var.name
variables.tf
  • Defines input variables (what caller must provide)
  • Example: variable "cidr_block" { type = string, default = "10.0.0.0/16" }
outputs.tf
  • Defines what the module returns to caller
  • Example: output "vnet_id" { value = azurerm_virtual_network.main.id }
terraform.tf & versions.tf
  • terraform.tf: terraform version requirement
  • versions.tf: provider versions needed
  • Ensures consistency across team
Say this in interview "Module structure: main.tf (resources), variables.tf (inputs), outputs.tf (returns), versions.tf (provider versions), README (docs). This makes modules reusable and maintainable."
Terraform Modules

What's the Terraform Registry and how do you use public modules?

The Terraform Registry is a repository of ready-made modules. Save time by reusing community modules.

Q&ACommunity modules›
What It Is
  • registry.terraform.io: public repository of modules
  • Community-maintained (AWS, Azure, Google, etc.)
  • Free to use
  • Official modules provided by Terraform/HashiCorp
How to Use
module "vnet" {
  source  = "Azure/vnet/azurerm"
  version = "~> 4.0"

  resource_group_name = azurerm_resource_group.rg.name
  vnet_name           = "my-vnet"
  address_space       = ["10.0.0.0/16"]
  subnet_prefixes     = ["10.0.1.0/24", "10.0.2.0/24"]
  subnet_names        = ["subnet-1", "subnet-2"]
}
Source Formats
  • Registry: source = "Azure/vnet/azurerm"
  • Local: source = "./modules/vnet"
  • Git: source = "git::https://github.com/org/repo.git"
  • HTTP: source = "https://example.com/module.zip"
Best Practices
  • Specify version constraints (e.g., ~> 3.0)
  • Review module source code before using
  • Check module ratings and download count
  • Read documentation
Say this in interview "Terraform Registry has community modules. Use source='Azure/vnet/azurerm' with version constraint. Saves time over writing from scratch. Always review code and pin versions."
Terraform 05

Workspaces & Backends

Terraform Workspaces

How do you manage state per workspace?

Workspaces let you run multiple instances of same code with separate state.

Q&AWorkspace management›
What Are Workspaces
  • Separate state files for different environments
  • Same code, different state
  • Example: dev workspace, staging workspace, prod workspace
Commands
  • terraform workspace list (see all workspaces)
  • terraform workspace new dev (create new)
  • terraform workspace select prod (switch)
  • terraform workspace delete staging (remove)
State Files
  • Local: terraform.tfstate.d/dev/terraform.tfstate, terraform.tfstate.d/prod/terraform.tfstate
  • Remote: different keys in backend (dev/terraform.tfstate, prod/terraform.tfstate)
Example
terraform workspace new dev
terraform apply # creates infrastructure in dev workspace

terraform workspace select prod
terraform apply # creates same infrastructure in prod workspace
Say this in interview "Workspaces separate state files for different environments. Same code, different state per workspace. Use terraform workspace commands to switch between them."
Terraform Workspaces

Best practices for managing multiple environments (dev, staging, prod)

Strategies for organizing infrastructure across environments.

Q&AMulti-environment setup›
Approach 1: Separate Directories
  • environments/dev, environments/staging, environments/prod
  • Each has its own .tf files and state
  • Best for production (more control, less risk)
  • More duplication but safer
Approach 2: Workspaces
  • One .tf file, multiple workspaces (dev, staging, prod)
  • Less duplication
  • Risk: one command affects wrong environment
Approach 3: Modules + Variables
  • Single module, different tfvars files per environment
  • terraform apply -var-file=prod.tfvars
  • Balanced: reusable + controlled
Best Practice
  • Use separate directories for prod (safety)
  • Use workspaces or tfvars for dev/staging (speed)
  • Enforce approval gates on prod (CI/CD)
  • Different AWS/Azure accounts per environment
Say this in interview "Production: separate directories (safer). Dev/Staging: workspaces or tfvars files (faster iteration). Enforce approvals on prod deployments. Use different cloud accounts per environment when possible."
Terraform Workspaces

How do you migrate from one backend to another?

Moving state from local to Azure Storage or S3, or changing backends entirely.

Q&ABackend migration›
Steps
  • 1. Backup current state: cp terraform.tfstate backup.tfstate
  • 2. Create new backend (e.g., Azure Storage, S3 bucket)
  • 3. Update backend block in code
  • 4. Run terraform init (prompts to migrate)
  • 5. Confirm yes to migrate state
  • 6. Verify state in new backend
Example: Local to Azure Storage
# Before: no backend (local state)
# terraform.tfstate in current directory

# Step 1: Create Azure Storage account with blob container
# az storage account create --name mystgacct --resource-group my-rg
# az storage container create --account-name mystgacct --name tfstate

# Step 2: Add backend block to terraform.tf
terraform {
  backend "azurerm" {
    resource_group_name  = "my-rg"
    storage_account_name = "mystgacct"
    container_name       = "tfstate"
    key                  = "prod/terraform.tfstate"
  }
}

# Step 3: Run terraform init
# terraform init
# (prompts: "Do you want to copy existing state to the new backend?" → yes)
Rollback
  • If migration fails, remove backend block
  • Run terraform init (to switch back to local)
  • Restore from backup if needed
Say this in interview "Backup state first. Update backend block. Run terraform init. Confirm yes to migrate. Terraform automatically copies state to new backend. Can rollback by removing backend block and reinitializing."
Terraform 06

Version Control & Best Practices

Terraform Version Control

Version control strategy for Terraform code

What should be in Git, what shouldn't, and how to structure repos.

Q&AGit best practices›
What TO Commit
  • *.tf files (main.tf, variables.tf, outputs.tf)
  • .terraform.lock.hcl (provider versions lock file)
  • .gitignore (what to exclude)
  • terraform.tfvars (only non-sensitive values)
  • Documentation, scripts, tests
What NOT to Commit
  • terraform.tfstate (state file) — sensitive data!
  • terraform.tfstate.backup
  • .terraform/ directory (provider binaries)
  • *.tfvars with secrets (especially prod.tfvars)
  • *.auto.tfvars
  • Override files
.gitignore Example
# Local .terraform directories
**/.terraform/*

# .tfstate files
*.tfstate
*.tfstate.*

# Crash log files
crash.log

# Exclude all .tfvars files (might contain sensitive data)
*.tfvars
*.tfvars.json

# Ignore override files
override.tf
override.tf.json
*_override.tf
*_override.tf.json

# Ignore CLI configuration files
.terraformrc
terraform.rc
Workflow
  • 1. Developer writes .tf code locally
  • 2. Commit to feature branch
  • 3. Push to GitHub/GitLab
  • 4. Open PR with terraform plan output
  • 5. Team reviews changes
  • 6. Merge to main
  • 7. CI/CD runs terraform apply on main
Say this in interview "Commit .tf files, .terraform.lock.hcl, documentation. NEVER commit state files or .tfvars with secrets. Use .gitignore to exclude sensitive files. Store secrets in external vaults."
Terraform Version Control

How do you handle Terraform state in CI/CD pipelines?

Making state safely accessible to CI/CD without exposing it.

Q&ACI/CD state management›
Remote State (Required)
  • Don't use local state in CI/CD
  • Use Azure Storage, S3, or Terraform Cloud
  • CI/CD reads state from backend, runs apply, state syncs back
Access Control
  • CI/CD agent uses managed identity or service principal (no hardcoded credentials)
  • RBAC role allows: Read/Write to storage account container
  • Restrict who can approve deployments (branch protection)
Workflow
  • 1. Push .tf changes to Git
  • 2. CI: terraform init (pull state from Azure Storage)
  • 3. CI: terraform plan (preview changes)
  • 4. Plan output posted to PR
  • 5. Team reviews in PR
  • 6. Approve → merge to main
  • 7. CD: terraform apply (state updates in Azure Storage)
Security
  • Enable Azure Storage encryption and versioning (blob snapshots)
  • Enable state locking (lease-based in Azure Storage)
  • Rotate CI/CD credentials regularly
  • Audit state access logs
Say this in interview "CI/CD requires remote state (Azure Storage or S3). Agent uses managed identity (no hardcoded credentials). State is fetched at init, updated at apply. Encryption + versioning + locking for security."
Terraform 07

CI/CD & Automation

Terraform CI/CD

How do you integrate Terraform with CI/CD pipelines?

Automation workflows that safely deploy infrastructure.

Q&AAutomated deployment›
CI Stage
  • Trigger: code pushed/PR created
  • terraform init, terraform validate
  • terraform plan
  • Post plan to PR for review
  • Run linting (tflint, checkov)
CD Stage
  • Trigger: merge to main (or manual approval)
  • terraform apply (with state from S3/backend)
  • Log changes for audit
Example: GitHub Actions
name: Terraform CI/CD

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]

jobs:
  plan:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - uses: hashicorp/setup-terraform@v2
      - run: terraform init
      - run: terraform plan -out=tfplan
      - uses: actions/github-script@v6
        with:
          script: |
            github.rest.issues.createComment({
              issue_number: context.issue.number,
              owner: context.repo.owner,
              repo: context.repo.repo,
              body: 'Terraform Plan:\n' + tfplan
            })

  apply:
    if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main' && github.event_name == 'push'
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - uses: hashicorp/setup-terraform@v2
      - run: terraform init
      - run: terraform apply -auto-approve
Say this in interview "CI runs terraform plan on every PR, posts output for review. CD runs terraform apply on merge. Automates safe deployments: code review → preview → approval → apply."
Terraform CI/CD

Explain the GitOps approach with Terraform

Infrastructure state defined and managed entirely through Git.

Q&AGit-driven infrastructure›
GitOps Principles
  • Git is source of truth for infrastructure
  • All changes made via Git commits, never manual
  • CI/CD automatically deploys when Git changes
  • Easy rollback: revert Git commit and redeploy
Workflow
  • 1. Developer changes .tf files in feature branch
  • 2. Push to GitHub, open PR
  • 3. CI: terraform plan (preview)
  • 4. Team reviews plan in PR comments
  • 5. Approval + merge
  • 6. CD: automatically runs terraform apply
  • 7. Infrastructure updated to match Git
Benefits
  • Audit trail: all changes in Git
  • No manual commands: consistent deployments
  • Easy rollback: git revert + redeploy
  • Code review: all infrastructure changes reviewed
Comparison: Manual vs GitOps
  • Manual: Dev runs terraform apply locally (error-prone, no audit trail)
  • GitOps: All changes via Git, CI/CD handles deployment (safe, auditable)
Say this in interview "GitOps: Git is source of truth. All changes via Git commits + PR review. CI/CD automatically deploys. Rollback = git revert + redeploy. Audit trail in Git history."
Terraform CI/CD

How do you test Terraform code?

Multiple testing strategies for infrastructure as code.

Q&ATerraform testing›
Validation & Linting
  • terraform validate (syntax check)
  • terraform fmt (formatting check)
  • tflint (advanced linting)
  • Catch errors before apply
Policy as Code (Checkov, OPA)
  • Enforce security policies (e.g., "all buckets encrypted")
  • Checkov: scans .tf files for misconfigurations
  • OPA: custom policies
Integration Testing (Terratest)
  • Actually deploy infrastructure (to test env)
  • Run tests: SSH into instance, verify software installed
  • Destroy after test
  • Expensive but comprehensive
Example: CI Testing Pipeline
- terraform init
- terraform validate
- terraform fmt -check
- tflint
- checkov -d .
- terratest/go test ./...  # Deploy to test env and verify
Say this in interview "Test at multiple levels: validate (syntax), lint (best practices), checkov (security policies), terratest (integration). Run all in CI before merging."
Terraform CI/CD

How do you handle approvals before applying Terraform changes?

Gates to prevent accidental infrastructure changes.

Q&AApproval workflows›
GitHub/GitLab Branch Protection
  • Require PR review before merge
  • Require CI checks pass
  • Require approval from specific users (e.g., platform team)
CI/CD Manual Approval
  • CD stage waits for manual approval
  • Only approved users can click "approve"
  • terraform apply runs only after approval
Policy as Code (OPA)
  • Automatically reject changes that violate policy
  • Example: require all resources tagged, require encryption
  • No human review needed for standard violations
Terraform Cloud/Enterprise
  • Built-in approval workflow
  • Cost estimation shown in plan
  • Require human approval before apply
Best Practice
  • Dev/staging: auto-approve after tests pass
  • Production: manual approval required
  • Branch protection + CI checks + manual approval
Say this in interview "Prod changes need approval gates: PR review, CI checks pass, manual approval before apply. Dev/staging can auto-approve. Use branch protection + approval workflows in CI/CD."
Terraform 08

Troubleshooting

Terraform Troubleshooting

How do you enable debug logging in Terraform?

Detailed logs for debugging Terraform issues.

Q&ADebug logging›
TF_LOG Environment Variable
  • TF_LOG=TRACE terraform plan (most verbose)
  • TF_LOG=DEBUG terraform plan
  • TF_LOG=INFO terraform plan
  • TF_LOG=WARN terraform plan
  • TF_LOG=ERROR terraform plan (least verbose)
Save Logs to File
  • TF_LOG=TRACE terraform plan > debug.log 2>&1
  • Or: TF_LOG_PATH=debug.log terraform plan
  • Can inspect huge logs offline
What Each Level Shows
  • TRACE: API calls, HTTP headers, response bodies
  • DEBUG: State changes, resource operations
  • INFO: High-level operations
  • WARN: Warnings and issues
  • ERROR: Errors only
Example
export TF_LOG=DEBUG
export TF_LOG_PATH=terraform.log
terraform plan
# View logs
tail -f terraform.log
Say this in interview "Enable debug with TF_LOG=DEBUG. Use TF_LOG_PATH to write to file. TRACE shows API details, DEBUG shows state/resources, ERROR shows errors only. Use when terraform plan/apply fails."
Terraform Troubleshooting

State drift — what is it and how do you detect it?

When real infrastructure doesn't match Terraform state.

Q&AState misalignment›
What Is State Drift
  • Actual infrastructure differs from Terraform state
  • Cause: manual changes (someone manually modified resource)
  • Example: Admin manually changed storage account encryption, but Terraform state still shows old config
How to Detect
  • terraform plan (will show "up for update" or "will be updated")
  • terraform refresh (updates state from real resources)
  • Automated drift detection (e.g., lambda that runs terraform plan nightly)
How to Resolve (Prevent)
  • Option 1: Correct state terraform apply (overwrite real infrastructure to match state)
  • Option 2: Correct state file terraform refresh (update state to match real)
  • Option 3: Prevent manual changes Enforce IAM policies: only allow changes via Terraform
Prevention
  • Enforce Terraform-only changes via IAM
  • Run terraform plan regularly (detect drift early)
  • Audit logs: who modified what
  • Automated drift detection (Lambda + SNS)
Say this in interview "State drift = real infrastructure differs from state (manual changes). Detect with terraform plan. Resolve: terraform apply to correct infrastructure, or refresh to update state. Prevent: enforce Terraform-only changes via IAM + audit logs."
Terraform Troubleshooting

How do you fix a corrupted state file?

Recovering from state file corruption or loss.

DisasterRecovery›
Prevention (Before Corruption)
  • Remote state with versioning (Azure Storage snapshots or S3 versioning)
  • Regular backups: cp terraform.tfstate backup.tfstate
  • State locking: prevents concurrent modifications
If Corrupted: Restore from Backup
  • cp backup.tfstate terraform.tfstate
  • If remote: Azure Storage snapshots or S3 versioning → download previous version
If No Backup: Reimport
  • terraform init (new clean state)
  • terraform import azurerm_linux_virtual_machine.web /subscriptions/xxx/resourceGroups/rg/providers/Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachines/web
  • For each resource: terraform import type.name azure-resource-id
  • Time-consuming but recovers state
As Last Resort: Destroy & Recreate
  • Manually delete cloud resources
  • Delete corrupted state: rm terraform.tfstate*
  • terraform apply (recreates everything)
  • Causes downtime
Say this in interview "Prevention: remote state + versioning + backups. If corrupted: restore from backup (Azure snapshots or S3 versioning). If no backup: reimport resources. As last resort: destroy + recreate."
Terraform Troubleshooting

Resource fails to delete — what do you do?

When terraform destroy fails to remove a resource.

ScenarioDeletion issues›
Common Causes
  • Dependencies: other resources still depend on it
  • Permissions: IAM role lacks delete permissions
  • Protection: resource has delete protection enabled
  • In-use: resource still in use (e.g., load balancer still routing)
Troubleshooting Steps
  • 1. Read error message carefully: it usually says why
  • 2. Check dependencies: what's still referencing this?
  • 3. terraform plan (see order of deletion)
  • 4. Check IAM permissions: can role delete this resource?
  • 5. Cloud console: check if protection enabled
Solutions
  • Disable protection: Set protection flag to false, then destroy
  • Remove dependencies: terraform destroy in correct order
  • Add permissions: Update IAM role with delete permission
  • Manual deletion: Delete in cloud console, then terraform refresh
Say this in interview "Resource won't delete? Check: (1) error message, (2) dependencies, (3) IAM permissions, (4) protection flag. Fix the underlying cause, then retry terraform destroy. If stuck, manual delete + refresh."
Terraform Troubleshooting

How do you handle API rate limiting with Terraform?

When cloud provider rate limits block Terraform operations.

Q&ARate limit handling›
What Causes It
  • Too many API calls in short time
  • Creating 100 resources at once
  • Large state files (many resources)
Solutions
  • Parallelism: Reduce with terraform apply -parallelism=1
  • Retry logic: Providers have built-in backoff/retry
  • Batch operations: Apply in smaller chunks
  • Request increase: Contact cloud provider for higher limits
Example
  • Creating 100 VMs at once → rate limited
  • Solution: terraform apply -parallelism=5 (create 5 at a time)
  • Takes longer but respects rate limits
Say this in interview "Rate limited? Reduce parallelism: terraform apply -parallelism=1. Providers have automatic backoff/retry. For large deployments, apply in batches. Contact provider for higher limits if needed."
Terraform 09

Advanced Patterns

Terraform Advanced

How do you manage multiple cloud providers with Terraform?

Using Terraform across AWS, Azure, GCP, etc.

Q&AMulti-cloud setup›
Multiple Providers in One Config
provider "azurerm" {
  features {}
}

provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-1"
}

provider "google" {
  project = "my-project"
}

# Create in Azure
resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "web_vm" {
  name                = "web-vm"
  location            = "eastus"
  resource_group_name = azurerm_resource_group.rg.name
  size                = "Standard_B1s"
}

# Create in AWS
resource "aws_instance" "backup_vm" {
  ami           = "ami-12345"
  instance_type = "t2.micro"
}
Separate State Files (Recommended)
  • azure/main.tf (azure resources, azure backend)
  • aws/main.tf (aws resources, aws backend)
  • gcp/main.tf (gcp resources, gcp backend)
  • Prevents: one broken cloud from affecting others
Single State File (Not Recommended)
  • All providers in one config, one state file
  • Simpler but riskier: one provider issue blocks all
Say this in interview "Separate state per provider: aws/, azure/, gcp/ folders. Different backends per cloud. If one fails, others unaffected. Avoids: single point of failure, state lock contention, cross-cloud blast radius."
Terraform Advanced

Explain count and for_each — when do you use each?

Creating multiple resources dynamically.

Q&ADynamic resources›
count
  • Create N identical resources based on a number
  • Example: count.index references 0, 1, 2, ...
  • Terraform reference: resource.name[0], resource.name[1]
  • Problem: if you delete first item, all indexes shift (risky)
for_each
  • Create resources based on map or set
  • Each item has a unique key (stable)
  • Terraform reference: resource.name["key1"]
  • Safer: deleting one key doesn't affect others
count Example
resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "web" {
  count               = 3  # Create 3 VMs
  name                = "web-vm-${count.index + 1}"
  location            = azurerm_resource_group.rg.location
  resource_group_name = azurerm_resource_group.rg.name
  size                = "Standard_B1s"

  tags = {
    Name = "web-${count.index + 1}"  # web-1, web-2, web-3
  }
  os_disk { caching = "ReadWrite" storage_account_type = "Premium_LRS" }
}

# Reference: azurerm_linux_virtual_machine.web[0], web[1], etc.
output "ips" {
  value = azurerm_linux_virtual_machine.web[*].private_ip_addresses
}
for_each Example
variable "environment_sizes" {
  type = map(string)
  default = {
    dev  = "Standard_B1s"
    prod = "Standard_D2s_v3"
  }
}

resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "web" {
  for_each            = var.environment_sizes
  name                = "web-${each.key}"
  location            = azurerm_resource_group.rg.location
  resource_group_name = azurerm_resource_group.rg.name
  size                = each.value  # Standard_B1s for dev, Standard_D2s for prod

  tags = {
    Name = "web-${each.key}"  # web-dev, web-prod
  }
  os_disk { caching = "ReadWrite" storage_account_type = "Premium_LRS" }
}

# Reference: azurerm_linux_virtual_machine.web["dev"], web["prod"]
When to Use
  • count: Simple: N identical resources, order doesn't matter
  • for_each: Safer: keyed resources, delete one without side effects
  • Recommendation: Prefer for_each (more maintainable)
Say this in interview "count = create N identical resources. for_each = create resources with stable keys. Prefer for_each: deleting one key doesn't affect others (index-shifting problem in count)."
Terraform Advanced

Cross-region and cross-account deployments with Terraform

Managing infrastructure across AWS regions and accounts.

Q&AMulti-region/account›
Multiple Regions (Same Subscription)
provider "azurerm" {
  alias    = "eastus"
  features {}
  subscription_id = var.subscription_id
}

provider "azurerm" {
  alias    = "westeurope"
  features {}
  subscription_id = var.subscription_id
}

resource "azurerm_resource_group" "us_rg" {
  provider = azurerm.eastus
  name     = "rg-us"
  location = "eastus"
}

resource "azurerm_resource_group" "eu_rg" {
  provider = azurerm.westeurope
  name     = "rg-eu"
  location = "westeurope"
}
Cross-Subscription (Different Subscriptions)
provider "azurerm" {
  alias           = "subscription-a"
  features {}
  subscription_id = "11111111-1111-1111-1111-111111111111"
}

provider "azurerm" {
  alias           = "subscription-b"
  features {}
  subscription_id = "22222222-2222-2222-2222-222222222222"
}

resource "azurerm_resource_group" "rg_a" {
  provider = azurerm.subscription-a
  name     = "rg-in-sub-a"
  location = "eastus"
}

resource "azurerm_resource_group" "rg_b" {
  provider = azurerm.subscription-b
  name     = "rg-in-sub-b"
  location = "eastus"
}
Best Practices
  • Separate terraform configurations per subscription (for safety)
  • Use different subscription IDs in provider blocks for cross-subscription
  • Different state files per subscription/region
  • Document relationships between deployments
Say this in interview "Use provider aliases for multiple regions/subscriptions. Separate subscriptions in provider blocks. Separate state files per subscription (safety). One subscription failing doesn't affect others."
Terraform 10

Real-world Scenarios

Terraform Scenarios

You have 1000 resources already provisioned manually. How do you bring them under Terraform?

The painful process of importing existing infrastructure.

ScenarioImport existing infra›
High-level Approach
  • 1. Generate resource stubs for all 1000 resources
  • 2. Use terraform import to associate Terraform code with real resources
  • 3. Test plan matches reality (no unwanted changes)
  • 4. Incrementally manage via Terraform
Step-by-step
  • Step 1: Inventory resources List all 1000 resources (VPCs, instances, etc.)
  • Step 2: Generate stubs Write resource blocks without properties
  • Step 3: terraform import Associate each resource ID with Terraform
  • Step 4: terraform plan Should show no changes (imports accurate)
  • Step 5: Incrementally manage Now changes are controlled by Terraform
Example
# Generate stub
resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "web_1" {
  # Empty for now
}

# Import real resource
terraform import azurerm_linux_virtual_machine.web_1 /subscriptions/xxx/resourceGroups/rg/providers/Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachines/web-vm-1

# Terraform now knows about this VM
terraform plan  # Should show no changes if import successful
Challenges
  • Time-consuming: 1000 resources = lots of imports
  • Not everything importable: some resources/attributes can't be imported
  • For large scale: write scripts to automate import (python boto3 + terraform)
Tools
  • terraformer: auto-generates Terraform code from existing cloud resources
  • Instaclustr Managed Terraform: auto-generate for databases
  • Python script: loop through AWS API, call terraform import
Say this in interview "Bring 1000 manual resources into Terraform: (1) inventory them, (2) generate resource stubs, (3) terraform import each ID, (4) verify plan shows no changes. For scale, use terraformer or write Python script to automate. Time-consuming but worth it for management."
Terraform Scenarios

A developer accidentally ran terraform destroy on production. How do you recover?

The worst-case scenario and recovery options.

DisasterRecovery steps›
Immediate Actions
  • 1. STOP: Don't run terraform apply again
  • 2. CHECK: What actually got destroyed? (check cloud console)
  • 3. SNAPSHOT: Note start time, affected resources, state
Recovery Options
  • Option A: Restore from state backup
  • If you have state versioning: restore previous state version
  • S3 versioning: download terraform.tfstate from previous point-in-time
  • Then: terraform apply (recreates resources)
  • Option B: Restore from snapshots
  • For persistent resources (EBS, RDS): restore from snapshot
  • For state: terraform import to associate restored resources
  • Option C: Backup + restore service
  • If RDS: backup exists → restore to point-in-time
  • If Azure Storage: geo-redundancy or cross-region copies → restore
Why It Happened & Prevention
  • Problem: No guardrails, destroy not gated
  • Prevention:
  • 1. Require approval before destroy (CI/CD gate)
  • 2. Set protect_destroy = true on critical resources
  • 3. Separate prod Terraform config (different folder, different permissions)
  • 4. MFA required for prod
  • 5. Audit: log who runs terraform, when
Timeline
  • T+0: Developer runs terraform destroy
  • T+5min: Alert fires (monitoring detects infra down)
  • T+15min: Restore from state snapshot
  • T+30min: terraform apply (recreates)
  • T+1hr: Back online, root cause analysis
Say this in interview "First: stop. Check what destroyed. If state backup exists: restore + apply. If snapshots: restore + import. Prevention: require approval before destroy, set protect_destroy on critical resources, separate prod config with different permissions, audit logs."
Terraform Scenarios

You need to rename a resource without destroying it. How?

Safely renaming resources in Terraform state.

ScenarioResource renaming›
The Problem
  • You have: resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "web" { ... }
  • You want: resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "frontend" { ... }
  • If you just rename in code: Terraform sees old as destroyed, new as created
  • Result: VM destroyed + recreated (downtime!)
Solution: terraform state mv
# Before: resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "web"
terraform state mv azurerm_linux_virtual_machine.web azurerm_linux_virtual_machine.frontend

# Update code:
# resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine" "frontend" { ... }

# Verify
terraform plan  # Should show no changes
Step-by-step
  • 1. Update Terraform code with new name
  • 2. Run: terraform state mv old_address new_address
  • 3. terraform plan (should show no changes)
  • 4. terraform apply (if needed, usually not)
Other Scenarios
  • Rename module: terraform state mv module.old module.new
  • Move between modules: terraform state mv azurerm_linux_virtual_machine.web module.frontend.azurerm_linux_virtual_machine.web
  • Rename in for_each: More complex, usually destroy + recreate
What NOT to Do
  • Don't just rename in code (destroys + recreates)
  • Don't manually edit state file (breaks state)
  • Don't forget to update code after state mv
Say this in interview "Use terraform state mv to rename without destruction. (1) Update code, (2) terraform state mv old new, (3) verify with plan. Never just rename in code; never manually edit state file."
Azure 01

Compute

Azure Compute

Explain IaaS, PaaS, SaaS services in Azure

Three service models with increasing levels of responsibility handed to Azure.

Q&AService models›
IaaS (Infrastructure as a Service)
  • You manage: applications, data, runtime, middleware, OS
  • Azure manages: virtualization, servers, storage, networking
  • Example: Azure VMs, Managed Disks, Virtual Networks
  • Most control, most responsibility
PaaS (Platform as a Service)
  • You manage: applications, data
  • Azure manages: runtime, middleware, OS, networking, servers
  • Example: Azure App Service, Azure SQL Database, Function Apps
  • Focus on code, not infrastructure
SaaS (Software as a Service)
  • You manage: users and access
  • Azure/Provider manages: everything else
  • Example: Microsoft 365, Dynamics 365, GitHub Copilot
  • Minimal management needed
Say this in interview "IaaS: you manage OS and above (VMs). PaaS: Azure manages OS and runtime (App Service). SaaS: provider manages everything (Office 365). More abstraction = less control but less work."
Azure Compute

Compare Azure VMs, App Service, Container Instances, and Batch

Four different compute options for different workload types.

Q&ACompute comparison›
Azure VMs (IaaS)
  • Full OS control, custom software, lift-and-shift workloads
  • You manage patching, updates, security
  • Highest cost and responsibility
App Service (PaaS)
  • Web apps, APIs, mobile backends
  • Built-in scaling, deployment slots, monitoring
  • Azure handles OS and runtime patches
  • Reduced management burden
Container Instances (Serverless)
  • Run containers without managing orchestration
  • Fast startup, pay per second
  • Good for short-lived tasks, not for always-on apps
Batch (High-performance computing)
  • Large-scale parallel computing jobs
  • Auto-scaling of compute nodes based on workload
  • Good for data processing, simulations, rendering
Say this in interview "VMs for full control. App Service for web apps. Container Instances for quick tasks. Batch for massive parallel processing. Choose based on management overhead vs control needed."
Azure Compute

What is Azure App Service? What are the pricing tiers and how do you scale?

PaaS for web apps, APIs, and mobile backends with built-in scaling.

Q&AApp Service›
What is App Service
  • Managed platform for web apps, REST APIs, mobile backends
  • Supports: .NET, Java, Node.js, Python, PHP, Ruby
  • Includes: deployment, monitoring, SSL certificates, custom domains
  • Built-in CI/CD from GitHub, GitLab, Azure DevOps
Pricing Tiers
  • Free/Shared: Development only, shared resources, 1GB storage
  • Basic (B1, B2, B3): Dedicated VM, no auto-scale, $0.0133/hour
  • Standard (S1, S2, S3): Auto-scale, deployment slots, $0.1/hour
  • Premium (P1V2, P2V2, P3V2): Better performance, more features, isolated environment
  • Isolated: Complete isolation, highest security/performance
Scaling
  • Scale Up: Move to larger VM size (manual)
  • Scale Out: Add more instances (manual or auto-scale)
  • Auto-scale based on CPU, memory, custom metrics
  • Min/max instance rules
Say this in interview "App Service is PaaS for web apps. Free/Basic for dev, Standard for production. Auto-scale available on Standard+. Scale out adds instances, scale up increases VM size."
Azure Compute

Explain auto-scaling in App Service — how does it work?

Automatically adjust resource capacity based on demand.

Q&AAuto-scale mechanics›
How Auto-scale Works
  • Monitor metrics: CPU %, memory, request count, queue length
  • Compare to rules: if CPU > 70%, add instance
  • Scale out: add instances to handle load
  • Scale in: remove instances when demand drops (cool-down period)
Auto-scale Rules
  • Scale out rule: if CPU > 70% for 10 minutes, add 2 instances
  • Scale in rule: if CPU < 30% for 10 minutes, remove 1 instance
  • Min instances: 1, Max instances: 10
  • Cool-down: wait 5 minutes before scaling again
Best Practices
  • Use custom metrics (queue length) for better decisions
  • Set reasonable min/max limits
  • Scale out quickly, scale in slowly (avoid thrashing)
  • Monitor costs: each instance has hourly cost
Say this in interview "Auto-scale monitors metrics and adds/removes instances based on rules. Scale out when demand high, scale in when low. Use custom metrics for better accuracy. Cool-down prevents flapping."
Azure Compute

What is Azure Function Apps? How do they scale?

Serverless compute: run code without managing servers.

Q&AFunctions scaling›
What are Function Apps
  • Serverless functions: run code in response to events
  • Triggers: HTTP, timer, blob storage, queue, Cosmos DB, Event Hub
  • Pay per execution (consumption plan)
  • Supports: C#, Node.js, Python, Java, PowerShell
Hosting Plans
  • Consumption: Auto-scale to 0, pay per invocation, $0.20 per million
  • Premium: Pre-warmed instances, higher throughput, predictable billing
  • App Service Plan: Share with App Service, fixed cost
Scaling Behavior
  • Consumption plan: scales to 200 instances automatically
  • No cold start on pre-warmed instances (Premium)
  • Per-function scaling: each function auto-scales independently
  • Built-in rate limiting to prevent runaway costs
Say this in interview "Function Apps are serverless: pay per execution. Consumption plan scales to 0 (cost-effective). Premium plan has pre-warmed instances (faster). Auto-scaling is built-in and transparent."
Azure Compute

What is Azure Logic Apps? Compare with Azure Automation

Two tools for orchestrating workflows, with different strengths.

Q&AWorkflow orchestration›
Azure Logic Apps
  • Visual designer for workflows: if-then, loops, conditions
  • Connectors: 600+ pre-built integrations (Salesforce, Teams, GitHub, etc.)
  • Triggers: HTTP, schedule, blob created, email received
  • Best for: cloud integration, API orchestration, business workflows
  • Low-code/no-code
Azure Automation
  • Runbooks: PowerShell/Python scripts for infrastructure automation
  • Process Automation: repeat tasks on schedule
  • Best for: VM management, patch automation, infrastructure tasks
  • Code-based (PowerShell/Python)
Comparison
  • Logic Apps: business processes, cloud integrations
  • Automation: infrastructure tasks, system management
  • Logic Apps: low-code, connectors. Automation: scripts, control
Say this in interview "Logic Apps for cloud workflows (Salesforce, Teams). Automation for infra tasks (patch VMs, backup). Logic Apps is visual, Automation is scripted. Use together for end-to-end automation."
Azure Compute

What is Azure Container Instances (ACI)? When would you use it vs AKS?

Serverless containers without orchestration overhead.

Q&AContainers comparison›
ACI (Azure Container Instances)
  • Run containers without managing orchestration
  • Serverless: pay per second
  • Fastest way to run a container
  • No cluster management needed
AKS (Azure Kubernetes Service)
  • Managed Kubernetes cluster
  • Multi-container applications, service discovery, auto-scaling
  • More complex, more features
  • Better for production at scale
When to use ACI
  • Simple applications: single container
  • Batch jobs, testing, CI/CD tasks
  • Occasional/bursty workloads
  • Quick prototyping
When to use AKS
  • Microservices: many containers with dependencies
  • Production workloads needing high availability
  • Complex networking, service discovery needed
  • Long-running applications at scale
Say this in interview "ACI: simple containers, serverless, pay-per-second. AKS: orchestrated clusters, microservices, production. ACI for jobs/testing, AKS for complex apps. ACI can be burst layer for AKS."
Azure Compute

How do you manage VM updates in Azure? (Patch management)

Keep VMs secure with automated and manual patching strategies.

Q&APatch strategies›
Update Management
  • Azure Update Management in Azure Monitor
  • Assess: see missing updates for all VMs
  • Schedule: deploy updates on specific dates/times
  • Report: track patch status across fleet
Patch Orchestration
  • Automatic: OS applies patches automatically (risky, no control)
  • Image default: patches applied by image updates
  • Manual: you control when patches apply
  • Configured per VM in OS settings
Best Practices
  • Use Update Management for visibility and scheduling
  • Patch on maintenance windows (Tuesday or early morning)
  • Test patches on non-prod first
  • Use availability sets/zones for zero-downtime patching
  • Enable auto-shutdown for dev/test VMs
Say this in interview "Use Update Management to schedule patches. Manual or automatic options. Patch during maintenance windows. Test first. Use availability sets/zones for zero-downtime."
Azure Compute

Explain Azure Virtual Machine Scale Sets (VMSS)

Auto-scale a group of identical VMs based on demand.

Q&AVMSS features›
What is VMSS
  • Group of identical VMs that scale automatically
  • Same image, configuration, size
  • Auto-scale based on: CPU, memory, custom metrics
  • Managed as single logical unit
Scaling Options
  • Uniform scaling: all VMs same size and config
  • Flexible scaling: mix of VM sizes and versions (preview)
  • Overprovisioning: temporarily create more instances, then remove
  • Termination policy: delete oldest or newest instances first
Use Cases
  • Web farms, microservices backend
  • Batch processing, data parallel workloads
  • High-availability applications (combine with Load Balancer)
Say this in interview "VMSS automatically scales identical VMs based on demand. Combine with Load Balancer for HA. Faster than App Service for low-level control. Update VMs via image upgrades."
Azure Compute

What are Azure managed disks? What are the benefits over unmanaged disks?

Simplified disk management with Azure-handled complexity.

Q&ADisk management›
Managed Disks
  • Azure manages storage accounts, replication, lifecycle
  • You just: create disk, attach to VM, use
  • Default for all new VMs
  • Sizes: Standard HDD, Standard SSD, Premium SSD, Ultra
Unmanaged Disks (Legacy)
  • You manage storage accounts
  • Handle replication, scaling limits (20 TB/account)
  • Complex lifecycle management
  • No longer recommended
Benefits of Managed Disks
  • Simpler: no storage account management
  • More reliable: 99.999% availability SLA
  • Better security: encryption at rest by default
  • Scalable: no 20 TB per account limit
  • Snapshots and disk cloning built-in
Say this in interview "Use managed disks (default now). Azure handles complexity. Unmanaged disks legacy, not recommended. Managed = simpler, more reliable, encrypted by default."
Azure 02

Networking

Azure Networking

What is Azure Virtual Network (VNet)? Explain subnets and NSGs

Isolated network space in Azure with subnets and security.

Q&ANetwork foundations›
Azure Virtual Network (VNet)
  • Isolated network in Azure (like VPC in AWS)
  • Define address space: 10.0.0.0/16
  • Resources in VNet can communicate privately
  • Connect to on-prem via VPN or ExpressRoute
Subnets
  • Subdivide VNet address space
  • Example: 10.0.1.0/24 for frontend, 10.0.2.0/24 for backend
  • Each subnet has NSG for security
  • Enables logical segmentation
Network Security Groups (NSGs)
  • Firewall rules at subnet/NIC level
  • Inbound rules: allow/deny traffic into subnet
  • Outbound rules: allow/deny traffic out
  • Rules evaluated in priority order
Say this in interview "VNet is isolated network (10.0.0.0/16). Subnets subdivide it (10.0.1.0/24). NSGs are firewalls with inbound/outbound rules. Use NSGs to restrict traffic between subnets."
Azure Networking

What is Azure Application Gateway? How does it differ from Load Balancer?

Layer 7 (application) vs Layer 4 (transport) load balancing.

Q&ALoad balancing comparison›
Azure Load Balancer (Layer 4)
  • Transport layer: TCP/UDP
  • Distributes based on IP protocol data
  • Stateful: remembers connections
  • Lower latency, simpler
  • Good for: millions of requests/second
Application Gateway (Layer 7)
  • Application layer: HTTP/HTTPS
  • Understands URLs, hostnames, paths
  • Route based on: /api/* → backend 1, /images/* → backend 2
  • SSL termination, WAF (Web Application Firewall)
  • Good for: web applications, microservices
When to use each
  • Load Balancer: non-HTTP protocols, extreme scale (millions/sec), UDP
  • App Gateway: web apps, path-based routing, SSL offload, WAF
Say this in interview "Load Balancer is Layer 4 (TCP/UDP), simple and fast. App Gateway is Layer 7 (HTTP), understands URLs and can route /api to backend1. Use App Gateway for web apps, Load Balancer for massive scale."
Azure Networking

Explain Azure Load Balancer — public vs internal

Two types of load balancers for different routing needs.

Q&ALB types›
Public Load Balancer
  • Frontend IP: public IP address
  • Incoming internet traffic → backend VMs
  • Translate public IP to private IP
  • Example: website load balancer
Internal Load Balancer
  • Frontend IP: private IP inside VNet
  • Traffic from within VNet only
  • No internet exposure
  • Example: backend service-to-service routing
Common Configuration
  • Public LB for frontend layer (web tier)
  • Internal LB for backend communication (business logic, database)
  • Multi-tier: public → frontend → internal LB → backend
Say this in interview "Public LB: internet facing with public IP. Internal LB: VNet-only with private IP. Multi-tier: public LB for web, internal LB for backend services."
Azure Networking

What is Azure Traffic Manager? When would you use it?

Global load balancing across regions and endpoints.

Q&AGlobal routing›
What is Traffic Manager
  • DNS-based routing across global endpoints
  • Routes based on: geography, performance, priority, weighted
  • Can route to Azure, on-prem, other clouds
  • Health checks: disable unhealthy endpoints
Routing Methods
  • Performance: route to closest region (lowest latency)
  • Geographic: route based on user location
  • Priority: primary region, failover to secondary
  • Weighted: 70% to region A, 30% to region B
Use Cases
  • Multi-region applications for low latency
  • Disaster recovery: failover to backup region
  • Blue-green deployments: route to new version gradually
  • Split traffic between on-prem and cloud
Say this in interview "Traffic Manager is DNS-based global load balancer. Routes based on performance (closest region), priority (DR), geography (location). Use for multi-region apps and failover."
Azure Networking

Explain Azure VPN Gateway vs ExpressRoute

Two ways to connect on-premises to Azure.

Q&AConnectivity options›
VPN Gateway
  • Encrypted tunnel over public internet
  • Setup: 10 minutes
  • Cost: ~$50/month
  • Bandwidth: up to 10 Gbps
  • Less reliable (internet-dependent)
ExpressRoute
  • Private dedicated connection (not internet)
  • Setup: weeks (carrier provisioning)
  • Cost: $500+/month
  • Bandwidth: 50 Mbps to 100 Gbps
  • High reliability (SLA: 99.99%)
When to use each
  • VPN: small offices, dev/test, low budget, quick setup
  • ExpressRoute: production, high bandwidth, high reliability, sensitive data
Say this in interview "VPN: encrypted tunnel over internet, quick setup, low cost. ExpressRoute: private connection, higher cost, better reliability. Use VPN for dev, ExpressRoute for production."
Azure Networking

What is Azure DDoS Protection? Standard vs Premium

Protection against distributed denial of service attacks.

Q&ADDoS protection›
Basic Protection (Free)
  • Included with all Azure subscriptions
  • Protects against common Layer 3/4 attacks
  • No cost, always on
Standard Protection ($2,944/month)
  • Enhanced protection for DDoS attacks
  • Mitigation: traffic analysis, adaptive policies
  • Cost reduction: mitigation credit for legitimate traffic
  • Support: DDoS experts available
When to Enable Standard
  • Public-facing applications (web, API)
  • Mission-critical services
  • E-commerce, financial services
Say this in interview "Basic DDoS protection free, always on. Standard (~$3k/month) adds advanced mitigation and expert support. Use Standard for public-facing/critical applications."
Azure Networking

How do you implement network segmentation in Azure?

Isolate resources for security and performance.

Q&ASegmentation strategy›
Segmentation Layers
  • VNets: separate address spaces for different environments (dev, prod)
  • Subnets: divide VNet by function (web, business, database)
  • NSGs: firewall rules between subnets
  • Private endpoints: restrict access to Azure services
Example: 3-tier Application
  • Web tier subnet: public LB → App Service/VMs
  • Business tier subnet: internal LB → microservices
  • Data tier subnet: SQL Database, Redis (no public access)
  • NSG rules: Web → Business → Data (one direction)
Best Practices
  • Deny by default, allow specific traffic
  • Use service tags (AzureServices, Internet)
  • Log NSG flows for audit/troubleshooting
  • Use Application Security Groups (ASGs) for easier rules
Say this in interview "Segment with VNets (environments), subnets (layers), NSGs (rules). Default-deny model. Example: web-tier NSG allows public, business-tier allows only from web, data-tier allows only from business."
Azure Networking

Explain private endpoints and service endpoints in Azure

Two ways to keep Azure services off the public internet.

Q&APrivate access›
Service Endpoints
  • Route traffic to Azure services through Microsoft backbone
  • Service public IP, but traffic doesn't leave Microsoft network
  • Simpler: no DNS changes, no VPN needed
  • Example: restrict SQL Database to specific VNet
Private Endpoints
  • Private IP inside VNet for Azure service
  • Service appears as resource in VNet
  • Uses private DNS zone
  • More granular control, better isolation
Comparison
  • Service Endpoint: simpler, subnet-level, free
  • Private Endpoint: more granular, NIC-level, charged (~$7/month)
  • Use Service Endpoint for quick setup, Private Endpoint for strict isolation
Say this in interview "Service Endpoints: route through Microsoft backbone (free). Private Endpoints: private IP in VNet (~$7/month). Both keep traffic off public internet. Service = simpler, Private = more control."
Azure 03

Storage

Azure Storage

What are the different Azure Storage account types?

Different tiers of storage performance and cost.

Q&AStorage tiers›
Storage Account Types
  • Standard (General-purpose v2): Blob, Queue, Table, Files. $0.0184/GB for hot
  • Premium (Specialized): Block blobs, page blobs, file shares. Low latency, high throughput
  • FileStorage: Premium file shares for enterprise
  • BlockBlobStorage: Premium for block blobs (transactional workloads)
Replication Types
  • LRS (Local): 3 copies in same datacenter, cheap but lowest durability
  • ZRS (Zone): 3 copies across zones, good for HA
  • GRS (Geo): 3 copies local + 3 copies in paired region
  • RAGRS (Read-access Geo): GRS + read-only access to secondary
Say this in interview "Standard: general purpose, affordable. Premium: high performance. Choose replication: LRS (single region, cheap), ZRS (zones), GRS (regions). Use GRS for production."
Azure Storage

Explain Azure Blob Storage — hot, cool, and archive tiers

Different access tiers optimize cost vs performance.

Q&ABlob tiers›
Hot Tier
  • Frequently accessed data
  • Cost: highest storage (~$0.018/GB), lowest access
  • Retrieval: immediate
  • Example: active website content, logs
Cool Tier
  • Infrequently accessed, 30+ days old
  • Cost: medium storage (~$0.01/GB), higher access charges
  • Retrieval: immediate but charged
  • Example: backups, archives
Archive Tier
  • Rarely accessed, 90+ days retention
  • Cost: lowest storage (~$0.004/GB), highest access/retrieval
  • Retrieval: 1-15 hours (rehydration needed)
  • Example: compliance archives, long-term backup
Lifecycle Policies
  • Auto-move blobs: Hot → Cool after 30 days → Archive after 90 days
  • Delete after 365 days
  • Reduces costs automatically
Say this in interview "Hot: frequent access, high storage cost. Cool: occasional access, lower storage, retrieval charges. Archive: rare access, lowest storage, slow retrieval. Use lifecycle policies to auto-tier."
Azure Storage

Explain Azure Data Lake Storage Gen2

Blob storage optimized for big data analytics.

Q&AData Lake›
What is Data Lake Gen2
  • Built on Blob Storage with hierarchy + ACLs
  • Optimized for: data lakes, analytics, big data
  • Hadoop-compatible: works with Spark, Hive, etc.
  • Atomic operations: POSIX-compliant
Key Features
  • Hierarchical namespace: /data/2024/01/file.parquet
  • ACLs: granular permissions at directory/file level
  • Performance: optimized for batch and interactive queries
  • Integration: works with Synapse, Databricks, Power BI
Use Cases
  • Centralized data lake for organization
  • ETL pipelines (Data Factory)
  • Analytics: Synapse SQL, Spark
  • Machine learning training data
Say this in interview "Data Lake Gen2 is Blob Storage + hierarchy + ACLs. Optimized for analytics. Works with Spark/Synapse. Use for data lakes, ETL, ML training."
Azure Storage

What is Shared Access Signature (SAS)? When would you use it?

Time-limited access tokens for storage without sharing account keys.

Q&ASAS tokens›
What is SAS
  • Secure URL with embedded access token
  • Example: https://account.blob.core.windows.net/file.pdf?sv=2021-06-08&sig=...
  • Temporary access (minutes to hours)
  • Grants specific permissions (read, write, delete)
SAS Types
  • Account SAS: access to multiple services (Blob, Queue, Table)
  • Service SAS: access to single service/resource
  • User delegation SAS: uses Azure AD credentials (more secure)
Use Cases
  • Share file temporarily: send SAS URL to client
  • Grant web app access without secrets: embed SAS in app
  • Mobile app: app calls API, API returns SAS, app uses it
  • Backup: SAS with limited time/permissions
Say this in interview "SAS is time-limited access token. Share it instead of account key. Set expiration and permissions. Example: share 1-hour read-only URL to download file."
Azure Storage

Explain Azure Storage replication options (LRS, GRS, RAGRS, ZRS)

Different strategies for data redundancy and availability.

Q&AReplication strategies›
LRS (Locally Redundant Storage)
  • 3 copies in same datacenter
  • Protects: disk failure
  • Doesn't protect: datacenter failure
  • Cost: lowest (~$18/TB/month)
ZRS (Zone Redundant Storage)
  • 3 copies across availability zones
  • Protects: zone failure
  • Same region, high availability
  • Cost: higher than LRS
GRS (Geo Redundant Storage)
  • 3 copies local + 3 copies in paired region
  • Protects: region failure, disaster recovery
  • Secondary read-only without RAGRS
  • Cost: higher (~$28/TB/month)
RAGRS (Read-access GRS)
  • GRS + read access to secondary region
  • Can read from secondary if primary down
  • Cost: highest
Say this in interview "LRS: single DC, cheap. ZRS: zones, HA. GRS: regions, DR. RAGRS: GRS + read secondary. Use LRS for dev, ZRS/GRS for production. RAGRS for mission-critical read-heavy."
Azure Storage

How do you optimize costs for Azure Storage?

Strategies to reduce storage spending.

Q&ACost optimization›
Tiering Strategy
  • Hot tier: $18/TB/month (expensive)
  • Cool tier: $10/TB/month (50% savings)
  • Archive tier: $4/TB/month (80% savings)
  • Lifecycle policies: auto-move old data to cool/archive
Replication Optimization
  • LRS for dev/test (not critical)
  • ZRS for HA (zones, better than GRS cost-wise if in single region)
  • GRS only for DR-critical data
  • RAGRS only for read-heavy DR scenarios
Other Tactics
  • Deduplication: remove duplicate blocks (50-70% savings)
  • Compression: compress data before upload
  • Monitoring: find and delete unused blobs
  • Snapshots: incremental backups (lower cost than full copies)
Say this in interview "Use lifecycle policies: Hot→Cool after 30 days→Archive after 90 days. Use LRS for non-critical. Archive old data. Monitor for unused blobs. Can save 50-80% with tiering."
Azure 04

Databases

Azure Databases

Compare Azure SQL Database vs SQL Managed Instance vs SQL Server on VM

Three options for SQL databases with different control/management tradeoffs.

Q&ASQL options›
Azure SQL Database (PaaS)
  • Fully managed, single database or elastic pool
  • You manage: nothing (Azure handles all)
  • High availability: built-in replicas
  • Cost: $5-100+/month (DTU/vCore pricing)
  • Fastest to deploy, least control
SQL Managed Instance (PaaS+)
  • Full SQL Server compatibility
  • Runs inside VNet (networking control)
  • You manage: databases only
  • Cost: $500+/month (more than SQL DB)
  • Migration path from on-prem SQL Server
SQL Server on VM (IaaS)
  • Full OS/SQL Server control
  • You manage: everything
  • Highest licensing cost (SQL Server + VM)
  • Good for: legacy features, specific versions
  • Most management burden
Quick Comparison
  • SQL DB: new app, fast deployment, lowest cost
  • Managed Instance: migrate from on-prem, need full compatibility
  • SQL on VM: legacy requirements, custom setup
Say this in interview "SQL Database: fully managed, PaaS, fast/cheap. Managed Instance: full SQL compatibility, migrate-friendly. SQL on VM: full control, high cost. Use DB for new apps, MI for migrations."
Azure Databases

Explain point-in-time restore in Azure SQL Database

Recover database to any point in the past.

Q&APITR›
How PITR Works
  • Automatic backups: full every week, differential every day, logs every 5-10 min
  • Can restore to any point in past 7-35 days (depends on tier)
  • Creates new database at that point in time
  • No downtime: original DB still running
Example Scenario
  • Accidental DELETE statement at 2:30 PM
  • Restore database to 2:29 PM
  • Copy data from restored DB back to production
  • Data recovered, original DB continues
Retention Periods
  • Basic: 7 days
  • Standard: 35 days
  • Premium: 35 days (can extend to 35 years with long-term backup)
Say this in interview "PITR: automatic backups allow restore to any point in past 7-35 days. No downtime. Example: accidental delete, restore to pre-delete time. Use for data loss recovery."
Azure Databases

What is read replicas in Azure databases? When do you use them?

Distribute read traffic across replicas for scale and DR.

Q&ARead scaling›
What are Read Replicas
  • Read-only copies of database
  • Replicated from primary (async)
  • Can be in same region or different regions
  • Slightly lagging data (eventual consistency)
Use Cases
  • Scale reads: send read queries to replicas, write to primary
  • Geo-distribution: replica in each region for low latency reads
  • Disaster recovery: failover to replica if primary fails
  • Analytics: run heavy queries on replica without impacting production
Example Architecture
  • Primary (Write) in East US
  • Read Replica in West US (users read from closest)
  • Read Replica in Europe (local latency)
  • Analytics queries run on analytics replica
Say this in interview "Read replicas distribute read traffic and enable geo-redundancy. Primary for writes, replicas for reads. Use for scaling, geo-distribution, and DR. Data slightly lagged (async replication)."
Azure Databases

Explain Azure Redis Cache — use cases and eviction policies

In-memory cache for ultra-fast data access.

Q&ACaching›
What is Redis Cache
  • In-memory data store (not persistent database)
  • Ultra-fast: microsecond latency
  • Data structures: strings, hashes, lists, sets, sorted sets
  • TTL: auto-expire keys after time
Use Cases
  • Session cache: store user sessions (avoid repeated auth)
  • Frequently accessed data: cache query results
  • Real-time: leaderboards, counters, suggestions
  • Pub/Sub: real-time messaging between services
Eviction Policies
  • noeviction: error when memory full (don't use!)
  • allkeys-lru: remove oldest accessed key (default)
  • volatile-lru: remove oldest accessed key with TTL set
  • allkeys-random: remove random key
  • volatile-ttl: remove key with shortest TTL
Say this in interview "Redis: in-memory cache, microsecond latency. Use for sessions, hot data, real-time. Set appropriate eviction: allkeys-lru keeps most-used data. Set TTL to auto-expire old data."
Azure 05

Security & Identity

Azure Security

Explain Azure Active Directory (AAD/Entra ID) — roles, users, groups

Identity management for Azure and hybrid environments.

Q&AIdentity management›
What is AAD/Entra ID
  • Cloud identity provider (like Active Directory for cloud)
  • Single sign-on (SSO) for Azure and Microsoft 365
  • Supports: users, groups, service principals, managed identities
  • Multi-factor authentication (MFA) built-in
Users
  • Cloud-only user: sanjay@company.com (AAD native)
  • Hybrid: synced from on-prem AD via Azure AD Connect
  • Guest users: external users with limited access
Groups
  • Organize users for easier access management
  • Dynamic groups: auto-add users based on rules (e.g., department = sales)
  • Security groups: assign permissions to groups
  • Distribution groups: email lists
Roles (in context of Azure RBAC)
  • Owner, Contributor, Reader (built-in)
  • Service-specific: SQL DB Contributor, VM Operator
  • Custom roles: define specific permissions
Say this in interview "AAD is cloud identity provider. Users (cloud-only or hybrid), groups (organize users), roles (permissions). Use groups for easier RBAC management. Enable MFA for security."
Azure Security

What is RBAC? Explain roles like Owner, Contributor, Reader

Role-Based Access Control for fine-grained permissions.

Q&ARBAC model›
RBAC Components
  • Security Principal: user, group, or service principal
  • Role: collection of permissions
  • Scope: resource, resource group, or subscription
  • Combine: principal + role + scope = permission
Built-in Roles
  • Owner: full access, can assign roles, delete resources
  • Contributor: create/modify resources, can't assign roles
  • Reader: view only, can't modify
  • User Access Administrator: manage access (assign roles)
Example Assignment
  • User: alice@company.com
  • Role: SQL Database Contributor
  • Scope: resource group prod-databases
  • Result: Alice can create/modify SQL databases in that RG
Say this in interview "RBAC = principal + role + scope. Owner: full control. Contributor: create/modify. Reader: view-only. Always use least privilege: assign minimum needed permissions."
Azure Security

Explain the principle of least privilege in Azure

Grant minimum permissions needed for work.

Q&ASecurity principle›
What is Least Privilege
  • Grant minimum permissions needed for role
  • Reduces blast radius if credentials compromised
  • Example: dev needs read SQL DB, not delete/modify
Anti-patterns (Don't Do This)
  • Don't assign Owner to everyone (too much power)
  • Don't use subscription-level roles for single resources
  • Don't share credentials (use managed identities)
Best Practices
  • Use custom roles: define exactly what's needed
  • Scope to resource level: not subscription or RG
  • Use managed identities: no secrets to compromise
  • Regular access reviews: remove unused permissions
  • Time-bound access: Privileged Identity Management (PIM)
Say this in interview "Least privilege: grant minimum permissions. Don't use Owner/Contributor broadly. Use custom roles. Scope to specific resources. Use managed identities. Review access regularly."
Azure Security

What is Azure Key Vault? How do you store and manage secrets?

Secure storage for secrets, keys, certificates.

Q&ASecret management›
What is Key Vault
  • Cloud HSM for storing secrets, keys, certificates
  • Encrypted at rest and in transit
  • Audit logs: see who accessed what
  • RBAC: control access with Azure roles
What to Store
  • Secrets: database passwords, API keys, connection strings
  • Keys: encryption keys for data
  • Certificates: SSL/TLS certs for HTTPS
Access Methods
  • Managed identity: app (VM, Function) retrieves secret automatically
  • Service principal: app authenticate with Azure AD, get secret
  • Azure CLI: az keyvault secret show --name dbpassword --vault-name prod
  • Azure Portal: view/manage via UI
Say this in interview "Key Vault: store secrets centrally, encrypted. Never hardcode passwords. Use managed identities for apps. Audit access. Rotate secrets regularly. Cost: ~$0.6/month + operations."
Azure Security

Explain managed identities in Azure — system-assigned vs user-assigned, MI and SP

Eliminate secrets: let Azure manage credentials.

Q&AIdentity types›
System-Assigned Managed Identity
  • Created automatically with resource (VM, App Service)
  • Tied to lifecycle: deleted with resource
  • Automatically authenticated
  • Simple, one identity per resource
User-Assigned Managed Identity
  • Created separately, reusable across resources
  • Multiple resources can use same identity
  • Independent lifecycle
  • More flexible
Service Principal
  • Not managed: you manage credentials (client ID + secret)
  • For: external apps, CI/CD, long-lived processes
  • High privilege, requires secret rotation
  • Use when managed identity not available
Comparison
  • System-MI: auto-created, simple, tied to resource
  • User-MI: shareable, flexible, independent
  • SP: manual creds, high privilege, CI/CD use
Say this in interview "Use managed identities (no secrets). System-assigned for single resource, user-assigned for sharing. Service principals (with secrets) only when MI unavailable. Never hardcode credentials."
Azure Security

Explain how to secure secrets in CI/CD pipelines with Azure

Protect secrets in build and deployment pipelines.

Q&APipeline security›
Anti-patterns (Don't Do)
  • Don't commit secrets to Git
  • Don't pass secrets as plaintext in pipeline logs
  • Don't embed secrets in container images
Best Practices
  • Azure Pipelines: use Secret Variables (marked as confidential, not logged)
  • GitHub Actions: use GitHub Secrets, not plaintext
  • Key Vault: store secrets, pipeline retrieves at runtime
  • Managed Identity: pipeline authenticates as service, no client ID needed
Azure Pipelines Example
  • Store DB password in Azure Pipelines Secret Variables
  • Reference: $(DatabasePassword) in pipeline
  • Logged as *** (masked in output)
  • Passed to app at runtime
Key Vault Integration
  • Pipeline retrieves secret from Key Vault
  • Uses managed identity (no credentials)
  • Secret never appears in logs or code
  • Centralized secret management
Say this in interview "Never commit secrets to Git. Use pipeline secret variables (masked in logs). Better: retrieve from Key Vault using managed identity. Rotate secrets regularly. Audit access."
Azure Security

What is an Azure policy? How do you enforce compliance? Audit

Governance: enforce standards and audit compliance.

Q&APolicy enforcement›
What is Azure Policy
  • Enforce organizational standards across Azure
  • Example: "all resources must have encryption enabled"
  • Audit: check compliance without blocking
  • Deny: block non-compliant resource creation
Policy Examples
  • Require tags on all resources
  • Deny unencrypted storage accounts
  • Require HTTPS for web apps
  • Enforce SQL Database backups
  • Require specific SKUs (prevent expensive instances)
Audit vs Deny
  • Audit: logs non-compliant resources but allows creation
  • Deny: blocks non-compliant resource creation
  • Start with Audit, move to Deny after review
Assignment
  • Assign policy to subscription or resource group
  • Applies to all new resources automatically
  • Can exclude resources/groups if needed
  • Compliance dashboard: see % compliant
Say this in interview "Azure Policy enforces standards. Audit mode: log violations. Deny mode: block violations. Examples: require tags, enforce encryption. Apply at subscription level for organization-wide compliance."
Azure Security

Explain Azure resource tagging — best practices for large organizations

Metadata for organization, billing, and automation.

Q&ATagging strategy›
Why Tags Matter
  • Organization: find resources by project, owner, environment
  • Billing: chargeback costs by project, department
  • Automation: auto-shutdown dev resources, trigger actions
  • Compliance: track HIPAA/GDPR resources
Common Tags
  • Environment: dev, staging, prod
  • Project: projectname
  • Owner: alice@company.com
  • CostCenter: 12345
  • Application: webapp, api, database
  • AutoShutdown: yes/no (for dev VMs)
Best Practices
  • Enforce tagging via Azure Policy
  • Require minimum tags: Environment, Owner, Project
  • Standardize tag values (dev not DEV or development)
  • Review tags quarterly, remove unused
  • Use Azure Cost Management to group by tags
Say this in interview "Tags provide metadata: organization, billing, automation. Enforce with Azure Policy. Require: Environment, Owner, Project. Review regularly. Enables cost allocation and resource discovery."
Azure 06

Monitoring & Logging

Azure Monitoring

What is Azure Monitor? Explain metrics vs logs

Observability: see what's happening in your infrastructure.

Q&AObservability platform›
What is Azure Monitor
  • Central platform for monitoring all Azure resources
  • Collects: metrics, logs, traces, events
  • Provides: dashboards, alerts, analysis
  • Enables: proactive problem detection
Metrics
  • Numerical data points over time
  • Example: CPU % = 75 at 10:00 AM, 80 at 10:05 AM
  • Real-time, fast retrieval
  • Good for: dashboards, alerts, trending
  • Retention: 93 days
Logs
  • Structured text events with context
  • Example: "Database connection timeout at 10:05:23, retry in 5s"
  • Rich data: timestamps, properties, exceptions
  • Good for: debugging, audit, compliance
  • Retention: 30-2555 days (configurable)
Comparison
  • Metrics: CPU, memory, response time (numbers)
  • Logs: errors, warnings, events (text)
  • Metrics: alert on thresholds. Logs: investigate failures
Say this in interview "Metrics: numerical data (CPU, latency). Logs: events/text (errors, requests). Metrics alert on thresholds, logs debug issues. Both feed Azure Monitor dashboards and alerts."
Azure Monitoring

Explain Application Insights — what does it monitor?

APM for applications: requests, performance, dependencies.

Q&AApplication performance›
What is Application Insights
  • Application Performance Monitoring (APM)
  • Add SDK to app: collects telemetry automatically
  • Works with: web apps, APIs, mobile apps, services
  • Cloud-based analysis
What It Monitors
  • Requests: HTTP request rate, response time, success rate
  • Performance: page load time, server response time
  • Failures: exceptions, failed requests, stack traces
  • Dependencies: database calls, external APIs, cache hits
  • Custom events: app-defined metrics (user signup, purchase)
Example Dashboard
  • Request rate: 500/sec
  • Avg response time: 150ms (OK) or 2000ms (slow!)
  • Failed requests: 2% (above 1% threshold, investigate)
  • Slowest dependencies: database queries (avg 800ms)
Say this in interview "Application Insights: APM for apps. Monitors requests, performance, failures, dependencies. Automatic via SDK. Great for: debugging slow requests, finding exceptions, tracking user behavior."
Azure Monitoring

What is Log Analytics? How do you query logs with KQL (Kusto Query Language)?

Query engine for analyzing logs from multiple sources.

Q&ALog analysis›
What is Log Analytics
  • Query engine for logs: Application Insights, Azure Monitor, VMs, etc.
  • Centralized log storage and analysis
  • SQL-like query language (KQL)
  • Fast full-text search across billions of log entries
KQL Query Examples
  • Find all errors: AppTraces | where SeverityLevel == "Error"
  • Count requests per URL: AppRequests | summarize count() by URL
  • Latency trend: AppRequests | summarize avg(DurationMs) by bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
  • Join: AppRequests | join kind=inner (AppDependencies) on OperationId
Use Cases
  • Debugging: "show all requests for user alice@company.com in last 2 hours"
  • Analytics: "what's causing slow requests?"
  • Compliance: "audit who accessed database X"
  • Capacity planning: "request volume trending up?"
Say this in interview "Log Analytics queries logs with KQL (SQL-like). Example: AppRequests | where Duration > 5000 ms. Great for debugging, trending, auditing. Faster than manual log searching."
Azure Monitoring

Explain Azure Alerts — action groups and alert rules

Automated notifications when things go wrong.

Q&AAlerting›
Alert Rule
  • Condition: "if CPU > 80% for 5 minutes"
  • Severity: Critical, Error, Warning, Informational
  • Fires when condition met
  • Triggers action group
Action Groups
  • Email: send to ops-team@company.com
  • SMS: text alert to on-call engineer
  • Webhook: call custom API (trigger script)
  • Logic App: complex automation
  • PagerDuty/Slack: integrate external tools
Example Alert
  • Condition: App Service CPU > 80% for 5 min
  • Severity: Critical
  • Actions: Email ops, SMS on-call, create ticket via webhook
  • Suppression: don't re-alert for 1 hour
Say this in interview "Alert rule: condition (CPU > 80%). Action group: notify (email, SMS, webhook, Slack). Example: CPU high → email ops → page on-call → create incident. Avoid alert fatigue: suppress duplicates."
Azure Monitoring

What is Azure Service Health vs Resource Health?

Two health monitoring features with different scopes.

Q&AHealth tracking›
Service Health
  • Azure region/service status: "East US SQL Database down"
  • Planned maintenance: "SQL Database maintenance tonight 2-4am"
  • Service issues: "East US experiencing connectivity delays"
  • Historic data: past incidents
Resource Health
  • Specific resource status: "your VM web-prod-01 is healthy"
  • Why unhealthy: "underlying platform issue", "user-initiated action"
  • Recommended action: "restart VM", "wait for recovery"
  • Access from resource dashboard
When to Use
  • Service Health: "why is SQL Database slow region-wide?"
  • Resource Health: "why is MY VM down?"
Say this in interview "Service Health: region/service status. Resource Health: individual resource health. Use Service Health for platform issues, Resource Health to debug your resources."
Azure Monitoring

How do you set up end-to-end monitoring for multi-tier applications?

Comprehensive monitoring across web, business, and data tiers.

Q&AFull stack monitoring›
Architecture
  • Web tier: App Service + Application Insights SDK
  • Business tier: microservices + App Insights
  • Database tier: SQL Database metrics + Query Performance Insights
  • Infrastructure: VM metrics, storage metrics
What to Monitor
  • User experience: page load time, error rate
  • Application: request latency, failed requests, exceptions
  • Dependencies: database response time, external API latency
  • Infrastructure: CPU, memory, disk, network
Setup
  • 1. Add Application Insights SDK to app
  • 2. Enable metrics on App Service, SQL Database, VMs
  • 3. Create dashboards: overview, per-tier, per-service
  • 4. Set alerts: CPU, latency, errors
  • 5. Test: access app, verify metrics/logs flow
Say this in interview "Monitor all tiers: user experience, app metrics, dependencies, infrastructure. Use App Insights for app, Azure Monitor for resources. Create dashboards. Set alerts on latency/errors."
Azure Monitoring

Explain distributed tracing in Application Insights

Follow a single request through multiple services.

Q&ARequest tracing›
What is Distributed Tracing
  • Follow one request: Web → Business → Database
  • See where time spent: web 50ms, business 300ms, DB 600ms
  • Identify bottleneck: database is slow!
  • Correlation ID: tracks request across services
Example Flow
  • User: clicks "buy" on website
  • Web tier: processes order (50ms)
  • Calls business tier: check inventory (200ms)
  • Business tier calls database: reserve stock (700ms)
  • Returns to web, renders confirmation
  • Total: 950ms (DB is bottleneck)
Using Traces
  • View in Application Insights: Transaction search
  • Filter by duration: show slow requests (> 1000ms)
  • Click request → see all steps, timing, exceptions
  • Optimize: fix database query, add caching
Say this in interview "Distributed tracing tracks request across services. Correlation ID links logs. Shows which service is slow. App Insights Transaction search: find slow requests, drill into steps."
Azure Monitoring

APM and Resource Graph

Advanced monitoring and resource inventory.

Q&AAdvanced tools›
APM (Application Performance Monitoring)
  • Application Insights = Azure's APM
  • Monitors: requests, performance, dependencies, exceptions
  • Proactive alerting on anomalies
  • Cost: starts free, pay per GB for data > 1GB/day
Resource Graph
  • Query all resources across subscriptions
  • Example: find all VMs in eastus with tag env=prod
  • KQL query: Resources | where type == "microsoft.compute/virtualmachines" and location == "eastus"
  • Use for: compliance, inventory, cost analysis
Use Cases
  • APM: application troubleshooting, performance optimization
  • Resource Graph: multi-subscription reporting, compliance audits, cost analysis
Say this in interview "Application Insights (APM): app performance, request tracing. Resource Graph: query all resources across subscriptions with KQL. Use together: App Insights for app health, Resource Graph for infrastructure inventory."
