What is Azure API Management? When would you use it?

Governance and monetization for APIs.

Q&AAPI gateway›
What is API Management
  • Gateway in front of APIs
  • Provides: rate limiting, authentication, versioning, analytics
  • Portal: developers discover and test APIs
  • Monetization: paid API tiers
Key Features
  • Rate limiting: prevent abuse, enforce quotas
  • Authentication: API keys, OAuth2, mutual TLS

  • Developer portal: self-service API discovery

Cost

  • Standard: ~$400/month

AKS 01

Fundamentals

AKS Fundamentals

What is Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS)? How does it differ from self-managed Kubernetes?

Azure's managed Kubernetes service eliminates operational burden of cluster management.

Q&AManaged vs self-managed›
What is AKS
  • Managed Kubernetes service: Azure operates control plane
  • Pay only for: worker nodes, not control plane
  • Integrated: with Azure services (Networking, Storage, Monitoring)
  • Auto-updates: Kubernetes patches applied automatically
Self-Managed Kubernetes
  • You manage everything: control plane + nodes
  • kubeadm, kops, or manual setup
  • Full control, full responsibility
  • Requires: networking, storage, security expertise
AKS Advantages
  • Azure manages control plane (etcd, API server, scheduler)
  • High availability: multi-master setup
  • Security patches automated
  • Integration with Azure AD, Container Registry, Key Vault
  • Cost: pay only for nodes
Say this in interview "AKS is managed Kubernetes: Azure runs control plane, you run workloads. No need to manage etcd, API server. Auto-updates, HA built-in. Pay only for compute nodes."
AKS Fundamentals

Explain the AKS cluster architecture — what are the control plane and worker nodes?

Two layers: managed control plane and customer-managed worker nodes.

Q&ACluster components›
Control Plane (Azure-Managed)
  • API Server: REST API for Kubernetes
  • etcd: cluster state storage
  • Scheduler: assigns pods to nodes
  • Controller Manager: runs controllers
  • You don't pay for this, you don't manage it
Worker Nodes (You Manage)
  • kubelet: runs pods
  • Container runtime: Docker or containerd
  • kube-proxy: networking
  • System pods: CNI, DNS, monitoring
  • You pay for VMs, you manage node patches
Architecture Diagram
  • Azure (Azure-managed): API Server, etcd, Scheduler, Controller Manager
  • Your Subscription: Node Pool 1 (VMs), Node Pool 2 (VMs)
  • Communication: secure tunnel to API server
Say this in interview "AKS: Azure manages control plane (API, scheduler, etcd). You manage worker nodes (VMs). Communication via secure channel. You pay only for nodes. Control plane HA built-in."
AKS Fundamentals

What is Azure Container Registry (ACR)? How do you integrate it with AKS?

Private container registry integrated with AKS for secure image pulls.

Q&ARegistry integration›
What is ACR
  • Private Docker registry in Azure
  • Store container images securely
  • Integrated: with AKS, CI/CD, DevOps
  • Built-in: vulnerability scanning, retention policies
Integration Methods
  • Managed Identity: AKS pulls images automatically (recommended)
  • Service Principal: auth via credentials
  • Import: az acr import to copy images between registries
Setup Steps
  • 1. Create ACR: az acr create --name myacr --resource-group rg
  • 2. Attach to AKS: az aks update --attach-acr myacr
  • 3. Deploy pods with images from ACR
  • 4. AKS uses managed identity to authenticate
Say this in interview "ACR is private container registry. Integrate with AKS via managed identity (no secrets). AKS automatically pulls images from ACR. Built-in scanning for vulnerabilities."
AKS Fundamentals

Explain the difference between managed node pools and system node pools in AKS

Two types of node pools for different purposes.

Q&ANode pool types›
System Node Pool
  • Runs system pods: kube-proxy, CoreDNS, monitoring
  • Every cluster has at least 1 system pool
  • Must have: 1 available node at all times
  • Usually: small VMs (Standard_DS2_v2)
User Node Pools
  • Runs your application workloads
  • Multiple pools: different sizes, types, configs
  • Example: Standard pool (general), GPU pool (ML), Memory-optimized (databases)
  • Can scale independently
Configuration
  • Taints: prevent user pods on system nodes
  • Labels: schedule pods to specific pools
  • Example: node pool "gpu" with taints/labels for GPU workloads
Say this in interview "System pools run cluster infrastructure (DNS, monitoring). User pools run apps. Create multiple pools for different workloads (general, GPU, memory-optimized). Use taints to isolate system pods."
AKS Fundamentals

What is Kubernetes RBAC? How do you implement it in AKS?

Role-Based Access Control for cluster API access.

Q&ARBAC implementation›
RBAC Components
  • Role/ClusterRole: permissions (e.g., "can create pods")
  • RoleBinding/ClusterRoleBinding: assign role to user/group
  • ServiceAccount: identity for pods
Example: Namespace Admin
  • Create Role: permissions to create/update/delete in namespace
  • Create RoleBinding: bind user to Role
  • User can now manage resources in that namespace
Built-in Roles
  • cluster-admin: full access
  • admin: namespace admin
  • edit: create/modify resources
  • view: read-only
Say this in interview "RBAC: Roles define permissions, RoleBindings assign to users. ServiceAccounts for pods. Use least privilege: namespace-scoped roles. Combine with Azure AD for enterprise SSO."
AKS Fundamentals

How do you enable Azure AD integration with AKS for authentication?

SSO for cluster access via Azure Active Directory.

Q&AAD authentication›
Benefits
  • Single sign-on: users authenticate via Azure AD
  • No separate credentials for Kubernetes
  • MFA support: if Azure AD has MFA
  • Audit logs: who accessed cluster
Setup
  • 1. Create AKS cluster: with --enable-aad
  • 2. Register app in Azure AD (Kubernetes server)
  • 3. Register app for kubectl (client)
  • 4. Get kubeconfig: az aks get-credentials
  • 5. kubectl commands prompt Azure AD login
Workflow
  • User runs: kubectl get pods
  • Azure AD login prompt appears (browser)
  • User authenticates with AAD credentials
  • kubectl proceeds with authorization
Say this in interview "Enable Azure AD on AKS cluster. Users authenticate via AAD (not kubeconfig passwords). Combines with RBAC for authorization. Enterprise standard: SSO + MFA."
AKS 02

Networking

AKS Networking

What are the networking models in AKS — kubenet vs Azure CNI?

Two container networking interfaces with different performance/complexity tradeoffs.

Q&ACNI comparison›
Kubenet (Simple)
  • Each pod gets IP from pod CIDR (e.g., 10.240.0.0/16)
  • Routing via user-defined routes
  • Lower resource usage
  • Limitation: pod count limited by node count
  • Good for: development, testing
Azure CNI (Advanced)
  • Pod IPs from Azure subnet (same as nodes)
  • Better performance: direct Azure networking
  • Can use Windows containers
  • More pod density: multiple IPs per node
  • Good for: production, scale
Comparison
  • kubenet: simple, cost-effective, limited scale
  • Azure CNI: complex, better performance, higher scale
  • Choose at cluster creation (can't change later)
Say this in interview "Kubenet: simple, limited scale. Azure CNI: better performance, more pods. Choose CNI for production/scale, kubenet for dev. Decision at cluster creation time."
AKS Networking

Explain ingress in AKS — how do you route external traffic to pods?

HTTP/HTTPS routing from outside cluster to services.

Q&ATraffic routing›
What is Ingress
  • Kubernetes resource: defines routing rules
  • Example: /api → backend-service, /web → frontend-service
  • HTTP/HTTPS only (Layer 7)
  • Requires Ingress Controller to implement
Ingress Controller (AKS Options)
  • nginx-ingress: popular open source
  • Azure Application Gateway: native Azure integration
  • Traefik, HAProxy: alternatives
Example Flow
  • 1. Client: GET example.com/api/users
  • 2. DNS: resolves to ingress IP
  • 3. Ingress rule: /api → backend-service:8080
  • 4. Service: routes to pod
  • 5. Pod: processes request
Say this in interview "Ingress: routing HTTP/HTTPS to services. Requires controller (nginx or Application Gateway). Rules: path/host based routing. Most common way to expose apps."
AKS Networking

What is Azure Application Gateway Ingress Controller (AGIC)?

Native Azure integration for ingress using Application Gateway.

Q&AAzure ingress›
What is AGIC
  • Controller that uses Application Gateway as ingress
  • Native Azure service: WAF, URL routing, SSL
  • Better integration: Azure monitoring, policies
  • Cost: Application Gateway pricing
Advantages over nginx-ingress
  • WAF (Web Application Firewall) built-in
  • Azure native: managed, monitored by Azure
  • URL-based routing, hostname-based routing
  • SSL/TLS termination
Setup
  • 1. Create Application Gateway in VNet
  • 2. Install AGIC via Helm or add-on
  • 3. Create Ingress resources (standard Kubernetes)
  • 4. AGIC configures Application Gateway automatically
Say this in interview "AGIC: native Azure ingress via Application Gateway. Built-in WAF. Better than nginx-ingress for Azure deployments. Use for: security, enterprise integrations."
AKS Networking

How do you implement network policies in AKS?

Firewall rules for pod-to-pod communication.

Q&APod firewalls›
What are Network Policies
  • Kubernetes resource: ingress/egress rules for pods
  • Default: all pods can talk to all pods (open network)
  • Policy: restrict traffic between pods
  • Example: frontend only talks to backend, not database
Example Policy
  • Deny all ingress (default)
  • Allow ingress from frontend pods only
  • Pod can now only accept traffic from specific source
Implementation in AKS
  • Azure CNI required (kubenet doesn't support)
  • Calico for network policy enforcement
  • Integrates with NSGs for node-level rules
Say this in interview "Network policies: pod-level firewall rules. Default deny, allow specific traffic. Requires Azure CNI + Calico. Example: database pod only accepts from app pod, not internet."
AKS Networking

Explain service types in AKS — ClusterIP, NodePort, LoadBalancer, ExternalName

Different ways to expose pods internally and externally.

Q&AService exposure›
ClusterIP (Internal)
  • Pod-to-pod communication only
  • Internal IP: 10.0.0.100
  • Not accessible from outside
  • Good for: microservices communication
NodePort (Node-level)
  • Expose on every node, high port (30000-32767)
  • Access: node-ip:30000
  • Good for: development, testing
  • Problem: high ports, multiple IPs
LoadBalancer (External)
  • Azure Load Balancer + public IP
  • Access: single IP, port 80/443
  • Good for: exposing single app
  • Cost: Azure LB billing ($0.025/hour)
ExternalName
  • DNS CNAME to external service
  • Example: point to on-prem database
  • No load balancing, just DNS alias
Say this in interview "ClusterIP: internal pods. NodePort: node-level (dev). LoadBalancer: external (prod, costs). ExternalName: DNS alias. Use Ingress for multiple services on one IP."
AKS Networking

How do you implement private AKS clusters?

Cluster with no public IP, accessible only via private network.

Q&APrivate clusters›
What is Private Cluster
  • No public API endpoint
  • Access via: private IP only (VNet peering, VPN, bastion)
  • Enhanced security: no internet exposure
  • Requires: jumpbox or private link to manage
Setup
  • Create cluster: --enable-private-cluster
  • Private DNS zone: for cluster FQDN resolution
  • Access: via VNet peering, VPN, or bastion VM
Management
  • Azure portal: works (uses private endpoint)
  • kubectl: must access via VPN/bastion
  • CI/CD: use private agent or VPN
Say this in interview "Private cluster: no public API endpoint. Access via VNet/VPN. Enhanced security for sensitive workloads. Requires bastion/VPN for management. Automatic Private DNS zone."
AKS Networking

What is Azure Private Link in AKS context?

Private connectivity to Azure services without internet exposure.

Q&APrivate connectivity›
Use Cases
  • Pods access Azure Storage via private endpoint (not public)
  • Pods access SQL Database via private endpoint
  • Pods access Key Vault securely
  • No internet routing, stays in Microsoft backbone
Example: Private Storage
  • Create private endpoint for Storage Account in VNet
  • Pod accesses storage via private IP (10.0.1.x)
  • Traffic never leaves Microsoft network
  • Storage firewall blocks public access
Benefits
  • Security: no public exposure
  • Performance: lower latency, higher throughput
  • Compliance: data stays within organizational boundary
Say this in interview "Private Link: pods access Azure services via private endpoint, not internet. Example: secure database access. Combine with private cluster for complete isolation."
AKS 03

Storage

AKS Storage

Explain persistent volumes (PV) and persistent volume claims (PVC) in AKS

Decoupling storage from pods for data persistence.

Q&APersistent storage›
Persistent Volume (PV)
  • Cluster-level resource: actual storage (Azure Disk, File Share)
  • Created by: admin or dynamic provisioning
  • Lifecycle: independent of pods
Persistent Volume Claim (PVC)
  • Pod-level request for storage
  • Example: "I need 10GB of fast storage"
  • Bound to PV automatically (or via StorageClass)
Workflow
  • 1. Admin creates PV (or StorageClass for dynamic)
  • 2. Pod creates PVC (size, access mode)
  • 3. Kubernetes binds PVC to PV
  • 4. Pod mounts volume
  • 5. Data persists after pod deletion
Say this in interview "PV: storage resource. PVC: pod's storage request. Decouples pods from storage. Data persists after pod deletion. Use for databases, caches, logs."
AKS Storage

What storage classes are available in AKS?

Different storage performance tiers.

Q&AStorage tiers›
Built-in Storage Classes
  • default: Azure Managed Disk (Standard SSD)
  • managed-premium: Premium SSD (fast, expensive)
  • azurefile: Azure Files (SMB, shareable)
  • azurefile-premium: Premium Files
When to Use
  • Managed Disk: single pod, fast access (databases)
  • Azure Files: multiple pods need same storage
  • Premium: high-performance workloads
Dynamic Provisioning
  • PVC references StorageClass
  • Kubernetes automatically provisions PV
  • No manual PV creation needed
Say this in interview "StorageClasses define provisioning. Default: Standard SSD. Use managed-premium for fast access. Use azurefile for shared storage. Dynamic provisioning automates PV creation."
AKS Storage

How do you use Azure Disk for stateful applications in AKS?

Block storage for high-performance workloads.

Q&ABlock storage›
Azure Disk
  • Block storage: managed disks attached to VM
  • Single pod access (not shareable)
  • High performance: low latency
  • Good for: databases (SQL, PostgreSQL)
Example: PostgreSQL
  • 1. Create PVC with managed-premium StorageClass
  • 2. Mount to PostgreSQL pod: /var/lib/postgresql
  • 3. Data persists on Azure Disk
  • 4. Snapshots available for backup
Limitations
  • Single-pod access (node affinity required)
  • Can't move between nodes easily
  • Requires: Helm charts for stateful apps (StatefulSets)
Say this in interview "Azure Disk: high-performance block storage. Single pod only. Use for stateful apps: databases, caches. Use managed-premium for fast access. Use StatefulSets for coordination."
AKS Storage

How do you use Azure Files (SMB) in AKS?

Shared file storage accessible from multiple pods.

Q&AShared storage›
Azure Files
  • SMB 3.0 protocol (standard file share)
  • Multiple pods can mount same share
  • Good for: shared data, legacy apps
  • Slower than disk: network latency
Setup
  • 1. Create storage account in Azure
  • 2. Create file share in storage account
  • 3. Pod uses azurefile StorageClass
  • 4. Mount path shared across pods
Use Cases
  • Shared configuration files
  • Shared logs directory
  • Shared data between microservices
  • Legacy apps expecting NAS
Say this in interview "Azure Files: SMB share, multiple pods. Good for shared data. Slower than Disk. Use for config, logs, legacy apps. Create storage account + file share, reference via StorageClass."
AKS Storage

What is Azure Blob Storage integration with AKS?

Object storage accessible from AKS pods.

Q&AObject storage›
Blob Storage from AKS
  • Pods access blobs via: Azure SDK, Helm secret (connection string)
  • Not mounted as volume (object storage, not filesystem)
  • Good for: backups, analytics, large files
Integration Methods
  • Managed Identity: pod authenticates without secrets
  • Secret: connection string in Secret object
  • Private endpoint: secure VNet-only access
Example: Backup
  • 1. Pod has managed identity with Storage Contributor
  • 2. Pod runs backup job, uploads to Blob Storage
  • 3. No credentials in code or pod
Say this in interview "Blob Storage from AKS: pods access via Azure SDK. Use managed identity for security. Good for backups, archives. Not mounted as volume (object storage)."
AKS 04

Scaling

AKS Scaling

Explain the Kubernetes Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) — how does it work?

Automatically scale number of pods based on demand.

Q&APod scaling›
How HPA Works
  • Monitor metrics: CPU, memory, custom metrics
  • Compare to target: if CPU > 70%, scale up
  • Scale action: create new pods
  • Cool-down: wait before scaling again (prevent flapping)
Configuration
  • Min replicas: 1
  • Max replicas: 10
  • Target CPU: 70%
  • Scale up: fast (when demand high)
  • Scale down: slow (when demand low)
Example
  • Deployment: 2 pods running
  • Traffic spike: CPU goes to 90%
  • HPA: create pods until CPU at 70%
  • Traffic drops: HPA removes pods (cool-down 5 min)
Say this in interview "HPA: monitor CPU/memory, scale pods. Min/max replicas. Scale up quickly, down slowly. Requires: metrics available (metrics-server). Use with resource requests for accurate scaling."
AKS Scaling

Explain the Kubernetes Vertical Pod Autoscaler (VPA) in AKS

Right-size pod resource requests and limits.

Q&AResource optimization›
What is VPA
  • Adjusts pod resource requests/limits
  • Example: change from 512MB to 256MB if unused
  • Saves costs: smaller pods = smaller nodes
  • Improves scheduling: better resource matching
How It Works
  • 1. Monitor pod resource usage
  • 2. Recommend resource adjustments
  • 3. Optionally auto-update requests/limits
  • 4. May restart pods to apply changes
HPA vs VPA
  • HPA: scale NUMBER of pods
  • VPA: scale SIZE of pods
  • Can use both: HPA + VPA = optimal scaling
Say this in interview "VPA: right-size resources (CPU, memory). Lower requests = lower cost. Can restart pods to apply. Use with HPA: VPA adjusts size, HPA adjusts count."
AKS Scaling

What is the Cluster Autoscaler in AKS? How do you configure it?

Automatically scale number of nodes based on pod demand.

Q&ANode scaling›
How Cluster Autoscaler Works
  • Monitor pods: are any pending (waiting for resources)?
  • If pending: add node to the cluster
  • If nodes underutilized: remove node
  • Integrates with node pools
Configuration
  • Min nodes: 1 (always have at least this many)
  • Max nodes: 10 (don't exceed this)
  • Scale up: fast (when pods pending)
  • Scale down: slow (when nodes empty)
Example Flow
  • 1. New deployment: 10 pods requested
  • 2. Current nodes can fit: 5 pods
  • 3. 5 pods are Pending
  • 4. Cluster Autoscaler: add 2 nodes
  • 5. Pods scheduled
Say this in interview "Cluster Autoscaler: add nodes when pods pending, remove when empty. Coordinate with HPA: HPA scales pods, Autoscaler scales nodes. Min/max node limits prevent runaway costs."
AKS Scaling

How do you implement multi-node pools for different workload types in AKS?

Different node pools for different application requirements.

Q&APool specialization›
Use Cases
  • General pool: standard workloads (web apps)
  • GPU pool: ML/AI workloads
  • Memory pool: in-memory caches, databases
  • Spot pool: cost-effective, interruptible jobs
Configuration
  • Create pool: az aks nodepool add --name gpu-pool --node-count 1
  • VM size: different per pool
  • Labels: pool=gpu (for pod scheduling)
  • Taints: prevent general pods on GPU nodes
Pod Scheduling
  • Regular pod: scheduled to general pool
  • ML pod: nodeSelector/toleration → GPU pool
  • Example: training job runs on GPU, inference on CPU
Say this in interview "Create node pools for different workloads: general, GPU, memory, spot. Use labels/taints to control scheduling. Scale each pool independently. Cost optimization: right-size hardware for workload."
AKS Scaling

Explain node pool scaling — manual vs automatic

How to control number of nodes in a pool.

Q&ANode management›
Manual Scaling
  • az aks scale --resource-group rg --name cluster --node-count 5
  • You specify exact number of nodes
  • Good for: testing, predictable workloads
Automatic Scaling
  • Enable cluster autoscaler on node pool
  • Min: 1, Max: 10
  • Automatically adjusts based on pod demand
  • Good for: variable workloads
Best Practice
  • Use autoscaler for production (cost savings)
  • Set reasonable min/max to prevent surprises
  • Monitor: nodes scaling up/down via metrics
Say this in interview "Manual: set exact node count. Automatic: autoscaler adjusts within min/max. Use autoscaler for production. Set reasonable limits to prevent costs spiraling."
AKS Scaling

How do you scale a cluster from 1 node to 100 nodes safely?

Best practices for large-scale growth.

Q&ASafe scaling›
Potential Issues
  • API server overload
  • DNS (CoreDNS) can't handle load
  • Network exhaustion (IP addresses)
  • Storage I/O bottlenecks
Mitigation
  • 1. Scale gradually: 1→5→25→100 (test at each step)
  • 2. Monitor: CPU, memory, network, API latency
  • 3. Scale CoreDNS: more replicas for DNS load
  • 4. Use Azure CNI: better networking at scale
  • 5. Test: load testing before production scale
Kubernetes Limits (Hard Limits)
  • Max 5000 nodes per cluster
  • Max 110 pods per node (default)
  • Total 150,000 pods per cluster
Say this in interview "Scale gradually: 1→5→25→100. Monitor API, DNS, network. Scale CoreDNS for DNS load. Use Azure CNI for better networking. Kubernetes limits: 5k nodes, 110 pods/node. Test beforehand."
AKS 05

Security

AKS Security

How do you secure container images in AKS?

Protect images from vulnerabilities and unauthorized access.

Q&AImage protection›
Best Practices
  • Use ACR (private registry), not Docker Hub
  • Scan images for vulnerabilities (ACR built-in)
  • Use managed identity for ACR pulls (no secrets)
  • Sign images: content trust verification
  • Base image hardening: minimal images (alpine, scratch)
ACR Scanning
  • Automatic: scan on push
  • Find CVEs: known vulnerabilities
  • Block: prevent deployment of vulnerable images
Image Signing
  • Docker Content Trust (DCT): verify image origin
  • Notation: sign images with keys
  • Prevent: supply chain attacks
Say this in interview "Use ACR (private). Scan for vulnerabilities. Use managed identity (no secrets). Sign images for integrity. Minimal base images (alpine). Prevent deployment of vulnerable images."
AKS Security

Explain pod security policies (PSP) in AKS

Enforce pod security standards cluster-wide.

Q&APod constraints›
What are PSPs
  • Define minimum security requirements for pods
  • Example: "pods must run as non-root"
  • Enforced at admission time (before pod creation)
  • Note: PSP deprecated in K8s 1.25+, use Pod Security Standards
Example Policy
  • Run as non-root user (not UID 0)
  • Read-only root filesystem
  • No privileged containers
  • No host network access
Pod Security Standards (Modern)
  • Restricted: most secure
  • Baseline: reasonable security
  • Unrestricted: no constraints (development only)
  • Applied via labels
Say this in interview "PSP enforces pod security (non-root, no privileged). PSP deprecated, use Pod Security Standards instead. Baseline for prod, Restricted for sensitive. Enforce at namespace level."
AKS Security

How do you implement RBAC for AKS cluster access?

Control who can do what in the cluster.

Q&AAccess control›
Two-Level Control
  • Azure RBAC: who can manage cluster (Azure portal, API)
  • Kubernetes RBAC: who can access API resources (kubectl)
Example: Developer Access
  • Azure RBAC: Azure Kubernetes Service Cluster User
  • Kubernetes RBAC: Namespace admin (edit pods, deployments)
  • Result: can develop in assigned namespace, not cluster-wide
Least Privilege Setup
  • Default: deny all access
  • Create role: specific permissions needed
  • Bind to user/group: minimal necessary
  • Regular audits: remove unused access
Say this in interview "Two layers: Azure RBAC (cluster management) + Kubernetes RBAC (resource access). Namespace-scoped roles reduce blast radius. Least privilege: deny by default."
AKS Security

What is Azure AD pod-managed identity in AKS?

Pods authenticate to Azure services without secrets.

Q&APod authentication›
What is Pod-Managed Identity
  • Pod gets Azure identity (managed identity)
  • Pod can authenticate to Azure services (SQL, Storage, Key Vault)
  • No credentials in pod or code
  • Automatic token refresh
Setup
  • 1. Create user-managed identity
  • 2. Assign RBAC role (e.g., Storage Reader)
  • 3. Add pod label: aadpodidbinding=myidentity
  • 4. Pod automatically authenticates
Example: Access Storage
  • Pod needs to read blobs
  • Pod has managed identity with Storage Blob Reader
  • Pod: client = BlobClient() (no credentials needed)
  • Works: automatic Azure authentication
Say this in interview "Pod-managed identity: pods authenticate to Azure without secrets. Add label to pod, grant RBAC role. Pod automatically gets token. Best practice for security."
AKS Security

How do you implement secrets management in AKS?

Store and manage sensitive data securely.

Q&ASecret handling›
Kubernetes Secrets
  • Base64 encoded (NOT encrypted by default)
  • Stored in etcd (managed by Azure)
  • Can enable encryption at rest
Better Option: Key Vault
  • Store secrets in Key Vault (encrypted)
  • Pod retrieves at runtime via managed identity
  • No secrets in Kubernetes or code
  • Centralized secret management
Setup
  • 1. Create secret in Key Vault
  • 2. Pod has managed identity with Key Vault Reader
  • 3. Pod retrieves secret at startup
  • 4. No Kubernetes Secret needed
Say this in interview "Don't rely on K8s Secrets (base64 only). Use Azure Key Vault: encrypted, centralized, auditable. Pod retrieves via managed identity. Best practice: no secrets in pod."
AKS Security

Explain network policies for pod-to-pod communication in AKS

Firewall rules between pods (microsegmentation).

Q&APod firewalls›
Default: Open Network
  • All pods can communicate with all pods
  • No built-in segmentation
  • Vulnerability: lateral movement if one pod compromised
Network Policies
  • Define: frontend only talks to backend
  • Backend only talks to database
  • Database rejects all other sources
Implementation
  • Requires: Azure CNI + Calico
  • Define policies: ingress/egress rules
  • Use labels: app=backend, tier=data
Say this in interview "Network policies create pod-level firewall. Default deny, allow specific flows. Requires Azure CNI + Calico. Example: frontend→backend→database (one-way). Reduces blast radius."
AKS Security

How do you enable Azure Defender for AKS?

Threat detection and vulnerability management.

Q&AThreat detection›
What is Azure Defender
  • Threat detection for Kubernetes clusters
  • Detect: suspicious activities, attacks, vulnerabilities
  • Integration: with Security Center
Features
  • Audit log analysis: detect attack patterns
  • Vulnerability scanning: container images
  • Runtime threat detection: suspicious pod behavior
  • Alerts: actionable recommendations
Setup
  • Enable in Security Center
  • Select: Defender for Kubernetes
  • Cost: ~$1-2/node/month
Say this in interview "Azure Defender: threat detection + vulnerability scanning for AKS. Analyzes audit logs for attacks. Scans images for CVEs. Alerts on suspicious behavior. Worth cost for prod."
AKS Security

What are runtime security options in AKS?

Monitor and prevent malicious behavior during pod execution.

Q&ARuntime protection›
Options
  • AppArmor: Linux kernel security module
  • seccomp: system call filtering
  • SELinux: mandatory access control (not supported in AKS)
  • Falco: detect anomalous behavior
AppArmor in AKS
  • Restrict syscalls: pod can't access sensitive files
  • Protect: /proc, /sys filesystems
  • Example: nginx pod doesn't need to access /proc/sysrq-trigger
Falco
  • Detect: suspicious system calls
  • Example: shell spawned in container (unexpected)
  • Alert: suspicious behavior
Say this in interview "Runtime security: AppArmor/seccomp for syscall filtering. Falco for behavior detection. Combine with Pod Security Standards. Defense in depth: multiple layers."
AKS 06

Monitoring & Logging

AKS Monitoring

How do you enable Container Insights for AKS?

Application performance monitoring for Kubernetes.

Q&AContainer monitoring›
What is Container Insights
  • Application Insights for Kubernetes
  • Monitor: pod CPU/memory, node health, container logs
  • Integrated: with Azure Monitor
  • Automatic: works after enabling
Setup
  • 1. Create AKS cluster with Container Insights enabled
  • Or: az aks enable-addons --addons monitoring
  • 2. Automatically collects metrics from all nodes/pods
  • 3. View in Azure Monitor / Log Analytics
What It Collects
  • CPU %: per pod, per node
  • Memory usage: working set
  • Pod count per node
  • Container logs
  • Node status: ready/notready
Say this in interview "Container Insights: K8s monitoring built-in. Tracks pod/node CPU, memory, logs. Enable as add-on. View in Azure Monitor. Use for troubleshooting and performance analysis."
AKS Monitoring

Explain Prometheus and Grafana integration with AKS

Open-source monitoring stack.

Q&AOpen source monitoring›
Prometheus
  • Metrics collection: pull metrics from targets (Kubelet, pods)
  • Storage: time-series database
  • Query language: PromQL
Grafana
  • Visualization: dashboards for Prometheus metrics
  • Query: PromQL on top of Prometheus
  • Beautiful graphs: easier than Prometheus UI
Setup in AKS
  • Deploy Prometheus: Helm chart or Operator
  • Deploy Grafana: Helm chart
  • Configure scrape: collect from nodes, kubelet, pods
  • Create dashboards: visualize metrics
Say this in interview "Prometheus collects metrics (pull-based). Grafana visualizes. Deploy via Helm in AKS. Use for custom metrics, alerting. Alternative to Container Insights (more control, self-hosted)."
AKS Monitoring

How do you enable audit logging in AKS?

Track who did what in the cluster.

Q&AAudit trail›
What is Audit Logging
  • Log all API requests to Kubernetes API
  • Example: "user alice created pod nginx"
  • Who, What, When, Where, Result
  • Required for: compliance, troubleshooting, security
Setup
  • Enabled by default in AKS
  • Logs sent to: Log Analytics workspace
  • Query: KubePodInventory, AzureDiagnostics tables
Example Query
  • Show all pod creations: AzureDiagnostics | where verb == "create" and objectRef_kind == "Pod"
  • Show failed API requests: where statusCode != 200
Say this in interview "Audit logging enabled by default in AKS. Tracks API requests: who created pod, deleted service. Query in Log Analytics. Essential for compliance and security investigations."
AKS Monitoring

What is the AKS diagnostics and troubleshooting guide?

Built-in diagnostic tools for AKS issues.

Q&ADiagnostics tools›
AKS Diagnostics
  • Azure Portal feature: Diagnose and solve problems
  • Analyzes: cluster health, node status, pod logs
  • Provides: insights and recommendations
  • No configuration needed
Common Scenarios
  • Cluster not starting
  • Nodes not ready
  • Pods not running
  • Networking issues
How to Use
  • 1. Go to AKS cluster in Azure Portal
  • 2. Click: Diagnose and solve problems
  • 3. Select issue category
  • 4. Get recommendations
Say this in interview "AKS Diagnostics: built-in troubleshooting in Portal. Analyzes cluster health, provides recommendations. First stop when cluster misbehaves. No cost, included with cluster."
AKS Monitoring

How do you monitor node and pod resource usage in AKS?

Track CPU, memory, and other metrics.

Q&AResource monitoring›
Sources
  • Container Insights: automatic, all pods
  • Prometheus: pull-based, custom setup
  • kubectl top: command-line quick check
kubectl commands
  • kubectl top nodes: show node CPU/memory
  • kubectl top pods: show pod CPU/memory
  • kubectl describe node: detailed info
Container Insights Dashboard
  • Cluster node status: ready/notready
  • CPU/memory utilization % per node
  • Pod count: scheduled, pending, failed
Say this in interview "Monitor resources: Container Insights (automatic), Prometheus (custom), kubectl top (quick). Track CPU/memory per node and pod. Set alerts on thresholds (e.g., >80% CPU)."
AKS Monitoring

How do you set up alerts for AKS cluster health?

Proactive notifications on cluster issues.

Q&AAlert setup›
Common Alerts
  • Node not ready: > 0 for 5 minutes
  • Pod crash looping: restarts > 5 in 30 minutes
  • CPU usage: > 80% for 10 minutes
  • Memory pressure: nodes reporting memory pressure
Setup in Azure Monitor
  • 1. Create alert rule: condition (e.g., CPU > 80%)
  • 2. Severity: Critical, Error, Warning
  • 3. Action group: email, SMS, webhook
  • 4. Enable alert
Example
  • Condition: AzureDiagnostics | where pod_status == "Failed"
  • Severity: Error
  • Action: email ops-team@company.com
Say this in interview "Alert on: node status, pod crashes, CPU/memory usage. Azure Monitor or Prometheus+AlertManager. Action group: email, SMS, webhook. Avoid alert fatigue: tune thresholds."
AKS 07

Upgrades & Maintenance

AKS Upgrades

How do you upgrade an AKS cluster (Kubernetes version)?

Update to newer Kubernetes version safely.

Q&AVersion upgrades›
Kubernetes Version Support
  • AKS supports N-2 versions (current and 2 prior)
  • Example: if latest is 1.28, AKS supports 1.28, 1.27, 1.26
  • Versions older than 1.26 no longer supported
Upgrade Process
  • 1. Check available versions: az aks get-upgrades
  • 2. Control plane upgrade: Azure handles, no downtime
  • 3. Node pool upgrades: one at a time
  • 4. Total time: depends on cluster size
Safety
  • Test on dev/staging cluster first
  • Backup: etcd snapshots (automatic in AKS)
  • Drain nodes: remove pods before upgrade
  • Monitor: watch for pod scheduling issues
Say this in interview "Upgrade control plane first (no downtime). Then node pools one at a time. Test on dev cluster. Drain nodes: pods moved to other nodes. Total: few hours for medium cluster."
AKS Upgrades

Explain node image updates vs Kubernetes version upgrades

Two different update types with different impacts.

Q&AUpdate types›
Kubernetes Version Upgrade
  • Update API server, kubelet, kubectl
  • New features, bug fixes, security patches
  • Example: 1.27 → 1.28
  • Planned, infrequent (quarterly)
Node Image Update
  • Update OS (Ubuntu/Windows) patches, security
  • Example: Ubuntu kernel updates
  • May require node restart
  • Frequent (monthly or more)
Management
  • Kubernetes: manual upgrade (or auto)
  • Node image: automatic if auto-upgrade enabled
  • Both: schedule during maintenance windows
Say this in interview "K8s version: features/fixes (quarterly). Node image: OS patches (monthly). Separate upgrade paths. Enable auto node image update. Schedule K8s during maintenance window."
AKS Upgrades

How do you handle pod disruptions during node maintenance?

Graceful pod termination and rescheduling.

Q&ADisruption handling›
Pod Disruption Events
  • Node upgrade: pods must move
  • Node drain: remove all pods before maintenance
  • Pod eviction: Kubernetes removes pod to free resources
Graceful Termination
  • 1. Pod receives SIGTERM signal
  • 2. App cleans up (close connections, drain queue)
  • 3. Pod terminates (default 30 seconds)
  • 4. New pod scheduled on another node
Best Practices
  • Set terminationGracePeriodSeconds (app-specific)
  • Use Pod Disruption Budgets (PDB)
  • Multiple replicas: stateless apps tolerate disruption
  • Health checks: ensure pod readiness
Say this in interview "Pod disruption: pod eviction during maintenance. SIGTERM → graceful shutdown. Use PDB to ensure min replicas available. Multiple replicas tolerate disruption."
AKS Upgrades

What is pod disruption budgets (PDB) in AKS?

Ensure minimum pod availability during maintenance.

Q&AAvailability guarantees›
What is PDB
  • Kubernetes resource: defines min available replicas
  • Example: minAvailable=2 (always keep 2 pods running)
  • Prevents: all pods evicted simultaneously
  • Respected by: node drains, maintenance operations
Example Scenario
  • Deployment: 3 pod replicas
  • PDB: minAvailable=2
  • Node drain: evicts max 1 pod (keeps min 2 running)
  • Service: always available during maintenance
Configuration
  • minAvailable: exact number OR percentage
  • maxUnavailable: alternative (max pods that can be down)
  • Selects pods: via labels
Say this in interview "PDB: min available pods during disruption. Example: minAvailable=2 keeps 2 running during drain. Prevents full outage. Essential for HA apps. Use with multiple replicas."
AKS Upgrades

How do you perform rolling updates of deployments in AKS?

Update app versions safely without downtime.

Q&AZero-downtime updates›
Rolling Update Strategy
  • Update pods gradually: old → new
  • Default: replace 1 pod at a time
  • Configurable: maxSurge (new pods), maxUnavailable (old pods)
Configuration
  • maxSurge: 1 (create 1 new pod while old running)
  • maxUnavailable: 0 (keep all running)
  • Result: 1 old pod → 1 new pod → 1 old pod → 1 new pod (etc)
How It Works
  • 1. kubectl set image: update deployment image
  • 2. New ReplicaSet created
  • 3. Pods gradually shifted from old to new
  • 4. Old ReplicaSet scaled down to 0
Say this in interview "Rolling update: gradually replace pods. maxSurge=1, maxUnavailable=0 = zero downtime. kubectl set image triggers update. Automatic rollback if health checks fail."
AKS 08

Advanced

AKS Advanced

How do you implement multi-tenancy in AKS clusters?

Isolate multiple teams/applications in one cluster.

Q&AIsolation›
Isolation Levels
  • Namespace isolation: separate namespaces per tenant
  • RBAC: restrict tenant access to own namespace
  • Network policies: block cross-tenant traffic
  • Resource quotas: limit tenant resource usage
Example Setup
  • Tenant A: namespace tenant-a
  • Tenant B: namespace tenant-b
  • User alice: role admin in tenant-a only
  • Network policy: tenant-a pods only talk to tenant-a services
Resource Quotas
  • Limit CPU: tenant max 10 cores
  • Limit memory: tenant max 20GB
  • Limit pods: tenant max 50 pods
  • Prevents: one tenant starving others
Say this in interview "Multi-tenancy: separate namespaces, RBAC per namespace, network policies (no cross-tenant), resource quotas (prevent hogging). Cost savings: single cluster vs multiple."
AKS Advanced

Explain GitOps with AKS — Flux vs ArgoCD

Infrastructure as code for Kubernetes deployments.

Q&AGitOps tools›
What is GitOps
  • Git as source of truth: YAML in repo = desired state
  • Tool syncs: cluster state matches Git
  • Pull-based: tool in cluster fetches from Git
  • Benefits: code review for deployments, easy rollback
Flux
  • CNCF-incubating project
  • Lightweight: lower resource usage
  • GitOps only: doesn't do much else
  • Good for: teams preferring simplicity
ArgoCD
  • CNCF-graduated project (more mature)
  • Rich UI: dashboard for deployments
  • Application management: extra features
  • Good for: teams wanting visibility
Workflow
  • 1. Developer: push YAML to Git
  • 2. Flux/ArgoCD: detect change, sync to AKS
  • 3. Cluster: pods updated automatically
  • 4. Rollback: revert Git commit, redeploy
Say this in interview "GitOps: Git as source of truth. Flux (simple) vs ArgoCD (feature-rich). Auto-sync cluster to Git. Code review for deployments. Easy rollback: git revert."
AKS Advanced

How do you implement service mesh (Istio/Linkerd) in AKS?

Advanced networking for microservices.

Q&AService mesh›
What is Service Mesh
  • Proxy layer for pod-to-pod communication
  • Features: traffic management, security, observability
  • Sidecar pattern: proxy in every pod
Istio
  • Feature-rich: traffic splitting, canary, retries
  • Complex: steep learning curve
  • Resource-heavy: more CPU/memory
  • Good for: complex traffic requirements
Linkerd
  • Lightweight: minimal resource usage
  • Simpler: easier to operate
  • Focused: does one thing (service mesh) well
  • Good for: teams wanting simplicity
Say this in interview "Service mesh: Istio (feature-rich, complex) or Linkerd (lightweight, simple). Enables: traffic splitting, canary, mTLS, retries. Sidecar proxies in every pod."
AKS Advanced

What is Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes? How does it relate to AKS?

Manage any Kubernetes cluster from Azure.

Q&AArc for Kubernetes›
What is Arc-enabled K8s
  • Connect any K8s cluster to Azure
  • On-prem, AWS, GCP, other clouds
  • Manage from Azure Portal
  • Deploy apps via Azure Policy
Relationship to AKS
  • AKS: native managed Kubernetes in Azure
  • Arc-enabled: on-prem or other K8s managed by Azure
  • Both: can be managed unified in Azure Portal
Use Cases
  • Hybrid multi-cloud: connect all K8s to Azure
  • On-prem K8s: manage like AKS (policies, RBAC)
  • Edge Kubernetes: bring management consistency
Say this in interview "Arc-enabled K8s: connect any cluster to Azure. Unified management portal. Deploy policies across clouds. AKS is native, Arc is for existing/external clusters."
AKS Advanced

How do you implement blue-green deployments in AKS?

Two versions running, instant switchover.

Q&ADeployment strategies›
Blue-Green Strategy
  • Blue: current version (stable)
  • Green: new version (being tested)
  • Switch: service selects green once ready
  • Rollback: instant switch back to blue
Implementation
  • 1. Deploy green version (new)
  • 2. Run tests against green
  • 3. Service selector: switch from blue to green
  • 4. If issue: revert selector to blue (instant rollback)
Advantages vs Disadvantages
  • Advantages: instant rollback, no gradual traffic shift
  • Disadvantages: need 2x resources during deployment
Say this in interview "Blue-green: two full deployments. Instant switchover via service selector. Good for: immediate rollback needed. Cost: double resources during deployment."
AKS Advanced

How do you implement canary deployments with Flagger in AKS?

Gradual rollout to subset of users.

Q&AProgressive rollout›
What is Canary
  • Route small % to new version (e.g., 5%)
  • Monitor: errors, latency
  • If good: increase %, eventually 100%
  • If bad: rollback immediately
Flagger
  • Automated canary deployments
  • Works with: Istio, Linkerd, App Mesh
  • Progressive traffic shift: 5% → 10% → 50% → 100%
  • Metrics: latency, error rate, custom metrics
Example
  • Deploy new version
  • Flagger: 5% traffic to new
  • Monitor: if errors < 1%, increase to 10%
  • Continue: gradual increase
  • Complete: 100%, old version removed
Say this in interview "Canary: gradual traffic shift to new version. Flagger automates it. Monitor: errors, latency. Rollback if issues. Good for: reducing risk, catch bugs early."
AKS Advanced

Explain helm charts and package management in AKS

Templating and packaging for Kubernetes apps.

Q&AApp packaging›
What is Helm
  • Package manager for Kubernetes
  • Charts: pre-configured app bundles
  • Templating: customize deployment values
  • Releases: versioned app deployments
Helm Chart Structure
  • Chart.yaml: metadata
  • values.yaml: default configuration
  • templates/: K8s YAML templates
  • charts/: dependencies
Common Commands
  • helm install: deploy app
  • helm upgrade: update deployment
  • helm rollback: revert to previous version
  • helm search: find charts
Say this in interview "Helm: K8s package manager. Charts = app bundles. Templates = customize. Values.yaml = configuration. Easy: one command to deploy full stack. Popular charts in Helm Hub."
AKS 09

Troubleshooting

AKS Troubleshooting

Pod not starting — how do you troubleshoot?

Debug pod startup failures.

TroubleshootingPod failures›
Check Pod Status
  • kubectl describe pod: events, conditions
  • kubectl logs: app logs
  • kubectl logs --previous: logs from crashed container
Common Issues
  • Pending: insufficient resources, PVC not bound
  • CrashLoopBackOff: app crashed, restarting
  • ImagePullBackOff: image not found or pull failed
  • Not Ready: health check failing
Debugging Steps
  • 1. Check events: kubectl describe pod
  • 2. Check logs: kubectl logs
  • 3. Check resources: kubectl top pods
  • 4. Check config: kubectl get configmap
  • 5. Exec into pod: kubectl exec -it pod /bin/bash
Say this in interview "Troubleshoot pod: describe (events), logs (output), check resource requests/limits, verify image exists. Exec into pod for manual testing. Check ConfigMaps/Secrets."
AKS Troubleshooting

Explain common AKS networking issues and how to debug them

Network connectivity problems in clusters.

TroubleshootingNetwork issues›
Common Issues
  • Pod can't reach service: DNS, network policy, or firewall
  • Ingress not working: controller not installed, rules wrong
  • No internet: outbound rules blocked
  • High latency: wrong CNI, node CPU high
Debugging Tools
  • kubectl exec pod -c container -- nslookup service: DNS resolution
  • kubectl exec pod -- curl http://service: connectivity
  • kubectl get networkpolicy: review policies
  • kubectl get svc: check service endpoints
Checklist
  • 1. DNS working? (nslookup)
  • 2. Service IP correct? (kubectl get svc)
  • 3. Pods in service? (kubectl get ep service)
  • 4. Network policy blocking? (kubectl describe networkpolicy)
  • 5. NSG blocking? (check Azure portal)
Say this in interview "Network debug: DNS (nslookup), connectivity (curl), endpoints (kubectl get ep), network policies, NSGs. Test from pod: exec -it pod /bin/sh. Check service has endpoints."
AKS Troubleshooting

How do you troubleshoot application performance in AKS?

Debug slow applications.

TroubleshootingPerformance›
Check Resource Usage
  • kubectl top pods: CPU/memory per pod
  • kubectl top nodes: CPU/memory per node
  • Container Insights: visualize trends
Common Causes
  • Pod CPU throttled: requests too high, set lower limits
  • Memory OOM: pod using more than limit, increase
  • Slow disk: PV latency, check storage class
  • Network latency: pods on different nodes, network busy
Investigation
  • Check request/limits: kubectl get deployment -o yaml | grep -A5 resources
  • Check PV: kubectl get pvc, check storage class
  • Check logs: app debug logs
  • Profile app: CPU profiler, memory profiler
Say this in interview "Slow app: check CPU/memory usage (kubectl top), review requests/limits, check disk I/O, profile with tools. Common: throttling (high CPU limit), OOM (high memory usage)."
AKS Troubleshooting

Explain CrashLoopBackOff and ImagePullBackOff errors

Two common pod error states.

ErrorsCommon failures›
CrashLoopBackOff
  • Pod crashed, Kubernetes restarting it
  • Restart delay increases: 100ms → 5m → 5m (max)
  • Causes: app bug, missing config, permissions
  • Fix: check logs, fix app, redeploy
ImagePullBackOff
  • Can't pull container image
  • Causes: image doesn't exist, auth failed, registry unreachable
  • Fix: verify image name, check registry credentials, test registry access
Debugging
  • CrashLoop: kubectl logs -p pod (previous logs)
  • ImagePull: kubectl describe pod (events show auth/not found)
Say this in interview "CrashLoopBackOff: app crashed. Check logs: kubectl logs -p. ImagePullBackOff: image unavailable. Check: registry access, image name, credentials."
AKS Troubleshooting

How do you access AKS cluster nodes for debugging?

SSH into nodes for deep troubleshooting.

TroubleshootingNode access›
Methods
  • kubectl debug node: Kubernetes native, creates debug pod
  • SSH: traditional SSH (requires key)
  • Bastion: bastion VM in VNet for access
kubectl debug node
  • kubectl debug node nodename -it --image=ubuntu
  • Mounts node filesystem at /host
  • Inspect: /host/var/log, /host/etc, /host/proc
What to Check
  • dmesg: kernel logs
  • journalctl -u kubelet: kubelet logs
  • docker ps: running containers
  • free -m: memory usage
Say this in interview "Access nodes: kubectl debug node or SSH. kubectl debug creates debug pod with node filesystem. Check: kernel logs (dmesg), kubelet logs, container status."
AKS Troubleshooting

How do you debug Helm chart deployment failures in AKS?

Troubleshoot Helm deployment issues.

TroubleshootingHelm issues›
Common Issues
  • Rendering fails: template syntax error
  • Missing values: required chart value not provided
  • Version mismatch: chart K8s version incompatible
Debugging Commands
  • helm lint: validate chart syntax
  • helm template: render YAML (no deploy)
  • helm install --dry-run: simulate install, show output
  • helm get values: see actual values used
Example
  • helm install my-app myrepo/mychart --dry-run: see rendered YAML
  • If error: helm template myrepo/mychart: see full output
  • Check: values-production.yaml has all required fields
Say this in interview "Helm debug: lint (validate), template (render YAML), dry-run (simulate), get values (check actuals). Errors usually: typo in values.yaml, missing required value, or template syntax."
AKS 10

Cost Optimization

AKS Cost

How do you optimize costs in AKS — reserved instances, spot VMs?

Reduce cloud spending for AKS clusters.

Q&ACost savings›
Reserved Instances (RIs)
  • Commit to 1 or 3 year usage
  • Discount: 30-55% vs pay-as-you-go
  • Good for: stable production workloads
Spot VMs
  • Spare Azure capacity, up to 90% discount
  • Risk: can be evicted (deallocated)
  • Good for: batch jobs, non-critical workloads
  • Use PDB: ensure pod availability during eviction
Strategy
  • Spot pool: batch jobs, ML training, analytics
  • RI pool: critical production workloads
  • Combination: save 60-70% overall
Say this in interview "RIs: 30-55% discount (1-3 year commitment). Spot: 90% cheaper (eviction risk). Strategy: Spot for batch, RI for production. Multi-pool approach saves 60-70%."
AKS Cost

What is Azure Advisor for AKS recommendations?

Automated recommendations for optimization.

Q&AAzure Advisor›
What Advisor Does
  • Analyzes AKS configuration
  • Provides recommendations: cost, performance, reliability
  • Categories: Reliability, Security, Performance, Cost
Common Recommendations
  • Use RIs instead of pay-as-you-go
  • Right-size VMs (smaller SKU for less-used pools)
  • Use Spot VMs for non-critical workloads
  • Enable autoscaler to scale down unused nodes
How to Access
  • Azure Portal: Advisor (top menu)
  • Filter: resource type = AKS
  • Review: recommendations with impact ratings
Say this in interview "Azure Advisor: automated recommendations for AKS. Check regularly for cost-saving opportunities. Typical: use RIs, right-size nodes, use Spot, enable autoscaler."
AKS Cost

How do you right-size node pools to reduce costs?

Use appropriate VM sizes for workloads.

Q&AVM sizing›
Analysis
  • Monitor: actual CPU/memory usage (Container Insights)
  • Compare: pod requests/limits vs actual
  • Find: smallest VM size that handles peak load
Example
  • Currently: Standard_D4s_v3 (4CPU, 16GB) per node
  • Actual usage: max 1CPU, 4GB memory
  • Recommendation: Standard_B2s (2CPU, 4GB) = 50% cost savings
Implementation
  • 1. Create new pool with smaller SKU
  • 2. Migrate workloads (rolling restart)
  • 3. Delete old pool
  • 4. Monitor: ensure workloads fit
Say this in interview "Right-sizing: monitor actual resource usage, pick smallest VM that handles peak. Common: over-provisioned by 2-3x. Savings: 30-60% possible."
AKS Cost

Explain Kubernetes resource requests and limits — how do they impact costs?

Resource requests affect scheduling and cost.

Q&AResource management›
Requests
  • Pod guaranteed this much: 1 core, 512MB
  • Scheduler uses requests to place pods
  • Cost: high requests = need larger nodes
Limits
  • Pod can't exceed: 2 cores, 2GB
  • Enforced: CPU throttled, Memory evicted
  • Default: no limit (dangerous!)
Cost Impact
  • High requests: large nodes needed, higher cost
  • Low requests: small nodes, bins pods together, save cost
  • No limits: pod can consume all node resources (bad!)
Best Practice
  • Measure actual usage: CPU profiler, memory profiler
  • Set requests to: actual usage + 20% buffer
  • Set limits to: requests * 2 or 3 (peak burst)
  • Reduces cost: smaller nodes + better bin packing
Say this in interview "Requests: pod guaranteed amount + affects cost. Measure actual usage. High requests = large nodes = high cost. Set requests accurate to reduce costs, improve bin packing."
CI/CD 01

Fundamentals

CI/CD Fundamentals

CI/CD Pipeline Flow

Standard stages from code to production monitoring.

Q&APipeline stages›
CI/CD Pipeline with DevSecOps Tools
Developer pushes code to Git
    ↓
Pre-commit Scanning (Gitleaks, Truffle Hog) - Detect secrets in code
    ↓
Webhook triggers pipeline
    ↓
1. CHECKOUT: Pull code from repo
    ↓
2. BUILD: Compile, lint, build artifacts
    ├─→ SonarQube (SAST: Static code analysis)
    ├─→ ESLint/Pylint (Code linting)
    └─→ BuildTools (Maven, Gradle, npm)
    ↓
3. TEST: Unit tests, integration tests
    ├─→ JUnit, pytest (Unit testing)
    ├─→ Postman, REST Assured (API testing)
    └─→ TestContainers (Integration testing)
    ↓
4. DEPENDENCY SCAN: Check for vulnerable libraries
    ├─→ Snyk (Dependency vulnerabilities)
    ├─→ Dependabot (Automated dependency updates)
    ├─→ Black Duck (OSS vulnerabilities)
    └─→ OWASP Dependency-Check
    ↓
5. BUILD DOCKER IMAGE & SCAN
    ├─→ Docker build image:tag
    ├─→ Trivy (Container image scanning for CVEs)
    ├─→ Aqua Security (Runtime scanning)
    └─→ Snyk Container (Image vulnerabilities)
    ↓
6. INFRASTRUCTURE SCAN: IaC security
    ├─→ Checkov (Terraform/CloudFormation scan)
    ├─→ Bridgecrew (Policy enforcement)
    └─→ tfsec (Terraform security)
    ↓
7. PUBLISH: Push image to registry
    ├─→ Sign image (Notation, cosign)
    ├─→ Push to ECR/ACR/DockerHub
    └─→ Update manifests with new tag
    ↓
8. DEPLOY (staging): Deploy to test environment
    ├─→ kubectl apply -f k8s/
    ├─→ Falco (Runtime threat detection)
    └─→ AppArmor/seccomp (Pod security)
    ↓
9. DYNAMIC SECURITY TESTING (DAST)
    ├─→ OWASP ZAP (Web app penetration testing)
    ├─→ Burp Suite (Security scanning)
    ├─→ Nikto (Web server scanning)
    └─→ SQL injection/XSS/CSRF testing
    ↓
10. SMOKE TEST: Quick sanity check
    └─→ Health checks, basic functionality
    ↓
11. SECURITY APPROVAL GATE
    └─→ Manual review of security scan results
    ↓
12. DEPLOY (production): Deploy to prod
    ├─→ Blue-green or canary deployment
    ├─→ Network policies applied
    └─→ Pod security policies enforced
    ↓
13. MONITOR & DETECT
    ├─→ Runtime Application Self-Protection (RASP)
    ├─→ Falco (Kernel event monitoring)
    ├─→ Prometheus + Grafana (Metrics)
    ├─→ ELK Stack (Log aggregation)
    ├─→ Azure Defender / AWS GuardDuty (Cloud threats)
    └─→ Alert on security events
Continuous Integration (CI)
  • Stages 1-7: automated code validation
  • Happens on every commit
  • Fast feedback: minutes
Continuous Deployment (CD)
  • Stages 8-11: deployment and monitoring
  • Manual approval gate before prod
  • Automated everything else
Say this in interview "CI/CD pipeline: checkout → build → test → scan → publish → deploy staging → smoke test → deploy prod → monitor. CI: automated validation. CD: automated deployment + monitoring."

In CI/CD stages, what tools have you used?

Tools for each pipeline stage.

Q&ATool stack›
Build Stage
  • Maven, Gradle (Java)
  • npm, yarn (Node.js)
  • Docker (containers)
  • Make, Bazel (polyglot)
Test Stage

Scan Stage

  • Snyk (dependency vulnerabilities)
  • Trivy (container image scan)
  • Checkov (infrastructure scan)
Publish Stage

  • Nexus, Artifactory (artifacts)
  • npm registry, PyPI (packages)
Deploy Stage

  • ArgoCD, Flux (GitOps)
  • Terraform (infrastructure)
  • Ansible (config management)
  • AWS CodeDeploy, Azure DevOps Release
Monitor Stage
  • Prometheus, Grafana (metrics)
  • ELK Stack, Splunk (logs)
  • DataDog, New Relic (APM)
Say this in interview "Build: Maven/npm/Docker. Test: JUnit/pytest/Postman. Scan: SonarQube/Snyk/Trivy. Publish: Docker Registry/ECR. Deploy: kubectl/ArgoCD/Terraform. Monitor: Prometheus/ELK/DataDog."
CI/CD Fundamentals

Comparison: GitHub Actions vs Azure DevOps vs Jenkins

Three major CI/CD platforms: strengths and weaknesses.

Q&APlatform comparison›
GitHub Actions
  • Pros: free tier generous, tight Git integration, simple YAML, large marketplace
  • Cons: limited self-hosted runner support, younger ecosystem
  • Best for: open-source projects, startups, GitHub-first teams
  • Pricing: free up to 2000 min/month, then per minute
Azure DevOps
  • Pros: integrated repos/boards/artifacts, Microsoft ecosystem, parallel jobs free, YAML powerful
  • Cons: steeper learning curve, less market adoption
  • Best for: enterprises on Azure/Microsoft stack
  • Pricing: free tier strong (1800 min/month)
Jenkins
  • Pros: mature, plugin ecosystem huge, on-prem control, highly customizable
  • Cons: maintenance burden, YAML support added recently, configuration complex
  • Best for: enterprises with legacy investments, complex customization needs
  • Pricing: self-hosted, cost is infrastructure
Quick Comparison
  • GitHub Actions: simple, free, but less mature
  • Azure DevOps: powerful, integrated, enterprise-grade
  • Jenkins: most flexible, but requires more management
Say this in interview "GitHub Actions: easy, free, good for OSS. Azure DevOps: enterprise, integrated, powerful. Jenkins: mature, customizable, self-hosted. Choose based on: team, budget, existing tools."
CI/CD 02

GitHub Actions

GitHub Actions

GitHub Actions Simple YAML Syntax

Basic structure of a GitHub Actions workflow.

Q&AWorkflow syntax›
Basic Structure
name: CI Pipeline

on:
  push:
    branches: [ main, develop ]
  pull_request:
    branches: [ main ]

jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest

    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3

      - name: Set up Node
        uses: actions/setup-node@v3
        with:
          node-version: '18'

      - name: Install dependencies
        run: npm install

      - name: Run tests
        run: npm test

      - name: Build
        run: npm run build
Key Concepts
  • on: triggers (push, pull_request, schedule, manual)
  • jobs: parallelizable units of work
  • runs-on: runner OS (ubuntu, windows, macos)
  • steps: commands or actions
  • uses: reference to action (action-name@version)
  • run: shell command
Environment & Secrets
  • env: workflow-level variables
  • secrets: sensitive data (passwords, tokens)
  • matrix: run job with multiple configurations
Say this in interview "GitHub Actions YAML: on (triggers), jobs, runs-on (runner), steps (uses actions or run commands). Actions are reusable units. Secrets stored securely, accessed via ${{ secrets.NAME }}."
GitHub Actions

How GitHub Actions and ArgoCD Work Together

GitOps pipeline: GitHub Actions builds, ArgoCD deploys.

Q&AGitOps workflow›
GitHub Actions (CI)
  • Trigger: code push to main branch
  • Steps: checkout, build, test, scan, build Docker image
  • Output: push image to Docker registry (ECR, ACR, DockerHub)
  • Update: commit image tag to Git (ArgoCD GitOps repo)
ArgoCD (CD/GitOps)
  • Watch: Git repo for changes to deployment YAML
  • Detect: image tag updated
  • Sync: automatically deploy new image to Kubernetes
  • Monitor: ensure cluster matches Git
Flow Example
  • 1. Dev pushes code to GitHub
  • 2. GitHub Actions: build → test → publish image:v1.2.3
  • 3. GitHub Actions: update deployment.yaml with new image tag
  • 4. ArgoCD detects change, syncs to cluster
  • 5. Kubernetes rolls out new pods with new image
Benefits
  • Full audit trail in Git
  • Easy rollback: revert Git commit
  • Automatic sync: infrastructure always matches desired state
  • Clear separation: GitHub Actions = CI, ArgoCD = CD
Say this in interview "GitHub Actions builds + publishes image. Updates Git with new tag. ArgoCD watches Git, syncs to K8s automatically. GitOps: Git is source of truth. Easy rollback, full audit trail."
CI/CD 03

Azure Pipelines

Azure Pipelines

What are Azure Pipelines Agents? (Microsoft-hosted vs self-hosted)

Two types of compute environments for running jobs.

Q&AAgent types›
Microsoft-hosted Agents
  • Azure-managed VMs: Windows, Linux (Ubuntu, Debian), macOS
  • Preinstalled: build tools, SDKs, Docker, Kubernetes
  • Free tier: 1800 minutes/month
  • Pros: no setup, automatic updates, reliable
  • Cons: limited customization, slower (cold start)
Self-hosted Agents
  • Your VM/machine running Azure Pipelines agent
  • Full control: install custom tools, cache
  • Faster: no cold start, cache between runs
  • Pros: speed, customization, on-prem access
  • Cons: maintenance burden, cost of infrastructure
When to Use
  • Microsoft-hosted: public repos, OSS, simple builds
  • Self-hosted: on-prem databases, specialized tools, high volume
Configuration
  • In pipeline YAML: pool: name (agent pool)
  • Agent pools: group agents with similar capabilities
  • Demands: specify required tools (VS2019, Docker, etc.)
Say this in interview "Microsoft-hosted: managed, no setup, free tier included. Self-hosted: full control, faster, more customization. Choose based on: needs (on-prem access?), scale (volume?), cost."
Azure Pipelines

How do you implement multi-stage pipelines?

Organize jobs into logical stages with dependencies.

Q&AMulti-stage design›
Stage Structure
stages:
  - stage: Build
    displayName: 'Build Stage'
    jobs:
      - job: BuildJob
        steps:
          - script: npm build

  - stage: Test
    displayName: 'Test Stage'
    dependsOn: Build
    jobs:
      - job: TestJob
        steps:
          - script: npm test

  - stage: Deploy_Staging
    displayName: 'Deploy to Staging'
    dependsOn: Test
    jobs:
      - job: DeployJob
        steps:
          - script: kubectl apply -f k8s/

  - stage: Deploy_Prod
    displayName: 'Deploy to Production'
    dependsOn: Deploy_Staging
    condition: succeeded()
    jobs:
      - job: ApproveDeploy
        pool: server
        steps:
          - task: ManualValidation@0
            inputs:
              notifyUsers: 'admin@company.com'
      - job: ProdDeploy
        dependsOn: ApproveDeploy
        steps:
          - script: kubectl apply -f k8s/ --context prod
Key Features
  • dependsOn: stage waits for dependency to complete
  • condition: run stage if condition met (e.g., succeeded())
  • ManualValidation: human approval gate
  • Jobs run parallel by default within stage
Say this in interview "Multi-stage: Build → Test → Deploy Staging → Deploy Prod. Each stage depends on previous. Add manual approval gate before prod. Stages run sequentially, jobs parallel."
Azure Pipelines

How do you deploy to multiple environments?

Parameterized deployments to dev, staging, production.

Q&AEnvironment deployment›
Approach 1: Variables per Environment
  • Variable groups: dev-vars, staging-vars, prod-vars
  • Each group has: resource group, cluster, image registry
  • Pipeline selects group based on branch or parameter
Approach 2: Stage-based Deployment
  • Deploy_Dev: runs on every commit
  • Deploy_Staging: runs after merge to main
  • Deploy_Prod: requires manual approval
  • Each stage uses environment-specific agent pools
Example YAML
stages:
  - stage: Deploy_Dev
    variables:
      - group: dev-vars
    jobs:
      - deployment: DeployDev
        environment: 'development'
        strategy:
          runOnce:
            deploy:
              - task: KubernetesManifest@0
                inputs:
                  kubernetesServiceConnection: $(K8S_DEV)
                  manifests: $(Pipeline.Workspace)/manifests

  - stage: Deploy_Prod
    variables:
      - group: prod-vars
    jobs:
      - deployment: DeployProd
        environment: 'production'
        strategy:
          runOnce:
            deploy:
              - task: KubernetesManifest@0
                inputs:
                  kubernetesServiceConnection: $(K8S_PROD)
                  manifests: $(Pipeline.Workspace)/manifests
Say this in interview "Multi-environment: use variable groups (dev-vars, prod-vars). Separate stages per environment. Use environment: 'production' for approval gates. Each stage references correct cluster/creds."
CI/CD 04

Security & Secrets

CI/CD Security

How to use secrets in CI/CD?

Store and access sensitive data securely.

Q&ASecret management›
GitHub Actions Secrets
  • Portal: Settings → Secrets and variables → Actions
  • Create: MY_SECRET = "value"
  • Access: ${{ secrets.MY_SECRET }} in YAML
  • Masked in logs: GitHub hides secret values
Azure Pipelines Secrets
  • Variable groups: Pipeline → Library → Variable groups
  • Create: MY_SECRET = "value" (lock icon to secure)
  • Link to pipeline: variables: - group: secret-group
  • Access: $(MY_SECRET) in YAML
Jenkins Secrets
  • Credentials: Manage Jenkins → Credentials
  • Types: Username/password, SSH keys, tokens
  • Use in pipeline: withCredentials block
Best Practices
  • Never hardcode secrets in Git
  • Use secrets management (AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault)
  • Rotate secrets regularly
  • Audit access logs
  • Principle of least privilege: minimal permissions per secret
Say this in interview "Secrets in CI/CD: store in platform vault (not Git). Access via: GitHub ${{ secrets.NAME }}, Azure $(NAME). Masked in logs. Better: use AWS Secrets Manager or Azure Key Vault + managed identity."
CI/CD 05

Deployment Strategies

Deployment Strategies

Explain blue-green deployments in CI/CD

Two environments, instant switchover.

Q&ABlue-Green›
Strategy
  • Blue: current production (v1.0)
  • Green: new version (v1.1) being tested
  • Switch: LB/router sends traffic to green
  • Rollback: instant switch back to blue if issues
Implementation
  • 1. Deploy green version (parallel to blue)
  • 2. Run smoke tests against green
  • 3. Switch load balancer: blue → green
  • 4. Monitor: watch error rate, latency
  • 5. Keep blue running for quick rollback
Pros & Cons
  • Pros: instant rollback, zero downtime, easy to test
  • Cons: 2x infrastructure during deployment, data migration complex
Say this in interview "Blue-green: two full environments. Deploy to green, test, switch traffic. Instant rollback. Cost: 2x resources. Good for: critical apps where instant rollback needed."
Deployment Strategies

Explain canary deployments — how do you implement them?

Gradual rollout to subset of users.

Q&ACanary releases›
Strategy
  • Deploy new version alongside current
  • Route 5% traffic to new version (canary)
  • Monitor: errors, latency, custom metrics
  • If good: increase 5% → 10% → 50% → 100%
  • If bad: rollback immediately (abort)
Implementation in Kubernetes
  • Tool: Flagger (automated canary)
  • Works with: Istio, Linkerd, App Mesh
  • Metrics: latency p99 < 500ms, error rate < 1%
  • Workflow: 5% → 10% → 25% → 50% → 100% (each step 5 minutes)
Example Flagger Config
apiVersion: flagger.app/v1beta1
kind: Canary
metadata:
  name: webapp
spec:
  targetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: webapp
  service:
    port: 80
  analysis:
    interval: 5m
    threshold: 5
    maxWeight: 50
    stepWeight: 5
    metrics:
    - name: error-rate
      thresholdRange:
        max: 1
    - name: latency
      thresholdRange:
        max: 500
Pros & Cons
  • Pros: catch bugs early, gradual rollout, low risk
  • Cons: complex to setup, slower deployment, dual infrastructure
Say this in interview "Canary: 5% traffic to new, monitor metrics, gradually increase. Tool: Flagger. Good for: reduce risk, catch bugs. Automatic rollback if metrics bad. Slower but safer."
Deployment Strategies

Explain rolling deployments — when do you use them?

Replace pods one at a time, always serving traffic.

Q&ARolling updates›
Strategy
  • 3 pods running: all old version
  • Step 1: launch 1 new pod, terminate 1 old → 2 old + 1 new
  • Step 2: launch 1 new, terminate 1 old → 1 old + 2 new
  • Step 3: launch 1 new, terminate 1 old → 3 new
  • Always serving traffic (zero downtime)
Kubernetes Config
  • Default deployment strategy in Kubernetes
  • maxSurge: 1 (create 1 new while old running)
  • maxUnavailable: 0 (keep all running)
  • gradualRolloutPercent: k8s watches pod health
When to Use
  • Most common strategy
  • Backward compatible changes (non-breaking)
  • Stateless applications
  • Good for: reduce risk of bad rollout
Pros & Cons
  • Pros: zero downtime, simple, no extra infrastructure
  • Cons: gradual rollout (slower), hard to rollback, mixed versions
Say this in interview "Rolling: replace pods one at a time. Kubectl default: maxSurge=1, maxUnavailable=0. Zero downtime, simple. Use for: most deployments. Rollback slower (need to roll out old version again)."
Deployment Strategies

How do you implement zero-downtime deployments?

Keep service available during updates.

Q&AHA deployments›
Requirements
  • Multiple replicas: at least 2-3 pods
  • Health checks: liveness & readiness probes
  • Graceful shutdown: handle SIGTERM (30s timeout)
  • Connection draining: finish in-flight requests
Kubernetes Setup
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: webapp
spec:
  replicas: 3
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      maxSurge: 1
      maxUnavailable: 0
  template:
    spec:
      terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 30
      containers:
      - name: app
        readinessProbe:
          httpGet:
            path: /health
            port: 8080
          initialDelaySeconds: 5
          periodSeconds: 5
        livenessProbe:
          httpGet:
            path: /alive
            port: 8080
          initialDelaySeconds: 15
          periodSeconds: 10
Pod Disruption Budget
  • Ensure min pods available during maintenance
  • minAvailable: 2 (keep at least 2 running)
  • Prevents: Kubernetes draining all pods at once
Deployment Best Practices
  • Use rolling update (Kubernetes default)
  • Health checks must pass before serving traffic
  • Graceful shutdown: app closes connections on SIGTERM
  • PDB: minAvailable ensures availability
Say this in interview "Zero-downtime: multiple replicas + rolling update + health checks + graceful shutdown + PDB. Kubernetes drains pod → waits for termination grace period → removes. App finishes requests."
Deployment Strategies

How do you handle rolling updates in Kubernetes CI/CD?

Automated rolling deployments in Kubernetes.

Q&AKubernetes updates›
Trigger Rolling Update
  • kubectl set image deployment/webapp webapp=webapp:v1.1
  • Or: update deployment YAML, kubectl apply
  • Kubernetes detects spec change, starts rolling update
Kubernetes Controller Behavior
  • 1. Create new ReplicaSet with new image
  • 2. Scale up new RS: 1 pod
  • 3. Wait: pod is ready (readiness probe passes)
  • 4. Scale down old RS: 1 pod
  • 5. Repeat: until all pods updated
Monitor Progress
  • kubectl rollout status deployment/webapp
  • kubectl rollout history deployment/webapp (see revisions)
  • kubectl rollout undo deployment/webapp (rollback)
Configuration
  • maxSurge: 1 (allow 1 extra pod during update)
  • maxUnavailable: 0 (no pods down)
  • result: slower but zero-downtime
Say this in interview "Kubernetes rolling: update deployment YAML or set image → controller scales new RS up while scaling old down → waits for readiness → automatic. Monitor: kubectl rollout status. Rollback: kubectl rollout undo."
Monitoring 01

Fundamentals

Monitoring Fundamentals

Monitoring Tools Overview & Alternatives

Master the landscape of monitoring tools. Know the key competitors and use cases for each. This shows you understand the DevOps ecosystem.

Q&ATools & competitors›
Prometheus + Grafana (Open Source)
  • Prometheus: Time-series database, pull-based scraping, PromQL language
  • Grafana: Visualization & dashboarding (not TSDB itself, works with any backend)
  • Use case: Kubernetes, microservices, self-hosted monitoring
  • Pros: Free, lightweight, cloud-native, CNCF standard
  • Cons: Single node (limited scaling), need ops expertise, manual setup
  • Scaling: For scale, use VictoriaMetrics (Prometheus-compatible, better clustering)
ELK Stack (Open Source + Paid)
  • E = Elasticsearch: search engine, indexes & stores logs
  • L = Logstash: pipeline for parsing, filtering, enriching logs
  • K = Kibana: web UI for searching, analytics, dashboards
  • Use case: Log-heavy environments, self-hosted, cost-sensitive
  • Pros: Flexible, handles TB+ of logs, full control
  • Cons: Complex setup, storage/CPU intensive, not great for metrics
  • Extra features: Metricbeat (metrics), Elastic APM (tracing)
Jaeger (Open Source, Tracing)
  • Purpose: Distributed tracing platform (request flow across services)
  • Not for metrics: Jaeger is specifically for traces, not metrics/logs
  • Architecture: Client → Agent → Collector → Elasticsearch/Cassandra storage
  • Compatible: Works with OpenTelemetry, W3C traceparent standard
  • Use case: Microservices debugging, latency analysis, call chain visibility
  • Deployment: All-in-one mode (dev), or distributed (production)
OpenTelemetry (Standard)
  • Not a tool: OpenTelemetry is a standard/specification for observability
  • Three signals: Traces (request flow), Metrics (numbers), Logs (events)
  • Language SDKs: Python, Go, Java, Node.js, .NET, C++, etc.
  • Instrumentation types: Automatic (agent), Manual (code), or Hybrid
  • Export to: Any backend (Prometheus, Jaeger, Datadog, Splunk, Azure Monitor, etc.)
  • Vendor-neutral: Not locked to one tool, future-proof
Datadog (Paid SaaS)
  • Full-stack: Metrics, logs, traces, APM, security all in one
  • Setup: Agent-based collection, auto-discovery for cloud (K8s, AWS, Azure)
  • UI: Unified dashboard, auto-correlation of metrics/logs/traces
  • Use case: Enterprise, multi-cloud, compliance, minimal ops wanted
  • Pricing: Per host/per GB ingested (expensive if not careful)
  • Pros: Minimal ops, integrated, powerful analytics
  • Cons: High cost ($$$), vendor lock-in, can get expensive fast
Dynatrace (Paid SaaS)
  • Focus: AI-powered APM and application monitoring
  • Auto-instrumentation: OneAgent (auto-discovers apps, minimal config)
  • PurePath technology: Traces every transaction, shows dependency map
  • Use case: Large enterprises, application-centric monitoring, digital experience
  • Features: Automatic problem detection, AI-driven anomalies, RUM (real user monitoring)
  • Pricing: Expensive, consumption-based, good for large organizations
  • Pros: Deep insights, low configuration, no need to add code
  • Cons: High cost, complex UI, vendor lock-in
Quick Comparison Table
Tool Type Metrics Logs Traces Cost Setup Complexity
Prometheus + Grafana Self-hosted ✓ Strong ✗ No ✗ No Free Medium
ELK Self-hosted ✓ Via Metricbeat ✓ Strong ✓ Via APM Free High
Jaeger Self-hosted ✗ No ✗ No ✓ Only Free Medium
OpenTelemetry Standard ✓ Via SDK ✓ Via SDK ✓ Via SDK Free (SDKs) Low (SDK)
Datadog SaaS ✓ Strong ✓ Strong ✓ Strong $$$ Low
Dynatrace SaaS ✓ Strong ✓ Via Logs ✓ Strong $$$ Low
Selection Guide: Which to Choose?
Scenario Best Choice Why
Kubernetes + microservices, budget tight Prometheus + Grafana Free, CNCF standard, cloud-native
Lots of logs to process, self-hosted ELK Stack Flexible, handles TB+ logs, full control
Need distributed tracing only Jaeger Lightweight, open-source, traces-focused
Instrument apps, stay vendor-neutral OpenTelemetry Not a tool, standards-based, portable
All-in-one SaaS, multi-cloud Datadog Integrated platform, easy setup, auto-discovery
Enterprise APM, deep app insights Dynatrace AI-powered, auto-instrumentation, dependency maps
Say this in interview "Prometheus+Grafana for self-hosted, cost-effective Kubernetes. ELK for log-heavy environments. Jaeger specifically for distributed tracing. OpenTelemetry for vendor-neutral instrumentation (not a tool, a standard). Datadog for all-in-one SaaS, multi-cloud integration. Dynatrace for enterprise APM with AI-driven insights. Choose based on: budget (free vs SaaS), signals needed (metrics/logs/traces), self-hosted vs managed, and team expertise."
Monitoring Fundamentals

What are SLIs, SLOs, and SLAs? How do they relate to monitoring?

SLI/SLO/SLA form the foundation of reliability monitoring. They define what success looks like and drive alerting strategy.

Q&AReliability metrics›
SLI (Service Level Indicator)
  • Measured metric: actual performance data
  • Examples: 99.5% requests under 200ms, 99.9% availability
  • What you measure
SLO (Service Level Objective)
  • Target for the SLI: the goal you set
  • Example: "achieve 99.9% uptime" (target based on SLI)
  • Drives alerting: if actual < SLO, alert
  • What you aim for
SLA (Service Level Agreement)
  • Contract/business commitment: consequences if missed
  • Example: "99.99% availability or customer gets refund"
  • Contractual + financial impact
  • What you promise
How They Relate to Monitoring
  • 1. Define SLIs: choose what to measure (latency, errors, availability)
  • 2. Set SLOs: target for each SLI (e.g., p95 latency < 100ms)
  • 3. Monitor & Alert: track actual SLI vs SLO in real-time
  • 4. Error Budget: if SLO = 99.9% uptime, you have 0.1% "errors" allowed
  • 5. Remediation: if approaching error budget limit, escalate/page on-call
Error Budget Concept
  • SLO of 99.9% = 0.1% downtime allowed per month = ~43 seconds/month
  • If you burn error budget too fast, slow deployments/changes
  • Alerting threshold: set to warn before you burn all budget
Common SLOs for Services
  • Availability: 99% (2.88 hrs/month down), 99.9% (43 min), 99.99% (4.3 min)
  • Latency: p95 < 100ms, p99 < 500ms
  • Error rate: < 0.1% errors
Say this in interview "SLI = what you measure (e.g., latency), SLO = your target (e.g., p95 < 100ms), SLA = contract (e.g., 99.9% uptime or refund). Monitoring tracks SLI vs SLO. Error budget = allowed downtime; if burning too fast, pause changes and focus on stability. Alerting fires when SLI approaches SLO threshold."
Monitoring Fundamentals

What are the best practices for monitoring in microservices architectures?

Microservices introduce complexity. Distributed tracing, correlation, and service dependency mapping are critical.

Q&AMicroservices strategy›
Instrument Every Service
  • Add application metrics: request count, latency, errors
  • Use OpenTelemetry or language-specific libs
  • Export to central Prometheus/Datadog
Distributed Tracing
  • Trace requests across all services (Request ID propagation)
  • Use Jaeger, Zipkin, or managed solutions
  • Correlate logs using trace ID
  • Identify slow/failing service in the call chain
Centralized Logging
  • All services log to central repository (ELK, Splunk, Datadog)
  • Include trace ID, service name, instance ID in every log
  • Query logs by trace ID to correlate across services
Service Dependency Mapping
  • Auto-discover dependencies (service mesh or tracing)
  • Build service topology map
  • Know: Service A calls Service B calls Database
RED Method (for services)
  • Rate: requests per second
  • Errors: failed requests %
  • Duration: latency (p50, p95, p99)
  • Alert on deviation from baseline
Correlation & Context
  • Propagate trace ID through all logs/metrics
  • Add context: user ID, request type, etc.
  • Single Trace ID ties together: metrics, logs, traces
Monitor Dependencies
  • Alert on: service timeouts, retries, circuit breakers
  • Watch downstream service health (if Service B is down, alert)
  • Cascading failures: detect when failure ripples
