Say this in interview "Microservices: instrument each service, use distributed tracing (Jaeger) to see request flow, centralize logs with trace IDs, build dependency maps. Monitor RED metrics (rate/errors/duration) per service. Alert on latency spikes and error rate. Use correlation to link logs/metrics/traces by trace ID—critical for debugging cross-service issues."
Monitoring 02

Prometheus

Prometheus

How do you set up Prometheus scrape configs for monitoring targets?

Prometheus scrape configs define what metrics to collect and from where. Understanding YAML structure and service discovery is essential.

Q&AScrape configuration›
Basic Scrape Config Structure
global:
  scrape_interval: 15s
  evaluation_interval: 15s

scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'prometheus'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['localhost:9090']

  - job_name: 'node-exporter'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['192.168.1.10:9100', '192.168.1.11:9100']
Key Scrape Config Fields
  • job_name: identifier for this job (added as label)
  • scrape_interval: how often to scrape (default 15s)
  • scrape_timeout: timeout for each scrape (default 10s)
  • metrics_path: HTTP path for metrics (default /metrics)
  • scheme: http or https (default http)
  • static_configs: hardcoded list of targets
Service Discovery (Dynamic)
  • Instead of static IPs, use kubernetes_sd_config
  • Auto-discover K8s pods, nodes, services
  • Example: discover all pods with label app=api
  • Other SD: consul_sd_config, ec2_sd_config, azure_sd_config
Kubernetes Service Discovery Example
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'kubernetes-pods'
    kubernetes_sd_config:
      - role: pod
    relabel_configs:
      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_scrape]
        action: keep
        regex: 'true'
      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_path]
        action: replace
        target_label: __metrics_path__
        regex: (.+)
      - source_labels: [__address__, __meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_port]
        action: replace
        regex: ([^:]+)(?::\d+)?;(\d+)
        replacement: $1:$2
        target_label: __address__
Relabeling (Filtering & Enriching)
  • keep action: only scrape if condition matches
  • drop action: skip scraping if matches
  • replace action: modify labels
  • Example: keep only pods with scrape: "true" annotation
Common Targets to Scrape
  • Node Exporter (node_exporter): CPU, memory, disk, network
  • Kube-State-Metrics: K8s object state (pods, deployments)
  • Kubelet: K8s node metrics (container CPU, memory)
  • Application metrics: /metrics endpoint (custom counters, gauges)
  • Database exporters: MySQL, PostgreSQL, MongoDB exporter
Say this in interview "Prometheus scrape_configs: static_configs for hardcoded targets, or kubernetes_sd_config for auto-discovery. Use relabel_configs to filter (keep/drop) and enrich targets. Example: scrape pods with prometheus.io/scrape: 'true' annotation. Common targets: node-exporter, kube-state-metrics, kubelet, app metrics. Default scrape_interval 15s, metrics_path /metrics."
Monitoring 03

Logging

Logging

What are the differences between structured logging and unstructured logging?

Structured logging is queryable and machine-parseable. Unstructured is free-form text. Know the trade-offs.

Q&ALog formats›
Unstructured Logging
  • Free-form text: "User john logged in from 192.168.1.1 at 2024-01-15 10:30:45"
  • Fast to write, requires parsing to search
  • Hard to query programmatically
  • Typical format: plain text, printf-style logs
Structured Logging
  • JSON or key-value: {"user":"john", "ip":"192.168.1.1", "action":"login", "timestamp":"2024-01-15T10:30:45Z"}
  • Machine-parseable, queryable fields
  • Enables filtering: all logins from IP X, or by user ID, timestamp range
  • Better for aggregation, correlation, analytics
Structured: JSON Example
{
  "timestamp": "2024-01-15T10:30:45.123Z",
  "level": "ERROR",
  "service": "api-server",
  "trace_id": "abc123def456",
  "user_id": "john",
  "event": "database_connection_failed",
  "duration_ms": 2500,
  "error": "connection timeout",
  "tags": ["database", "critical"]
}
Benefits of Structured Logging
  • Query by field: find all errors from service X
  • Correlation: link logs by trace_id across services
  • Aggregation: count errors, calculate percentiles
  • Alerting: alert if error_count > threshold in last 5 min
  • Analytics: trending, dashboards
Challenges with Structured
  • Requires discipline: developers must add relevant fields
  • Schema evolution: adding new fields needs thought
  • Slightly more verbose (JSON overhead)
Best Practice Hybrid
  • Use structured logging (JSON)
  • Include: timestamp, level, service, trace_id, user_id, action, error, tags
  • Include a "message" field for human-readable context
  • Log to stdout/stderr, let container orchestration forward to ELK
Say this in interview "Structured logging (JSON) is better: queryable, correlatable by trace_id, enables alerting and dashboards. Unstructured is free-form text—fast to write but hard to query. Best: use JSON with timestamp, level, service, trace_id, user_id, action fields. Include message for context. Send to ELK/Splunk for aggregation."
Logging

How do you send logs to ELK from applications?

ELK ingests from multiple sources. Know the pipeline: logging library → stdout → agent → Logstash → Elasticsearch.

Q&AELK integration›
Architecture Overview
  • Application → Logging Library (log4j, winston, pino) → stdout/file
  • Container/Agent (Filebeat, Logstash) → reads logs
  • Logstash → parses, enriches, filters logs
  • Elasticsearch → stores indexed logs
  • Kibana → UI for searching and dashboards
Option 1: Direct to Elasticsearch (Simple)
  • Application sends JSON logs via HTTP to Elasticsearch
  • Pros: direct, no middle layer
  • Cons: app dependency on ES, no parsing, less resilient
  • Library: es-client or HTTP client
Option 2: Stdout + Filebeat (Kubernetes)
  • App logs to stdout (JSON format)
  • Container runtime captures stdout
  • Filebeat daemonset reads /var/log/containers/*.log
  • Filebeat → Elasticsearch (or Logstash)
  • Pros: decoupled, cloud-native, resilient
Option 3: Logstash Pipeline
  • App → stdout or UDP/TCP
  • Filebeat/Logstash → Input (file, TCP, Kafka)
  • Logstash Filter: parse JSON, add fields, drop sensitive data
  • Logstash Output: send to Elasticsearch
  • Pros: flexible parsing, can aggregate multiple sources
Example: Node.js App with Winston Logger
const winston = require('winston');
const logger = winston.createLogger({
  format: winston.format.json(),
  transports: [
    new winston.transports.Console() // stdout
  ]
});

logger.info('User login', {
  userId: 'john',
  traceId: 'abc123',
  ip: '192.168.1.1'
});

// Output: {"level":"info","message":"User login","userId":"john","traceId":"abc123","ip":"192.168.1.1","timestamp":"2024-01-15T10:30:45.123Z"}
Filebeat Configuration
filebeat.inputs:
- type: container
  enabled: true
  paths:
    - '/var/lib/docker/containers/*/*.log'

processors:
  - add_kubernetes_metadata: ~

output.elasticsearch:
  hosts: ["elasticsearch:9200"]
  index: "filebeat-%{+yyyy.MM.dd}"
Best Practices
  • Log to stdout, not files (container-native)
  • Use JSON format for easy parsing
  • Include trace_id, service name, level in every log
  • Use Filebeat for K8s (lighter than Logstash)
  • Set ES retention policy (e.g., 7 days, or index rollover)
  • Avoid logging PII, passwords, API keys
Say this in interview "Best practice: app logs JSON to stdout → Filebeat daemonset reads container logs → sends to Elasticsearch. Include trace_id, service, level, message. For complex parsing use Logstash. Set retention policy. Kibana for searching. Avoid logging PII. In Kubernetes: Filebeat daemonset + Elasticsearch StatefulSet + Kibana Service."
Logging

What are log levels? (DEBUG, INFO, WARN, ERROR, FATAL)

Log levels control verbosity and alert severity. Know when to use each.

Q&ALog severity›
DEBUG (lowest)
  • Detailed info for developers, variable values, function calls
  • Example: "Entering function getUserById with id=123"
  • Usually disabled in production (too verbose)
  • Enable only when troubleshooting
INFO
  • General info about app flow, milestones
  • Example: "Service started", "Request processed", "User logged in"
  • Normal level in production
  • Helps understand what the system is doing
WARN
  • Something unexpected, but app continues
  • Example: "Deprecated API used", "Retry attempt 2/3", "Cache miss for key X"
  • Not an error, but noteworthy
  • May need attention
ERROR
  • Something failed, but app might recover
  • Example: "Database connection failed", "Request timeout", "Invalid input"
  • Requires attention, alert should fire
  • Action: log details for debugging
FATAL / CRITICAL (highest)
  • App cannot continue, immediate shutdown
  • Example: "Out of memory", "Panic", "Unrecoverable state"
  • Page on-call immediately
  • Post-mortem required
Log Level Hierarchy
  • DEBUG ≤ INFO ≤ WARN ≤ ERROR ≤ FATAL
  • If level set to WARN: DEBUG and INFO hidden, WARN/ERROR/FATAL shown
  • Production: usually set to INFO or WARN
Best Practices
  • INFO: log normal flow, user actions, deployments
  • WARN: retry attempts, deprecation, unusual conditions
  • ERROR: exceptions, failures, include stack trace
  • Include context: user_id, trace_id, duration for all levels
  • Avoid logging at ERROR+ in loops (can spam)
Say this in interview "Log levels: DEBUG (dev only), INFO (normal flow, user actions), WARN (unexpected but recoverable), ERROR (failures, needs attention), FATAL (crash, page on-call). Production: set to INFO/WARN. Include trace_id for correlation. Alert when ERROR+ is high. Avoid ERROR in loops."
Logging

Explain log aggregation tools: ELK, Splunk, Datadog, New Relic

Each tool has different strengths. Know when to pick which.

Q&ATool comparison›
ELK Stack (Elasticsearch, Logstash, Kibana)
  • Open source, self-hosted
  • Elasticsearch: search engine, indexes logs
  • Logstash: pipeline for parsing, filtering, enriching
  • Kibana: dashboarding, searching UI
  • Best for: High log volume, full control, budget-conscious
  • Pros: flexible, free, can handle TB+ of logs
  • Cons: needs ops expertise, storage/CPU intensive, no native APM
Splunk (Enterprise)
  • Powerful log aggregation + analytics + SIEM
  • Indexed search, machine learning, threat detection
  • Best for: Large enterprises, compliance, security logs
  • Pros: powerful querying, built-in ML, audit trail
  • Cons: very expensive (licensing model per GB/day), complex, vendor lock-in
Datadog
  • Unified platform: logs + metrics + traces + APM
  • Agent-based collection, auto-discovery for K8s
  • Best for: Multi-cloud, integrated monitoring needed, managed solution
  • Pros: all-in-one, minimal ops, cloud-native, smart alerting
  • Cons: expensive (per host, per GB), steep bill if not careful
New Relic
  • APM + logs + traces, AI-driven anomaly detection
  • Best for: Application performance focus, developers first
  • Pros: auto-instrumentation, service maps, easy onboarding
  • Cons: billing per GB ingested (can add up), less flexible than Splunk
Comparison Table
Tool Cost Model Setup Logs + Metrics + Traces Best For
ELK Free (ops cost) Complex Logs only Cost-sensitive, full control
Splunk $$$ (per GB/day) Complex Logs + some metrics Enterprise, SIEM, compliance
Datadog $$$ (per host/GB) Easy (SaaS) All three Integrated monitoring, cloud-first
New Relic $$$ (per GB) Easy (SaaS) All three APM focus, developers
Selection Criteria
  • Budget tight? → ELK (self-hosted)
  • All-in-one monitoring? → Datadog or New Relic
  • Security/compliance requirement? → Splunk
  • Want simplicity? → Datadog (SaaS, less ops)
  • Full control, cloud-native? → ELK + Prometheus
Say this in interview "ELK: free, self-hosted, need ops skills. Splunk: enterprise, SIEM, expensive. Datadog: all-in-one SaaS, easy, costly. New Relic: APM-focused, per-GB billing. Pick ELK if budget is tight and team can ops it. Pick Datadog if you want integrated logs/metrics/traces and accept SaaS cost. Splunk for regulatory compliance."
Monitoring 04

Distributed Tracing

Distributed Tracing

Explain OpenTelemetry and instrumentation

OpenTelemetry is the standard for instrumenting applications. It enables tracing, metrics, and logs from any app.

Q&AOTel basics›
What is OpenTelemetry?
  • Standard framework for observability (tracing, metrics, logs)
  • Vendor-neutral: works with any backend (Jaeger, Datadog, New Relic, etc.)
  • Spec + libraries for multiple languages (Python, Go, Node, Java)
  • Replaces older tools like Zipkin, Jaeger specific instrumentation
Three Signals: Traces, Metrics, Logs
  • Traces: request flow across services, latency per service
  • Metrics: counters, gauges, histograms (CPU, memory, throughput)
  • Logs: structured or unstructured events
  • All three linked by trace_id for correlation
Instrumentation Types
  • Automatic: agent or library auto-instruments (no code change)
  • Manual: developer adds tracing code explicitly
  • Hybrid: auto + manual combined
Manual Instrumentation (Python)
from opentelemetry import trace

tracer = trace.get_tracer(__name__)

def process_order(order_id):
  with tracer.start_as_current_span("process_order") as span:
    span.set_attribute("order_id", order_id)
    # business logic
    span.add_event("order_validated")
    # more logic
Automatic Instrumentation (Java)
  • OpenTelemetry Java agent: java -javaagent:opentelemetry-javaagent.jar app.jar
  • Auto-instruments HTTP, database, messaging libraries
  • No code changes needed
Exporting Traces
  • OpenTelemetry Collector: agent that receives, processes, exports telemetry
  • Exporters: OTLP (standard), Jaeger, Zipkin, Datadog, etc.
  • Example: app → OTel SDK → Collector → Jaeger/Datadog backend
OTel Collector Architecture
  • Receiver: accepts telemetry (OTLP, Jaeger, Prometheus)
  • Processor: batch, sample, filter, enrich
  • Exporter: send to backend (Jaeger, Prometheus, Datadog)
Trace Propagation (Context)
  • Trace ID + Span ID propagated via HTTP headers (W3C traceparent)
  • Example: traceparent: 00-0af7651916cd43dd8448eb211c80319c-b7ad6b7169203331-01
  • Service B reads header, continues trace under same Trace ID
  • Enables correlation across all services
Say this in interview "OpenTelemetry: vendor-neutral standard for traces, metrics, logs. Automatic instrumentation via agent (no code), or manual (add span/attribute code). Export via OTel Collector to any backend (Jaeger, Datadog, etc.). Traces propagated via W3C traceparent header for cross-service correlation. OTel Collector: receiver → processor → exporter pattern."
Distributed Tracing

What is Jaeger? How does it work?

Jaeger is the open-source distributed tracing platform. Understand the architecture and query model.

Q&AJaeger architecture›
What is Jaeger?
  • Open-source distributed tracing system (CNCF)
  • Monitors microservices, shows request flow across services
  • Identifies slow/failing services in a call chain
  • Compatible with OpenTelemetry
Jaeger Architecture
  • Jaeger Client: sends spans from app (OpenTelemetry SDK)
  • Jaeger Agent: UDP daemon receives spans, batches them
  • Jaeger Collector: receives, processes, stores spans
  • Storage Backend: Elasticsearch or Cassandra (indexes spans)
  • Query Service: UI for searching traces
Flow: Request to Trace Visualization
  • 1. Request enters Service A, generates Trace ID
  • 2. Service A: code creates spans (work units), sends to Jaeger Agent
  • 3. Request → Service B: Trace ID in headers, B continues same trace
  • 4. Service B creates spans, sends to Jaeger Agent
  • 5. All spans → Jaeger Collector → indexed in Elasticsearch
  • 6. Jaeger UI: query by Trace ID, shows waterfall: A→B timing
Jaeger Terms: Trace, Span
  • Trace: entire request flow (one Trace ID)
  • Span: single operation (function call, DB query, HTTP request)
  • Trace = collection of spans linked by Trace ID + parent Span ID
  • Example Trace: A:Query→B:Fetch→DB:Select (3 spans)
Deployment Options
  • All-in-one: dev/testing, single container, embedded storage
  • Production: separate Collector + Storage (Elasticsearch, Cassandra)
  • Kubernetes: Jaeger operator automates deployment
Example: Python with Jaeger
from opentelemetry import trace
from opentelemetry.exporter.jaeger.thrift import JaegerExporter
from opentelemetry.sdk.trace import TracerProvider
from opentelemetry.sdk.trace.export import SimpleSpanProcessor

jaeger_exporter = JaegerExporter(
  agent_host_name="jaeger-agent",
  agent_port=6831,
)
trace.set_tracer_provider(TracerProvider())
trace.get_tracer_provider().add_span_processor(SimpleSpanProcessor(jaeger_exporter))

tracer = trace.get_tracer(__name__)
with tracer.start_as_current_span("my_operation"):
  # do work
  pass
Say this in interview "Jaeger: open-source distributed tracing. App sends spans to Jaeger Agent → Collector → indexed in Elasticsearch. Span = operation, Trace = request flow (linked by Trace ID). Jaeger UI: query by Trace ID, shows waterfall diagram of latency per service. Identify slow services, debug cross-service issues. Compatible with OpenTelemetry."
Distributed Tracing

Explain trace sampling: when do you sample traces?

Sampling reduces storage costs. Know the strategies and when to sample vs not.

Q&ASampling strategies›
Why Sample?
  • High-traffic systems: 10k+ RPS → millions of traces/second
  • Storing all traces = massive storage costs, slow queries
  • Sampling = keep percentage of traces, representative sample
Sampling Strategies
  • Always: sample=100%, all traces stored (for low traffic)
  • Never: sample=0%, no traces stored (debugging only)
  • Probabilistic: sample 10% randomly (1 in 10 traces)
  • Rate-based: max N traces/second (adaptive)
  • Decision-based: sample if error_rate > threshold, or duration > 1s
Probabilistic Sampling
  • Random 10% of all requests
  • Pro: simple, uniform distribution
  • Con: might miss rare errors if unlucky
  • Trade-off: storage vs visibility
Priority/Decision-based Sampling
  • Sample 100% of errors (errors_sampled = true)
  • Sample 100% if duration > 5 seconds (slow requests)
  • Sample 10% of normal/fast requests
  • Pro: catch problems, reduce storage for normal requests
Example: OpenTelemetry Sampler
from opentelemetry.sdk.trace.sampling import (
    TraceIdRatioBased,
)
from opentelemetry.sdk.trace import TracerProvider

sampler = TraceIdRatioBased(0.10)  # 10% of traces
tracer_provider = TracerProvider(sampler=sampler)
trace.set_tracer_provider(tracer_provider)
When to Sample
  • Development: no sampling (100%), catch all issues
  • Low-traffic staging: no sampling (100%)
  • Production low RPS (<100): 100% (storage not issue)
  • Production high RPS (>1000): 10-20% (probabilistic)
  • Production high RPS + errors: 100% errors, 5-10% normal
Trace Context Propagation with Sampling
  • Sampling decision made at entry (Service A)
  • Propagated via headers: trace-flags: sampled=true
  • Service B respects parent's sampling decision
  • Result: entire trace (A→B→C) has consistent sampling
Say this in interview "Sampling reduces storage costs. Probabilistic: 10% of all traces. Decision-based: 100% errors, duration > 5s, rest 10%. Dev: 100%, Prod low RPS: 100%, Prod high RPS: 10-20%. Sample decision made at entry point, propagated via trace headers so all services in call chain respect it. Use priority sampling for errors."
Distributed Tracing

How do you correlate traces with logs and metrics?

Correlation via trace ID is critical for debugging. All three signals must share the same ID.

Q&ASignal correlation›
The Correlation Problem
  • Request fails in microservices: which service is slow?
  • Traces show flow, logs show errors, metrics show CPU spike
  • Without correlation, debugging is hard (find logs for same request?)
  • Solution: use same Trace ID across traces, logs, metrics
Trace ID as Universal Identifier
  • Generated at request entry (Service A)
  • Propagated via HTTP headers: traceparent: 00-{TRACE_ID}-{SPAN_ID}-{FLAGS}
  • Each service logs with same trace_id
  • Metrics tagged with trace_id
  • All three signals (traces, logs, metrics) linked by trace_id
Implementation: Trace ID in Logs
  • Application logging library extracts trace_id from context
  • Every log includes trace_id field
  • Example log: {"trace_id":"abc123", "service":"api", "message":"order processed"}
  • ELK/Datadog: query logs by trace_id → all related logs
Implementation: Trace ID in Metrics
  • Add trace_id as label/tag to metrics
  • Example: requests_total{trace_id="abc123", service="api"} 1
  • Prometheus query: get all metrics for specific trace_id
  • See CPU, memory, latency during that specific request
Example: Tracing Flow
  • 1. Client request → Service A, generate Trace ID = "abc123"
  • 2. Service A log: {"trace_id":"abc123", "event":"request_received"}
  • 3. Service A metric: http_request_duration{trace_id="abc123"} 45ms
  • 4. Service A calls Service B, passes traceparent header with trace_id
  • 5. Service B log: {"trace_id":"abc123", "event":"processing"}
  • 6. Service B metric: db_query_duration{trace_id="abc123"} 120ms
  • 7. Query by trace_id in Datadog: see all logs + metrics for this request
Observability Platform Integration
  • Datadog: auto-correlates traces/logs/metrics by trace_id
  • New Relic: similar auto-correlation
  • Jaeger + ELK: manual correlation (link by trace_id in UI)
  • Click on trace in Jaeger → see related logs in ELK with same trace_id
Best Practices
  • Use OpenTelemetry SDK for auto trace ID generation
  • Configure logging library to extract trace_id from context
  • Tag metrics with trace_id (for APM platforms)
  • Propagate trace_id in RPC/HTTP headers automatically
  • Test: verify same trace_id appears in traces, logs, metrics
Say this in interview "Correlation via Trace ID: generated at request entry, propagated via traceparent header. Include trace_id in every log and metric. Query by trace_id to see traces + logs + metrics for single request. Identifies which service is slow/failing. Use OpenTelemetry SDK for auto propagation. Datadog/New Relic auto-correlate; with Jaeger+ELK link manually."
Monitoring 05

Infrastructure

Infrastructure Monitoring

How do you monitor cloud infrastructure? (AWS CloudWatch, Azure Monitor, GCP Stackdriver)

Each cloud provider has native monitoring. Know the models and export options.

Q&ACloud monitoring›
Azure Monitor (Your Focus)
  • Native Azure monitoring service
  • Components: Metrics, Logs (Log Analytics), Application Insights (APM)
  • Auto-collects metrics for: VMs, App Services, AKS, databases
  • Metrics stored in metrics database, queryable via Azure Portal or REST API
Azure Monitor: Metrics
  • CPU%, memory, disk, network I/O for VMs
  • Request count, response time for App Services
  • Pod count, node CPU, memory for AKS
  • 1-minute granularity (default)
  • Query via Azure Metrics Explorer or REST API
Azure Monitor: Logs (Log Analytics)
  • Centralized log storage, KQL (Kusto Query Language) for querying
  • Ingest logs from: VMs, App Services, AKS containers, custom sources
  • Retention: configurable (default 30 days free tier)
  • Pricing: per GB ingested
Azure Monitor: Application Insights (APM)
  • APM for app monitoring: requests, exceptions, dependencies
  • Auto-instruments: .NET, Java, Node.js, Python
  • App Map: visualize service dependencies
  • Smart detection: anomalies in error rate, latency
Azure Monitor: Alerting
  • Metric Alerts: if CPU > 80% for 5 min, alert
  • Log Alerts: if error_count > 10 in last hour, alert
  • Action Groups: email, SMS, webhook, runbook
AWS CloudWatch (Comparison)
  • Native AWS monitoring, auto-collects metrics
  • Metrics: CloudWatch Metrics (EC2, RDS, Lambda, etc.)
  • Logs: CloudWatch Logs (centralized log aggregation)
  • Pricing: per metric, per GB logs
  • CloudWatch Insights: SQL-like querying of logs
GCP Stackdriver / Cloud Monitoring
  • Native GCP monitoring
  • Metrics: Cloud Monitoring (Compute Engine, Kubernetes, App Engine)
  • Logs: Cloud Logging (centralized logs)
  • Trace: Cloud Trace (distributed tracing, OpenTelemetry compatible)
  • Pricing: per metric, per GB logs
Cross-Cloud: Export to Prometheus/ELK
  • Azure Monitor: export metrics via metric exporter to Prometheus
  • AWS CloudWatch: use Prometheus CloudWatch exporter
  • GCP: use Prometheus GCP exporter
  • Why: unified multi-cloud dashboarding in Grafana
Recommendation
  • Single cloud? Use native (Azure Monitor for Azure)
  • Multi-cloud? Use Prometheus + Grafana (export metrics from each cloud)
  • Enterprise unified platform? Datadog, New Relic (ingest from all clouds)
Say this in interview "Azure Monitor: native, auto-collects metrics/logs/APM. Metrics Explorer, Log Analytics (KQL), Application Insights. Alerts via Action Groups. AWS has CloudWatch, GCP has Cloud Monitoring. For single cloud: use native. For multi-cloud: export to Prometheus/Grafana or use Datadog/New Relic. Know the pricing: Azure per-GB, AWS per-metric."
Infrastructure Monitoring

How do you monitor Kubernetes clusters?

Kubernetes monitoring is multi-layered: node metrics, pod metrics, API server, and etcd. Know the components.

Q&AK8s monitoring stack›
Kubernetes Monitoring Layers
  • Cluster: control plane health (API server, etcd, scheduler)
  • Nodes: CPU, memory, disk, network utilization
  • Pods: container CPU, memory usage, restart count
  • Applications: custom app metrics (requests, errors)
Core Monitoring Components
  • Kubelet metrics-server: collects node and pod metrics
  • Kube-state-metrics: exposes K8s object state (deployment replicas, pod status)
  • Node-exporter: exposes node-level metrics (CPU, disk, network)
  • Prometheus: scrapes and stores metrics
  • Grafana: visualize dashboards
Metrics-Server (Kubelet)
  • Deployed in kube-system namespace
  • Collects metrics from kubelet endpoint on each node
  • Powers: kubectl top nodes, kubectl top pods, HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler)
  • Metrics: CPU, memory per pod/node
  • Install: kubectl apply -f https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/metrics-server/releases/latest/download/components.yaml
Kube-State-Metrics
  • Exposes state of K8s objects as Prometheus metrics
  • Examples: kube_deployment_status_replicas (actual), kube_deployment_spec_replicas (desired)
  • Alert on: deployment replicas not matching desired (faulty rollout)
  • Install: helm install kube-state-metrics prometheus-community/kube-state-metrics
Node-Exporter (System Metrics)
  • Daemonset on every node, exposes: CPU, memory, disk, network
  • Metrics: node_cpu_seconds_total, node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes
  • Prometheus scrapes from each node-exporter instance
  • Install: helm install node-exporter prometheus-community/prometheus-node-exporter
Monitoring Setup (Helm)
helm repo add prometheus-community https://prometheus-community.github.io/helm-charts
helm repo update

# Install kube-prometheus-stack (includes Prometheus, Grafana, node-exporter, kube-state-metrics)
helm install prometheus prometheus-community/kube-prometheus-stack \
  -n monitoring --create-namespace

# Access Grafana
kubectl port-forward -n monitoring svc/prometheus-grafana 3000:80
# username: admin, password: prom-operator
Key Kubernetes Alerts
  • Node not ready: node_status == NotReady
  • Pod crash loop: container_last_seen > 5 min ago
  • Deployment replicas mismatch: desired != actual
  • PVC usage high: kubelet_volume_stats_used_bytes / capacity > 85%
  • API server latency: apiserver_request_duration_seconds p99 > 1s
Say this in interview "K8s monitoring: metrics-server (kubelet metrics), kube-state-metrics (object state), node-exporter (system metrics), Prometheus (TSDB), Grafana (UI). Install via helm: kube-prometheus-stack gets all. Key metrics: node status, pod CPU/memory, deployment replicas, PVC usage. Alert on: node NotReady, replica mismatch, API server latency, PVC full."
Infrastructure Monitoring

What is kubelet metrics endpoint?

The kubelet metrics endpoint exposes node and pod metrics. Understanding what's available is essential.

Q&AKubelet metrics›
What is Kubelet?
  • Node agent running on every Kubernetes node
  • Manages pods: start, stop, health checks
  • Exposes metrics endpoint at: http://{node-ip}:10250/metrics
  • Also exposes: /metrics/cadvisor (container metrics), /metrics/probes (probe latencies)
Kubelet Metrics Endpoints
  • /metrics: kubelet operational metrics (API latency, pod lifecycle events)
  • /metrics/cadvisor: container resource usage (CPU, memory, disk, network per container)
  • /metrics/probes: liveness/readiness probe latencies
  • Default port: 10250 (HTTPS, requires auth)
Example Metrics from /metrics/cadvisor
  • container_cpu_usage_seconds_total: cumulative CPU time (calculate rate)
  • container_memory_usage_bytes: current memory usage
  • container_fs_usage_bytes: filesystem usage per container
  • container_network_receive_bytes_total: network input
  • Each metric labeled with pod_name, pod_namespace, container_name
Example Metrics from /metrics
  • kubelet_node_name: name of the node
  • kubelet_pod_start_duration_seconds: how long it takes to start a pod
  • kubelet_pod_worker_duration_seconds: pod lifecycle processing time
  • kubelet_runtime_operations_duration_seconds: container runtime operations (pull image, create container)
Querying Kubelet Metrics
  • Prometheus scrapes kubelet /metrics/cadvisor
  • Query example: rate(container_cpu_usage_seconds_total[5m]) (CPU % over 5 min)
  • Query example: container_memory_usage_bytes (current memory)
  • Filter by pod_name, pod_namespace to get specific pod metrics
Accessing Kubelet Metrics
# Port-forward to kubelet on a node
kubectl port-forward node/{node-name} 10250:10250 -n kube-system

# Query metrics
curl https://localhost:10250/metrics/cadvisor -k --insecure

# From Prometheus: Prometheus scrape_config for kubelet
- job_name: 'kubelet'
  scheme: https
  tls_config:
    insecure_skip_verify: true
  kubernetes_sd_config:
    - role: node
  relabel_configs:
    - source_labels: [__address__]
      regex: ([^:]+)(?::\d+)?
      replacement: ${1}:10250
      target_label: __address__
Metrics-Server vs Kubelet Metrics
  • Metrics-server: reads kubelet /metrics endpoint, aggregates, powers kubectl top
  • Kubelet /metrics/cadvisor: raw metrics, all containers including system pods
  • Prometheus scrapes kubelet directly for raw data
Say this in interview "Kubelet exposes metrics at /metrics and /metrics/cadvisor (port 10250). cadvisor: container CPU/memory/disk/network. metrics: kubelet operations. Prometheus scrapes kubelet for raw metrics, powers HPA decisions. Query: rate(container_cpu_usage[5m]) for CPU %, container_memory_usage_bytes for memory. Metrics-server reads kubelet, aggregates, powers kubectl top."
Monitoring 06

Troubleshooting

Troubleshooting

How do you troubleshoot missing metrics?

Metrics disappearing is a common issue. Know the diagnostic checklist.

Q&ADiagnostic steps›
Checklist: Is the Exporter Running?
  • Check pod/process status: kubectl get pods -n monitoring (if in K8s)
  • Check logs: kubectl logs -f {exporter-pod} (any errors?)
  • Is the exporter listening on expected port?
  • Example: node-exporter should listen on 9100
Checklist: Can Prometheus Reach the Exporter?
  • Check Prometheus targets: visit Prometheus UI → Status → Targets
  • Look for red "DOWN" status: target is unreachable
  • Common reason: network policy blocking, firewall, wrong IP:port
  • Debug: kubectl exec -it {prometheus-pod} -- curl http://{exporter-ip}:9100/metrics
Checklist: Scrape Config Error
  • Typo in job_name, targets, metrics_path?
  • Check prometheus.yml: scrape_configs section
  • Reload Prometheus after fix: curl -X POST http://prometheus:9090/-/reload
  • Check Prometheus logs for config errors
Checklist: Metrics Disabled or Missing Labels
  • Query in Prometheus: does the metric exist at all?
  • Visit Prometheus UI → Graph → search metric name
  • Check relabel_configs: are you dropping the target by accident?
  • Example: if relabel action:drop filters out your target
Checklist: Metrics Retention Exceeded
  • Prometheus has default retention of 15 days
  • Old metrics are deleted automatically
  • Check prometheus startup args: --storage.tsdb.retention.time=15d
  • If looking for very old data, data is gone (increase retention if needed)
Checklist: Application Not Exposing Metrics
  • App code doesn't create metrics (gauges, counters)
  • App exports to wrong port/path
  • Debug: curl http://{app}:8080/metrics (does it return Prometheus format?)
  • Fix: add instrumentation library (prometheus-client, micrometer, etc.)
Troubleshooting Workflow
  • 1. Query in Prometheus: does metric exist?
  • 2. Check Prometheus Targets page: is exporter UP?
  • 3. Check exporter logs: any errors?
  • 4. Can Prometheus curl the exporter endpoint directly?
  • 5. Check scrape_config: job_name, targets, metrics_path correct?
  • 6. Reload Prometheus config, check for errors
Say this in interview "Missing metrics checklist: (1) Exporter pod running? Check logs. (2) Prometheus can reach exporter? Check Targets page (UP/DOWN). (3) Scrape config correct? Reload Prometheus. (4) Metrics exist in query? Search in Prometheus UI. (5) Relabel rules dropping target? Check config. (6) App exposing /metrics? Curl it. Workflow: query → targets page → exporter logs → scrape config → reload."
Troubleshooting

How do you diagnose high cardinality problems in Prometheus?

High cardinality = too many unique label combinations. This explodes storage and query time.

Q&ACardinality explosion›
What is Cardinality?
  • Cardinality = number of unique label combinations for a metric
  • Example: request_duration_seconds{method="GET", path="/users", status="200"} = 1 series
  • If path has 1000 unique values → 1000 series (for each method/status combo)
  • High cardinality: millions of series → Prometheus slow/crash
Common Cardinality Mistakes
  • Using user_id as metric label (millions of users = millions of series)
  • Using request_id (every request unique)
  • Using timestamp as label
  • API path as label without limiting (hundreds of endpoints)
  • Database query parameters
Example: Cardinality Explosion
  • Metric: api_requests_total{method, endpoint, user_id, status}
  • Combinations: 10 methods × 50 endpoints × 1M users × 10 statuses = 5 billion series!
  • Storage: 5B series × 8 bytes = 40GB+ just for metadata
  • Query "sum(api_requests_total)" takes minutes or crashes
Diagnosing High Cardinality: Prometheus UI
  • Visit Prometheus: Status → TSDB Status
  • Shows: "Top metrics by series count"
  • Look for metrics with millions of series
  • Example output: http_requests_total 50M series (too high!)
Query to Find High Cardinality Metrics
  • PromQL: count({__name__=~".+"}) by (__name__) (series count per metric)
  • Sort by series count, find worst offenders
  • Drill down: count({metric_name=~".+"}) by (label_name) (series per label)
Fixing High Cardinality
  • Remove unbounded labels: don't label user_id, request_id, timestamp
  • Use metric relabeling: drop or limit label values
  • Use recording rules: aggregate high-cardinality metrics into low-cardinality
  • Example relabeling: drop label source_labels: [user_id]
Recording Rule Example
# High cardinality
api_requests_total{method, endpoint, user_id, status}

# Recording rule: aggregate by method + status only
- record: api:requests_by_method_status:5m
  expr: sum by (method, status) (rate(api_requests_total[5m]))
Label Guidelines
  • Good labels: job, instance, method, status, service, environment (low cardinality)
  • Bad labels: user_id, request_id, query_string, timestamp, trace_id
  • Rule of thumb: label cardinality < 100 per label
  • Total series for metric: label1_cardinality × label2_cardinality × ...
Say this in interview "Cardinality = series count. High cardinality: metrics with millions of series (user_id, request_id labels). Diagnose: Prometheus TSDB Status page → top metrics by series. Fix: remove unbounded labels, use relabeling rules to drop, aggregate via recording rules. Rule: each label < 100 cardinality. Total series ≤ 1M for health. User_id as label = cardinality explosion."
Troubleshooting

Explain monitoring blind spots and how to avoid them

Blind spots are gaps in observability. Know common ones and how to cover them.

Q&AObservability gaps›
What Are Blind Spots?
  • Areas of the system with no monitoring/visibility
  • When failures happen in blind spots, you have no data
  • Example: monitoring app metrics but not database locks
Common Blind Spots
  • Infrastructure: missing node-exporter, no disk usage alerts
  • Database: monitoring throughput but not connection exhaustion
  • External APIs: no monitoring of third-party API latency/errors
  • Batch jobs: cron jobs run, but no metrics/logs if failing
  • Cache layers: Redis hit rate, eviction not monitored
  • Message queues: queue depth, lag not tracked
  • DNS: DNS resolution failures silent
  • Network: packet loss, latency to external services
  • Security: no audit logging of administrative actions
Blind Spot: Database Connections
  • Monitor: active connections, connection pool utilization
  • Alert: if connections > 80% of max
  • Why: connection exhaustion causes app failures silently
  • Missing this → app hangs, looks like latency problem, actually connection pool empty
Blind Spot: External Dependency Latency
  • App calls third-party API (payment, SMS, email)
  • No monitoring of API latency/errors
  • API goes slow → your app slow, but you don't know why
  • Fix: instrument HTTP calls, measure API latency, timeout alerts
Blind Spot: Batch Jobs
  • Cron job runs: process_nightly_reports.sh
  • No success/failure metrics, no completion time tracking
  • Job fails, nobody notices (no alert)
  • Fix: emit success/failure metric, alert if job hasn't completed in 24h
Blind Spot: Cache Effectiveness
  • Redis/Memcached used, no hit rate monitoring
  • Cache full? Evicting too fast? No visibility
  • Application slows, could be cache misses
  • Fix: monitor cache hits/misses, eviction rate, memory usage
How to Avoid Blind Spots
  • 1. Map system architecture: all components, dependencies
  • 2. For each component: define key metrics (throughput, latency, errors)
  • 3. Add monitoring: instrument code, add exporters
  • 4. Define alerts for failure modes (connection pool exhaustion, timeout, lag)
  • 5. Test: simulate failures, verify alerts fire
  • 6. Retrospective: every incident, ask "did we have visibility?"
Coverage Checklist
  • Application: request rate, latency, errors ✓
  • Database: throughput, latency, connection pool, lock waits ✓
  • Cache: hit rate, eviction, memory ✓
  • Queue: depth, lag, consumer lag ✓
  • Infrastructure: CPU, memory, disk, network ✓
  • External APIs: latency, error rate, timeouts ✓
  • Security: login attempts, permission changes, audit logs ✓
  • Batch jobs: success/failure, duration ✓
Say this in interview "Blind spots: missing monitoring for critical components. Common: database connection pool, external API latency, cache hit rate, batch job success, message queue lag. Avoid by: (1) map architecture, (2) define key metrics per component, (3) instrument, (4) alert, (5) test alerts. Every incident: was this visible? If not, add monitoring. Use coverage checklist: app, DB, cache, queue, infra, external APIs, security."
Troubleshooting

How do you handle monitoring system failures?

When monitoring itself fails, you're blind. Have a plan.

Q&AMeta-monitoring›
Scenarios: Monitoring Failures
  • Prometheus down: can't scrape metrics, no alerting
  • Elasticsearch down: can't store/query logs
  • Jaeger down: can't see traces, no distributed tracing
  • Alertmanager down: alerts generated but not sent (email/Slack)
  • Database for metrics storage full: writes fail silently
Prevention: High Availability
  • Prometheus: run 2+ replicas, same scrape configs (duplicated data)
  • Elasticsearch: cluster of 3+ nodes, replicas, snapshot to S3
  • Alertmanager: cluster of 3 nodes for gossip protocol resilience
  • Storage: separate, disk-backed, not embedded in single node
Detection: Meta-Monitoring
  • Monitor the monitoring system itself
  • Alert if: Prometheus scrape fails, Elasticsearch errors, Alertmanager down
  • Example alerts: up{job="prometheus"} == 0 (Prometheus down), elasticsearch_cluster_health_status < 2 (cluster unhealthy)
Response: Fallback / Escalation
  • Prometheus down: use replica Prometheus for queries, failover
  • Alerts not sent: on-call gets paged by separate "watchdog" alert
  • No APM visibility: fall back to log analysis (ELK), manual investigation
  • Have runbook: what to do if monitoring fails
Watchdog Alert Pattern
  • Separate monitor: pings if Alertmanager sends test alert every 5 min
  • If test alert not received, send direct alert to on-call
  • Protects against: Alertmanager down, notification channel down
Example: Kubernetes HA Setup
Prometheus StatefulSet: 2 replicas, shared storage (PVC)
Alertmanager StatefulSet: 3 replicas, gossip protocol for HA
Elasticsearch cluster: 3+ nodes, index replication
Backup: daily snapshots to Azure Blob Storage
Mitigation Strategies
  • 1. HA: run multiple instances, replicate data
  • 2. Alerting on alerting: watch Alertmanager health
  • 3. Fallback: manual checks (curl endpoints, SSH to nodes) if monitoring down
  • 4. Documentation: runbook for each monitoring component failure
  • 5. Testing: simulate monitoring failure, verify fallback works
Say this in interview "Monitoring failures: Prometheus down → no metrics/alerts. Fix: HA (2+ Prometheus replicas, shared storage). Alertmanager down → alerts not sent (watchdog pattern: alert if alert not received every 5 min). Elasticsearch down → can't query logs (backup to S3). Meta-monitoring: alert on Prometheus up{}, Alertmanager up{}, ES cluster health. Runbook: what to do if monitoring fails (manual checks, fallback queries)."
Landing Zone 01

Fundamentals

Fundamentals

Real-World Azure Landing Zone Implementation

Enterprise approach: Infrastructure as Code with Terraform, peer-reviewed changes, security scanning, automated deployment pipeline.

ImplementationProduction approach›
How Azure Landing Zone is Managed in Production

In our organization, the Azure Landing Zone was managed entirely through Infrastructure as Code using Terraform. The Platform Engineering team maintained reusable Terraform modules for networking, RBAC, Azure Policy, monitoring, Key Vault, and other shared services. Changes were developed in feature branches and submitted through pull requests. The CI pipeline performed formatting, validation, linting, security scanning with Checkov, and generated a Terraform plan. After the plan was reviewed and approved, the changes were merged into the main branch, which triggered the CD pipeline. The deployment pipeline ran terraform init and terraform apply, using a remote backend in Azure Storage to manage the Terraform state. This process ensured all Landing Zone changes were version-controlled, peer-reviewed, auditable, and consistently deployed across environments.

Key Benefits of This Approach
  • Version-Controlled: Every change tracked in git (who, what, when, why)
  • Peer-Reviewed: 2+ approvals required before production deployment
  • Auditable: Complete audit trail (git history + approval records + deployment logs)
  • Security-Scanned: Checkov prevents hardcoded secrets, missing encryption, RBAC violations
  • Consistently Deployed: Same Terraform module = identical infrastructure every time
  • Automated: No manual steps (faster, fewer errors)
Say this in interview "In our organization, ALZ was fully managed through Terraform IaC. Platform Engineering maintained reusable modules. Workflow: feature branch → PR → CI pipeline (Checkov security scan, terraform plan) → peer review → merge → CD pipeline (terraform apply). State in Azure Storage remote backend. Result: all changes version-controlled, peer-reviewed, security-scanned, consistently deployed. Zero insecure configs reached production (Checkov caught 100% of issues). Complete audit trail for compliance."
Fundamentals

What is Azure Landing Zone?

Azure Landing Zone is a pre-configured cloud environment with security, governance, and operations built-in. It's the foundation every workload runs on.

Q&ADefinition & purpose›
What is ALZ?
  • Pre-configured Azure environment following Microsoft Cloud Adoption Framework (CAF)
  • Not just infrastructure (VNets). Comprehensive governance: subscriptions, policies, monitoring, security, operations
  • Provides secure, scalable foundation for enterprise workloads
  • Reduces time-to-market, improves security posture from day one
  • Repeatable patterns: deploy same template for multiple workloads
Common Misconception
  • ❌ Wrong: "ALZ is just VNets and networking"
  • ✓ Right: "ALZ is comprehensive governance model: subscriptions, network, security policies, monitoring, RBAC, compliance automated"
Three Layers of ALZ
  • Platform (Central): Hub VNet, firewall, identity services, monitoring, management
  • Landing Zones (Workload): Dedicated subscriptions per app, spoke VNets, RBAC per team
  • Management (Governance): Policies enforce standards, audit logs, compliance checks
Key Benefits
  • Security: Zero-trust network, centralized firewall, encryption enforced
  • Governance: Policies prevent bad configs, consistency across subscriptions
  • Scalability: Add subscriptions easily, all follow same patterns
  • Cost: Central billing, cost tracking per workload, budget alerts
  • Compliance: Audit trail (who did what, when), logging centralized, automated compliance checks
  • Operations: Monitoring, alerting, disaster recovery all pre-configured
Say this in interview "Azure Landing Zone is pre-configured enterprise environment following CAF. Not just networking—it's governance framework. Three layers: central platform (hub, firewall, identity), landing zones (workload subscriptions), management (policies, monitoring). Benefits: security by default, governance automated, scalable, compliance built-in. Deploy template once, repeat for 100s of workloads."
Fundamentals

Azure Landing Zone Implementation with Terraform (Real-World)

Best practice: manage entire ALZ through Infrastructure as Code. All changes version-controlled, peer-reviewed, automated deployment.

Q&AIaC-driven ALZ›
Platform Engineering Approach
  • Ownership: Platform Engineering team maintains reusable Terraform modules
  • Modules: Networking (VNet, NSG, UDR), RBAC, Azure Policy, Monitoring, Key Vault, Storage
  • Repository: Central repo with module library (git version control)
  • Workflow: Feature branch → Pull Request → Review → Approve → Merge → Deploy
Development Workflow: Feature Branch → PR
1. Platform engineer creates feature branch
   git checkout -b feat/alz-multi-region-networking

2. Develop Terraform modules
   ├─ networking/
   │  ├─ main.tf (VNet, subnets, NSGs)
   │  ├─ variables.tf
   │  └─ outputs.tf
   ├─ rbac/
   │  └─ main.tf (RBAC assignments)
   └─ policies/
      └─ main.tf (Azure Policy definitions)

3. Commit and push
   git commit -m "Add multi-region networking module"
   git push origin feat/alz-multi-region-networking

4. Create Pull Request
   └─ Description: What changed, why, which subscriptions affected
CI Pipeline: Validation & Security Scanning
GitHub Actions / Azure Pipelines triggers on PR:

Stage 1: Format Check
  ├─ terraform fmt -check
  └─ Ensures consistent code style

Stage 2: Validation
  ├─ terraform init
  ├─ terraform validate
  └─ Catches syntax errors before review

Stage 3: Linting
  ├─ tflint (Terraform linter)
  └─ Checks for best practices, style violations

Stage 4: Security Scanning (CRITICAL)
  ├─ Checkov scan
  │  ├─ Check: No hardcoded secrets (API keys, passwords)
  │  ├─ Check: Encryption enabled (storage, databases)
  │  ├─ Check: RBAC least-privilege
  │  ├─ Check: Public IPs not allowed
  │  └─ Check: NSGs properly configured
  ├─ Results: Pass/Fail with detailed report
  └─ If fail: PR blocked until fixed

Stage 5: Plan Generation
  ├─ terraform plan -out=tfplan
  ├─ Generates: what will be created/modified/deleted
  ├─ Output: human-readable plan file
  └─ Artifact: uploaded to PR (reviewers see exact changes)

Result: PR shows:
  ✓ Format: OK
  ✓ Validation: OK
  ✓ Linting: OK
  ✓ Security: OK (0 violations)
  ✓ Plan: 3 resources added, 1 modified, 0 destroyed
Code Review & Approval Process
PR Review (2+ approvers required):

Reviewer 1: Platform Lead
├─ Checks: Architecture, design, best practices
├─ Questions: Why these subnets? CIDR allocation correct?
├─ Approval: "Looks good, LGTM"
└─ Comments: Suggest improvements

Reviewer 2: Security Team
├─ Checks: Security controls (encryption, RBAC, NSG rules)
├─ Validates: Checkov findings addressed
├─ Questions: Why public? Ensure necessary
└─ Approval: "Security cleared"

Approval Checklist:
  ☑ CI pipeline all green
  ☑ 2+ approvals (no self-approval)
  ☑ No conflicts with main branch
  ☑ Plan reviewed and understood
  ☑ Tests passed (if applicable)

Result: All checks pass → Ready to merge
Merge & Trigger CD Pipeline
1. Merge PR to main branch
   └─ Requires approvals + all CI checks pass

2. Merge event triggers CD pipeline (automatic)
   └─ GitHub Actions / Azure Pipelines

3. CD Pipeline: Deployment to Azure

   Stage 1: Initialize
   ├─ terraform init \
   │  -backend-config="storage_account_name=platformterraform" \
   │  -backend-config="container_name=tfstate" \
   │  -backend-config="key=prod/terraform.tfstate"
   └─ Loads remote state from Azure Storage account

   Stage 2: Plan (validation)
   ├─ terraform plan -out=tfplan
   ├─ Compares: current state vs desired (main branch)
   └─ Output: exact changes to be applied

   Stage 3: Apply (deployment)
   ├─ terraform apply tfplan
   ├─ Creates/modifies/deletes resources in Azure
   ├─ Updates state file in Storage account
   └─ Duration: 5-15 min (parallel resource creation)

   Stage 4: Post-Deploy
   ├─ Run compliance checks (Azure Policy dashboard)
   ├─ Verify resources exist and are configured
   ├─ Send notification: "Deployment successful"
   └─ Update documentation

Result: Landing zone live in production
Terraform State Management (Critical)
  • Remote Backend: Azure Storage Account (not local laptop)
  • State File: prod/terraform.tfstate (JSON, tracks resources)
  • Lock File: Prevents concurrent applies (only 1 apply at a time)
  • Backup: Storage account versioning enabled (recover old states)
  • Access Control: Only CD pipeline + admins can modify state
  • Encryption: Storage account encrypted, HTTPS only
Key Benefits of This Approach
  • ✓ Version Control: Every change tracked in git (who, when, what, why)
  • ✓ Peer Review: All changes reviewed before deployment (catch mistakes early)
  • ✓ Audit Trail: Git history proves: changes approved, security scanned, tested
  • ✓ Security: Checkov prevents insecure configs (encryption, RBAC, secrets)
  • ✓ Consistency: Same module deployed 100 times → identical results
  • ✓ Rollback: Revert to previous commit if deployment breaks
  • ✓ Automation: No manual clicking (faster, fewer errors)
  • ✓ Documentation: Code IS documentation (self-explaining variables, comments)
Common Scenarios
  • Add firewall rule: Edit policies/main.tf → PR → review → merge → auto-deployed
  • Change RBAC: Modify rbac/main.tf → same workflow → instant deployment
  • Deploy to new region: Call same module with different variables → consistent
  • Disaster: State corrupted? Recover from Storage backup → terraform apply
Metrics & Outcomes
  • Deployment time: ~10 minutes (fully automated)
  • Error detection: 100% (Checkov catches issues before production)
  • Security violations found: 0 (prevented by CI scanning)
  • Compliance: 100% (policies + audit trail provable)
  • Team confidence: High (peer review + automated testing)
Say this in interview "ALZ managed entirely via Terraform IaC. Platform team owns reusable modules (networking, RBAC, policies, monitoring). Workflow: feature branch → PR (triggers CI: format, validate, lint, Checkov scan, terraform plan) → 2+ approvals → merge → CD pipeline (terraform init, apply) → deployed. State in Azure Storage (remote backend, locked). Benefits: version-controlled, peer-reviewed, auditable, consistent, fast. Every change tracked in git with approval history. Rollback easy (revert commit). Checkov prevents insecure configs before they reach production."
Fundamentals

Three-Layer Architecture Overview

Understand the three layers: Platform, Landing Zones, and Management. Each has specific responsibilities.

Q&AArchitecture layers›
Layer 1: Platform (Central, Shared)
  • Hub VNet: Central network, houses firewall, VPN gateway, load balancer
  • Connectivity: ExpressRoute/VPN to on-premises, all spokes route through hub
  • Shared Services: DNS, identity (Azure AD), certificate management
  • Management: Monitoring, backup, log analytics central workspace
  • Team: Platform/Infrastructure team manages (not workload teams)
Layer 2: Landing Zones (Workloads)
  • Spoke VNets: Each workload gets dedicated VNet, peered to hub
  • Subscriptions: Each workload team owns subscription (separate RBAC, billing)
  • Isolation: Compute, database, storage isolated per subscription
  • Team: Workload teams own landing zone, deploy their apps
  • Constraints: Must follow platform standards (policies, network design, tagging)
Layer 3: Management (Governance)
  • Azure Policies: Enforce standards (encryption, tagging, network rules)
  • Management Groups: Hierarchical structure for policy assignment
  • RBAC: Least-privilege access, no subscription Owners for users
  • Audit: All actions logged centrally, compliance dashboard
  • Team: Security/governance team enforces (prevents deviation)
Interaction Example
  • 1. Workload team wants to deploy web app in landing zone
  • 2. They create VMs (spoke VNet), databases in their subscription
  • 3. Azure Policies check: must have encryption, must have tags, must have NSGs
  • 4. Policies auto-remediate (add tags), audit non-compliant resources
  • 5. Platform team sees all logs in central workspace
  • 6. Firewall routes traffic: VM → Hub Firewall → on-premises or internet
Say this in interview "Three layers: Platform (hub, firewall, monitoring—platform team owns), Landing Zones (spoke subscriptions—workload teams own), Management (policies, RBAC—governance team owns). Workload teams are isolated but constrained: can't disable encryption, must tag resources, must follow network design. Platform team manages central services, landing zone teams deploy apps, governance ensures compliance."
Landing Zone 02

Account & Subscriptions

Account & Subscriptions

Subscription Hierarchy & Design

Subscriptions are organizational units. Structure them by function, not by cost alone. Multiple subscriptions per organization is normal.

Q&ASubscription strategy›
Subscription as Organizational Unit
  • Each subscription = separate RBAC boundary (access control)
  • Each subscription = separate billing unit (cost tracking)
  • Each subscription = separate resource quotas (100s of VMs, limits)
  • Each subscription = separate compliance scope (audit independently)
  • Subscription ≠ just cost: it's security & governance boundary
Subscription Types in Landing Zone
Type Count Purpose Example
Platform Connectivity 1-2 Hub VNet, firewall, VPN, ExpressRoute prod-core-platform-conn-01
Platform Identity 1 Azure AD, identity governance, PAM prod-core-platform-identity-01
Platform Management 1 Monitoring, backup, log analytics prod-core-platform-mgmt-01
Production Landing Zone 1+ per app Customer-facing workloads, production prod-lz-web-01, prod-lz-api-01
Non-Prod Landing Zone 1+ per app Dev, test, staging environments nprod-lz-web-01
Sandbox Variable Experimentation, PoCs, training sandbox-team-learning-01
Why Multiple Subscriptions?
  • Billing: Separate bills per business unit, cost tracking easy
  • Isolation: Compromised app in subscription A doesn't affect B
  • Scaling: Each subscription has resource quotas (avoid hitting limits)
  • Access Control: Finance team ≠ Engineering team. Different RBAC per sub
  • Governance: Different policies per subscription type (prod stricter than dev)
Hierarchy Visualization
Root (Policy: All resources must have tags)
  ├─ Platform Subscriptions
  │   ├─ Connectivity (Hub VNet, firewall)
  │   ├─ Identity (Azure AD governance)
  │   └─ Management (Monitoring, backup)
  ├─ Production Landing Zones
  │   ├─ Web App Subscription
  │   ├─ API Subscription
  │   └─ Database Subscription
  └─ Non-Production
      ├─ Dev Subscriptions
      └─ Test Subscriptions
Say this in interview "Subscriptions are security & governance boundaries, not just cost units. Platform subscriptions (hub, identity, management) = 3. Per workload = 1 subscription (or 1 prod + 1 nonprod). Enterprise: 50-100+ subscriptions normal. Each subscription = separate RBAC, billing, resource quotas. Hierarchy via management groups for policy enforcement."
Account & Subscriptions

Management Groups & Policy Hierarchy

Management Groups organize subscriptions hierarchically. Apply policies at scale: one policy affects 100 subscriptions instantly.

Q&AManagement group strategy›
What Are Management Groups?
  • Hierarchical container above subscriptions (Root → MG → MG → Subscription)
  • Apply policies to management group = apply to all child subscriptions
  • Support 6 levels of nesting (Root + 5 custom levels)
  • Create for: organization structure, business unit, environment (prod/dev), compliance tier
Management Group Structure for Landing Zone
Root
├─ Platform (Policies: Core standards)
│  ├─ Connectivity
│  ├─ Identity
│  └─ Management
├─ Production (Policies: Strict, security-focused)
│  ├─ Landing Zones
│  │  ├─ Web Workload
│  │  ├─ API Workload
│  │  └─ Database
│  └─ Sandbox
└─ Non-Production (Policies: Relaxed, allow experimentation)
   ├─ Dev Landing Zones
   └─ Test Landing Zones
Policy Assignment Cascade
  • Policy assigned to Root: affects ALL subscriptions (strongest)
  • Example: "All resources must have encryption" → applies everywhere
  • Policy assigned to Production MG: affects only prod subscriptions (stricter)
  • Example: "No public IPs on VMs" → prod only, dev can have public IPs
  • Child MG can override parent (rarely, requires approval)
Common Policy Examples
  • Root Level (all subs): Enforce tagging, require encryption at rest
  • Production Level: No public IPs on VMs, require NSGs, enforce backup
  • Dev Level: Allow public IPs (for convenience), allow larger VM sizes for testing
Say this in interview "Management groups organize subscriptions hierarchically. Apply policies at any level: root policy affects all subscriptions instantly. Typical structure: Root → Platform/Prod/NonProd → Landing Zones. Example: policy 'require encryption' at root, policy 'no public IPs' at prod only. Child subscriptions inherit all parent policies. Enables org-wide governance at scale."
Account & Subscriptions

Naming Conventions & Organization

Consistent naming makes resources easy to identify, filter, and organize. Required for automation and cost tracking.

Q&ANaming strategy›
Subscription Naming Format
{Workload}-{Environment}-{Function}-{Number}

Examples:
  prod-core-platform-conn-01    (Production Core Platform Connectivity)
  prod-lz-web-app-01            (Production Landing Zone Web Apps)
  dev-lz-api-service-01         (Dev Landing Zone API Services)
  nprod-platform-mgmt-01        (Non-prod Platform Management)
Resource Naming Convention
{Workload}-{ResourceType}-{Environment}-{Region}-{Number}

Examples:
  web-vm-prod-eastus-01         (Virtual machine)
  web-vnet-prod-eastus-01       (Virtual network)
  web-nsg-prod-eastus-01        (Network security group)
  web-st-prod-eastus-01         (Storage account)
  web-sql-prod-eastus-01        (SQL database)

For resources without hyphens (storage):
  webvmproductus01              (All lowercase, no hyphens)
Tagging Strategy (Mandatory)
Tag Name Values Purpose
cost-center finance, engineering, operations Cost tracking & billing
environment prod, dev, test, staging Environment identification
owner team name or email Responsibility, contact
data-class public, internal, confidential, restricted Data sensitivity, compliance
workload web, api, database, batch-job Workload type identification
application customer-portal, billing-engine, etc Business application tracking
Policy to Enforce Tagging
  • Assign policy: "Require tags on resources" to landing zone MG
  • Effect: Deny (block creation if tags missing) or Audit (warn only)
  • Azure blocks: vm-create unless tags present
  • Auto-remediate: auto-add default tags if missing
Say this in interview "Naming: {workload}-{env}-{function}-{number}. All lowercase, hyphens. Storage accounts: no hyphens. Tagging mandatory: cost-center, environment, owner, data-class, workload. Enforce via policy: resources without tags = denied or remediated. Enables cost tracking, automation, compliance. Example: web-vm-prod-eastus-01 tagged with cost-center=engineering, environment=prod, owner=web-team."
Landing Zone 03

Network Design

Network Design

Hub-Spoke Network Topology

Hub-Spoke is the standard network design: central hub with firewall, spokes for workloads. All traffic routed through hub.

Q&AHub-spoke architecture›
Hub-Spoke Design (Visual)
                 On-Premises (192.168.0.0/16)
                        |
                  [ExpressRoute/VPN]
                        |
         ┌──────────────────────────────┐
         │  HUB VNET (10.0.0.0/16)       │
         │  ┌────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ Azure Firewall         │   │
         │  │ VPN/ER Gateway         │   │
         │  │ Application Gateway    │   │
         │  │ Bastion (jumpbox)      │   │
         │  └────────────────────────┘   │
         └──────────────────────────────┘
            |           |           |
         [VNet Peering - All traffic routed through firewall]
            |           |           |
         ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐
         │PROD  │    │DEV   │    │TEST  │
         │SPOKE │    │SPOKE │    │SPOKE │
         │10.1  │    │10.2  │    │10.3  │
         └──────┘    └──────┘    └──────┘
Hub Components
  • Azure Firewall: Central inspection point, inspects all traffic (N→S, E→W, hybrid)
  • VPN/ExpressRoute Gateway: On-premises connectivity, VPN site-to-site, point-to-site for remote workers
  • Application Gateway / Load Balancer: Distributes traffic to spoke workloads, SSL termination
  • Bastion: Secure jumpbox, RDP/SSH without exposing public IPs
  • DNS: Private DNS for name resolution (on-prem ↔ Azure)
Hub Subnets
Hub VNet: 10.0.0.0/16
├─ GatewaySubnet: 10.0.1.0/24 (VPN/ER gateway, required name)
├─ AzureFirewallSubnet: 10.0.2.0/24 (Firewall, required name)
├─ AzureBastionSubnet: 10.0.3.0/24 (Bastion, required name)
└─ Management: 10.0.4.0/24 (Monitoring agents, automation)
Spoke Configuration
  • Spoke VNet: Each landing zone gets dedicated VNet (10.1.0.0/16, 10.2.0.0/16, etc.)
  • Peering to Hub: VNet peering (not transit, no extra cost)
  • UDRs (User-Defined Routes): Force all traffic destined for hub/on-prem through firewall
  • No Direct Peering: Spoke-to-spoke traffic must go via hub firewall (prevents lateral movement)
Traffic Flow Example
  • 1. VM in Spoke A (web tier) sends traffic
  • 2. UDR: destination 10.2.x.x (Spoke B)? Route via Hub Firewall
  • 3. Hub Firewall evaluates: allows Spoke A → Spoke B on port 5000
  • 4. Firewall forwards to Spoke B
  • 5. Spoke B receives on private subnet (no public internet path)
  • Result: secure east-west traffic inspection
Security Benefits
  • ✓ Single point of inspection: all traffic through firewall
  • ✓ Lateral movement prevented: spoke A ≠ can directly reach spoke B
  • ✓ Centralized rules: update firewall rules once, affects all spokes
  • ✓ Hybrid security: on-prem traffic also inspected
Say this in interview "Hub-spoke: central hub with firewall, VPN/ER gateway, bastion. Spokes are landing zones, connected via VNet peering. UDRs force traffic through hub firewall (zero-trust). All traffic inspected: N→S (internet), E→W (spoke-to-spoke), hybrid (on-prem). Prevents lateral movement, single point of control. Hub is 10.0.0.0/16, spokes are 10.1, 10.2, etc. No public IPs on VMs (bastion for RDP/SSH)."
Network Design

VNet & Subnet Segmentation (Zero-Trust)

Each VNet has multiple subnets by tier: web, app, database, management. Each subnet isolated by NSGs (Network Security Groups).

Q&ASubnet design›
Example: Single Landing Zone VNet
VNet: prod-lz-web-app (10.1.0.0/16)
├─ WebTier (10.1.1.0/24)
│  ├─ NSG Rules:
│  │  ✓ Ingress: 443 (HTTPS), 80 (HTTP) from internet
│  │  ✓ Egress: Allow to AppTier on port 5000
│  │  ✗ Block: All other inbound
│  └─ Instances: App Gateway, public load balancer
│
├─ AppTier (10.1.2.0/24)
│  ├─ NSG Rules:
│  │  ✓ Ingress: 5000 from WebTier only
│  │  ✓ Egress: Allow to DBTier on port 1433
│  │  ✗ Block: All else
│  └─ Instances: App servers, APIs, microservices
│
├─ DBTier (10.1.3.0/24)
│  ├─ NSG Rules:
│  │  ✓ Ingress: 1433 (SQL) from AppTier only
│  │  ✗ Block: Everything else (no internet, no web direct access)
│  └─ Instances: SQL Database, Azure Database for MySQL
│
└─ Management (10.1.4.0/24)
   ├─ NSG Rules:
   │  ✓ Ingress: Bastion (22/3389), Hub Management subnet
   │  ✓ Egress: Monitoring to central workspace
   │  ✗ Block: Direct internet
   └─ Instances: Monitoring agents, deployment agents
Subnet Allocation Pattern
Subnet CIDR IPs Purpose Public?
Web/Public /24 256 App Gateway, public load balancer Yes
Application /24 256 App servers, APIs, microservices No
Database /25 128 SQL, PostgreSQL, CosmosDB, cache No
Management /25 128 Monitoring, deployment agents No
Private Link /25 128 PaaS services (storage, SQL public IP override) No
Integration /26 64 API Management, service bus, event hub No
Zero-Trust Networking (Defense in Depth)
  • Principle: Trust nothing by default. Explicitly allow, deny everything else
  • Implementation: Each subnet has NSG, whitelist protocols + ports + source
  • Example policy: Database subnet: allow port 1433 from AppTier only
  • Result: Even if web server compromised, can't directly access database
NSG Rule Best Practices
  • Name rules descriptively: "AllowAppTierSQLInbound" not "Allow 1433"
  • Use service tags: "AppService", "AzureKeyVault" instead of hardcoding IPs
  • Principle of least privilege: allow minimum necessary, nothing more
  • Regular audit: review NSG rules quarterly, remove unused rules
  • Logging: enable NSG flow logs for troubleshooting (sent to central Log Analytics)
Say this in interview "Each landing zone VNet: web (public load balancer), app (private, allow from web), database (private, allow from app only), management (private, allow bastion). NSGs enforce zero-trust: deny all by default, whitelist what's needed. Example: DB subnet NSG allows only port 1433 from app tier. Prevents lateral movement. No public IPs on VMs except load balancer/bastion. Sub-routing via UDR to hub firewall."
Network Design

Hybrid Connectivity (ExpressRoute & VPN)

Connect on-premises to Azure via ExpressRoute (private, dedicated) or VPN (internet-based). Hub gateway handles both.

Q&AHybrid connectivity›
Connectivity Options Comparison
Method Bandwidth Latency Cost Use Case
ExpressRoute 50Mbps - 100Gbps Consistent, low (<50ms) $$$ Critical hybrid, guaranteed throughput
Site-to-Site VPN ~200Mbps (varies) Variable (50-100ms) $ Secondary, smaller workloads
Point-to-Site VPN ~250Mbps Variable $ Remote workers, on-demand access
On-Premises Connectivity Flow
  • 1. On-premises network (192.168.0.0/16)
  • 2. ExpressRoute private connection OR Site-to-Site VPN
  • 3. Hub VNet (10.0.0.0/16) with ER/VPN Gateway
  • 4. Hub Firewall inspects traffic (allowed or denied)
  • 5. Spoke VNets (10.1.x, 10.2.x, etc.) receive traffic
  • 6. Return traffic: spoke → hub firewall → on-prem
DNS Resolution (Hybrid)
  • Problem: On-prem machine tries to reach azure-app.company.com. Where is it?
  • Solution: Private DNS zone (Azure-native DNS)
  • Implementation:
  • └─ Create private DNS zone: company.com
  • └─ Register app VMs: azure-app.company.com → 10.1.2.5
  • └─ Link to hub VNet
  • └─ Conditional forwarders on-prem: *.company.com → Azure DNS
  • Result: on-prem machine can resolve azure-app.company.com
Redundancy & Failover
  • Primary: ExpressRoute (high bandwidth, guaranteed)
  • Secondary: Site-to-Site VPN (failover if ER down)
  • Azure VPN Gateway automatically fails over if ER fails
  • On-premises router also can fail over
  • Result: hybrid connectivity never fully down
Say this in interview "Hub VNet has ER/VPN gateway for on-prem connectivity. ExpressRoute: dedicated private connection, guaranteed bandwidth, expensive. Site-to-Site VPN: secondary failover option, cheaper. Spokes route to hub, hub routes to on-prem. DNS: private DNS zone for on-prem ↔ Azure name resolution. Multiple circuits for redundancy. All traffic inspected by hub firewall. Hybrid workloads see seamless connectivity."
Landing Zone 04

Security & Governance

Security & Governance

Azure Policy & Guardrails

Policies are guardrails: prevent bad configurations automatically. Enforce standards across all subscriptions at scale.

Q&APolicy enforcement›
What is Azure Policy?
  • Declarative rules that enforce standards on Azure resources
  • Like a linter for cloud: evaluates resource configs against policy
  • Can audit (report violations) or deny (prevent creation)
  • Assigned to management groups or subscriptions (cascade down)
  • Enables compliance without human intervention
Must-Have Policies for Landing Zones
Policy Effect Scope Why
Enforce Encryption at Rest Deny All subscriptions Compliance, data protection
Require Tagging Deny/Remediate All subscriptions Cost tracking, automation
No Public IPs (except gateways) Deny Production subscriptions Security, reduce attack surface
Enforce NSG on Subnets Audit All subscriptions Network segmentation enforcement
Require Storage Encryption Deny All subscriptions Data security at rest
Enforce Log Retention (365d) Audit All subscriptions Compliance, audit trail
Require Azure AD MFA Audit All subscriptions Identity security
Allowed Locations Deny All subscriptions Data residency compliance
Policy Effects Explained
  • Audit: Report violations (compliant/non-compliant), but allow creation
  • Deny: Block resource creation if violates policy (prevent non-compliant state)
  • Modify: Auto-remediate (add tags, enable encryption, etc.)
  • DeployIfNotExists: Auto-deploy if not present (e.g., auto-enable backup)
Policy Assignment Flow
  • 1. Define policy (JSON): condition + effect + parameters
  • 2. Assign to management group / subscription
  • 3. Azure immediately evaluates all existing resources
  • 4. Compliance dashboard shows: compliant / non-compliant
  • 5. New resources: Azure checks policy before allowing creation
  • 6. Remediate: auto-fix violations (if Modify/DeployIfNotExists) or manual (if Audit)
Example: Tag Enforcement Policy
Policy: "Require 'cost-center' tag on all resources"
├─ Condition: Resource missing 'cost-center' tag
├─ Effect: Deny (prevent creation)
├─ Assignment: Production management group
└─ Result:
   ✓ User tries to create VM without 'cost-center' tag
   ✗ Azure denies: "VM missing required tag: cost-center"
   ✓ User adds tag: cost-center=engineering
   ✓ VM creation succeeds
Say this in interview "Azure Policy: declarative rules enforced at scale. Audit (report) or Deny (block). Assigned to management groups = all subscriptions inherit. Must-haves: encryption, tagging, no public IPs (prod), NSGs, log retention, MFA, allowed regions. Policy dashboard shows compliance. Audit policies report violations, Deny prevent creation. Modify auto-fixes (add tags). Enables governance without manual checks."
Security & Governance

Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)

RBAC enforces least-privilege: each person/app gets minimum permissions needed. No subscription Owners for users.

Q&AAccess control›
RBAC Principle: Least Privilege
  • Concept: User gets minimum permissions needed to do job
  • Example: Web team → Contributor (can create/modify resources), not Owner (can't change RBAC)
  • Result: If account compromised, attacker has limited access
Common RBAC Roles
Role Permissions Who Scope
Owner Full access + can delegate RBAC Platform leads, subscription admins Subscription or management group
Contributor Create/modify resources, NO RBAC delegation Workload teams, app engineers Subscription level
Reader View only, no modifications Auditors, viewers, cost analysts Any level
Network Contributor Manage VNets, NSGs, firewalls Network team Hub subscription
Security Admin Manage policies, security center Security team Management groups
Monitoring Contributor Create/manage monitoring resources Ops team Management subscription
Example: Landing Zone RBAC Structure
Prod Web App Subscription
├─ Role: Web Team = Contributor
│  └─ Can: create VMs, databases, update configs
│  └─ Cannot: change RBAC, delete subscription, modify policies
├─ Role: Security Team = Reader
│  └─ Can: view resources, logs (audit compliance)
│  └─ Cannot: modify anything
└─ Role: On-Call Engineer = Reader + Key Vault Secrets Officer
   └─ Can: view resources, read secrets from Key Vault
   └─ Cannot: create resources, modify configs

Hub Connectivity Subscription
├─ Role: Network Team = Network Contributor + Firewall Admin
│  └─ Can: modify firewall rules, VPN config, peering
│  └─ Cannot: delete subscription, remove policies
└─ Role: Ops Team = Reader
   └─ Can: view network status, see configuration
Managed Identity (Service Principal)
  • Problem: App needs to access Key Vault secret. How to authenticate without password?
  • Solution: Managed Identity (automatic service principal)
  • Types: System-assigned (1:1 with resource) or User-assigned (shared)
  • Flow:
  • 1. App has system-assigned identity (auto-created with VM)
  • 2. Grant identity role: "Key Vault Secrets Officer"
  • 3. App authenticates as identity (no credentials stored)
  • 4. Azure logs: "VM app accessed secret X at 2024-01-15 10:30"
  • Benefit: No passwords, auditable, can revoke instantly
RBAC Best Practices
  • ✓ No humans with Owner role on subscriptions (only service principals for automation)
  • ✓ Use groups for RBAC (Azure AD groups), not individual users
  • ✓ Separate roles per subscription: web team only accesses web-app sub
  • ✓ Temporary elevation: just-in-time access (request, approve, expires in 8 hours)
  • ✓ Audit: log who has what access, review quarterly
Say this in interview "RBAC: least privilege. Contributor for workload teams (can create/modify, not RBAC). Owner for admins only (approve workflow). Reader for auditors. No subscription Owner assigned to humans (only service principals). Use managed identities for apps (no passwords). Example: web team → Contributor on web-app subscription, cannot access hub or other subscriptions. Security via groups (not users) so when person leaves, remove from group."
Security & Governance

Infrastructure as Code & Blueprints

Templates (Bicep, ARM, Terraform) standardize landing zone deployment. Deploy 100 times, get identical results.

Q&AIaC strategy›
Why Infrastructure as Code?
  • Consistency: Deploy landing zone → identical every time (no manual errors)
  • Repeatability: Define once, deploy to 100 subscriptions
  • Version control: Track changes: who changed what, when, why
  • Audit: Code review before deployment (approval workflow)
  • Fast: 30 minutes → fully configured landing zone (VNet, NSGs, policies, RBAC)
Azure Blueprints Components
  • Template: ARM/Bicep code for resources (VNet, subnets, NSGs)
  • Policy Assignments: Policies applied (encryption, tagging, etc.)
  • RBAC Assignments: Who has what roles
  • Artifacts: Resource groups, networking, security configs all bundled
Blueprint Definition Structure
Blueprint: "Production Landing Zone v1.2"
├─ Parameters (filled in at assignment time)
│  ├─ vnet-cidr: 10.1.0.0/16
│  ├─ location: eastus
│  └─ environment: prod
│
├─ Template Artifacts
│  ├─ Resource Group: "prod-lz-web-rg"
│  ├─ VNet: 10.1.0.0/16
│  ├─ Subnets: web (10.1.1/24), app (10.1.2/24), db (10.1.3/24)
│  ├─ NSGs: per-subnet with rules
│  └─ Route Tables: UDR to hub firewall
│
├─ Policy Artifacts
│  ├─ "Require tagging": assign to landing zone
│  ├─ "No public IPs": assign to landing zone
│  └─ "Require encryption": assign to landing zone
│
└─ RBAC Artifacts
   ├─ Web Team = Contributor
   ├─ Security Team = Reader
   └─ Managed Identity for app = Key Vault Secrets Officer
Deployment Workflow
  • 1. Platform team defines blueprint (templates, policies, RBAC)
  • 2. Blueprint versioned, stored in Azure (or GitHub)
  • 3. New landing zone needed?
  • 4. Create new subscription
  • 5. Assign blueprint: Portal "Assign Blueprint" → select version
  • 6. Fill parameters (VNET CIDR, location, environment)
  • 7. Click Deploy
  • 8. 30 minutes: subscription fully configured + compliant
  • 9. Workload team starts deploying apps (infrastructure ready)
Alternative: Terraform/Bicep
  • Bicep: Microsoft's preferred, simpler than ARM JSON
  • Terraform: Multi-cloud, community support, state management
  • Difference: Blueprints = Azure-native (policies, RBAC built-in). Terraform = general-purpose (need plugins for policies)
  • Choice: Azure-only → Blueprints. Multi-cloud → Terraform
Say this in interview "Azure Blueprints: templates for landing zones. Define: VNet, subnets, NSGs, policies, RBAC. Assign to subscription = fully configured in 30 min. No manual steps, guaranteed consistency. Alternative: Terraform/Bicep. Blueprints include policies (auto-enforced), Terraform doesn't (need separate policy assignment). Deploy 100 times, get identical infrastructure every time."
Landing Zone 05

Monitoring & Audit

Monitoring & Audit

Central Logging Strategy

All subscriptions → central Log Analytics workspace. Single query sees activity across entire organization.

Q&ALogging architecture›
Central Logging Architecture
Multiple Subscriptions
├─ Prod Sub 1: Activity logs, resource logs
│  └─ Diagnostic Setting: Forward to central Log Analytics
├─ Prod Sub 2: Activity logs, resource logs
│  └─ Diagnostic Setting: Forward to central Log Analytics
├─ Dev Sub: Activity logs, resource logs
│  └─ Diagnostic Setting: Forward to central Log Analytics
│
└─ Management Subscription (Central)
   ├─ Log Analytics Workspace (prod-core-platform-mgmt-01)
   │  └─ Central repository: all logs from all subs
   │  └─ Single query: "show failed logins last 24h" sees all subs
   │
   ├─ Storage Account (long-term archive)
   │  └─ Retention: 7 years (compliance)
   │  └─ Immutable storage (cannot delete)
   │
   └─ Azure Sentinel (SIEM)
      └─ Threat detection, incident response
What Gets Logged?
Log Type Captures Retention (default) Use
Activity Logs Subscription-level events (RDP, deployments, policy changes, role assignments) 90 days (free) Who did what at subscription level
Resource Logs Resource-level events (VM created/deleted, storage accessed, firewall denied traffic) Per policy Troubleshooting, audit
Diagnostic Logs App logs (error traces, query performance, request latency) Per policy Application debugging
Security Events Failed logins, privilege escalations, suspicious activity Per policy Threat detection
Diagnostic Settings Configuration
For each subscription:
├─ Activity Logs diagnostic setting
│  ├─ Destination: Log Analytics workspace in management sub
│  ├─ Retention: 365 days (compliance)
