│  └─ Categories: Administrative, Security, Operational
│
├─ Resource diagnostic settings (per resource type)
│  ├─ VNet Flow Logs: Firewall denied traffic
│  ├─ App Service Logs: Application errors
│  ├─ SQL Database Logs: Query execution, deadlocks
│  └─ Storage Logs: Access patterns, errors
Example Query (KQL - Kusto Query Language)
// Find all failed RDP login attempts last 24 hours across all subscriptions
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where EventID == 4625  // Failed login
| where LogonType == 10  // RDP (type 10)
| summarize AttemptsCount=count() by Computer, Account, IPAddress
| sort by AttemptsCount desc

Result:
Computer      | Account        | IPAddress       | AttemptsCount
laptop-prod-1 | user@company   | 192.168.1.50   | 45
vm-app-02     | admin          | 10.1.2.100     | 3
Best Practices
  • ✓ Central workspace = single source of truth
  • ✓ Retention = 365 days minimum for compliance
  • ✓ Archive to storage account for 7-year retention (legal requirement)
  • ✓ Diagnostic settings configured at subscription + resource level
  • ✓ Enable for: Activity logs, all resource types, security events
Say this in interview "Central logging: all subscriptions forward logs to central Log Analytics workspace in management subscription. Single query sees activity across organization. Activity logs 365d retention, resource logs per policy, security events continuous. Archive to storage (7yr for compliance). Query example: SecurityEvent | where EventID==4625 (failed logins) shows attempts across all resources. Enables compliance audits and incident investigation."
Monitoring & Audit

Monitoring & Alerting

Monitor infrastructure and application health: CPU, memory, failed logins, policy violations. Alert when thresholds breach.

Q&AMonitoring strategy›
What to Monitor?
  • Infrastructure: VM CPU/memory/disk, network latency, firewall rules violations
  • Applications: Request rate, errors, latency, dependency calls
  • Security: Failed login attempts, privilege escalations, policy violations, unauthorized access
  • Operations: Backup success/failure, resource utilization, quota usage
Alert Examples
Alert Threshold Action
High CPU on VM > 80% for 5 min Notify ops team, auto-scale
Failed Logins > 10 attempts/hour Notify security, block IP (temp)
Backup Failed 24h since last backup Alert on-call, investigate immediately
Policy Violation Any non-compliant resource Audit report, remediate or escalate
Storage Near Full > 85% capacity Notify admin, expand or archive
Alerting Best Practices
  • ✓ Avoid alert fatigue: set realistic thresholds
  • ✓ Escalation: first alert to team, if no ack in 30min → on-call
  • ✓ Context: include logs/metrics in alert (don't force manual lookup)
  • ✓ Runbook: link to troubleshooting guide in alert
  • ✓ Test alerts monthly: verify they actually fire
Say this in interview "Monitor: infrastructure (CPU/disk/network), apps (latency/errors), security (failed logins, policy violations), operations (backups, quotas). Alerts: high CPU auto-scale, failed logins trigger security investigation, backup failure pages on-call. Azure Monitor metrics + Application Insights. Avoid alert fatigue: realistic thresholds. Include logs in alert body (context). Runbooks for common issues."
Monitoring & Audit

Compliance & Audit Trail

Every action logged: who accessed what, when, why. Full audit trail for compliance certifications (SOC 2, HIPAA).

Q&ACompliance audit›
Compliance Requirements
  • Audit Trail: Who did what, when, from where (IP address), using what method
  • Immutability: Logs cannot be deleted (proves authenticity)
  • Retention: Minimum 365 days for SOC 2, often 7 years for regulated industries
  • Encryption: All logs encrypted in transit + at rest
  • Access Control: Only authorized users can view logs (separation of duties)
Audit Events Captured
  • Data Access: Who accessed what data, when, success/failure
  • Administrative Actions: Resource created/deleted/modified, policy changes, RBAC changes
  • Authentication: Login attempts (success/failure), MFA, privilege elevation
  • Configuration Changes: Firewall rules, encryption settings, network policies
  • Data Movement: Export/download of sensitive data, API calls
Compliance Dashboard Example
  • Policy Compliance: 95% of resources compliant (5% need remediation)
  • Encryption: 100% of storage accounts have encryption enabled
  • Access Control: All users have MFA enabled
  • Audit Trail: All logs retained 365+ days, immutable
  • Incident Response: Latest incident 2024-01-15, resolved in 4 hours
Say this in interview "Audit trail: everything logged (who, what, when, where, why). Activity logs 365d+ retention. Immutable (cannot delete). For SOC 2/HIPAA/PCI-DSS compliance. Azure shows: policy compliance %, encryption status, access control, incident history. Regular compliance audits: run queries, generate reports. Example: 'show all data access last 30 days' → proves or fails audit."
Landing Zone 06

Standards & Patterns

Standards & Patterns

Golden Standards & Enforcement

Platform team defines standards: every landing zone must follow. Enforced via policies, not requests.

Q&AStandard enforcement›
Golden Standards (Non-Negotiable)
Standard Rule Enforcement Why
Naming {workload}-{env}-{function}-{number} Policy: naming pattern Searchability, automation
Tagging Required: cost-center, env, owner, data-class Policy: require tags Cost tracking, RBAC, compliance
Encryption AES-256 at rest, TLS 1.2+ in transit Policy: deny unencrypted storage Security, compliance (HIPAA, PCI)
Network Hub-spoke, NSG on all subnets, no public IPs on VMs Policy: enforce network design Zero-trust, prevent data exfil
Monitoring Logs → central workspace, metrics to Azure Monitor Diagnostic settings mandatory Auditing, incident response
Backup All VMs, DBs, file shares backed up. 30d min retention Policy: enable backup or deny Disaster recovery, business continuity
RBAC Least privilege, no user Owners, managed identities for apps Policy: restrict Owner assignments Security, prevent privilege escalation
Policy Assignment Example
  • 1. Platform team creates policy: "Enforce encryption on storage accounts"
  • 2. Assign to Management Group: Production Landing Zones
  • 3. Effect: Deny (block unencrypted creation)
  • 4. 100% of prod storage accounts are now encrypted (audit shows no violations)
  • 5. If team tries to create unencrypted storage: Azure denies with message
  • 6. Team must create encrypted storage (policy enforced)
Policy Compliance Dashboard
  • View: All policies + compliance status per subscription
  • Production Landing Zones: 98% compliant (2 non-compliant resources)
  • Details: Show which resources violate which policies
  • Action: Remediate (auto-fix if Modify effect) or manually fix
  • Trend: 95% → 97% → 98% (improving)
Say this in interview "Golden standards: naming, tagging, encryption, network design (hub-spoke), centralized logging, backup, RBAC. Enforced via Azure Policy: no negotiation. Policy assignment → prevents non-compliant creation. Audit dashboard shows: 98% compliant. Violations remediated or escalated. Example: policy 'all storage encrypted' → new storage account must be encrypted or denied."
Standards & Patterns

Landing Zone Deployment Template

Step-by-step checklist for deploying new landing zones. Ensures nothing is missed.

Q&ADeployment checklist›
Pre-Deployment (Planning)
  • ☐ Define workload: web, API, database, batch job
  • ☐ Select environment: prod, dev, test
  • ☐ Assign: workload team, cost-center, owner
  • ☐ Define: VNET CIDR (10.X.0.0/16), region(s)
  • ☐ Determine: data classification (public, internal, confidential)
Subscription & Governance
  • ☐ Create subscription with proper naming: {workload}-{env}-lz-{number}
  • ☐ Assign to Landing Zone Management Group
  • ☐ Assign RBAC: Contributor for team, Reader for auditors
  • ☐ Setup billing alerts: alert if spend exceeds budget
  • ☐ Assign policies: tagging, encryption, network, etc.
Networking
  • ☐ Create VNet (10.X.0.0/16) + subnets (web, app, db, mgmt)
  • ☐ Create NSGs: one per subnet with ingress/egress rules
  • ☐ Create Route Tables: UDR forcing all traffic to hub firewall
  • ☐ Create VNet Peering: link to hub VNet
  • ☐ Configure DNS: link to private DNS zone (on-prem resolution)
  • ☐ Test connectivity: hub ↔ spoke ↔ on-prem
Security
  • ☐ Create Key Vault: for secrets, certificates
  • ☐ Setup Managed Identities: for apps (no passwords)
  • ☐ Enable Azure Defender: vulnerability scans, threat detection
  • ☐ Configure Private Link: for PaaS services (Storage, SQL)
  • ☐ Verify: no public IPs on VMs (except load balancers)
Monitoring & Audit
  • ☐ Configure Diagnostic Settings: activity logs → central workspace
  • ☐ Enable Resource Logs: app services, databases, firewalls
  • ☐ Create Alerts: CPU high, backup failed, policy violations
  • ☐ Setup Application Insights: for app monitoring
  • ☐ Verify: logs visible in central workspace
Compliance Verification
  • ☐ Run compliance scan: Azure Policy Compliance dashboard
  • ☐ Check: all resources have required tags
  • ☐ Verify: encryption enabled on storage, databases
  • ☐ Confirm: NSGs attached to subnets
  • ☐ Test: firewall rules work (allowed traffic, denied traffic blocked)
Handoff to Workload Team
  • ☐ Document: subscription ID, VNET, subnet allocation, access info
  • ☐ Train: team on policies, naming, tagging, monitoring
  • ☐ Provide: runbook for common tasks (troubleshooting, escalation)
  • ☐ Assign: support contact (who to call if issues)
  • ☐ Walkthrough: live demo of subscription access, deploy first app
Say this in interview "Landing zone deployment: pre-plan (workload, env, CIDR), create subscription + MG assignment + RBAC, deploy network (VNet, subnets, NSGs, UDR, peering), configure security (Key Vault, managed identity, defender), enable monitoring (logs to central workspace, alerts), verify compliance (policy dashboard), handoff to team (docs + training). Takes 30 min if automated (blueprint), 2-3 hours if manual. Checklist ensures nothing missed."
Standards & Patterns

Common Deployment Patterns

Different workloads need different patterns. Single-region simple apps, multi-region for HA, isolated for regulated.

Q&ADeployment patterns›
Pattern 1: Simple Single-Region Web App
Subscription: 1 (prod-lz-web-01)
Network:
  └─ VNet: 10.1.0.0/16 (single region: eastus)
     ├─ Web tier: public load balancer
     ├─ App tier: private app servers
     └─ DB tier: Azure SQL managed
Redundancy: Single VM (no HA)
Failover: None (acceptable for non-critical)
Cost: Low
Suitable for: Startup, MVP, non-critical apps
Pattern 2: Mission-Critical Multi-Region HA
Subscriptions: 2-3 (per region: eastus, westus)
Network:
  ├─ Region 1 VNet: 10.1.0.0/16 (eastus)
  ├─ Region 2 VNet: 10.2.0.0/16 (westus)
  └─ Hub VNet: 10.0.0.0/16 (connectivity)
High Availability:
  ├─ Active-active multi-region
  ├─ Global Load Balancer (healthcheck-based routing)
  ├─ SQL geo-replication
  ├─ Traffic Manager (failover)
  └─ RTO/RPO < 5 min
Cost: High (multi-region duplication)
Suitable for: Enterprise, mission-critical, SLA 99.99%
Pattern 3: Regulated Workload (Banking/Healthcare)
Subscriptions: 1-2 (isolated)
Network:
  ├─ VNet: 10.1.0.0/16 (no internet outbound)
  ├─ All resources private (no public IPs)
  ├─ Private Link: PaaS services only
  └─ No anonymous access (all auth required)
Security:
  ├─ Encryption key: customer-managed (HSM)
  ├─ Audit logging: every action
  ├─ Compliance: SOC 2, HIPAA, PCI-DSS automated
  ├─ Advanced threat protection: enabled
  └─ Penetration testing: approved by security
Cost: Very high (enhanced security)
Suitable for: Banking, healthcare, compliance-heavy industries
Pattern Selection Flowchart
Workload requirements?
├─ Simple, non-critical? → Pattern 1 (single-region, cheap)
├─ Critical, 99.99% SLA? → Pattern 2 (multi-region HA)
└─ Regulated (HIPAA/PCI)? → Pattern 3 (isolated, encryption-heavy)
Say this in interview "Three patterns: (1) Simple single-region for MVP/startups, (2) Multi-region active-active for mission-critical (99.99% SLA), (3) Isolated for regulated (banking/healthcare, no internet, customer-managed keys). Choose based on criticality + compliance requirements. Pattern 2 costs more (multi-region), justified by HA. Pattern 3 costs most (security overhead), required by regulation."
Important 01

High Availability & Disaster Recovery

High Availability & DR

High Availability & Disaster Recovery Overview

HA ensures continuous service. DR ensures recovery after disasters. Design everything around RTO and RPO targets.

Q&AHA/DR strategy›
HA vs DR vs Business Continuity
  • High Availability (HA): System stays UP. Redundancy removes single points of failure. Seamless failover within region.
  • Disaster Recovery (DR): System goes DOWN. Planned recovery after catastrophic failure. Failover to different region. May accept minutes/hours of downtime.
  • Business Continuity: Encompasses both HA + DR. Ensures business operations continue despite failures.
When to Use Each
  • HA: Mission-critical apps (financial services, healthcare, e-commerce). Downtime = revenue loss. Target: 99.95-99.99% uptime
  • DR: Important but not critical apps. Can tolerate hours of downtime. Target: RTO 4-24 hours, RPO 1-4 hours
  • Both: Critical apps need both HA (survive component failures) + DR (survive region/datacenter failure)
Architecture Pattern: HA + DR
Primary Region (Active)
├─ Multiple Availability Zones (AZ1, AZ2, AZ3)
├─ Resources spread for HA
├─ App Gateways, LBs in each AZ
├─ Databases with sync replication
└─ Monitoring 24/7

Secondary Region (Standby)
├─ Async replication from primary
├─ Warm standby (some resources running)
├─ Ready for failover
└─ RTO/RPO tested quarterly

Failover Triggers
├─ Primary region unavailable (health check fails)
├─ Database sync replication lag > threshold
├─ Automated failover via Traffic Manager/Front Door
└─ Manual verification before bringing secondary live
Say this in interview "HA = uptime during failures (redundancy within region). DR = recovery after disaster (failover to different region). Design everything around RTO/RPO targets. For mission-critical: HA + DR. Multiple AZs for redundancy, secondary region for disaster recovery. Active-Passive cheaper than active-active."
Important Concepts

Load Balancer Comparison: ALB vs AG vs Traffic Manager vs Front Door

Four different load balancers for different use cases. Understand the differences: layer, protocol, region, cost.

Q&ALoad balancer options›
Load Balancer Comparison Table
Feature ALB (AWS) App Gateway (Azure) Traffic Manager (Azure) Front Door (Azure)
Layer Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS) Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS) Layer 3 (DNS) Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS)
Protocols HTTP, HTTPS only HTTP, HTTPS only All (TCP, UDP, HTTP, etc.) HTTP, HTTPS only
Region Single region Single region Global (multi-region) Global (multi-region)
Failover Speed Instant (within region) Instant (within region) Slow (DNS TTL, ~1-5 min) Fast (edge network, <10s)
Health Checks HTTP/HTTPS probes HTTP/HTTPS probes TCP/HTTP probes HTTP/HTTPS probes
SSL/TLS Terminate at ALB Terminate at gateway No (DNS level) Terminate at edge
WAF (DDoS) No (AWS WAF separate) Built-in WAF No Built-in WAF + DDoS
CDN No (CloudFront separate) No No Built-in (edge caching)
Routing Options Path, hostname, query params Path, hostname, HTTP headers Priority, weighted, performance, geographic Path, hostname, weighted, geographic
Multi-region No (use Route 53) No (use Traffic Manager) Yes (primary use) Yes (primary use)
Cost $$ (per hour + LCU) $$$ (capacity units) $ (per DNS query) $$$ (per request + data)
Best For Single-region HTTP/S apps Single-region HTTP/S + WAF Non-HTTP, cost-sensitive global Global web apps, DDoS needed
RPO: Recovery Point Objective
  • Definition: Maximum acceptable data loss. How much data is OK to lose?
  • Measured in: Hours, minutes, seconds of data
  • Examples:
    • RPO = 1 hour → If disaster at 10am, recover data from 9am (1 hour of data lost)
    • RPO = 5 minutes → Sync replication required (expensive)
    • RPO = 0 (zero data loss) → Requires real-time replication + double-write
  • Impacts: Replication strategy (sync vs async), storage costs
RTO vs RPO Trade-offs
Scenario RTO RPO Strategy Cost
E-commerce (mission-critical) 15 min 5 min Active-active + sync replication $$$$
Financial services 1-4 hours 1 hour Active-passive + sync replication $$$
Internal tools 4-8 hours 4 hours Warm standby + async replication $$
Dev/test environment 24+ hours 24 hours Cold standby + daily backups $
Design Everything Around RTO/RPO
  • Step 1: Define RTO/RPO with business stakeholders (not IT alone)
  • Step 2: Calculate costs for each RTO/RPO tier (higher = more expensive)
  • Step 3: Choose infrastructure that meets RTO/RPO targets
  • Step 4: Test quarterly to verify actual RTO/RPO matches targets
  • Step 5: Document in DR runbook with exact failover steps
Say this in interview "RTO = how long down (15 min? 4 hours?). RPO = how much data lost (5 min? 1 hour?). These drive all HA/DR decisions. High RPO (zero data loss) expensive: sync replication + active-active. Define with business first: CEO won't accept 24h downtime, but dev env can. Test RTO/RPO quarterly. Document in runbook."
Important Concepts

When to Use Each Load Balancer

Choose the right LB for your use case: region scope, protocol support, failover speed, and cost.

Q&ASelection guide›
ALB (AWS Application Load Balancer)
  • Layer: Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS only)
  • Region: Single region (no global routing)
  • Protocols: HTTP, HTTPS only
  • Best for: Single-region microservices, web apps, REST APIs
  • Routing: Path-based, hostname-based, query parameters, HTTP headers
  • Cost: $$ (per hour + LCU)
  • Failover: Instant within region (health checks every 15s)
Application Gateway (Azure)
  • Layer: Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS only)
  • Region: Single region (no global routing)
  • Protocols: HTTP, HTTPS only
  • Best for: Single-region web apps, APIs with WAF needs
  • Routing: Path-based, hostname-based, HTTP headers
  • WAF: Built-in Web Application Firewall
  • Cost: $$$ (capacity units)
  • Failover: Instant within region
Traffic Manager (Azure)
  • Layer: Layer 3 DNS-based (protocol-agnostic)
  • Region: Global multi-region
  • Protocols: ALL (TCP, UDP, HTTP, HTTPS, FTP, anything)
  • Best for: Non-HTTP workloads, cost-sensitive global failover, database routing
  • Routing: Priority, weighted, performance, geographic, MultiValue
  • Failover Speed: Slow (DNS TTL 1-5 minutes, client cache)
  • Cost: $ (cheapest, per DNS query)
  • No: WAF, CDN, SSL termination
Front Door (Azure)
  • Layer: Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS only)
  • Region: Global multi-region + edge network
  • Protocols: HTTP, HTTPS only
  • Best for: Global web apps, APIs, DDoS protection, low latency
  • Routing: Path-based, hostname-based, weighted, geographic
  • WAF: Built-in Web Application Firewall
  • CDN: Built-in edge caching for static content
  • DDoS Protection: Layer 3, 4, 7
  • Failover Speed: Fast (< 10 seconds, via edge network)
  • Cost: $$$ (per request + data transfer)
Quick Selection Guide
  • Single region + HTTP/HTTPS? → ALB (AWS) or Application Gateway (Azure)
  • Single region + WAF needed? → Application Gateway (Azure) or ALB + AWS WAF
  • Global + non-HTTP (database, cache)? → Traffic Manager (Azure only)
  • Global + HTTP/HTTPS + DDoS protection? → Front Door (Azure) or CloudFront + Route 53 (AWS)
  • Cost-sensitive global routing? → Traffic Manager (cheapest)
  • Instant global failover needed? → Front Door (< 10s)
Say this in interview "ALB/App Gateway: single region Layer 7 routing. Traffic Manager: Layer 3 DNS, all protocols, global, cheap, slow failover. Front Door: Layer 7, global, instant failover, has WAF/CDN, expensive. Choose: single region → ALB/App Gateway. Global non-HTTP → Traffic Manager. Global HTTP + DDoS → Front Door."
Important Concepts

Cloud Cost Optimization

Cost visibility and right-sizing. Eliminate waste. Pay only for what you use.

StrategyCost reduction tactics›
Visibility & Tagging
  • Tag every resource: cost-center, team, app, environment
  • Azure Policy enforces tagging: Deny resource creation without tags
  • Azure Cost Management: View cost by tag, team, service
Right-Sizing
  • Azure Advisor: Recommends VM size reductions, shutdown idle VMs
  • Reserved Instances: 1-3 year commit → 30-72% savings vs on-demand
  • Spot VMs: Batch jobs, non-critical → up to 90% discount (preemptible)
  • Spot Node Pools (AKS): Auto-scale with spot nodes for cost
Scaling Efficiency
  • HPA + Cluster Autoscaler: AKS scales in/out based on demand (no idle nodes)
  • VPA (Vertical Pod Autoscaler): Right-size pod CPU/memory requests
Storage Optimization
  • Storage Lifecycle Policy: Hot → Cool → Archive auto-tiering (90% savings)
  • Orphaned disks: Silent cost killer → cleanup monthly
  • Unused snapshots: Delete after backup retention expires
Scheduling & Cleanup
  • Dev/Test shutdown: Schedule non-prod VMs off-hours (50%+ savings)
  • Idle Public IPs: Silent cost killers → remove unused IPs
  • Regular cleanup cadence: Weekly/monthly orphaned resource audit
FinOps Governance
  • Monthly cost reviews: Per team, per service
  • Chargeback model: Cost allocated to teams (drives accountability)
  • Budget alerts: Alert when spend approaches threshold
Say this in interview "Cost optimization: visibility (tag all resources), Azure Advisor (right-size), Reserved Instances (commit for savings), Spot VMs (90% discount), HPA/autoscaler (scale demand), lifecycle policy (archive old storage), dev/test shutdown (off-hours), cleanup orphaned disks/IPs. Monthly cost reviews per team. Chargeback drives accountability."
Important Concepts

Terraform: Multi-Environment & Multi-Subscription

Scale Terraform across environments without duplication. Root + child modules, separate state, provider aliases.

ArchitectureEnvironment isolation›
Root + Child Module Pattern
  • Child modules: Shared, reusable (networking, compute, database, storage)
  • Root modules: Per environment (dev, staging, prod) call child modules
  • Benefit: DRY - write module once, use in all environments
  • Example: modules/networking/ used by dev/main.tf, staging/main.tf, prod/main.tf
Per-Environment Directory Structure
  • dev/ → own backend, vars, tfvars
  • staging/ → own backend, vars, tfvars
  • prod/ → own backend, vars, tfvars
  • modules/ → shared (networking, compute, database)
  • Each environment: terraform init points to different backend (full isolation)
State Management
  • Remote state: Azure Blob Storage (not local laptop)
  • Separate state per environment: dev.tfstate, staging.tfstate, prod.tfstate
  • Blob Lease locking: Prevents concurrent applies (only 1 apply at a time)
  • Full isolation: Dev corruption doesn't affect prod
Multi-Subscription (Provider Aliases)
  • Problem: Deploy to different subscriptions without code duplication
  • Solution: Provider aliases
  • Example:
  • provider "azurerm" { alias = "prod" subscription_id = "xxx" }
  • provider "azurerm" { alias = "dev" subscription_id = "yyy" }
  • module "prod_network" { providers = { azurerm = azurerm.prod } }
  • Result: Single code, multiple subscriptions
Terragrunt (DRY Wrapper)
  • Problem: Repeated backend config, provider config in each environment
  • Solution: Terragrunt (meta-tool on top of Terraform)
  • terragrunt.hcl: Centralized backend, provider, inputs config
  • Benefit: Eliminates code duplication across environments
  • Example: One terragrunt.hcl for all envs instead of 3 separate backend.tf files
CI/CD Pipeline Integration
  • Stage 1 - fmt: Code formatting check
  • Stage 2 - validate: Syntax validation
  • Stage 3 - plan (PR): terraform plan shows what will change (artifact attached to PR)
  • Stage 4 - apply (merge): Only on merge to main. terraform apply what was planned.
  • Critical rule: No local terraform apply in prod. Pipeline only.
  • Plan artifact: What's shown in plan is exactly what gets applied (no surprises)
Workspaces (NOT Recommended)
  • Workspaces: Same subscription, different state files (dev.tfstate, prod.tfstate in same backend)
  • Limitation: Same subscription only ⚠️
  • Problem: No real isolation. State file selection is manual (easy to apply to wrong workspace)
  • Recommendation: Don't use for multi-environment. Use per-environment directories instead.
Say this in interview "Root + child modules: DRY. Per-env directories: dev/staging/prod own backends. Provider aliases: multi-sub without duplication. Remote state (Azure Blob): Blob Lease locking. Terragrunt: eliminate backend config duplication. CI/CD: fmt→validate→plan(PR)→apply(merge). NO local applies in prod. Workspaces: same-sub only, not recommended."
Important Concepts

Platform Security & Governance

Defense-in-depth. Zero trust. Verify everything. Assume breach. Encrypted always.

SecurityMulti-layer protection›
Defense-in-Depth Layers
  • DDoS Protection: Azure DDoS (L3/L4/L7 attacks)
  • Firewall: Application Gateway / Front Door (WAF rules)
  • WAF: SQL injection, XSS, bot attack prevention
  • NSG: Network Security Groups (Layer 4 rules)
  • Network Policy: Kubernetes pod-to-pod communication rules
  • Application Auth: OAuth 2.0, OIDC, managed identity
  • Result: Multi-layer defense. Breach at one layer ≠ full compromise
Zero Trust Architecture
  • Verify explicitly: Every request authenticated + authorized (not implicit trust)
  • Least privilege: Grant minimum permissions needed (deny by default)
  • Assume breach: Design as if perimeter is compromised. Monitor everything.
  • Segmentation: Trust network boundaries = 0. Verify at every hop.
Access Control
  • PIM (Privileged Identity Management): Just-in-time elevated access with approval workflow
  • Conditional Access: MFA + device compliance enforcement (no outdated devices)
  • Managed Identity: Apps authenticate as identity (no passwords, rotated automatically)
Policy & Compliance
  • Azure Policy: Enforce at management group level (no public IPs, require tags, restrict regions)
  • Defender for Cloud: Secure score + compliance dashboard (continuous monitoring)
  • Compliance frameworks: PCI DSS, ISO 27001, HIPAA, CIS Benchmarks (automated checks)
Data Protection
  • Encryption at-rest: CMK (Customer-Managed Keys) - you own the keys
  • Encryption in-transit: TLS 1.2+ (no unencrypted communication)
  • Key Vault: Centralized secrets, keys, certificates (no hardcoded secrets ever)
Audit & Logging
  • Audit Logs: Immutable control plane trail (who, what, when, where)
  • Log Analytics: Centralized logging (query control plane events)
  • Compliance audit: Prove security controls are working
Say this in interview "Defense-in-depth: DDoS→Firewall→WAF→NSG→Network Policy→App Auth. Zero trust: verify explicitly, least privilege, assume breach. PIM: JIT elevated access. Policy: enforce tags/IPs/regions at MG. Defender: secure score dashboard. Encryption: CMK at-rest, TLS in-transit. Key Vault: no hardcoded secrets. Audit logs: immutable trail. Compliance: PCI/ISO/HIPAA/CIS."
Important Concepts

Azure Networking

Hub-and-spoke design. Defense layers. Zero-trust segmentation. Enterprise connectivity.

ArchitectureNetwork design & security›
Hub-and-Spoke Topology
  • Hub: Contains Firewall + VPN Gateway + ExpressRoute + Bastion
  • Spokes: Workload VNets (web, app, database tiers)
  • VNet Peering: Hub ↔ Spoke connections (low latency, no extra cost)
  • UDR (User-Defined Routes): Forces all traffic through hub firewall (no bypass)
  • Benefit: Central control point, scalable, zero-trust
Subnet Design (Required Names)
  • GatewaySubnet: VPN/ExpressRoute gateway (required exact name)
  • AzureFirewallSubnet: Azure Firewall (required exact name)
  • AzureBastionSubnet: Bastion service (required exact name)
  • App Subnet: Application workloads (private, no public IPs)
  • Data Subnet: Databases, caching (private only)
  • Mgmt Subnet: Monitoring agents, automation
Network Segmentation
  • NSG (Network Security Group): Subnet + NIC level rules (allow/deny by port/IP/protocol)
  • Azure Firewall: Stateful inspection, threat intelligence filtering
  • Private Endpoints: PaaS gets private IP (Storage, SQL, Key Vault)
  • Private DNS Zone: Name resolution for private endpoints
  • Zero-trust result: Explicit allow, deny by default
Access & Security
  • Azure Bastion: No public IP on VMs. RDP/SSH over HTTPS through Bastion.
  • DDoS Protection: Standard plan = L3/L4 volumetric attack mitigation
  • WAF (Front Door/App Gateway): OWASP Top 10 protection (SQL injection, XSS, etc.)
Hybrid Connectivity
  • ExpressRoute: Dedicated private on-prem connection (enterprise grade, no internet)
  • VPN Gateway: Internet-based fallback (slower but cheaper)
  • On-prem to Azure: All traffic routed through hub firewall
Monitoring & Troubleshooting
  • Network Watcher: NSG flow logs (packet analysis, who talks to whom)
  • Connection Monitor: End-to-end connectivity testing
  • Topology: Visualize network relationships
  • Network Policy: Kubernetes pod-to-pod traffic control (same zero-trust principle)
Say this in interview "Hub-spoke: hub has Firewall+VPN/ER+Bastion, spokes for workloads. UDR forces traffic through firewall. Subnets: GatewaySubnet, AzureFirewallSubnet, AzureBastionSubnet (required names), App, Data, Mgmt. NSG at subnet+NIC level. Private Endpoints for PaaS. Bastion = no public IPs on VMs. Firewall = central inspection. ExpressRoute = dedicated on-prem. Network Policy = k8s pod segmentation."
Important Concepts

AKS Cluster Design, Deploy & Operate

Multi-cluster, multi-region, GitOps, HA, secure by default. Enterprise-grade operations.

OperationsComplete AKS strategy›
Cluster Design
  • System Pool: kube-system workloads only (dedicated, isolated)
  • User Pools: Application workloads (separate pools per workload type)
  • VM SKU Strategy: Compute-intensive → GPU nodes. Memory-heavy → high-memory SKUs.
  • Node Count: Min 3 nodes per pool (HA, rolling updates)
Networking
  • Azure CNI: Pods get VNet IPs (not overlay)
  • Network Policy: Calico/Azure Network Policy (pod-to-pod + namespace-to-namespace control)
  • NGINX Ingress: Routes external HTTP/HTTPS traffic to internal services, TLS termination
  • Private Cluster: API server not internet-accessible. Access via private endpoint or jumpbox.
Security
  • Managed Identity: Pods authenticate without passwords (auto-rotated)
  • RBAC: Cluster-level + namespace-level access control
  • AKV CSI Driver: Mount secrets from Key Vault (no sidecars needed)
  • ACR (Azure Container Registry): Private image registry with RBAC
  • Defender for Containers: Vulnerability scanning, runtime threat detection
Scaling Strategy
  • HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler): Scale pods based on CPU/memory/custom metrics
  • VPA (Vertical Pod Autoscaler): Right-size pod CPU/memory requests (recommendations)
  • Cluster Autoscaler: Auto-add nodes when pods pending. Remove idle nodes.
  • KEDA (Kubernetes Event Autoscaling): Scale based on events (queue depth, timers, etc.)
High Availability
  • Availability Zones: Spread nodes across AZ1/AZ2/AZ3
  • PDB (Pod Disruption Budget): Ensure N replicas during voluntary disruptions
  • Topology Spread: Spread pods across zones, nodes
  • Anti-affinity: Prevent all replicas on same node (failure isolation)
  • Readiness/Liveness Probes: Detect unhealthy pods, restart automatically
GitOps Deployment
  • Helm Charts: Templated deployments (values override per environment)
  • ArgoCD: Drift detection (actual vs declared state), auto-sync or manual approval
  • Pipeline: DEV → Staging → Manual Approval → PROD
  • Benefit: Git = source of truth. Rollback = git revert.
Observability
  • Prometheus + Grafana: Metrics + dashboards
  • EFK (Fluent Bit): Log aggregation to central workspace
  • Jaeger: Distributed tracing (request flow across services)
  • Container Insights: Azure native cluster health (nodes, pods, memory, CPU)
  • Datadog DAVIS: AI-driven anomaly detection
Operations & Maintenance
  • Node Image Auto-upgrade: Security patches applied automatically
  • Cluster Version Upgrades: AKS control plane updated (announced before upgrade)
  • Maintenance Windows: Schedule upgrades during low-traffic windows
  • Node Drain with PDB: Gracefully evict pods before node maintenance
Real-World Example: MindBridge
  • Scale: 10 AKS clusters, 5 regions (primary + DR per region)
  • Deployment: Azure DevOps CI/CD + ArgoCD GitOps
  • Pipeline: Commit → CI (build, scan, push image) → ArgoCD drift detection → PROD
  • HA: Multi-region, multi-cluster. Zone-spread within each cluster.
  • Scalability: HPA+Cluster Autoscaler handles traffic spikes
  • Security: Private clusters, managed identity, ACR, Network Policy
  • Observability: Prometheus+Grafana for metrics, Container Insights for health
Say this in interview "System+user pools, Azure CNI+Network Policy for segmentation. Private cluster, managed identity, AKV CSI. HPA+Cluster Autoscaler+KEDA for scaling. AZs+PDB+anti-affinity for HA. Helm+ArgoCD GitOps (DEV→PROD). Prometheus+EFK+Jaeger+Container Insights for observability. Auto-upgrade nodes, drain with PDB. Example: MindBridge 10 clusters, 5 regions, Azure DevOps+ArgoCD."
Important Concepts

3-Tier Application Architecture on Azure

Presentation → Application → Data. Separate tiers, auto-scale, secure, HA across regions.

ArchitectureComplete 3-tier design›
Architecture Flow Diagram
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ INTERNET (Users)                                                │
└────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┘
                             │ HTTPS
                    ┌────────▼────────┐
                    │  Front Door +   │
                    │  CDN + WAF      │
                    └────────┬────────┘
                             │
        ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
        │ PRESENTATION TIER  │ (VNet Subnet 1)   │
        │ ┌──────────────────▼─────────────────┐ │
        │ │  App Service / Static Web Apps     │ │
        │ │  (React, Vue, Angular frontend)    │ │
        │ └──────────────────┬─────────────────┘ │
        └────────────────────┼────────────────────┘
                             │ HTTPS (Private)
        ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
        │ APPLICATION TIER   │ (VNet Subnet 2)   │
        │ ┌──────────────────▼─────────────────┐ │
        │ │ App Service / AKS / Functions      │ │
        │ │ + API Management + App Insights    │ │
        │ │ (REST APIs, microservices)         │ │
        │ └──────────────────┬─────────────────┘ │
        └────────────────────┼────────────────────┘
                             │ Private Endpoint
        ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
        │ DATA TIER          │ (VNet Subnet 3)   │
        │ ┌──────────────────▼─────────────────┐ │
        │ │ SQL Database + Cosmos DB           │ │
        │ │ + Redis Cache + Blob Storage       │ │
        │ │ (No public IPs, private access)    │ │
        │ └────────────────────────────────────┘ │
        └─────────────────────────────────────────┘

Security: WAF filters attacks → NSGs restrict tier-to-tier → Private Endpoints isolate data
Scale: Front Door CDN caches → App Service auto-scales → DB read replicas
Presentation Tier (Web/UI)
  • Azure App Service: host frontend (Node, .NET, Python, etc.)
  • Azure Front Door: global CDN + DDoS protection, SSL/TLS termination
  • Azure Static Web Apps: serverless frontend (React, Vue, Angular), auto CI/CD from GitHub
  • Azure CDN: cache static content (JS, CSS, images) globally
  • Web Application Firewall (WAF): protect against SQL injection, XSS, DDoS
Application/Business Tier (API)
  • Azure App Service: host REST APIs / microservices
  • Azure Container Instances (ACI): containerized workloads (no orchestration overhead)
  • Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS): containerized microservices at scale
  • Azure Functions: serverless compute for event-driven APIs (auto-scale to 0)
  • Azure API Management: API gateway, rate limiting, authentication, versioning
  • Application Insights: request tracing, performance monitoring, error detection
Data Tier (Database & Storage)
  • Azure SQL Database: managed relational (SQL Server), auto-backups, geo-replication
  • Azure PostgreSQL/MySQL: managed open-source, read replicas, high availability
  • Azure Cosmos DB: NoSQL for distributed/global apps (multi-region writes)
  • Azure Storage: blob (files), table (NoSQL), queue (async messaging)
  • Redis Cache: in-memory cache for fast reads (session, query cache)
Networking & Security
  • Virtual Network (VNet): subnets for each tier (presentation, application, data)
  • Network Security Groups (NSG): firewall rules per subnet (allow inbound only from previous tier)
  • Service Endpoints: restrict PaaS access to specific VNets (no public exposure)
  • Private Endpoints: connect to PaaS over private network (no internet)
  • VPN Gateway / ExpressRoute: secure on-prem to cloud connectivity
Inter-Tier Communication
  • Presentation → Application: HTTPS only (TLS 1.2+)
  • Application → Data: Private Endpoints (VNet-scoped, no public IPs)
  • Async: Azure Service Bus / Event Grid (decoupled, event-driven)
  • Connection pooling: reuse DB connections (reduce latency)
Scalability & High Availability
  • Application Tier: App Service Auto-scale (scale out on CPU/memory/schedule)
  • AKS: HPA + Cluster Autoscaler (auto-scale pods & nodes)
  • Database: Read replicas + failover groups (primary + secondary region)
  • Traffic Manager / Front Door: route traffic, failover across regions
  • Availability Zones: spread resources across AZ1/AZ2/AZ3
Backup & Disaster Recovery
  • SQL Database: auto 7-day backup, geo-replication to secondary region
  • Blob Storage: Geo-Redundant Storage (GRS) copies to secondary region
  • Recovery Services Vault: backup VMs, recovery plan orchestration
  • RPO/RTO: typically RPO <1hr, RTO <15min for managed services
Real-World Example: E-Commerce Platform
  • Presentation: Front Door (CDN) → Static Web Apps (React SPA) + App Service (checkout)
  • Application: App Service (Product API) + AKS (Order Service) + Functions (Email notifications)
  • Data: SQL Database (orders, users) + Cosmos DB (product catalog, global reads) + Redis (session cache) + Blob Storage (images)
  • Security: WAF on Front Door, NSGs per tier, Private Endpoints for DB access, VPN for admin
  • Scale: Auto-scale App Service on traffic spike, HPA in AKS, read replicas for DB
  • DR: SQL geo-replication, GRS blob storage, Traffic Manager failover to secondary region
  • Monitoring: Application Insights traces end-to-end requests, alerts on errors/latency
Say this in interview "3-tier: Presentation (App Service/Static Apps + CDN + WAF), Application (App Service/AKS/Functions + API Mgmt + Insights), Data (SQL/Cosmos + Redis + Storage). Separate subnets per tier. NSGs restrict traffic (inbound only from previous tier). Private Endpoints for DB (no public IPs). Auto-scale on traffic. Geo-replication for DR. Example: e-commerce with Front Door → web apps → AKS services → SQL + Cosmos + cache."
Important Concepts

Multi-Region Architecture

Active-active or active-passive across regions. Geo-distributed redundancy, compliance, low latency. Design around RTO/RPO targets.

ArchitectureMulti-region patterns›
Multi-Region Architecture Flow Diagram
ACTIVE-ACTIVE PATTERN (Traffic Split Both Regions)
═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
          Internet Users (Global)
                    │
          ┌─────────┴─────────┐
          │ Traffic Manager/  │
          │ Front Door (DNS)  │
          └─────────┬─────────┘
                    │
        ┌───────────┴───────────┐
        │ Geo-Latency Routing   │
        │ (50% US, 50% EU, etc) │
        │
        ▼                       ▼

ACTIVE-PASSIVE PATTERN (Primary + Standby)
═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

HYBRID MULTI-REGION (Zone-Redundant + Geo-Distributed)
═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
          Internet Users
                    │
          Front Door + WAF + CDN
                    │
        ┌───────────┴───────────┐
        │                       │
        ▼                       ▼
┌──────────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────┐
│ REGION 1: US EAST    │  │ REGION 2: EUROPE     │
├──────────────────────┤  ├──────────────────────┤
│ Zone 1  │ Zone 2     │  │ Zone 1  │ Zone 2     │
│ ┌─────┐ ┌──────┐    │  │ ┌─────┐ ┌──────┐    │
│ │ AKS │ │ AKS  │    │  │ │ AKS │ │ AKS  │    │
│ └──┬──┘ └───┬──┘    │  │ └──┬──┘ └───┬──┘    │
│    └────┬───┘       │  │    └────┬───┘       │
│         │           │  │         │           │
│      Traffic Manager◄──┼─┐      │          │
│      (Zone Failover)   │         │           │
├──────────────────────┤  ├──────────────────────┤
│ SQL: Primary + Hot   │  │ SQL: Geo-Replica    │
│ Standby (AZ 1 + 2)   │  │ (Async, RPO ~30s)   │
│                      ◄──┤                      │
└──────────────────────┘  └──────────────────────┘

Failure Scenarios:
  • AZ1 fails → AZ2 takes over (within region, fast)
  • Region fails → Traffic Manager → failover to other region
Multi-Region Design Patterns
  • Active-Active: Both regions handle traffic simultaneously. All deployments identical. Requires geo-distributed data consistency.
  • Active-Passive: Primary region handles all traffic. Secondary region standby (warm or cold). Failover triggered on primary failure.
  • Active-Active-Passive: Two primary regions active, one secondary cold. Balance traffic between two, failover to cold.
  • Hub-and-Spoke Multi-Region: Hub in each region for shared services (firewall, gateway), spokes for workloads.
Data Replication Strategies
  • Synchronous Replication: RPO = 0 (zero data loss). Slower writes. Use for critical data (orders, financial).
  • Asynchronous Replication: RPO = minutes. Faster writes. Accept slight lag. Use for non-critical data (analytics, logs).
  • Geo-Replication (SQL/Cosmos): Automatic failover groups. Primary writes, secondary reads. RTO ~30sec.
  • Backup-Based DR: Regular snapshots to secondary region. RPO = backup interval (1-24hr). RTO = restore time (hours).
Database Failover Options
  • SQL Failover Groups: Automatic failover, read-only secondary in another region, DNS alias always points to primary
  • Cosmos DB Multi-Region Writes: Every region is primary (conflict resolution rules). Lowest latency.
  • PostgreSQL/MySQL Read Replicas: Primary in region A, read replica in region B. Manual promotion on failure.
  • Storage Geo-Replication: GRS = automatic backup copy. Access only on primary failure (RA-GRS = read-access always).
Traffic Routing & Failover
  • Azure Traffic Manager: DNS-level routing (performance, failover, weighted). TTL-based (slow failover ~5min).
  • Azure Front Door: Application-level routing (layer 7), faster failover (~1-2sec), geo-latency routing, WAF built-in.
  • Azure Load Balancer: Network-level (layer 4), regional only (not multi-region).
  • ExpressRoute Multi-Region: Dedicated private circuits to both regions. Failover via secondary circuit.
Deployment Strategy
  • Infrastructure as Code (Terraform/ARM): Same template deployed to both regions. Separate state per region. Avoid hardcoded region names.
  • Container Registries: Mirror images across regions (ACR replication). Pull locally (faster, cheaper).
  • Synchronized Configuration: Key Vault in each region with same secrets. App config service (drift detection).
  • Failover Testing: Regular failover drills. Document RTO/RPO for each component.
High Availability Patterns
  • Zone-Redundant Services: Resources spread across AZs within a region (automatic failover within region).
  • Cross-Region Redundancy: Resources in multiple regions (handles entire region failure).
  • Combination: Zones within each region + regions geo-distributed = "zone-redundant, geo-redundant".
  • Availability Zones + Multi-Region: Max resilience. AZ failure → failover within region. Region failure → failover to secondary region.
Cost Optimization
  • Data Transfer Costs: Inter-region replication is expensive. Compress, deduplicate, selective sync.
  • Reserve Instances: Buy RI commitments per region (1yr/3yr discounts).
  • Spot VMs: Cheap compute for batch/analytics in secondary region (acceptable to lose).
  • Bandwidth Optimization: Cache at CDN (Front Door), reduce DB queries, compress API responses.
Monitoring & Alerting
  • Health Probes: Monitor each region independently. Alert on degradation (latency >200ms, error rate >1%).
  • Application Insights: Multi-region dashboard. Track requests by region, error correlation.
  • Metrics to Watch: RPO lag (replication delay), failover time (RTO), inter-region latency, data consistency.
  • Alerting: Threshold breach → auto-failover or manual intervention (depends on criticality).
Real-World Example: Global SaaS Platform
  • Regions: Primary (US East), Secondary (Europe West), Tertiary (Asia Pacific)
  • Active-Active: Traffic split 50/50 between US+Europe via Traffic Manager. Asia routes to nearest (Asia or Europe).
  • Database: SQL with geo-replication. Async writes to Europe/Asia (RPO ~30sec). Sync failover group between US-Europe.
  • Caching: Redis in each region. Cache invalidation via Service Bus (eventual consistency).
  • Deployments: CI/CD deploys to US first, waits 5min for validation, auto-deploys to EU/APAC.
  • Failover: US region failure → Traffic Manager routes to Europe. DB promotes EU secondary to primary.
  • Cost: ~30% higher than single-region (replication, storage, data transfer). Mitigated by RIs and traffic shaping.
  • Compliance: US data in US, EU data in EU (GDPR). Separate Azure AD tenants per region.
Say this in interview "Active-active traffic split across regions via Traffic Manager/Front Door. Sync replication for critical data (orders), async for non-critical (logs). SQL geo-replication + failover groups (RTO ~30sec). Zone-redundant within each region for local HA. Terraform same template, separate state per region. Data transfer costs are real—compress and cache aggressively. Monitor replication lag and failover time. Example: 50/50 split US-Europe, async to APAC, CI/CD staged rollouts."
Important Concepts

Hub-Spoke Network Architecture

Central hub with shared services. Spokes for workloads. All traffic through firewall. Scalable, secure, centralized governance.

ArchitectureHub-spoke topology›
Architecture Flow Diagram
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ ON-PREMISES (10.0.0.0/8)                                     │
└────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
             │ ExpressRoute / VPN
             │
    ┌────────▼──────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ HUB VNet (172.16.0.0/16)                      │
    │ ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
    │ │ GatewaySubnet (172.16.1.0/27)             ││
    │ │ ├─ VPN Gateway                            ││
    │ │ └─ ExpressRoute Gateway                   ││
    │ └────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
    │ ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
    │ │ AzureFirewallSubnet (172.16.2.0/26)       ││
    │ │ └─ Azure Firewall (all traffic routed)    ││
    │ └────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
    │ ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
    │ │ AzureBastionSubnet (172.16.3.0/26)        ││
    │ │ └─ Azure Bastion (SSH/RDP access)         ││
    │ └────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
    │ ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
    │ │ Management Subnet (172.16.4.0/24)         ││
    │ │ └─ DNS, monitoring, shared services       ││
    │ └────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
    └────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┘
             │
    ┌────────┴──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐
    │ UDR Routes all traffic through Firewall           │
    │ (0.0.0.0/0 → Firewall IP)                        │
    │
    │ VNet Peering (Bidirectional, Allow Forwarded)
    │
    ▼                      ▼                ▼              ▼
┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐
│ SPOKE 1    │  │ SPOKE 2    │  │ SPOKE 3    │  │ SPOKE 4    │
│Production │  │Production  │  │Dev/Test    │  │Shared Svc  │
│Web        │  │Database    │  │            │  │            │
│           │  │            │  │            │  │            │
│10.1.0.0   │  │10.2.0.0    │  │10.3.0.0    │  │10.4.0.0    │
│           │  │            │  │            │  │            │
│Subnets:   │  │Subnets:    │  │Subnets:    │  │Subnets:    │
│-Web tier  │  │-Data tier  │  │-App tier   │  │-Monitor    │
│-App tier  │  │-Cache tier │  │-DB tier    │  │-Log/Backup │
│-Cache tier│  │            │  │-Cache      │  │-Shared DNS │
│           │  │NSGs block  │  │            │  │            │
│NSGs allow │  │app-to-db   │  │Isolated    │  │Accessible  │
│public in  │  │except port │  │from prod   │  │from all    │
│& app only │  │1433        │  │            │  │            │
└────────────┘  └────────────┘  └────────────┘  └────────────┘

Traffic Flow: Spoke1 → Spoke2
  1. App in Spoke1 (10.1.1.5) initiates connection to DB in Spoke2 (10.2.2.5)
  2. UDR matches 10.2.2.0/24 → routes to Firewall (172.16.2.4)
  3. Firewall checks policy (App Tier → Data Tier allowed? Yes)
  4. Firewall forwards packet to Spoke2 via VNet Peering
  5. DB responds through Firewall back to Spoke1
Hub Components & Services
  • Azure Firewall: Central inspection (network + application layer). All traffic forced through UDR.
  • VPN Gateway: Connect on-premises networks securely. Site-to-site or point-to-site VPN.
  • ExpressRoute Gateway: Dedicated private circuit to on-prem (higher bandwidth, lower latency than VPN).
  • Azure Bastion: SSH/RDP access to VMs without public IPs. Single bastion subnet (required name).
  • DNS (Private Resolver): Forward on-prem DNS queries, resolve Azure VNets via conditional forwarding.
  • Application Gateway / Load Balancer: Ingress for workload traffic.
Hub Subnet Layout (Required Names)
  • GatewaySubnet: VPN/ExpressRoute gateway (recommended /27 or larger)
  • AzureFirewallSubnet: Azure Firewall (dedicated, /26 minimum)
  • AzureBastionSubnet: Bastion (dedicated, /26 minimum)
  • Management: Management tools, monitoring agents
  • DNS: Private DNS resolver (optional, for hybrid resolution)
Spoke VNet Design
  • One VNet per workload: App spoke, DB spoke, Dev/Test spoke (isolation + scale)
  • Peering to Hub: Enabled in both directions. Allow forwarded traffic + allow gateway transit.
  • Subnets per spoke: App tier, database tier, cache tier (segmentation)
  • NSGs: Enforce network policies at spoke level. Hub firewall = defense in depth.
Routing & User-Defined Routes (UDRs)
  • Hub to Spoke: Firewall routes all inter-spoke traffic through UDR. Destination = spoke subnet, next hop = firewall private IP.
  • Spoke to Spoke: No direct peering. Must go hub → firewall → destination spoke.
  • Spoke to On-Prem: UDR directs to ExpressRoute/VPN gateway in hub.
  • UDR Best Practice: Create route table for "All Traffic" 0.0.0.0/0 → Firewall. Apply to all spoke subnets.
Security Model (Defense in Depth)
  • Perimeter: Firewall in hub filters all ingress/egress
  • Network segmentation: NSGs per subnet (spoke level)
  • Private Endpoints: PaaS services (SQL, Cosmos, Storage) accessed via private IPs (no internet)
  • Service Endpoints: Restrict PaaS to specific VNets (firewall whitelist)
  • No Public IPs on VMs: Access via Bastion only (in hub)
Multi-Region Hub-Spoke
  • Hub per region: Hub-spoke in region A, separate hub-spoke in region B
  • Inter-hub VNet peering: Connect hubs across regions (traffic between regions routed hub-to-hub)
  • Global Firewall Policy: Same rules in both hubs (consistency)
  • DNS replication: Private DNS zones replicated across regions
Cost Optimization
  • Firewall: ~$1.25/hour + data processing ($0.016/GB). Reduce costs by: selective forwarding, traffic filtering, packet compression
  • VNet Peering: No egress charges within regions. Inter-region peering charged ~$0.02/GB
  • ExpressRoute: Lower cost per GB than VPN for high volume. Fixed monthly fee + data transfer.
  • Bastion: ~$5/hour. Shared across multiple VMs (centralized access).
Monitoring & Troubleshooting
  • Network Watcher: Packet capture, flow logs, topology visualization
  • Firewall Logs: Log all allowed/denied flows. Query via Log Analytics.
  • NSG Flow Logs: Track network policies. Identify blocked traffic.
  • Connectivity Check: Test path from spoke to spoke, validate firewall rules
  • Latency: Inter-spoke latency ~1-2ms (through hub). Acceptable for most workloads.
Real-World Example: Multi-Tier Enterprise
  • Hub: Central firewall, VPN to on-prem, Bastion for admin access, DNS resolver
  • Spoke 1 (Production Web): Frontend subnet (web tier) + backend subnet (API) + cache
  • Spoke 2 (Production DB): SQL databases, encrypted at rest, accessible only from app spoke
  • Spoke 3 (Dev/Test): Separate VNet, same structure, isolated from prod, cheaper compute
  • Spoke 4 (Shared Services): Monitoring, logging, backup, shared by all spokes
  • Routing: All traffic through firewall. Exception: DNS queries to resolver in hub.
  • Security: WAF on Application Gateway (app spoke), NSGs per subnet, Private Endpoints for storage
  • Scale: Add new spoke → create VNet, peer to hub, add UDR, add firewall rules. No downtime.
Say this in interview "Hub-spoke: central hub with firewall, VPN, ExpressRoute, Bastion, DNS. Spokes for workloads (app, DB, dev). All inter-spoke traffic through firewall via UDRs. GatewaySubnet, AzureFirewallSubnet, AzureBastionSubnet (required names). NSGs at subnet level. Private Endpoints for PaaS. No public IPs on VMs—access via Bastion. Multi-region: hub per region, inter-hub peering. Cost: firewall + data transfer. Example: Prod web spoke, prod DB spoke, dev/test spoke, shared services spoke—all routed through central firewall."
Important Concepts

Production-ready subscriptions with governance, security, networking, compliance built-in. Fast deployment + enterprise control.

  Policy Engine Evaluates
  ├─ Encryption required? → Deny (fail)
  ├─ Tags present? → Deny (fail)

  ├─ Audit Log → Central Log Analytics
  └─ Tags → Cost Management (Finance cost center billed)

MULTI-REGION LANDING ZONES

                  (Cross-region traffic)
Landing Zone Overview
  • Definition: Pre-configured, production-ready Azure subscription with governance, security, networking, and compliance built-in
  • Purpose: Enable fast app deployment while maintaining compliance, cost control, and security standards
  • Benefits: Reduced time-to-deploy, consistent governance, compliance out-of-box, cost visibility, security posture
  • When to use: Enterprise deployments, multi-team environments, compliance-heavy workloads (healthcare, finance, government)
Landing Zone Core Components
  • Management Subscription: Central governance (policies, blueprints, audit logging)
  • Connectivity Subscription: Hub VNet, firewalls, VPN/ExpressRoute gateways, DNS
  • Identity Subscription: Azure AD, identity governance, conditional access (optional)
  • Landing Zone Subscriptions: Workload subscriptions (app, database, dev/test), one per team or project
Management Groups & Subscription Hierarchy
  • Root: Tenant-level policies (apply to all subscriptions)
  • Management: Audit logs, backup vaults, policy definitions
  • Connectivity: Hub VNet, gateways, firewall, DNS resolver
  • Identity: Azure AD config, conditional access, MFA policies
  • Landing Zones: Production (prod subscriptions), Non-Production (dev/test), Sandbox (experimentation)
  • Policy Inheritance: Policies at parent MG apply to all children (enforced globally)
Policy as Code (Governance Framework)
  • Azure Policy: Enforce compliance (deny non-compliant, audit-only, modify)
    • Deny public IP on VMs (whitelist exceptions)
    • Require encryption at rest (storage, databases)
    • Enforce tagging (cost center, environment, owner)
    • Prevent deletion of critical resources
  • Azure Blueprints: Template deployments (VNet, NSGs, policies, RBAC applied together)
  • Management Groups Policies: Inherited by all subscriptions below (apply once, enforce globally)
Network Design (Hub-Spoke Multi-Subscription)
  • Hub Connectivity Subscription: Central firewall, VPN/ExpressRoute, DNS resolver
  • Spoke Landing Zone Subscriptions: Each has own VNet, peered to hub via hub subscription
  • Routing: All inter-spoke traffic through hub firewall (UDRs)
  • Isolation: NSGs per subnet, no spoke-to-spoke direct peering
  • Multi-region: Hub per region, inter-hub peering for cross-region traffic
Identity & Access Management (RBAC)
  • Subscription Owner: Team lead (can delegate RBAC)
  • Contributor: Developers (create/modify resources)
  • Reader: Auditors, read-only access
  • Role-Based (Custom Roles): App Owner, Database Admin, Network Admin (fine-grained)
  • Conditional Access: MFA required for production, IP restrictions for sensitive resources
Cost Management & Chargeback
  • Tags Mandatory: Environment (prod/dev), cost center, owner, app (required on all resources)
  • Azure Cost Management: Dashboard per subscription, alerts on budget overruns
  • Reserved Instances: Buy RIs per subscription (1yr/3yr commitments, cost saving)
  • Spot VMs: Non-critical workloads (acceptable to lose) use Spot for 60-90% savings
  • Chargeback: Finance can map tags to cost centers, bill teams accordingly
Compliance & Monitoring
  • Policy Compliance Dashboard: View which subscriptions violate which policies
  • Azure Security Center: Vulnerability scanning, threat detection, compliance scoring
  • Audit Logging: All resource changes logged to central Log Analytics (cannot be modified)
  • Alerts: Non-compliance triggers alert, manual remediation or auto-remediation via policy
  • Compliance Frameworks: CIS, PCI-DSS, HIPAA, SOC2 (policies enforce requirements)
Real-World Example: Multi-Department Enterprise
  • Management Subscription: Azure Policy for tagging, encryption, NSG rules. Blueprints for standard VNet deployments.
  • Connectivity Subscription: Hub VNet (172.16.0.0/16), Azure Firewall, VPN to on-prem, DNS resolver
  • Landing Zone: Finance (Prod): Finance-Web (10.1.0.0/16), Finance-DB (10.2.0.0/16). Policy: MFA required, IP restrictions (finance building only), audit all DB access
  • Landing Zone: HR (Prod): HR-App (10.3.0.0/16), HR-DB (10.4.0.0/16). Policy: Encryption mandatory, no public IPs, backup required (HIPAA)
  • Landing Zone: Dev (Non-Prod): Dev-General (10.5.0.0/16). Cheaper SKUs allowed, auto-shutdown VMs at 7pm. Tagging required but less strict than prod.
  • Sandbox: 10.6.0.0/16, free to experiment, no compliance requirements, auto-purge after 30 days
  • Monitoring: All subscriptions log to central Log Analytics workspace (Finance's, HR's, Dev's logs visible to ops team)
  • Cost Visibility: Each subscription sees own costs. Finance team sees all. Cost tags map to departments.
Say this in interview "Landing zone = pre-configured production-ready subscription with governance built-in. Hub connectivity subscription (firewall, VPN, ExpressRoute, DNS). Hub-spoke network per subscription, all traffic through firewall. Management groups hierarchy enforces policies globally. Azure Policy denies non-compliant resources (encryption, tagging). RBAC per subscription (owner, contributor, reader). Tags mandatory (cost center, environment, owner). Audit logs centralized, immutable. Cost management via tags and chargeback. Example: Finance prod subscription, HR prod subscription, Dev subscription, Sandbox—all inherit policy from management group."
Important Concepts

Migration Strategy: On-Prem → Azure

Assess, plan, migrate, optimize. Phased or big bang. Lift-shift, refactor, or rearchitect. Minimize downtime, manage risk.

OperationsCloud migration roadmap›
Migration Journey Flow Diagram
MIGRATION PHASES (4-6 Months Typical)
═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

PHASE 1: ASSESS (2-4 Weeks)
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ Azure Migrate (Discovery)        │
├─────────────────────────────────┤
│ 1. Network scan (find all VMs)   │
│ 2. OS/app/db inventory           │
│ 3. Dependency analysis           │
│ 4. Performance baseline          │
│ 5. Cost estimation               │
└──────────────┬──────────────────┘
               │ Output: Readiness Report
               ▼
        On-Prem Assets:
        ├─ 150 Windows VMs
        ├─ 200 Linux VMs
        ├─ 10 SQL Databases
        ├─ 5 Oracle DBs (third-party tools)
        ├─ 100 app dependencies
        └─ Estimated Azure cost: $800k/year vs $2.8M on-prem

PHASE 2: PLAN (2-4 Weeks)
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ Migration Strategy & Roadmap     │
├─────────────────────────────────┤
│ 1. Grouping strategy             │
│    ├─ Wave 1: Dev/Test (low risk)│
│    ├─ Wave 2: Non-Critical (mid) │
│    ├─ Wave 3-5: Critical Prod    │
│    └─ Wave 6: Decommission       │
│                                   │
│ 2. Migration patterns per app    │
│    ├─ 200 apps: Lift-Shift (IaaS)│
│    ├─ 40 apps: Refactor (PaaS)   │
│    ├─ 10 apps: Rearchitect       │
│    └─ 5 apps: Keep on-prem       │
│                                   │
│ 3. Dependencies mapped           │
│    ├─ Finance app depends on DB  │
│    ├─ HR app depends on AD       │
│    └─ All go together (1 wave)   │
└──────────────┬──────────────────┘
               │ Output: Migration Roadmap
               ▼
        Migration Schedule:
        ├─ Wave 1 (Week 1-4): 50 VMs
        ├─ Wave 2 (Week 5-8): 75 VMs
        ├─ Wave 3 (Week 9-12): 100 VMs (prod, parallel cutover)
        ├─ Wave 4 (Week 13-16): 80 VMs
        ├─ Wave 5 (Week 17-20): 45 VMs
        └─ Wave 6 (Week 21-24): Decommission on-prem

PHASE 3: MIGRATE (12+ Weeks)
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ For Each Wave:                  │
├─────────────────────────────────┤
│ 1. Replicate (ASR/DMS)          │
│    └─ Continuous sync on-prem→ Azure
│ 2. Validate (test failover)     │
│    └─ App works in Azure? DB ok?
│ 3. Cutover (DNS, failover)      │
│    └─ Point DNS to Azure        │
│ 4. Monitor (72 hours)           │
│    └─ Watch logs, performance   │
│ 5. Decommission (after 4 weeks) │
│    └─ Shutdown on-prem servers  │
└──────────────┬──────────────────┘
               │
               ▼ (Repeat for each wave)

PHASE 4: OPTIMIZE (Ongoing)
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ Right-Sizing & Cost Optimization│
├─────────────────────────────────┤
│ 1. Monitor 2 weeks in Azure     │
│ 2. Azure Advisor recommends     │
│    smaller SKUs (save 20-40%)   │
│ 3. Buy Reserved Instances       │
│    (save 30-60% on compute)     │
│ 4. Archive old data             │
│    (save 99% on storage)        │
│ 5. Retire on-prem (cancel lic)  │
│    (save 60% overall)           │
└─────────────────────────────────┘


MIGRATION PATTERNS (Choose Per App)
═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

│ backups, scaling (cost efficient)

│ Effort: ⭐⭐⭐ (3-6 months)      │

│ Timeline: SLOWEST               │
│                                  │
│ Use For:                         │
