│ ├─ Long-term ROI needed         │
│ └─ Want cloud-native benefits   │
│                                  │
│ Post-migrate: Best cost + scale │
│ (pay-per-use, auto-scale to 0)  │
└──────────────────────────────────┘


CUTOVER STRATEGY (Zero/Low Downtime)
═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

Timeline: Pre-cutover (1-4 weeks) → Cutover Window (2-4 hours) → Post-cutover (72hrs)

PRE-CUTOVER (Parallel Running)
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ On-Prem ACTIVE                  │
│ │ (Primary traffic)             │
│ └─ Replicated to Azure (warm)   │
│                                  │
│ Users → On-Prem (normal)        │
│ Data → Azure (continuous sync)  │
│                                  │
│ Duration: 1-4 weeks             │
│ Goal: Validate Azure works      │
│        before cutover           │
└─────────────────────────────────┘

CUTOVER WINDOW (2-4 Hours)
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ Step 1: Stop writes on-prem     │
│ Step 2: Final data sync         │
│ Step 3: Smoke tests (5 min)     │
│ Step 4: DNS cutover             │
│         Old: app.company.com→  │
│             on-prem             │
│         New: app.company.com→  │
│             Azure (IPs)         │
│ Step 5: Monitor (30 min)        │
│                                  │
│ Downtime: ~5-30 minutes         │
│ (DNS propagation TTL)           │
└─────────────────────────────────┘

ROLLBACK PLAN (If Issues)
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ Issues detected → Revert DNS    │
│                 → Point back to │
│                    on-prem      │
│                                  │
│ Rollback time: ~5 min           │
│ Data loss: ~5 min of writes     │
│ (if final sync caught it)       │
│                                  │
│ After rollback:                 │
│ ├─ Debug issues                 │
│ ├─ Fix in Azure                 │
│ └─ Re-test, retry cutover       │
└─────────────────────────────────┘

POST-CUTOVER (Validation)
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ Monitor 72 hours:               │
│ ├─ Application logs             │
│ ├─ Database performance         │
│ ├─ Network latency              │
│ ├─ User reported issues         │
│ └─ Alert thresholds             │
│                                  │
│ If all good after 72hrs:        │
│ ├─ Stop on-prem replication     │
│ ├─ Take final on-prem backup    │
│ ├─ Wait 4 weeks (confidence)    │
│ └─ Decommission on-prem servers │
└─────────────────────────────────┘


MIGRATION STRATEGY PATTERNS
═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

BIG BANG (All at Once)
Risk: ⭐⭐⭐ High
Timeline: Compressed (1-2 nights)
Cutover: 4-8 hours
Best for: Small environments (<50 VMs), high downtime tolerance

PHASED (Waves)
Risk: ⭐⭐ Medium
Timeline: Extended (3-6 months)
Cutover: Each wave 2-4 hours
Best for: Large environments (100-1000 VMs), business continuity critical

PILOT + VALIDATE (Safe Approach)
Risk: ⭐ Low
Timeline: Very long (6-12 months)
Cutover: Each step tested
Best for: First cloud migration, high compliance needs, complex dependencies
Migration Overview
  • Goal: Move applications, data, infrastructure from on-premises to Azure with minimal downtime and risk
  • Timeline: Weeks to months (depends on scale, complexity, resources)
  • Cost: Discovery free (Azure Migrate), migration tools vary ($1-50k for tools), Azure compute/storage costs post-migration
  • Risk: Downtime, data loss, performance issues if not planned carefully
Migration Phases
  • Phase 1 (Assess): Discover on-prem resources, estimate Azure costs, identify dependencies
  • Phase 2 (Plan): Group workloads, define migration order, choose patterns, set RTO/RPO targets
  • Phase 3 (Migrate): Move VMs, databases, applications, validate in Azure
  • Phase 4 (Optimize): Right-size, retire on-prem, optimize costs, monitor performance
Migration Patterns (Choose One Per Workload)
  • Lift-and-Shift (Rehost): Move VM as-is to Azure. Fastest (2-4 weeks), lowest risk, highest cost (no optimization). Use for: legacy apps, time-critical migrations
  • Refactor (Replatform): Minimal code changes, modernize OS/database. Medium effort (4-12 weeks), moderate cost. Use for: outdated databases, OS upgrades needed
  • Rearchitect (Reimagine): Redesign for cloud (microservices, containers). Highest effort (3-6 months), best cost optimization. Use for: strategic apps, long-term ROI
  • Rebuild (Reconfigure): Rewrite from scratch using cloud-native tech. Highest effort & cost, highest value. Use for: competitive advantage, new capabilities needed
Migration Tools & Services
  • Azure Migrate: Discovery, assessment, replication (free). Supports VMs, databases, web apps, physical servers
  • Azure Site Recovery (ASR): Continuous replication, failover testing, orchestrated cutover. RTO ~1-2 hours, RPO ~minutes
  • Azure Database Migration Service: Migrate SQL, MySQL, PostgreSQL, Oracle. Near-zero downtime with transactional consistency
  • Data Box: Offline data transfer (petabytes). Ship physical device to datacenter if WAN bandwidth limited
  • ExpressRoute: Dedicated network circuit. Better than VPN for large migrations (dedicated 1-100 Gbps bandwidth)
  • Azure DataFactory: ETL for data pipeline migrations. Scheduled or event-triggered data copies
Pre-Migration Assessment
  • Discovery: Scan on-prem network, identify all VMs, dependencies, OS versions, database types
  • Assessment: Estimate Azure VM SKUs (CPU/memory), monthly costs, on-prem vs Azure cost comparison
  • Dependency Analysis: Map app-to-app, app-to-database dependencies. Identify migration groups (apps that must migrate together)
  • Readiness Check: Which apps are Azure-ready? Which need refactoring? Which can't migrate (vendor lock-in)?
  • Report: Executive summary, cost analysis, risk matrix, migration roadmap
Migration Strategy Patterns
  • Big Bang: Migrate all at once. High risk, tight cutover window (1-2 nights), fastest to completion. Use for: small environments, high tolerance for downtime
  • Phased: Migrate 10-20% per wave. Lower risk, extended timeline (months), staged approach. Use for: large environments, business continuity critical
  • Pilot + Validation: Migrate 1-2 apps first, validate, then roll out rest. Lowest risk, longest timeline. Use for: first-time cloud adopters, complex dependencies
  • Hybrid: Some apps on-prem, some in Azure (multi-cloud). Complex, ongoing management burden. Use for: compliance restrictions, vendor dependencies, gradual transition
Cutover Strategy (Minimize Downtime)
  • Pre-cutover: Run parallel (on-prem + Azure) for validation (1-4 weeks)
  • Cutover Window: DNS failover (app points to Azure), data sync final copy, smoke tests
  • Rollback Plan: If issues, revert DNS to on-prem (RTO ~5 min), rollback database (RPO = pre-cutover snapshot)
  • Post-Cutover: Monitor for 48-72hrs, watch application logs, database performance, network connectivity
  • Decommission: After 4 weeks with no issues, shutdown on-prem servers, cancel licenses
Post-Migration Optimization (Right-Sizing)
  • Right-Size Compute: Run workloads on Azure for 2 weeks, use Azure Advisor to recommend smaller SKUs (save 20-40%)
  • Reserved Instances: Buy 1yr/3yr RIs for predictable workloads (save 30-60% on compute)
  • Spot VMs: Non-critical workloads use Spot (save 60-90% but risk disruption)
  • Storage Optimization: Archive old data (99.9% cheaper than hot), compress backups, deduplicate
  • Cost Management: Use Azure Cost Management to track spend, set budgets, alerts
Real-World Example: Financial Services (1000+ VMs)
  • Current State: On-prem datacenter (expensive, aging), vendor lock-in, compliance requirements (SOC2)
  • Goal: Migrate to Azure within 12 months, 30% cost reduction
  • Strategy: Phased migration (10 waves, 100 VMs/wave)
  • Wave 1 (Pilot): 10 non-critical VMs (web tier), validate tools, process, cutover procedure (4 weeks)
  • Wave 2-5 (Non-Critical Apps): Dev/test/analytics workloads (8 weeks)
  • Wave 6-9 (Business Services): Internal services, HR, finance apps (8 weeks)
  • Wave 10 (Production): Customer-facing apps, databases, cutover during maintenance window (2 weeks)
  • Tools Used: Azure Migrate (discovery), ASR (replication), DMS (SQL migration), ExpressRoute (network)
  • Timeline: 12 months total (Assess 4w → Plan 4w → Execute 12w → Optimize 4w)
  • Cost: Migration tools $50k + Azure infrastructure $800k/yr (vs $2.8M on-prem)
  • Result: 30% cost savings, 99.9% uptime, SOC2 compliance achieved, retire on-prem datacenter
Say this in interview "Migration: assess on-prem (Azure Migrate discovery, dependencies), plan migration order (phased vs big bang), choose pattern per app (lift-shift, refactor, rearchitect). Use ASR for VMs (near-zero downtime), DMS for databases. Run parallel during cutover (DNS failover, rollback plan). Post-migration: right-size VMs, buy RIs, archive old data, optimize storage. Example: 1000 VMs, phased 10 waves, 12 months, 30% cost savings. ExpressRoute for bandwidth, pilot + validate before full rollout."
Important Concepts

AKS Cluster Upgrade: Step-by-Step with Commands

Control plane upgrade (Azure managed) → Node pool upgrades (sequential). Minimal downtime, graceful pod eviction, validate at each step.

OperationsComplete upgrade workflow›
Pre-Upgrade Checklist (Week Before)
# 1. Check current cluster version
az aks show --resource-group myResourceGroup --name myCluster \
  --query kubernetesVersion

# 2. Check available upgrade versions
az aks get-upgrades --resource-group myResourceGroup --name myCluster

# 3. Verify node pools
az aks nodepool list --resource-group myResourceGroup --cluster-name myCluster

# 4. Check pod distribution (no single-node pods)
kubectl get pods -A --field-selector=status.phase=Running -o wide

# 5. Backup etcd (CRITICAL!)
kubectl exec -it -n kube-system \
  $(kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l component=etcd -o name | head -1) \
  -- etcdctl --endpoints=127.0.0.1:2379 snapshot save /tmp/etcd-backup.db

# 6. Review breaking changes for target version
# https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/using-api/deprecation-guide/
Pre-Upgrade Validation (Day Of)
# 1. Verify cluster health
kubectl get nodes
# Expected: All nodes Ready

# 2. Check for deprecated APIs
kubectl api-resources --verbs=list --output=wide | grep -i deprecat

# 3. Verify Pod Disruption Budgets (critical for zero-downtime)
kubectl get pdb -A
# Expected: All critical apps have PDB with MIN AVAILABLE ≥ 1

# 4. Check for stuck pods
kubectl get pods -A --field-selector=status.phase=Pending

# 5. Drain test on one node (verify pods evict gracefully)
kubectl cordon <node-name>
kubectl drain <node-name> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
# Wait... verify pods move to other nodes
kubectl uncordon <node-name>
Phase 1: Control Plane Upgrade (Azure Managed)
# 1. Initiate control plane upgrade ONLY (nodes stay old)
az aks upgrade --resource-group myResourceGroup \
  --name myCluster \
  --kubernetes-version 1.29.0 \
  --control-plane-only \
  --no-wait

# 2. Monitor upgrade progress (typical: 15-30 min)
az aks show --resource-group myResourceGroup --name myCluster \
  --query "kubernetesVersion,agentPoolProfiles[0].kubernetesVersion"

# Expected:
# "kubernetesVersion": "1.29.0"  (control plane upgraded)
# "kubernetesVersion": "1.28.0"  (nodes still old)

# 3. Watch for API server recovery (may be unavailable ~30 sec)
watch -n 5 'kubectl get nodes && echo "---" && kubectl get componentstatuses'

# 4. Verify control plane healthy
kubectl cluster-info
kubectl get componentstatuses
# Expected: All Healthy, Ready
Phase 2: Node Pool Upgrade (Sequential)
# 1. List node pools
az aks nodepool list --resource-group myResourceGroup \
  --cluster-name myCluster --output table

# 2. Upgrade SYSTEM pool first (one pool at a time!)
az aks nodepool upgrade --resource-group myResourceGroup \
  --cluster-name myCluster \
  --name system \
  --kubernetes-version 1.29.0 \
  --no-wait

# 3. Monitor node pool upgrade (takes 10-30 min per node)
watch -n 5 'az aks nodepool show --resource-group myResourceGroup \
  --cluster-name myCluster --name system | grep -i "provisioningState\|powerState"'

# 4. Watch nodes cordoning + pod eviction
kubectl get nodes -w -L kubernetes.io/hostname
kubectl get pods -A -w

# Expected: Nodes become NotReady → Evicted → Reboot → Ready

# 5. Upgrade other pools one-by-one
az aks nodepool upgrade --resource-group myResourceGroup \
  --cluster-name myCluster \
  --name compute \
  --kubernetes-version 1.29.0 \
  --no-wait

# 6. Verify ALL nodes upgraded
kubectl get nodes -o wide
# Expected: All nodes show v1.29.0 in VERSION column
Post-Upgrade Validation (Critical!)
# 1. Verify all nodes Ready
kubectl get nodes
# Expected: All NotReady → Ready (within 5 min)

# 2. Verify all system pods Running
kubectl get pods -n kube-system
# Expected: coredns, kube-proxy, azure-ip-masq-agent all Running

# 3. Health check
kubectl cluster-info

# 4. Check for crash loops
kubectl get pods -A | grep -E "0/1|CrashLoop|Pending"
# Expected: None (except expected pending)

# 5. Verify deployment health
kubectl get deployment -A
# Expected: READY X/X, AVAILABLE X/X for all

# 6. Test app functionality
curl -I https://myapp.example.com
# Expected: HTTP 200 OK

# 7. Check events for errors
kubectl get events -A --sort-by='.lastTimestamp' | tail -20

# 8. Monitor metrics (should be baseline)
kubectl top nodes
kubectl top pods -A

# 9. Verify persistent volumes still accessible
kubectl get pv
kubectl get pvc -A
# Expected: All Bound
Troubleshooting (If Issues)
# ⚠️ NOTE: Rollback is NOT supported by AKS!
# Control plane cannot be downgraded
# Instead: Mitigation strategies

# 1. If workload broken by API changes:
# Check deprecated APIs:
kubectl api-resources | grep -i deprecat

# Update manifests (e.g., apps/v1beta1 → apps/v1)
sed -i 's/apiVersion: apps\/v1beta1/apiVersion: apps\/v1/' *.yaml
kubectl apply -f updated-manifest.yaml

# 2. If pod not scheduling:
kubectl describe pod <pod-name> -n <namespace>
# Look for: FailedScheduling, Pending

# 3. If node stuck NotReady:
kubectl get node <node-name> -o yaml | grep -i condition

# Reboot node:
az aks nodepool scale --resource-group myResourceGroup \
  --cluster-name myCluster --name <pool> --node-count <new-count>

# 4. If etcd corrupted (rare):
# Restore from backup:
kubectl exec -it -n kube-system <etcd-pod> \
  -- etcdctl --endpoints=127.0.0.1:2379 snapshot restore /tmp/etcd-backup.db
Real-World Example: Production Cluster (5 Nodes)
TIMELINE: Maintenance 2 AM - 4 AM (low-traffic window)

PRE-UPGRADE (1 week before):
├─ Review 1.29 breaking changes
├─ Test in DEV cluster (validate workloads compatible)
├─ Backup etcd snapshot
└─ Notify stakeholders of maintenance window

DAY OF (2:00 AM):
├─ Control plane upgrade starts
│  ├─ az aks upgrade (control-plane-only flag)
│  ├─ API server briefly unavailable (~30 sec) - EXPECTED
│  └─ kubectl commands may fail - retry
├─ 2:15 AM: Control plane 1.29.0 ✓
└─ Nodes still 1.28.0

NODE UPGRADES (2:15 AM - 3:30 AM):
├─ System pool (3 nodes)
│  ├─ Cordon node-1 → evict pods → reboot → uncordon
│  ├─ Repeat for nodes 2, 3
│  └─ Duration: 15 min
├─ Compute pool (5 nodes)
│  ├─ Same process
│  └─ Duration: 30-40 min
└─ 3:30 AM: All nodes 1.29.0 ✓

VALIDATION (3:30 AM - 4:00 AM):
├─ kubectl get nodes → All Ready ✓
├─ kubectl get pods -A → All Running ✓
├─ curl myapp.com → HTTP 200 ✓
├─ kubectl top nodes → Baseline metrics ✓
└─ 3:45 AM: Send all-clear ✓

RESULT:
├─ Successful upgrade
├─ User-facing downtime: <5 minutes (transparent via API gateway)
├─ Total maintenance window: 1.75 hours
└─ Ready for next month's patches
Say this in interview "AKS upgrade: check current version (az aks show), available versions (az aks get-upgrades), backup etcd. Phase 1: Control plane upgrade (Azure managed, 15-30 min, API briefly unavailable). Phase 2: Node pools upgraded one-by-one (cordoned, pods evicted gracefully, RTO ~5 min per node). Pre-upgrade: verify PDB, no stuck pods. Post-upgrade: verify nodes Ready, pods Running, app health (curl). No rollback—test in dev first. Watch: kubectl get nodes, kubectl get pods -w. Example: prod cluster 5 nodes, 2 AM window, 1.28→1.29, 1.5 hrs, <5 min user downtime."
Flows 01

Kubernetes Pod Lifecycle

Flow Diagrams

Kubernetes Pod Lifecycle

Pod creation → Scheduling → Container start → Running → Termination. 5 phases with probe checks.

FlowPod phases›
Pod Lifecycle Flow
Developer / Controller creates Pod
    ↓ kubectl apply pod.yaml
    ↓ API Server receives → Validates → Stores in etcd
    ↓
POD PHASE 1: PENDING
├─ Status: Pod accepted, not yet scheduled
├─ Conditions: PodScheduled = False
└─ Duration: Few milliseconds
    ↓
Scheduler evaluates nodes (CPU, memory, taints, affinity)
    ↓
POD PHASE 2: PENDING (image pull phase)
├─ Status: Assigned to Node-3, waiting for kubelet
└─ Duration: Few seconds
    ↓
kubelet on Node-3 receives pod spec
    ↓
Container runtime pulls image from registry
    ↓
POD PHASE 3: CONTAINERIZING
├─ Status: Image pulled, container starting
├─ Container ID assigned
└─ Duration: Seconds to minutes
    ↓
Container started (app process running)
    ↓
STARTUP PROBE (if configured)
├─ Check: Is app starting up?
├─ Success? → Continue
└─ Failure? → Retry 3x, then CrashLoopBackOff
    ↓
POD PHASE 4: RUNNING
├─ Status: Container running, ready for traffic
└─ Duration: Indefinite (until pod terminates)
    ↓
READINESS PROBE (continuous, every 10 sec)
├─ Check: Is app ready to serve traffic?
├─ Success: Ready=True → Add to Service endpoints
└─ Failure: Ready=False → Remove from endpoints
    ↓
LIVENESS PROBE (continuous, every 10 sec)
├─ Check: Is app still alive?
├─ Success: Keep running
└─ Failure: Restart container
    ↓
Pod receives traffic via Service (normal operation)
    ↓
Termination signal received (kubectl delete, deployment update, node drain)
    ↓
POD PHASE 5: TERMINATING
├─ Status: Graceful shutdown starting
└─ Grace period: 30 seconds (default)
    ↓
PRE-STOP HOOK (if configured)
├─ Custom shutdown logic
└─ Close connections, flush logs
    ↓
SIGTERM signal sent to app process
    ↓
Wait for grace period OR app exit
    ↓
SIGKILL sent (if grace period exceeded)
    ↓
POD PHASE: SUCCEEDED/FAILED
├─ Succeeded: Exit code 0
├─ Failed: Exit code non-zero
└─ Pod removed from etcd
Say this in interview "Pod lifecycle: Pending (scheduling) → Pending (image pull) → Running (ready) → Terminating (graceful shutdown) → Succeeded/Failed. Probes: Startup (is starting?), Readiness (ready for traffic?), Liveness (still alive?). Grace period for clean shutdown. InitContainers for setup."
Flow Diagrams

Pod Scheduling & Node Assignment

Scheduler filters nodes → Scores best → Assigns pod. Taints, tolerations, affinity rules applied.

FlowScheduling decisions›
Pod Scheduling Flow
Pod created (Pending)
    ↓
Scheduler watches for unscheduled pods
    ↓
FILTERING PHASE (eliminate incompatible nodes)
├─ Node-1: CPU insufficient → FAIL ✗
├─ Node-2: Pod doesn't tolerate taint=prod → FAIL ✗
├─ Node-3: Node selector mismatch → FAIL ✗
├─ Node-4: All checks pass ✓
└─ Node-5: All checks pass ✓
    ↓
Candidate nodes: [Node-4, Node-5]
    ↓
SCORING PHASE (rank candidates)
├─ Pod affinity: "prefer pods with label tier=frontend"
│  Node-4: Has 2 frontend pods → Score: 90/100
│  Node-5: Has 0 frontend pods → Score: 50/100
├─ Node affinity: "prefer nodes with gpu=true"
│  Node-4: Has gpu=true → Score: +20 → 110/100
│  Node-5: No GPU → Score: 50/100
└─ Final scores: Node-4: 100/100 (best) ✓
    ↓
WINNER: Node-4 selected ✓
    ↓
Pod.spec.nodeName = "Node-4"
    ↓
kubelet on Node-4 receives pod spec → Start container
Say this in interview "Scheduler filters nodes (resources, taints, selectors), scores candidates (affinity, utilization), assigns best. Taints repel pods. Tolerations allow pods on tainted nodes. Node/Pod affinity for co-location."
Flow Diagrams

Kubernetes Service Discovery & Traffic Flow

DNS → Service ClusterIP → Endpoints → Pod IPs. kube-proxy load balances via iptables/IPVS.

FlowService routing›
Service Discovery & Load Balancing Flow
Client Pod connects to "myapp.default.svc.cluster.local:80"
    ↓
kube-dns (CoreDNS) resolves hostname
├─ Query: myapp.default.svc.cluster.local
└─ Response: Service ClusterIP = 10.0.1.5
    ↓
Client connects to 10.0.1.5:80
    ↓
Packet arrives at Service IP (virtual, not on any node)
    ↓
kube-proxy on each node intercepts (iptables/IPVS)
    ↓
Service lookup: Get Endpoints object
├─ Endpoint 1: 10.1.0.1:80 (Pod-1, Ready)
├─ Endpoint 2: 10.1.0.2:80 (Pod-2, Ready)
└─ Endpoint 3: 10.1.0.3:80 (Pod-3, Ready)
    ↓
Load balancing algorithm (round-robin by default)
├─ Request 1 → Pod-1 (10.1.0.1:80)
├─ Request 2 → Pod-2 (10.1.0.2:80)
├─ Request 3 → Pod-3 (10.1.0.3:80)
└─ Request 4 → Pod-1 (10.1.0.1:80) [cycle repeats]
    ↓
iptables/IPVS performs NAT
├─ Original: Client (10.0.2.5) → Service (10.0.1.5:80)
└─ Translated: Client (10.0.2.5) → Pod (10.1.0.1:80)
    ↓
Pod application processes request → Returns response
    ↓
Response packet: Pod → Client (with reverse NAT)
    ↓
If pod is NOT Ready:
├─ Endpoint removed from Service
└─ Traffic only routed to Ready pods
Say this in interview "Service: virtual IP (ClusterIP). DNS resolves to ClusterIP. kube-proxy intercepts (iptables/IPVS), load balances across Endpoints. If pod not Ready, removed from Endpoints. Round-robin load balancing."
Flow Diagrams

Kubernetes Ingress Traffic Flow

External request → Ingress controller → Service → Endpoints → Pods. Layer 7 routing (host, path).

FlowIngress routing›
Ingress Traffic Flow Diagram
External Client (internet)
    ↓ HTTP request: Host: myapp.example.com, Path: /api/users
    ↓
DNS resolves myapp.example.com → Ingress IP (20.1.2.3)
    ↓
Client connects to Ingress Controller (NGINX/App Gateway)
    ↓
Ingress Controller reads Ingress rules
├─ Rule 1: host=myapp.example.com, path=/api/* → service=myapp-api
├─ Rule 2: host=myapp.example.com, path=/web/* → service=myapp-web
└─ Rule 3: host=admin.example.com → service=admin-panel
    ↓
Match found: Rule 1 matches (host + path)
├─ Route to service: myapp-api:80
└─ Action: Forward request
    ↓
Service lookup (myapp-api)
├─ ClusterIP: 10.0.2.4:80
├─ Endpoints (Ready):
│  ├─ 10.1.1.1:8080 (Pod-1)
│  ├─ 10.1.1.2:8080 (Pod-2)
│  └─ 10.1.1.3:8080 (Pod-3)
└─ Load balance across endpoints
    ↓
kube-proxy route (iptables/IPVS)
├─ Ingress → Service IP → Pod IP
└─ NAT applied
    ↓
Request reaches Pod → Container processes → Response sent back
    ↓
Response path (reverse): Pod → Service IP → Ingress → Client ✓
    ↓
INGRESS FEATURES:
├─ TLS termination: HTTPS → Ingress → HTTP → Service
├─ Host/Path-based routing
├─ Rewrite rules
├─ Rate limiting
└─ Authentication (OIDC, mTLS)
Say this in interview "Ingress: Layer 7 routing (host, path). DNS points to Ingress IP. Ingress controller reads rules, routes to Services. TLS termination at Ingress. Host/path-based routing. Example: myapp.com/api → myapp-api service."
Important Concepts

Terraform Operations & Troubleshooting

State file corruption recovery, drift detection, resource deletion failures. Real-world incident scenarios with commands.

OperationsState & troubleshooting›
Terraform State File Corruption: Recovery
SCENARIO: State file corrupted during failed terraform apply
Symptoms: JSON parse error, lock file stuck, terraform commands hang

# 1. Detect corruption
terraform state list
# Error: Failed to read state file (corrupted JSON)

# 2. FIRST: Restore from backup (safest!)
az storage blob download --account-name mytfstate --container-name backups \
  --name terraform.tfstate.backup --file terraform.tfstate.backup
cp terraform.tfstate.backup terraform.tfstate
terraform state list  # Verify readable

# 3. If no backup: Try manual repair (risky!)
# Try to validate state JSON:
terraform state show | head -20
# If this works partially, some state may be salvageable

# 4. Last resort: Recreate from scratch (BREAKING)
rm terraform.tfstate terraform.tfstate.lock
terraform init
# WARNING: terraform apply will try to recreate ALL resources!
# Causes outage if resources already exist

# 5. Verify state is healthy after recovery
terraform validate
terraform plan
# Should show: No changes (green)

# 6. Prevent future corruption:
# Enable Azure Storage versioning
resource "azurerm_storage_account_blob_container_properties" "terraform_state" {
  storage_account_id            = azurerm_storage_account.terraform.id
  container_name                = "tfstate"
  container_access_type         = "private"
}

resource "azurerm_storage_account" "terraform" {
  name                     = "mytfstate"
  resource_group_name      = azurerm_resource_group.example.name
  location                 = azurerm_resource_group.example.location
  account_tier             = "Standard"
  account_replication_type = "GRS"  # Geo-redundant storage

  blob_properties {
    versioning_enabled = true  # Keep history
  }
}

# Enable Azure Table Storage for lock (prevent concurrent writes)
resource "azurerm_storage_table" "terraform_lock" {
  name                 = "terraformlock"
  storage_account_name = azurerm_storage_account.terraform.name
}

# Use lock in backend:
terraform {
  backend "azurerm" {
    resource_group_name  = "my-rg"
    storage_account_name = "mytfstate"
    container_name       = "tfstate"
    key                  = "prod/terraform.tfstate"
  }
}
Manual Infrastructure Changes: Drift Detection & Remediation
SCENARIO: Developer manually scaled VMSS outside Terraform
Impact: State says 3 instances, reality is 5 (drift detected)

# 1. Detect drift
terraform plan
# Output: -/+ azurerm_linux_virtual_machine_scale_set.app
#   sku.capacity: "5" -> "3"  (will downscale!)

# 2. Option A: Force infrastructure to match Terraform (destructive!)
terraform apply
# This DESTROYS 2 instances to match state (capacity=3)
# Causes downtime!

# 3. Option B: Import manual changes into state (preserve reality)
# Step 1: Update Terraform code to reflect actual state
# In main.tf:
resource "azurerm_linux_virtual_machine_scale_set" "app" {
  name                        = "my-vmss"
  location                    = azurerm_resource_group.example.location
  resource_group_name         = azurerm_resource_group.example.name

  sku {
    name     = "Standard_B2s"
    capacity = 5  # Change from 3 to 5
  }
}

# Step 2: Import actual infrastructure into state
terraform import azurerm_linux_virtual_machine_scale_set.app \
  /subscriptions/{sub-id}/resourceGroups/my-rg/providers/Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachineScaleSets/my-vmss
# Now state matches reality (5 instances)

# Step 3: Verify
terraform plan
# Should show: No changes (clean)

# 4. Option C: Remove & re-import (if state is completely out of sync)
terraform state rm azurerm_linux_virtual_machine_scale_set.app
terraform import azurerm_linux_virtual_machine_scale_set.app \
  /subscriptions/{sub-id}/resourceGroups/my-rg/providers/Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachineScaleSets/my-vmss

# 5. Prevent drift (best practice)
# Run terraform plan regularly (CI/CD scheduled job)
# Example: Daily drift check at 2 AM via Azure Automation
resource "azurerm_automation_runbook" "terraform_drift_check" {
  name                    = "terraform-drift-check"
  location                = azurerm_resource_group.example.location
  resource_group_name     = azurerm_resource_group.example.name
  automation_account_name = azurerm_automation_account.example.name
  runbook_type            = "PowerShell"

  publish_content_link {
    uri = "file:///path/to/drift-check.ps1"
  }
}

# Schedule it: Every day at 2 AM
resource "azurerm_automation_job_schedule" "drift_check_schedule" {
  resource_group_name     = azurerm_resource_group.example.name
  automation_account_name = azurerm_automation_account.example.name
  schedule_name           = "daily-2am"
  runbook_name            = azurerm_automation_runbook.terraform_drift_check.name
}

# Alert on drift:
# if terraform plan shows changes (not expected), notify team via Action Group

# 6. Enforce: No manual changes via Azure Policy
# Deny direct VMSS scaling (except via Terraform principal)
resource "azurerm_policy_definition" "deny_vmss_manual_scaling" {
  name         = "deny-vmss-manual-scaling"
  display_name = "Deny manual VMSS scaling"

  policy_rule = jsonencode({
    if = {
      allOf = [
        { field = "type", equals = "Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachineScaleSets" }
        { field = "Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachineScaleSets/sku/capacity", notEquals = "[parameters('allowedCapacity')]" }
      ]
    }
    then = { effect = "Deny" }
  })
}
Resource Delete Failure: Diagnosis & Solutions
SCENARIO 1: Storage Account with blobs (cannot delete)
# Error: error deleting Storage Account:
# The storage account contains blob(s) which prevents deletion

# Solution A: Empty first, then delete (manual)
az storage blob delete-batch --source mycontainer \
  --account-name mystorageacct
terraform destroy  # Now succeeds

# Solution B: Terraform cleanup first
# Declare storage container resource explicitly:
resource "azurerm_storage_container" "data" {
  name                  = "mycontainer"
  storage_account_name  = azurerm_storage_account.example.name
  container_access_type = "private"
}

# When destroying, Terraform deletes container (empties blobs), then account
terraform destroy  # Correct order maintained

---

SCENARIO 2: VNet still has subnets (cannot delete)
# Error: error deleting Virtual Network:
# Subnet(s) associated with the VNet still exist

# Solution: Delete subnets first (dependency order)
resource "azurerm_subnet" "app" {
  name                 = "app-subnet"
  resource_group_name  = azurerm_resource_group.example.name
  virtual_network_name = azurerm_virtual_network.example.name
  address_prefixes     = ["10.0.1.0/24"]
}

# Terraform knows dependency: destroy subnets before VNet
terraform destroy  # Correct order maintained

---

SCENARIO 3: Key Vault with keys/secrets (cannot delete)
# Error: error deleting Key Vault:
# Vault still contains keys/secrets

# Solution: Remove keys/secrets first
resource "azurerm_key_vault_key" "example" {
  name         = "my-key"
  key_vault_id = azurerm_key_vault.example.id
  key_type     = "RSA"
  key_size     = 2048
}

resource "azurerm_key_vault_secret" "example" {
  name         = "my-secret"
  value        = "secret-value"
  key_vault_id = azurerm_key_vault.example.id
}

# Terraform knows: delete keys/secrets before vault
terraform destroy  # Correct order maintained

---

SCENARIO 4: Force delete (if stuck)
# Option: Manually remove + state cleanup

# Step 1: Manually delete in Azure Portal or CLI
az storage account delete --name mystorageacct \
  --resource-group my-rg --yes

# Step 2: Remove from Terraform state (tell Terraform to forget it)
terraform state rm azurerm_storage_account.example

# Step 3: Verify clean
terraform plan
# Should show: No changes needed

# Step 4: Re-apply if needed
terraform apply

---

BEST PRACTICE: Target destroy specific resource
# If one resource is stuck, destroy only that one:
terraform destroy -target azurerm_storage_account.example

# Useful for partial rollback without destroying all
State File Best Practices (Prevention)
# 1. Remote state (never local for prod)
terraform {
  backend "azurerm" {
    resource_group_name  = "my-rg"
    storage_account_name = "mytfstate"
    container_name       = "tfstate"
    key                  = "prod/terraform.tfstate"
  }
}

# 2. Enable encryption at rest (Azure handles automatically)
# Storage account encryption enabled by default (AES-256)

# 3. Enable versioning (recover from corruption)
resource "azurerm_storage_account_blob_container_properties" "terraform" {
  storage_account_id     = azurerm_storage_account.terraform.id
  container_name         = "tfstate"
  container_access_type  = "private"
}

resource "azurerm_storage_account" "terraform" {
  name                     = "mytfstate"
  resource_group_name      = azurerm_resource_group.example.name
  location                 = azurerm_resource_group.example.location
  account_tier             = "Standard"
  account_replication_type = "GRS"  # Geo-redundant

  blob_properties {
    versioning_enabled = true  # Keep history
  }
}

# 4. Enable lock (prevent concurrent runs)
# Use Azure Table Storage for lock:
resource "azurerm_storage_table" "terraform_lock" {
  name                 = "terraformlock"
  storage_account_name = azurerm_storage_account.terraform.name
}

terraform {
  backend "azurerm" {
    # Lock is managed via Table Storage (terraformlock table)
  }
}

# 5. RBAC: Only Terraform service role can access
# Developers cannot read/modify state directly
resource "azurerm_storage_account_management_policy" "terraform" {
  storage_account_id = azurerm_storage_account.terraform.id

  rule {
    name    = "terraform-access-control"
    enabled = true

    actions {
      base_blob {
        delete_after_days_since_modification_greater_than = 0
      }
    }
    filters {
      prefix_match = ["tfstate"]
    }
  }
}

# Use Azure RBAC (only Terraform managed identity can access)
resource "azurerm_role_assignment" "terraform_state" {
  scope              = azurerm_storage_account.terraform.id
  role_definition_name = "Storage Blob Data Contributor"
  principal_id       = azurerm_user_assigned_identity.terraform.principal_id
}

# 6. Backup state regularly
# Use Azure Backup (managed backup)
resource "azurerm_storage_account_blob_container" "backups" {
  name                  = "tfstate-backups"
  storage_account_name  = azurerm_storage_account.terraform.name
  container_access_type = "private"
}

# Or use Azure Automation to backup daily:
resource "azurerm_automation_runbook" "backup_terraform_state" {
  name                    = "backup-terraform-state"
  location                = azurerm_resource_group.example.location
  resource_group_name     = azurerm_resource_group.example.name
  automation_account_name = azurerm_automation_account.example.name
  runbook_type            = "PowerShell"

  publish_content_link {
    uri = "file:///path/to/backup-state.ps1"
  }
}

# 7. Audit logging: Track all state access
resource "azurerm_storage_account_blob_service_properties" "terraform" {
  storage_account_id = azurerm_storage_account.terraform.id

  logging {
    enabled                 = true
    delete                  = true
    read                    = true
    write                   = true
    retention_policy_days   = 90
  }
}

# 8. Never commit state to Git!
# .gitignore:
echo "terraform.tfstate" >> .gitignore
echo "terraform.tfstate.*" >> .gitignore
echo ".terraform/" >> .gitignore
Real-World Incident: 2-Hour Recovery
TIMELINE: Production infrastructure locked

00:00 - terraform apply fails (network timeout mid-apply)
       State file partially written (corrupted JSON)
       Team cannot deploy OR destroy anything (complete lock)

00:05 - Alert: "terraform plan" hangs forever
       Lock file exists but no process running (stuck)

00:10 - Debug: Inspect state file
       terraform state show
       Error: "invalid JSON at line 147"

00:15 - Action: Restore from Azure backup
       az storage blob download --account-name mytfstate \
         --container-name tfstate-backups \
         --name terraform.tfstate.20240101 \
         --file terraform.tfstate
       terraform state show  ✓ Works!

00:20 - Verify: Check what we lost
       terraform plan -json | jq '.resource_changes[]'
       Found: 5 resources in backup, current disaster-state different

00:25 - Decision: Keep backup (safest)
       Last backup was 1 hour ago (acceptable RPO)

00:30 - Re-apply: Push changes again
       terraform apply
       New apply succeeds with recovered state

00:35 - Validation:
       Production services restored ✓
       All resources in expected state ✓
       Metrics normal ✓

00:45 - Post-mortem: Implement improvements
       ├─ Enable Azure Storage versioning (should auto-recover)
       ├─ Enable Table Storage lock (prevent concurrent writes)
       ├─ Set up daily backup job via Automation (1-hour RPO)
       ├─ Add Action Group alert (if state file not updated in 24hrs)
       └─ Restrict state file access (Azure RBAC)

01:00 - Implementation: Deploy fixes
       terraform apply -target azurerm_storage_account_blob_service_properties.terraform
       terraform apply -target azurerm_storage_table.terraform_lock

02:00 - Monitoring: Added alerting
       alert if: state file blob > 24hrs old without update
       alert if: lock file exists > 1 hour
       alert if: terraform apply takes > 30 min

RESULT:
├─ Total downtime: 45 minutes
├─ Root cause: No versioning, no lock, single backup
├─ Improvements: Versioning + lock + daily backups
└─ Time to prevent: ~1 hour
Say this in interview "State corruption: restore from S3 backup (enable versioning). Manual drift: detect with terraform plan, remediate by importing changes (terraform import) or updating code. Delete failures: empty resource first (aws s3 rm), use force_destroy flag, or remove from state. Best practices: remote state (S3 + DynamoDB lock), encryption, versioning, RBAC, daily backups. Never commit state to Git. Plan before apply. Example: Prod locked by corruption, recovered via S3 backup in 45 min, fixed with versioning + lock + audit."
Flow Diagrams

Zero-Trust / Defence-in-Depth Network Flow

Every request verified at every layer. Network policies, encryption, identity checks.

Flow8 security layers›
8 Defence Layers
LAYER 1: Perimeter (DDoS, WAF, Port filtering)
    ↓
LAYER 2: Network Segmentation (VNet, NSG, Private Endpoints)
    ↓
LAYER 3: Encryption in Transit (TLS, mTLS, Certificate pinning)
    ↓
LAYER 4: Identity & Authentication (Token validation, OAuth)
    ↓
LAYER 5: Authorization (RBAC - Cluster Admin, Developer, Reader)
    ↓
LAYER 6: Service-to-Service (mTLS, AuthorizationPolicy)
    ↓
LAYER 7: Application Logic (Input validation, Rate limiting)
    ↓
LAYER 8: Data Encryption at Rest (DB encryption, Key Vault)
    ↓
KEY PRINCIPLES:
├─ Trust nothing by default
├─ Verify every request at every layer
├─ Least privilege: Only permissions needed
├─ Assume breach: Detect anomalies
└─ Defense in depth: Multiple layers
Say this in interview "Zero-trust: 8 layers - Perimeter → Network → Encryption → Identity → RBAC → mTLS → App logic → Data encryption. Verify every request. Assume breach. Encrypt everything. Least privilege."
Flow Diagrams

Secret Management (AKV + CSI Driver)

Secrets in Key Vault. CSI mounts to pods. Automatic rotation. No hardcoding.

FlowAKV lifecycle›
AKV + CSI Driver Flow
Admin creates secret in Key Vault
├─ Secret: "db-password" = "P@ssw0rd123!" (encrypted)
├─ Rotation: Every 90 days
└─ All access logged
    ↓
Pod mounts secret via CSI driver
├─ Authenticate: Managed identity (no password)
├─ Authorize: Check pod permissions
└─ Fetch secret from AKV
    ↓
CSI mounts to pod filesystem
├─ Path: /mnt/secrets/db-password
├─ Permissions: Owner read-only (0600)
└─ Secret never on disk (memory only)
    ↓
App reads secret → Connects to database
    ↓
SECRET ROTATION (automatic, 90 days)
├─ Admin updates AKV
├─ CSI detects change (~15 min)
├─ Update file: /mnt/secrets/db-password
└─ App re-reads on next connection
    ↓
BENEFITS:
├─ No secrets in YAML/Git
├─ No secrets in env vars
├─ Audit trail: Who accessed when
├─ Automatic rotation
└─ Pod isolation
Say this in interview "AKV stores secrets (encrypted). CSI driver mounts via managed identity. App reads from /mnt/secrets. Rotation automatic (90 days). No secrets in YAML. Audit trail. Pod isolation. Example: DB password rotated without pod restart."
Flow Diagrams

GitOps Sync Loop (ArgoCD)

Git = single source of truth. ArgoCD syncs continuously. Drift detection.

FlowReconciliation loop›
GitOps Sync Flow
Developer commits: replicas: 2 → 3
    ↓
ArgoCD polls Git (every 3 min)
    ↓
Fetch desired state (from Git): replicas: 3
    ↓
Compare desired vs actual
├─ Desired (Git): 3
├─ Actual (cluster): 2
└─ DRIFT DETECTED ⚠️
    ↓
Sync mode decision
├─ Auto-sync: Apply automatically
└─ Manual: Wait for approval
    ↓
kubectl apply (if approved)
    ↓
Deployment creates Pod-3
    ↓
Verify: replicas: 3, all ready
    ↓
Sync status: SUCCESS ✓ (green)
    ↓
ROLLBACK SCENARIO:
    ↓
Manual change outside Git (kubectl scale --replicas=1)
    ↓
Next ArgoCD reconciliation detects drift
    ↓
Auto-sync enabled: Fix automatically (scale back to 3)
    ↓
Result: Git always matches cluster ✓
Say this in interview "GitOps: Git is source of truth. ArgoCD polls repo, compares desired vs actual (drift detection). Auto-sync fixes drift automatically. Manual-sync waits for approval. Rollback: git revert + auto-sync."
Flow Diagrams

Terraform Apply Flow

Parse → Load state → Build graph → Plan → Apply. Lock prevents concurrent modifies.

FlowApply workflow›
Terraform Apply Flow
terraform apply
    ↓
PARSE: Read .tf files → Validate syntax
    ↓
LOAD STATE: Fetch from remote backend
    ↓
BUILD GRAPH: Determine resource dependencies
    ↓
PLAN: Compare desired (code) vs actual (state)
├─ web_instance: not in code → DELETE
├─ security_group: min_rules 4→5 → MODIFY
└─ database: new in code → CREATE
    ↓
Show plan: "1 to add, 1 to change, 1 to destroy"
    ↓
Developer approves
    ↓
LOCK STATE: Acquire lock (DynamoDB)
    ↓
APPLY (execute in dependency order):
├─ CREATE database
├─ MODIFY security_group
└─ DESTROY web_instance
    ↓
Update state with new resource IDs
    ↓
UNLOCK STATE
    ↓
Display outputs
    ↓
RESULT: Infrastructure matches code ✓
    ↓
NEXT: terraform plan
└─ If match: "No changes"
└─ If mismatch: Show drift (manual changes)
Say this in interview "Terraform: Parse → Load state → Build graph → Plan → Lock → Apply (in order) → Update state → Unlock. State is source of truth. Lock prevents concurrent writes. Rollback: destroy or git revert + apply."
Flow Diagrams

Load Balancer Traffic Routing

Client → LB → Health checks → Active backends. Round-robin, least connections, IP hash.

FlowLB routing›
Load Balancer Flow
Clients request: myapp.example.com
    ↓
DNS → LB IP (20.1.2.3)
    ↓
LB receives requests
    ↓
HEALTH CHECK (every 10 sec)
├─ Backend-1: GET /health → 200 OK ✓ ACTIVE
├─ Backend-2: GET /health → 200 OK ✓ ACTIVE
└─ Backend-3: GET /health → 500 ERROR ✗ UNHEALTHY
    ↓
After 3 failed checks:
└─ Backend-3 REMOVED from pool
    ↓
TRAFFIC ROUTING (Backend-1 & Backend-2 only)
├─ Request 1 → Backend-1 (round-robin)
├─ Request 2 → Backend-2
├─ Request 3 → Backend-1
└─ Continue cycling...
    ↓
SESSION PERSISTENCE
├─ Sticky sessions enabled: Same client → same backend
└─ Disabled: May switch backends
    ↓
BACKEND-3 RECOVERY
├─ 3 successful health checks
└─ RE-ADDED to pool
    ↓
ALGORITHMS:
├─ Round-robin: 1→2→3→1→2→3
├─ Least connections: Route to backend with fewest active
├─ IP hash: Same IP → same backend
└─ Weighted: Backend-1: 50%, Backend-2: 30%, Backend-3: 20%
    ↓
SCALING: Add new backend
└─ Zero downtime (LB immediately routes traffic)
Say this in interview "LB: Health checks every 10 sec (GET /health). Remove unhealthy backend after 3 failed checks. Round-robin to healthy backends. Session persistence via sticky sessions or IP hash. Scale by adding/removing backends without downtime."
Flow Diagrams

Blue-Green Deployment

Blue (v1.0) active + Green (v2.0) parallel. Instant switch. Fast rollback. Zero downtime.

FlowZero-downtime deploy›
Blue-Green Flow
BLUE (v1.0) ACTIVE
├─ 3 pods running
└─ Traffic: 100% → Blue
    ↓
Deploy GREEN (v2.0) PARALLEL
├─ 3 pods created
└─ Traffic: 0% (testing phase)
    ↓
QA validates Green
├─ Health checks: ✓
├─ Functional tests: ✓
├─ Load test: ✓
└─ All tests passed ✓
    ↓
TRAFFIC SWITCH (THE FLIP)
├─ Service selector changes
└─ Traffic: 100% → Green (instantly)
    ↓
MONITOR (5 minutes)
├─ Error rate: Normal ✓
├─ Latency: Normal ✓
├─ Metrics: Normal ✓
└─ All green ✓
    ↓
SUCCESS SCENARIO:
├─ Blue kept for 30 min (grace period)
├─ After 30 min: No issues
└─ Delete Blue deployment
    ↓
ROLLBACK SCENARIO:
    ↓
5 seconds after switch:
├─ Error spike detected ⚠️
├─ Blue still running (grace period active)
└─ Switch traffic BACK to Blue (instant)
    ↓
Result:
├─ Error rate drops immediately ✓
└─ Rollback complete in 5 seconds
    ↓
BENEFITS:
├─ Zero downtime
├─ Instant rollback (seconds)
├─ Full validation before traffic
└─ Cost: 2x resources (both versions running)
Say this in interview "Blue-Green: v1.0 (Blue, active) + v2.0 (Green, parallel). Validate Green fully. Instant traffic switch. Monitor 5 min. If OK: delete Blue. If issues: rollback (5 seconds). Zero downtime, fast rollback, 2x resources."
Important Concepts

Canary & Blue-Green Deployment: Implementation Steps

Two deployment strategies: Blue-Green (instant, fast rollback) vs Canary (gradual, lower risk). Commands, YAML, real examples.

OperationsDeployment strategies›
Blue-Green: Step-by-Step
# 1. Deploy Blue (v1.0) - Current Production
kubectl apply -f deployment-blue-v1.0.yaml

# 2. Verify Blue Running
kubectl get pods -l app=myapp,version=blue

# 3. Deploy Green (v2.0) - New Version PARALLEL
kubectl apply -f deployment-green-v2.0.yaml

# 4. Wait for Green Ready
kubectl wait --for=condition=Ready pod -l version=green --timeout=300s

# 5. Smoke Test Green
kubectl run test-pod --image=curlimages/curl -it --rm --restart=Never -- \
  curl http://myapp-green-service/health
# Expected: 200 OK

# 6. Load Test Green (optional)
kubectl run load-test --image=grafana/k6 -it --rm --restart=Never -- \
  k6 run load-test.js --vus 100 --duration 5m

# 7. SWITCH Traffic to Green (1 command!)
kubectl patch service myapp -p '{"spec":{"selector":{"version":"green"}}}'
# All traffic → Green instantly

# 8. Monitor Green (5 minutes)
kubectl logs -l version=green -f
# Watch: No errors, expected behavior

# 9. Check Metrics
kubectl top pods -l version=green
# Expected: Normal CPU/Memory

# 10A (Success): Delete Blue
kubectl delete deployment myapp-blue

# 10B (Rollback): Revert to Blue
kubectl patch service myapp -p '{"spec":{"selector":{"version":"blue"}}}'
# Back to Blue in < 5 seconds ✓
Canary: Step-by-Step
# 1. Deploy Canary (v2.0) Parallel to Stable (v1.0)
kubectl apply -f deployment-canary-v2.0.yaml

# 2. Wait for Canary Ready
kubectl wait --for=condition=Ready pod -l version=canary --timeout=300s

# 3. Route 5% Traffic to Canary
kubectl apply -f virtualservice-canary-5percent.yaml
# VirtualService: weight stable=95%, canary=5%

# 4. Monitor Canary (5-10 minutes)
kubectl logs -l version=canary -f
# Watch for:
# - Error rate (should be ~0%)
# - Latency (should be <200ms)
# - CPU/Memory (should be normal)

# Abort if:
# - Error rate > 1%
# - Latency > 200ms (vs baseline)
# - CPU/Memory > 80%
# Then: kubectl apply -f virtualservice-stable-100percent.yaml

# 5. Increase to 25% Traffic
kubectl apply -f virtualservice-canary-25percent.yaml
# weight stable=75%, canary=25%
# Monitor 5-10 min

# 6. Increase to 50% Traffic
kubectl apply -f virtualservice-canary-50percent.yaml
# weight stable=50%, canary=50%
# Monitor 10 min

# 7. Increase to 100% Traffic
kubectl apply -f virtualservice-canary-100percent.yaml
# weight stable=0%, canary=100%
# All traffic on Canary (v2.0)

# 8. Final Monitoring
kubectl logs -l version=canary -f
# Expected: No errors, normal metrics

# 9. Delete Stable Version
kubectl delete deployment myapp-stable
# Deployment complete ✓

# Total time: 30-60 minutes (vs Blue-Green's 5-10 min)
Comparison Table
Feature                Blue-Green       Canary
─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Traffic Switch         Instant (all)    Gradual (5%→100%)
Rollback Speed         <5 seconds       5-10 minutes
Risk Level             Higher           Lower
Time to 100%           5-10 min         30-60 min
Resource Usage         2x               1.5x
Early Bug Detection    No               Yes
Cost                   Higher           Lower
─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Best For               Non-critical     Critical
                       Confident QA     Need Validation
Real-World Examples
BLUE-GREEN: E-Commerce New Wishlist Feature
├─ Blue (v1.0): No wishlist
├─ Green (v2.0): Wishlist feature
├─ Switch → All users get wishlist
├─ If bug: Rollback to v1.0 (instant)
└─ Time: 10 minutes

CANARY: E-Commerce New Payment System (CRITICAL)
├─ Stable (v1.0): Old payment system
├─ Canary (v2.0): New payment system
├─ Route 5% traffic (10 min) → No payment failures ✓
├─ Route 25% traffic (10 min) → All checks pass ✓
├─ Route 50% traffic (10 min) → Latency OK ✓
├─ Route 100% traffic → All users on new system
└─ Time: 45 minutes (safe, validated)
Say this in interview "Blue-Green: Deploy v2.0 parallel to v1.0. Full test. Instant Service selector switch (all traffic). Monitor 5 min. If OK: delete v1.0. If issue: instant rollback (5 sec). Zero downtime, fast rollback, 2x resources. Canary: Deploy v2.0 parallel. Route 5% traffic. Monitor metrics (error rate, latency). Increase gradually (5%→25%→50%→100%) over 30-60 min. Lower risk. Good for critical changes. Blue-Green for confident/tested changes, Canary for critical/high-risk changes."
